Book One

Tell me, Muse, of the man of many turns, who was driven far and wide after he sacked the holy citadel of Troy. He saw the cities of many peoples and came to know their minds, and on the sea he suffered many griefs in his own heart, fighting for his life and for the homecoming of his companions. Even so he did not save them, hard as he tried: they died by their own recklessness, the fools, who ate the cattle of Helios the Sun, and the god took from them the day of their return. Of these things, goddess, daughter of Zeus, begin where you will, and tell them to us.

By now all the others who had escaped being killed outright were home, safe from the war and the sea. He alone was still kept from his wife and his own country, held by the queenly nymph Calypso, a great goddess, in her hollow caves, because she wanted him for a husband. But when the years came round and the season arrived that the gods had marked for his return to Ithaca, he was not free of his troubles even then, even among his own people. All the gods pitied him except Poseidon, who stayed furious with godlike Odysseus until at last he reached his own land.

Now Poseidon had gone off to the Ethiopians, who live at the ends of the earth in two divisions, some where the Sun goes down and some where he rises, to receive a sacrifice of bulls and rams, and he was sitting at the feast enjoying himself. The rest of the gods were gathered in the halls of Olympian Zeus, and the father of gods and men was the first to speak. He was thinking of Aegisthus, whom Orestes, Agamemnon's famous son, had killed; and with that in his mind he said to the immortals:

"It is astonishing how ready human beings are to blame the gods. Everything bad comes from us, they say — when in fact it is their own recklessness that brings them suffering beyond their share. Look at Aegisthus. He took the wife of the son of Atreus, beyond his share, and killed the man when he came home, and he knew what it would cost him: we warned him ourselves. We sent Hermes, the sharp-eyed slayer of Argus, to tell him not to kill the man and not to court the wife, because Orestes would come to avenge the son of Atreus once he was grown and wanted his own land. That is what Hermes told him, meaning it kindly, and he could not persuade the heart of Aegisthus. Now Aegisthus has paid for all of it at once."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Father of us all, son of Cronos, highest of the powers, that man is lying where he deserves to lie, and so may anyone else die who does what he did. But my heart is torn for Odysseus, who is clever and unlucky, and who has been suffering a long way from anyone who loves him, on an island in the middle of the sea, where the navel of the water is. There is a wooded island, and a goddess lives there, the daughter of Atlas, who thinks bad thoughts and knows the depths of the whole sea, and who holds up the tall pillars that keep earth and sky apart. It is his daughter who keeps that unhappy man from going home. She works on him, day after day, with soft coaxing words, so that he will forget Ithaca. And Odysseus, who would be glad just to see the smoke going up from his own country, wants to die.

"Does none of this touch you, Olympian? Did Odysseus not do everything for you beside the Argive ships in the wide land of Troy? Why are you so set against him, Zeus?"

Then Zeus who gathers the clouds answered her. "My child, what a thing to let past your teeth. How could I forget godlike Odysseus, who has more sense than any man alive, and who gave more than any man to the immortal gods who hold the wide sky? It is Poseidon, who holds the earth, who keeps up this stubborn anger, because of the Cyclops whose eye Odysseus put out — godlike Polyphemus, the strongest of all the Cyclopes. The nymph Thoosa bore him, the daughter of Phorcys who rules the restless sea, after she lay with Poseidon in the hollow caves. That is why Poseidon the earth-shaker does not kill Odysseus, but keeps him wandering, away from his own country.

"Come, though — let all of us here work out how he is to get home. Poseidon will let his anger go. He will not be able to hold out alone against the will of every immortal."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Father of us all, son of Cronos, highest of the powers, if it truly is the pleasure of the blessed gods now that clever Odysseus should get home, then let us send Hermes the messenger, the slayer of Argus, to the island of Ogygia, to tell the nymph with the lovely hair, as quickly as he can, what we have firmly decided: that Odysseus, who endures everything, is to go home. And I will go to Ithaca myself, to stir up his son and put some courage into him — to have him call the long-haired Achaeans to an assembly and speak his mind to the suitors, who go on killing his crowding sheep and his slow-moving cattle. And I will send him to Sparta and to sandy Pylos to ask after his father's homecoming, in case he hears something, and so that men may think well of him."

With that she bound on her feet the beautiful sandals, immortal and golden, that carry her over the water and over the endless land as fast as a gust of wind, and she took up her heavy spear, tipped with sharp bronze, huge and strong, the spear she breaks the ranks of fighting men with when she is angry with them, she who is the daughter of a mighty father. Then she went darting down from the peaks of Olympus and stood in Ithaca, at the outer gate of Odysseus, on the threshold of the courtyard, holding the bronze spear in her hand, in the likeness of a stranger — Mentes, the chief of the Taphians.

She found the arrogant suitors there. They were playing draughts in front of the doors, sitting on the hides of oxen they had killed themselves, while the heralds and the busy attendants worked round them, some mixing wine and water in the bowls, some wiping the tables down with sponges and setting them out, and some carving up a great deal of meat.

Telemachus saw her long before anyone else. He was sitting among the suitors, sick at heart, imagining his noble father — how he might come from somewhere and scatter the suitors through the house, and take back his honour and rule over what was his. That was what he was thinking about, sitting among them, when he saw Athena, and he went straight to the outer door, ashamed in his own mind that a stranger should be left standing at the gate. He went up to her and took her right hand and took the bronze spear from her, and spoke to her with winged words:

"Welcome, stranger. You will be treated well in this house, and when you have eaten you can tell us what you need."

He led the way and Pallas Athena followed him. When they were inside the high house he carried the spear to a tall pillar and set it in the polished spear-rack, where a good many other spears already stood, the spears of Odysseus, who endures everything. He led Athena to a chair and sat her down, spreading a linen cloth under her — a beautiful chair, finely made, with a footstool below it — and drew up an inlaid seat for himself beside her, away from the others, so that the noise of the arrogant crowd would not spoil the stranger's meal, and so that he could ask him about his father, who was gone.

A maid brought water in a lovely golden jug and poured it over a silver basin for them to wash their hands, and drew a polished table up beside them. The housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it out, and plenty of good things with it, giving freely from her stores. A carver lifted platters of every kind of meat and set them down, and put out golden cups, and a herald kept walking to and fro pouring the wine.

Then the arrogant suitors came in and sat down in rows on the chairs and the high seats. Heralds poured water over their hands, women heaped bread in baskets beside them, and boys filled the bowls to the brim, and they put out their hands to the good food in front of them. When they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, they turned their minds to other things, to singing and dancing, which are the crown of a feast. A herald put a beautiful lyre into the hands of Phemius, who sang for the suitors because they made him. He struck the strings and began his lovely song.

But Telemachus leaned his head close to grey-eyed Athena, so that the others would not hear, and said:

"Dear guest, will you be angry with me if I say something? These men care about nothing but this, the lyre and the singing — easily enough, when they are eating up another man's living and paying nothing for it, a man whose white bones are rotting in the rain somewhere on land, or rolling in the surf. If they saw him come back to Ithaca, every one of them would pray to be quicker on his feet rather than richer in gold and fine clothes. As it is, he has died badly, and there is no comfort for us, not even if somebody somewhere on earth says he is coming. The day of his homecoming is gone.

"But tell me this, and tell me the truth. Who are you, and where do you come from? Where is your city, and where are your parents? What sort of ship did you come on, and how did the sailors bring you to Ithaca? Who did they say they were? Because you certainly did not get here on foot. And tell me this too, so that I know: is this your first time here, or were you a guest-friend of my father's? A great many men used to come to this house — he went about among people himself."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "I will tell you all of it plainly. I am Mentes, son of Anchialus, who was a wise man, and I am lord of the Taphians, who love the oar. I have put in here with my ship and my crew, as you see, sailing over the wine-dark sea to people of another language, on my way to Temese for copper; I am carrying shining iron. My ship is lying out there beyond the town, away in the fields, in the harbour of Rheithron, under wooded Neion. Our families are guest-friends, and have been from the old days. Go and ask the old soldier Laertes, if you like. They tell me he does not come into the town any more, but suffers out in the country with an old woman to look after him, who puts food and drink in front of him when his legs give out from dragging himself along the slope of his vineyard.

"I came because they told me he was back among his people — your father, I mean. But it seems the gods are keeping him from the road home. Because noble Odysseus is not dead, not yet, not anywhere on this earth. He is alive, I am sure of it, and held back on the wide sea, on an island in the middle of the water, and hard men have him, savages, and they are keeping him there against his will.

"Now I will make you a prophecy, the way the immortals put it in my mind, and the way I think it will turn out — though I am no prophet and I know nothing about the meaning of birds. He will not be away from his own country much longer. Not even if they had him in chains. He will work out a way home; he is a man who works things out.

"But tell me this, and tell me the truth. Tall as you are, are you really the son of Odysseus himself? Your head and your fine eyes are astonishingly like his. We were often together, before he sailed for Troy, where the rest of them went too, the best of the Argives, in their hollow ships. Since that day I have not seen Odysseus, and he has not seen me."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "I will tell you plainly too, stranger. My mother says I am his son. I do not know myself. Nobody has ever known for certain who fathered him. I wish I had been the son of some lucky man who grew old among his own possessions. But since you ask me, they say I am the son of the unluckiest man who ever lived."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Well, the gods have not given you a nameless family for the future, if Penelope bore a son like you. But tell me this, and tell me the truth. What is this feast, this crowd? What do you want with it? Is it a drinking party, or a wedding? It is certainly not a meal that each man brought his share to. They seem to me to be eating in your house with an insolence past bearing. Any decent man coming in here and seeing all this would be disgusted."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Stranger, since you ask me about it — this house was once on its way to being rich and respected, as long as that man was still among his people. But the gods have willed it otherwise, and thought up something worse: they have made him vanish as they have made no other man vanish. I would not grieve so much for his death, if he had been killed among his companions in the land of the Trojans, or had died in the arms of his friends when he had wound up the whole skein of the war. Then the whole Achaean army would have built him a tomb, and he would have won great glory for his son as well, in the days to come. As it is, the storm winds have taken him away and left nothing behind. He is out of sight, out of hearing, and what he has left me is pain and weeping.

"And I do not grieve and cry only for him. The gods have brought me other troubles as bad. All the princes who hold power over the islands — Dulichium and Same and wooded Zacynthus — and the ones who lord it over rocky Ithaca, every one of them is courting my mother and wearing out my house. And she will not refuse the marriage, which she hates, and cannot bring the matter to an end, while they eat up everything I own. Before long they will finish me too."

Then Pallas Athena, angry now, said to him: "This is disgraceful. You need Odysseus back, and badly, to lay his hands on these shameless men. If only he would come now and stand at the outer gate of the house with his helmet and shield and his two spears — the man I first saw in our house, drinking and enjoying himself, on his way back from Ephyre, from the house of Ilus son of Mermerus. Odysseus had gone there in his fast ship looking for a poison to smear on his bronze arrowheads, and Ilus would not give it to him, because he was afraid of the gods who live for ever; but my father gave it to him, because he was extraordinarily fond of him. If Odysseus in that condition were to walk in among the suitors, every one of them would find a quick death and a bitter courtship.

"But all this lies on the knees of the gods, whether he comes back and takes his revenge in his own halls or whether he does not. What I want you to think about is how you are going to drive the suitors out of the house. Listen to me now, and take in what I say. Tomorrow morning call the Achaean lords to an assembly and speak your mind to all of them, with the gods as your witnesses. Tell the suitors to scatter, each to his own place. As for your mother, if her heart is set on marrying, let her go back to the house of her powerful father, and they will make the wedding there and put together the gifts, all of them, everything that ought to go with a much-loved daughter. And I will give you good advice for yourself, if you will take it.

"Fit out the best ship you have with twenty rowers, and go and look for news of your father, who has been gone so long. Somebody may tell you something, or you may hear a word from Zeus, which is what most often brings men news. Go first to Pylos and ask noble Nestor, and from there to Sparta, to fair-haired Menelaus, who was the last of the bronze-armoured Achaeans to get home. If you hear your father is alive and on his way, then you could hold out for another year, hard as it is. And if you hear he is dead and gone, then come back to your own country, heap up a mound for him, pay him the funeral rites, all of them, as is proper, and give your mother to a husband. Then, when all that is done and finished, think hard about how you are going to kill the suitors in your own hall, by a trick or in the open. You are not the right age any more to be behaving like a child.

"Or have you not heard what a name Orestes made for himself, everywhere, when he killed the man who murdered his father — sly Aegisthus, who had killed his glorious father? You too, my friend — and I can see you are tall and good-looking — be brave, so that men who are not born yet will speak well of you.

"But I must go down now to my fast ship and my crew, who will be getting impatient waiting for me. Look after yourself, and think about what I have said."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Stranger, you have said all this out of kindness, the way a father speaks to his son, and I will not forget any of it. But stay a little, however eager you are to be off. Have a bath, and enjoy yourself, and then go back to your ship in good spirits with a gift from me, something valuable and very beautiful, to keep as an heirloom — the kind of gift friends give to friends."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Do not hold me here any longer. I want to be off. Whatever gift your heart tells you to give me, give it to me when I come back, to take home with me. Choose a really beautiful one; you will get its worth in return."

And with that grey-eyed Athena was gone, flying upward like a bird. She had put strength and courage into him, and made him think about his father even more than before. He turned the thing over in his mind and was amazed, because he understood that she had been a god; and he went straight back among the suitors, godlike himself.

The famous singer was still singing to them, and they sat in silence, listening. He was singing of the Achaeans' journey home from Troy, the bitter one that Pallas Athena laid on them. And upstairs the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, heard the wonderful song, and came down the high staircase from her room, not alone — two women came with her. When she reached the suitors, that lovely woman, she stood by the doorpost of the well-built hall, holding her shining veil in front of her face, with a faithful maid on either side of her. Then she began to cry, and said to the divine singer:

"Phemius, you know many other things to hold an audience with, the deeds of men and gods that singers make famous. Sing them one of those, and let them drink their wine in silence. But stop this miserable song. It tears at the heart in my breast every time, because a grief no one could forget has come to me above all women. That is the head I always remember and long for — my husband, whose fame goes wide through Hellas and the middle of Argos."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Mother, why do you grudge the singer the right to entertain us however his heart moves him? Singers are not to blame. Zeus is to blame, I think, who gives to men who work for their living, to each one as he pleases. There is no being angry with this man for singing the bad end of the Danaans. People always praise the newest song they hear most highly. Make your own heart and mind endure the listening. Odysseus was not the only man who lost the day of his homecoming at Troy. Plenty of others died as well.

"Go back to your room and see to your own work, the loom and the distaff, and tell the women to get on with theirs. Talking will be the men's business, all of them, and mine most of all: the authority in this house is mine."

She went back upstairs astonished, taking her son's good sense to heart. She went up to her room with her women, and there she cried for Odysseus, her dear husband, until grey-eyed Athena put sweet sleep on her eyelids.

The suitors broke into uproar through the shadowy hall, and every one of them prayed to be the man who lay beside her. Then clear-headed Telemachus was the first to speak.

"Suitors of my mother, with your intolerable arrogance — let us enjoy the feast for now, and no shouting, since it is a fine thing to listen to a singer as good as this man, whose voice is like a god's. But in the morning let us go to the assembly and take our places, all of us, so that I can tell you outright to get out of these halls. Make your feasts somewhere else. Eat your own food, and move from house to house. Or if this looks better to you and more profitable, that one man's living should be destroyed and nothing paid for it, then eat it. But I will call on the gods who live for ever, in the hope that Zeus grants that the work be paid for; and then you will die inside this house, and nothing will be paid for that either."

So he spoke, and every one of them bit his lip and was astonished at Telemachus, at how boldly he had spoken.

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered him: "Telemachus, it must be the gods themselves teaching you to talk so big and to speak so boldly. I hope the son of Cronos never makes you king in sea-girt Ithaca, which is yours by birth."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Antinous, will you be angry with me if I say something? I would be glad to take even that from the hand of Zeus. Do you think it is the worst thing that can happen to a man? It is no bad thing to be a king. Your house grows rich straight away and you are held in more honour yourself. But there are plenty of other Achaean kings in sea-girt Ithaca, young ones and old ones, and one of them may well have the place, now that noble Odysseus is dead. What I will be is master of my own house, and of the slaves that noble Odysseus won for me."

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered him: "Telemachus, this lies on the knees of the gods, which of the Achaeans is to be king in sea-girt Ithaca. Your possessions you may keep, and be master in your own house. No man will ever come and take what is yours away from you by force, against your will, while there are men living in Ithaca. But I should like to ask you about the stranger, my friend. Where does the man come from? What country does he say he belongs to? Where is his family, and the fields he was born on? Does he bring some news of your father coming home, or did he come here on business of his own? How he jumped up and was gone at once! He did not wait to be recognised — and he did not look like a common man, either."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Eurymachus, my father's homecoming is lost and gone. I no longer believe any news, wherever it comes from, and I take no notice of the prophecies my mother gets out of some seer she has called into the hall. That stranger is a guest-friend of my father's from Taphos. He says he is Mentes, son of Anchialus, who was a wise man, and that he is lord of the Taphians, who love the oar."

That is what Telemachus said. But in his heart he knew the immortal goddess.

The suitors turned to dancing and to the pleasure of singing, and enjoyed themselves, and waited for the evening to come; and while they were enjoying themselves the dark evening came. Then they went off, each man to his own house, to sleep. But Telemachus went up to his bedroom, which had been built in the beautiful courtyard, high up, with a wide view all round it, and he went to bed there with a great deal on his mind. Faithful Eurycleia went with him, carrying blazing torches — the daughter of Ops, son of Peisenor. Laertes had bought her long ago with his own money, when she was a girl, and had paid the price of twenty oxen for her, and he honoured her in his house as much as he honoured his own faithful wife, but he never slept with her, because he was afraid of his wife's anger. She was the one who carried the blazing torches for Telemachus. Of all the women she loved him most, and she had nursed him when he was small.

He opened the doors of the well-built room and sat down on the bed and pulled off his soft tunic and put it into the wise old woman's hands, and she folded it and smoothed it and hung it on a peg beside the corded bedstead, and then went out of the room, pulling the door to by its silver handle and driving the bolt home with the strap. And there, all night, wrapped in a fleece of wool, he thought about the journey Athena had shown him.

Book Two

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, the son of Odysseus got out of bed and put on his clothes. He slung his sharp sword over his shoulder, bound the fine sandals under his shining feet, and came out of his room looking like a god. Straight away he told the clear-voiced heralds to call the long-haired Achaeans to an assembly. They made the call and the men gathered quickly.

When they had come together, Telemachus went out to the meeting place with a bronze spear in his hand — not alone; two swift dogs came with him. And Athena poured a marvellous grace over him, so that the whole crowd stared as he came. He sat down in his father's seat, and the elders made room for him.

The first to speak among them was lord Aegyptius, an old man bent with age who knew a great deal. He spoke because a son of his had gone in the hollow ships to Ilium, famous for horses, with godlike Odysseus — Antiphus, who was a fighter. The savage Cyclops had killed him in his hollow cave and made his last meal of him. He had three other sons: one, Eurynomus, went about with the suitors, and two kept the family farm. Even so he could not stop thinking of the one who was gone, grieving and sorrowing for him, and it was with tears in his eyes that he spoke to the assembly.

"Listen to me, men of Ithaca, and hear what I have to say. We have not held an assembly or a session since the day noble Odysseus went off in the hollow ships. Who has called us together now? What need has come on him, on one of the young men or one of the older ones? Has he heard some news of the army coming back, that he could tell us plainly, having heard it first himself? Or is there some other public business he means to raise? He seems to me a good man, and a fortunate one. May Zeus bring to pass for him whatever good thing his heart is set on."

He spoke, and the son of Odysseus was glad at the words, and took them for an omen. He did not stay in his seat any longer; he was eager to speak. He stood up in the middle of the assembly, and the herald Peisenor, a man of good sense, put the staff into his hands. Then he spoke, turning first to the old man.

"Old man, the one who called this gathering is not far off, as you will see for yourself. It is I. Trouble has come on me more than on anyone. I have heard no news of the army coming home that I could tell you plainly, having heard it first myself, and there is no other public business I mean to raise. It is my own need, and the evil that has fallen on my house — two evils. First I have lost my noble father, who was once king over you here and was gentle as a father to you. And now a far greater one has come, which is going to wreck my whole house before long and destroy everything I live on.

"Suitors have fastened on my mother against her will, the sons of the very men who are the leading men here. They shrink from going to the house of her father Icarius, so that he can fix the bride-price for his daughter himself and give her to whichever of them he chooses, the one he likes. Instead they crowd into our house day after day, killing our oxen and our sheep and our fat goats, feasting, drinking the bright wine as though it were nothing — and all this wealth is being wasted, because there is no man here as Odysseus was, to keep ruin away from the house. I am nothing like him, not at all. If I try, I shall only be found weak and knowing nothing about fighting. Truly, I would defend myself if I had the strength, because what is being done now is past bearing, and my house has been destroyed past all decency.

"Be ashamed of yourselves. Think what the neighbours living round about will say. And be afraid of the anger of the gods, in case they turn on you, disgusted at what you are doing. I beg you by Olympian Zeus, and by Themis, who breaks up the assemblies of men and calls them together — stop, my friends, and leave me alone with my bitter grief. Unless, of course, my father, noble Odysseus, once did some deliberate harm to the well-greaved Achaeans, and you are paying me back for it deliberately now by egging these men on. It would be better for me if you ate up my treasures and my flocks yourselves. If you did, there might be some compensation one day: we would go up and down the town pressing our claim and demanding our goods back until every bit was returned. As it is, you are loading griefs on my heart that there is no cure for."

That is what he said, in anger, and he threw the staff on the ground and burst into tears; and pity ran through the whole crowd. Then they all sat silent, and nobody had the heart to answer Telemachus harshly.

Only Antinous answered him. "Telemachus, you are a big talker with no check on your nerve. What a thing to say — trying to shame us, wanting to hang the blame on us. It is not the Achaean suitors who are at fault here in any way. It is your own mother, who is cleverer than any woman alive. It is three years now, and the fourth is nearly gone, since she started deceiving the hearts of the Achaeans in their breasts. She holds out hope to all of us and makes promises to each man separately, sending messages round, when her mind is on something else entirely.

"And here is another trick she thought up. She set up a great loom in the hall and started weaving, on a web fine in the thread and very wide, and said to us straight away:

"'Young men, my suitors — since noble Odysseus is dead, be patient about marrying me, however eager you are, until I finish this cloth. I do not want my spinning to be wasted. It is a shroud for lord Laertes, for the time when the cruel fate of death lays him low, so that none of the Achaean women in the country can be angry with me for letting a man who won so much lie without a shroud.'

"That is what she said, and our proud hearts agreed to it. And every day she went on weaving at that great web, and every night she had torches set beside her and unravelled it. For three years she kept the Achaeans from finding out, and fooled us. But when the fourth year came round with the turning seasons, one of her women, who knew all about it, told us, and we caught her unravelling the splendid thing. So she had to finish it, whether she wanted to or not.

"So this is the answer the suitors give you, and you may know it yourself in your own heart, and let all the Achaeans know it. Send your mother away. Tell her to marry whichever man her father says, and whoever pleases her. But if she goes on much longer wearing out the sons of the Achaeans, keeping in mind what Athena has given her above other women — skill in beautiful work, and a fine mind, and cunning tricks such as we have never heard of even in the women of the old days, the fair-haired Achaean women, Tyro and Alcmene and Mycene with the lovely crown, not one of whom had Penelope's turn of mind — well, this at least she has not thought out right. Because for just as long as she keeps to the plan the gods have now put in her heart, men will go on eating up your living and your property. She is winning herself a great name, and winning you the loss of a great deal of substance. As for us, we are not going to our own lands or anywhere else until she marries whichever of the Achaeans she chooses."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Antinous, I cannot possibly throw the woman who bore me and brought me up out of the house against her will. My father is somewhere in another country, alive or dead. It would be a bad thing for me to pay Icarius back the great price I would owe him if I sent my mother away of my own accord. Her father would make me suffer for it, and heaven would send other troubles besides, because as my mother left the house she would call down the dread Avengers on me; and men would blame me too. So I will never say that word.

"And as for you — if your own hearts are angry at this, get out of my halls and make your feasts somewhere else. Eat your own food, and move from house to house. Or if this looks better to you and more profitable, that one man's living should be destroyed and nothing paid for it, then eat it. But I will call on the gods who live for ever, in the hope that Zeus grants that the work be paid for; and then you will die inside this house, and nothing will be paid for that either."

So spoke Telemachus, and in answer Zeus, whose voice carries far, sent out two eagles, flying down from a mountain peak. For a while they went on the wind, side by side, with their wings spread wide; but when they came over the middle of the noisy assembly they wheeled about, beating their wings hard, and looked down on the heads of all of them with death in their eyes. Then they tore at each other's cheeks and necks with their talons and darted away to the right, over the houses and over the city.

The men were astonished when they saw the birds, and turned over in their minds what was going to happen. Then the old lord Halitherses, son of Mastor, spoke among them, because there was no man of his generation who knew as much about birds and could say what was fated. He meant it kindly, and he spoke to the assembly.

"Listen to me, men of Ithaca, and hear what I have to say. It is to the suitors above all that I am declaring this, since it is on them that a great disaster is rolling. Odysseus is not going to be away from his own people much longer. I think he is near now, sowing death and doom for these men, every one of them. And there are many more of us here in clear-seen Ithaca whom he will ruin. Let us think, long before that, how we may put a stop to this — or rather let them stop it themselves, which is at once the better course for them. I am not prophesying as a man untried; I know what I am saying. Everything I told Odysseus has come out as I told it, when the Argives embarked for Ilium and Odysseus of the many tricks went with them. I said he would come home in the twentieth year after suffering a great deal and losing all his companions, and unrecognised by anyone. And now it is all coming to pass."

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered him. "Old man, get up and go home and prophesy to your children, in case something bad happens to them in the days to come. I am a great deal better than you at prophesying about this. Plenty of birds go to and fro under the rays of the sun, and not all of them mean anything. As for Odysseus, he has died a long way off, and I wish you had died with him. Then you would not be spouting so much interpretation, or stirring Telemachus up in his anger, hoping for some present for your house if he happens to give you one.

"But I will say this to you outright, and it is going to happen. If you, with all your old wisdom, talk a younger man round and set him on to be angry, then in the first place it will go worse for him, and there will be nothing whatever he can do against these men here; and on you, old man, we shall lay a fine that will hurt you to pay, and it will be bitter to bear.

"And here in front of everyone I will give Telemachus this advice myself. Let him tell his mother to go back to her father's house, and they will make the wedding there and put together the gifts, all of them, everything that ought to go with a much-loved daughter. Before that, I do not think the sons of the Achaeans are going to give up their hard courting, since we are not afraid of any man in any case — not of Telemachus, for all his talk — and we take no notice of any prophecy you may deliver, old man. It will come to nothing and you will be hated all the more for it. His possessions will be eaten up, and badly, and there will be no compensation for it ever, as long as she keeps putting the Achaeans off about her marriage. And we, waiting here day after day, are rivals for her because she is so remarkable, and we do not go after other women, any one of whom each of us might suitably marry."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Eurymachus, and the rest of you lordly suitors — I am not going to beg you about this any longer or say another word about it, because now the gods know and all the Achaeans know. But give me a fast ship and twenty companions who can take me there and back. I am going to Sparta and to sandy Pylos to ask after my father's homecoming, who has been gone so long — in case any mortal man can tell me something, or I hear a word from Zeus, which is what most often brings men news. If I hear my father is alive and on his way, then I could hold out for another year, hard as it is. And if I hear he is dead and gone, then I will come back to my own country, heap up a mound for him, pay him the funeral rites, all of them, as is proper, and give my mother to a husband."

With that he sat down, and Mentor stood up among them, a companion of noble Odysseus. When Odysseus sailed away he had put his whole household in the old man's charge, to obey him and to keep everything safe. He meant it kindly, and he spoke to the assembly.

"Listen to me, men of Ithaca, and hear what I have to say. From now on let no sceptred king be deliberately kind and gentle, or take any care for what is right in his heart. Let him be harsh, always, and do injustice — seeing that nobody remembers godlike Odysseus, among the people he was lord over, and he was gentle as a father. I do not grudge the arrogant suitors their acts of violence and the wickedness they think up, because it is their own lives they are risking when they eat up the house of Odysseus by force, telling themselves he will never come back. It is the rest of you I am angry with, that you sit here in silence, saying not one word to check the suitors, when you are many and they are few."

Then Leocritus, son of Euenor, answered him. "Mentor, you troublemaker, you have lost your wits. What a thing to say, telling men to stop us. It would be a hard business fighting over a dinner with men who outnumber you. Even if Odysseus of Ithaca came himself, and had it in his heart to drive the lordly suitors out of his hall while they were feasting in his house, his wife would get no joy from his coming, however much she longed for him. He would meet a shameful death right here, if he fought against greater numbers. What you have said is not right.

"Come, then — break up, all of you, and go to your own lands. Mentor and Halitherses will speed this fellow's journey; they are friends of his father's house from the old days. But I think he will sit here a long while and get his news in Ithaca, and never make this journey at all."

He spoke, and broke up the assembly in a hurry. They scattered, each man to his own house, and the suitors went to the house of godlike Odysseus.

But Telemachus went off by himself to the shore of the sea, and washed his hands in the grey water, and prayed to Athena. "Hear me, you who came yesterday to our house as a god and told me to go in a ship over the misty water to ask after my father's homecoming, who has been gone so long. The Achaeans are blocking every part of it, and the suitors most of all, in their wicked insolence."

That was his prayer, and Athena came up beside him in the likeness of Mentor, in his shape and in his voice, and spoke to him with winged words.

"Telemachus, you will not be a coward or a fool in the days ahead, if any of your father's fine spirit has been poured into you — the sort of man he was for carrying a thing through in word and deed. So this journey of yours will not be pointless or unfinished. But if you are not his son and Penelope's, then I have no hope you will get what you want. Few sons are like their fathers. Most are worse; a few are better. But since you are not going to be a coward or a fool in the days ahead, and the good sense of Odysseus has not failed in you altogether, there is hope you will bring this thing off.

"So never mind the will and the plans of the suitors. They are fools. There is nothing sensible or just about them, and they know nothing about the death and the black fate that is standing close beside them, so that they will all die in a single day. The journey you have set your heart on will not be put off long — I am such an old friend of your father's house that I will fit out a fast ship for you and come with you myself. Now go back to the house and mix with the suitors. Get the stores ready and put it all in containers: wine in jars, and barley meal, which is the marrow of men, in strong skins. And I will go round the town and quickly gather a crew of volunteers. There are plenty of ships in sea-girt Ithaca, new ones and old; I will pick out the best of them for you, and we will fit her out quickly and launch her on the open water."

So spoke Athena, daughter of Zeus, and Telemachus did not wait long after hearing the goddess's voice. He went off to the house with a heavy heart, and found the arrogant suitors in the halls, skinning goats and singeing pigs in the courtyard. Antinous came straight up to him, laughing, and took his hand, and said:

"Telemachus, you big talker with no check on your nerve, do not carry any more bad deeds or bad words in your heart. Eat and drink, I tell you, the way you used to. The Achaeans will get all this ready for you, the ship and the picked oarsmen, so that you can go quickly to sacred Pylos and ask after your noble father."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Antinous, there is no possible way I can sit down to dinner quietly in your overbearing company and enjoy myself with an easy mind. Was it not enough, you suitors, that in the past you wasted a great deal of my good property while I was still a child? Now that I am grown, and learn by listening to what others say, and my spirit rises in me, I am going to try to hurl an evil fate on you — going to Pylos, or staying here in this country. Go I will, and the journey I am talking about will not be wasted, though I sail in another man's ship, since you will not let me be master of a ship or oarsmen. That, I suppose, seemed more to your advantage."

With that he snatched his hand out of Antinous's hand, without any ceremony. The suitors were busy with the feast through the hall, and they mocked him and jeered at him as they talked. One of the arrogant young men would say:

"Telemachus is certainly planning to murder us. He will bring men to help him from sandy Pylos, or even from Sparta — he is set on it, terribly. Or he means to go to Ephyre, that rich country, and bring back deadly drugs from there to throw into the wine-bowl and destroy the lot of us."

And another of the arrogant young men would say: "Who knows? He may go off in the hollow ship and die himself, wandering far from his own people, the way Odysseus did. That would make more work for us, because we should have to divide up all his property, and give the house to his mother to keep, and to whoever married her."

That is how they talked. But Telemachus went down to his father's high-roofed store-room, a wide room where gold and bronze lay piled up, and clothes in chests, and plenty of sweet-smelling oil. Great jars of wine stood there too, old and sweet, with an unmixed divine drink inside them, ranged in order along the wall, in case Odysseus should ever come home after all his hard suffering. The double doors were shut, close-fitting, and a housekeeper stayed there night and day guarding everything with a careful mind — Eurycleia, daughter of Ops, son of Peisenor. Telemachus called her into the store-room and said to her:

"Nurse, draw me off some wine in jars — the sweet wine, the best there is next to what you keep for that unlucky man, always thinking of him, in case Odysseus, born of Zeus, comes back from somewhere, having got clear of death and the fates. Fill twelve jars and fit lids to all of them, and pour me barley meal into well-sewn skins, twenty measures of ground meal. And keep it to yourself. Have it all put together, and I will come for it in the evening, when my mother goes up to her room and thinks about sleeping. I am going to Sparta and sandy Pylos to ask after my dear father's homecoming, in case I hear anything."

At that his dear nurse Eurycleia gave a shrill cry, and spoke to him in tears with winged words. "Dear child, how has this idea got into your head? Where do you mean to go, over the wide earth, an only son and loved as you are? He is dead, far from home, Odysseus, born of Zeus, in some strange country. And these men, the moment you are gone, will plot against you, so that you die by a trick, and they can divide all this property among themselves. Stay here and look after what is yours. There is no need for you to suffer and go wandering over the restless sea."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Courage, nurse. This plan of mine did not come without a god behind it. But swear you will say nothing of it to my mother until the eleventh or twelfth day comes, or until she misses me herself and hears that I am gone — so that she does not spoil her lovely face with crying."

So he spoke, and the old woman swore a great oath by the gods to say nothing. And when she had sworn and finished the oath, she drew the wine off into jars for him at once and poured barley meal into well-sewn skins; and Telemachus went back to the hall and mixed with the suitors.

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena thought of something else. In the likeness of Telemachus she went all through the city, and came up to each of the men and spoke to him, telling them to gather at evening beside the fast ship. And she asked Noemon, the fine son of Phronius, for a fast ship, and he promised it willingly.

The sun went down and all the roads grew dark. Then she drew the fast ship to the sea and put into it all the gear that well-benched ships carry, and moored it at the mouth of the harbour, and the good crew gathered round it, and the goddess put heart into every man.

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena thought of something else again. She went to the house of godlike Odysseus and began to pour sweet sleep over the suitors, so that they wandered in their drinking and the cups dropped out of their hands. They got up to go and rest through the city, and did not sit long, because sleep was falling on their eyelids. Then grey-eyed Athena spoke to Telemachus, calling him out in front of the stately hall, having made herself like Mentor in shape and in voice.

"Telemachus, your well-greaved companions are already sitting at the oars, waiting for you to start. Come, let us go, and not hold their journey up any longer."

With that Pallas Athena led the way, quickly, and he followed in the goddess's footsteps. When they had come down to the ship and the sea they found their long-haired companions on the shore, and the strong young Telemachus spoke to them.

"Come, friends, let us fetch the stores. They are all gathered together in the hall. My mother knows nothing about this, nor do the women; only one person heard what I said."

With that he led the way and they went with him, and they brought everything and stowed it in the well-benched ship, as the son of Odysseus told them. Then Telemachus stepped on board, with Athena going before him, and she sat down in the stern of the ship, and Telemachus sat beside her; and the men loosed the stern cables and came on board themselves and sat down at the benches.

And grey-eyed Athena sent them a good wind, a strong west wind singing over the wine-dark sea. Telemachus called to his men and told them to lay hold of the tackle, and they did as he called. They raised the fir mast and set it in the hollow socket and made it fast with the forestays, and hauled the white sail up with twisted ox-hide ropes. The wind filled the belly of the sail, and the dark wave sang loudly round the stem of the ship as she went, and she ran over the water on her way. Then, when they had made the tackle fast in the swift black ship, they set out bowls filled to the brim with wine and poured offerings to the immortal gods who live for ever, and most of all to the grey-eyed daughter of Zeus.

So all night long, and through the dawn, the ship cut her way forward.

Book Three

The sun rose, leaving the lovely water and climbing into the bronze sky to give light to the immortals and to mortal men on the grain-giving earth; and they came to Pylos, the well-built city of Neleus. The people there were on the shore of the sea, sacrificing black bulls to the dark-haired earth-shaker. There were nine companies, five hundred men sitting in each, and each company had nine bulls ready. They had just tasted the inner parts and were burning the thigh-pieces for the god when the ship put straight in to shore. They hauled up the sail of the trim ship and furled it and moored her, and stepped out, and Telemachus stepped out after them, with Athena leading the way.

The goddess grey-eyed Athena spoke to him first. "Telemachus, there is no call for you to be shy now, none at all. You sailed over the sea for this — to find out about your father, where the earth covers him and what death he met. So go straight to Nestor, who breaks horses, and let us find out what he is keeping to himself. Ask him yourself to tell you the exact truth. He will not lie to you; he is a man of real understanding."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Mentor, how am I to go, and how am I to greet him? I have no practice yet in careful speaking, and a young man is ashamed to question a man much older."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Telemachus, some of it you will work out for yourself, and heaven will prompt you with the rest. I do not think you were born and brought up without the goodwill of the gods."

With that Pallas Athena led the way quickly, and he followed in the goddess's footsteps, and they came to the gathering and the companies of the men of Pylos. Nestor was sitting there with his sons, and round about them his people were getting the feast ready, roasting some of the meat and putting other pieces on spits. When they saw the strangers they all came crowding round, and took their hands in welcome, and asked them to sit down.

Nestor's son Peisistratus came up first and took them both by the hand and sat them down at the feast, on soft fleeces on the sand of the sea, beside his brother Thrasymedes and his father. He gave them portions of the inner meat and poured wine into a golden cup, and lifted it to Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus who holds the aegis, and said:

"Pray now, stranger, to lord Poseidon, since it is his feast you have come in on. And when you have poured the offering and prayed, as is right, give your friend the cup of honey-sweet wine as well, so that he can pour too, since I imagine he also prays to the immortals. Every man needs the gods. But he is younger, of an age with me, so I will give the golden cup to you first."

With that he put the cup of sweet wine into her hand. Pallas Athena was pleased with the man's good sense and judgment, that he had given her the golden cup first, and she prayed hard at once to lord Poseidon.

"Hear me, Poseidon, who hold the earth, and do not grudge us the fulfilment of what we ask. First of all give Nestor and his sons a great name; and then grant the rest of them a gracious return for this splendid sacrifice, to all the men of Pylos. And grant besides that Telemachus and I may go home having done everything we came here for in our fast black ship."

That was her prayer, and she was bringing it all about herself. Then she gave Telemachus the fine two-handled cup, and the son of Odysseus prayed in the same way.

When they had roasted the outer flesh and drawn it off the spits, they divided out the portions and had a splendid meal. And when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, the horseman Nestor of Gerenia spoke first among them.

"Now it is more proper to ask the strangers who they are, since they have had the pleasure of their food. Strangers, who are you? Where do you sail from over the watery roads? Is it on some business, or are you wandering at random over the sea like pirates, who wander with their lives in their hands and bring harm to people in other countries?"

Then clear-headed Telemachus took courage and answered, because Athena herself put the courage in his heart, so that he might ask about his father who was gone, and so that men might think well of him.

"Nestor, son of Neleus, great glory of the Achaeans, you ask where we are from, and I will tell you. We have come from Ithaca, which lies below Neion. The business I speak of is my own and not the people's. I have come after the wide-scattered rumour of my father, in case I hear something of him — noble Odysseus, who endures everything, who once, they say, fought at your side and sacked the city of the Trojans. Of every other man who fought against the Trojans we know where he met his miserable end; but of him the son of Cronos has made even the death impossible to learn. No one can say for certain where he died — whether he was beaten by enemies on dry land, or on the deep among the waves of Amphitrite.

"That is why I have come to your knees now: in case you are willing to tell me about his miserable death, whether you happened to see it with your own eyes or heard the story of his wanderings from someone else. His mother bore him to more sorrow than any other man. Do not soften it out of pity for me or out of kindness. Tell me exactly how you came to see him. I beg you, if my father, noble Odysseus, ever promised you anything in word or deed and carried it out in the land of the Trojans, where you Achaeans suffered so much — remember it now, and tell me the exact truth."

Then the horseman Nestor of Gerenia answered him: "My friend, since you have brought back to my mind the grief we endured in that country, we sons of the Achaeans, with no check on our daring — all we endured aboard ship, roaming after plunder over the misty water wherever Achilles led; and all our fighting round the great city of king Priam. There all our best men were killed. Warlike Ajax lies there, and Achilles, and Patroclus, who was the equal of the gods in counsel; and there lies my own dear son, strong and faultless, Antilochus, who could outrun anyone and was a fighter besides.

"And we suffered a great many other things as well. Who among mortal men could tell it all? If you stayed here five years or six and asked about all the troubles the good Achaeans endured there, you would grow tired before the end and go back to your own country. For nine years we worked at their ruin with every kind of trick, and the son of Cronos brought it off only just. In all that time no man dared to compete with him in cleverness, because noble Odysseus was far ahead of everyone in every kind of trick — your father, if you really are his son. Amazement takes hold of me looking at you: your speech is like his, and nobody would think a younger man could speak so like him.

"All the time we were there, noble Odysseus and I never once spoke on opposite sides, either in the assembly or in the council. We were of one mind, and advised the Argives with good sense and shrewdness about how everything might be done best. But when we had sacked the high city of Priam and gone away in our ships, and a god had scattered the Achaeans, then Zeus planned a bitter homecoming for the Argives in his heart, because not all of them were sensible or just. So a good many of them met a bad end through the deadly anger of the grey-eyed goddess, the daughter of the mighty father, who set the two sons of Atreus quarrelling.

"Those two called all the Achaeans to an assembly, recklessly and out of all proper order, at sunset — and the sons of the Achaeans came heavy with wine — and they said their piece and explained why they had gathered the army. Then Menelaus told all the Achaeans to think about going home over the broad back of the sea; and that did not please Agamemnon at all, who wanted to hold the army back and make holy sacrifices to appease the terrible anger of Athena. The fool. He did not know she would not be listening: the mind of the gods who live for ever is not quickly turned. So those two stood there trading hard words, while the well-greaved Achaeans jumped up with a tremendous noise, in favour of two different plans.

"We rested that night, each side turning hard thoughts against the other, because Zeus was bringing an evil doom on us. In the morning some of us launched our ships on the bright sea and put our goods on board, and the women with their low belts. Half the army held back and stayed there with Agamemnon, son of Atreus, shepherd of the people, and half of us embarked and rowed away. The ships sailed fast; a god smoothed the deep sea for us. When we reached Tenedos we made sacrifice to the gods, in a hurry to get home; but Zeus did not yet intend our homecoming, the stubborn god, and he stirred up bad feeling all over again.

"So some of them turned their curved ships round and went back — lord Odysseus, the wise and cunning-minded, with his crew, doing Agamemnon a favour again. But I fled on with the whole squadron that followed me, because I knew the god was contriving trouble. The warlike son of Tydeus fled too, and urged his men on; and fair-haired Menelaus came after us late and caught us up at Lesbos, while we were debating the long voyage — whether to sail out to sea of rugged Chios, towards the island of Psyria, keeping Chios on our left, or inside Chios past windy Mimas. We asked the god for a sign, and he gave us one, and told us to cut through the middle of the sea to Euboea, so as to get out of danger soonest. A shrill wind got up to blow, and the ships ran quickly over the teeming roads, and put in at night at Geraestus. There we laid many thighs of bulls on Poseidon's altar, thankful to have crossed the great sea.

"It was the fourth day when the company of Diomedes, son of Tydeus, the horse-breaker, brought their trim ships to a stop in Argos; but I held on for Pylos, and the wind never once died from the moment the god sent it to blow.

"So I came home, dear child, with no news, and I know nothing about the others, which of the Achaeans were saved and which were lost. But what I have heard sitting in my own halls you shall hear, as is right, and I will not keep it from you. The Myrmidons who rage with the spear came home safely, they say, led by the famous son of great-hearted Achilles; and Philoctetes came safely, the fine son of Poias. Idomeneus brought all his company to Crete, all who escaped the war, and the sea took none of them from him. About the son of Atreus you have heard yourselves, far off as you are — how he came home, and how Aegisthus contrived a miserable end for him. And he paid for it terribly, so good a thing is it that a man leaves a son behind him when he dies, since that son took revenge on his father's killer, sly Aegisthus, for killing his glorious father. You too, my friend — and I can see you are tall and good-looking — be brave, so that men born long after may speak well of you."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Nestor, son of Neleus, great glory of the Achaeans, that son did take his revenge, and the Achaeans will spread his fame far, so that men yet to come will hear of it. I wish the gods would clothe me in that kind of strength, so that I could take revenge on the suitors for the grievous wrong they do, plotting outrages against me out of pure wantonness. But the gods have spun no such happiness for me, or for my father. As it is, I have to endure it."

Then the horseman Nestor of Gerenia answered him: "My friend, since you have brought it to my mind and spoken of it — they say a great many men are courting your mother and contriving harm in your halls against your will. Tell me, are you letting yourself be put down like this, or do the people through the country hate you, following some word from a god? Who knows but Odysseus may come one day and take revenge on them for their violence — either alone, or with the whole Achaean army behind him? I only wish grey-eyed Athena would choose to love you as she cared for glorious Odysseus, and cared enormously, in the land of the Trojans, where we Achaeans suffered so much. I have never seen the gods show love so openly as Pallas Athena showed it to him, standing there beside him where anyone could see. If she chose to love you like that, and to care about you in her heart, there is many a one of them who would forget marriage altogether."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Old man, I do not think that is ever going to happen. What you say is too much. It astonishes me. I have no hope of it happening, not even if the gods should will it so."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena said to him: "Telemachus, what a thing to let past your teeth. A god who wanted to could easily bring a man home safe, even from a long way off. As for me, I would rather go through a great deal of hard suffering before I got home and saw the day of my return, than come home and be killed at my own hearth, the way Agamemnon was killed by the trickery of Aegisthus and his own wife. But death is common to everyone, and not even the gods can keep it off a man they love, when the cruel fate of death comes to strike him down."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Mentor, let us not talk about this any more, however much it grieves us. There is no homecoming for him now, ever. The immortals arranged death and black fate for him long ago. But there is something else I want to ask Nestor about, since he knows more about judgment and wisdom than anyone else. Three generations of men he has been king over, they say, and to look at him he seems to me like an immortal. Nestor, son of Neleus, tell me the truth: how did the son of Atreus die, wide-ruling Agamemnon? Where was Menelaus? What death did sly Aegisthus plan for the king, since he killed a man far stronger than himself? Was Menelaus not in Achaean Argos, but wandering somewhere else among men, so that Aegisthus took heart and did the murder?"

Then the horseman Nestor of Gerenia answered him: "Then I will tell you the whole truth, my child. You can guess for yourself how it would have turned out if the son of Atreus, fair-haired Menelaus, had come back from Troy and found Aegisthus alive in his halls. They would not have heaped the earth over him even in death; the dogs and the birds would have torn him where he lay out on the plain, far from the city, and not one of the Achaean women would have wept for him. It was a monstrous thing he thought up.

"We were away in Troy getting through our many labours, while he sat comfortably in a corner of horse-pasturing Argos and worked away with words at Agamemnon's wife. At first she refused the shameful thing, beautiful Clytemnestra, because she had a good mind in her; and besides, there was a singer with her whom the son of Atreus had ordered strictly, when he set out for Troy, to look after his wife. But when the doom of the gods bound her to be overcome, then Aegisthus took the singer to a desert island and left him there for the birds to prey on and tear; and he took her home, willing as he was willing. And he burned many thigh-pieces on the holy altars of the gods and hung up many offerings, woven stuffs and gold, for having brought off a huge thing that he had never hoped for in his heart.

"Now we were sailing home from Troy together, the son of Atreus and I, on the best of terms. But when we came to holy Sunium, the cape of Athens, Phoebus Apollo attacked the helmsman of Menelaus with his gentle arrows and killed him while he held the steering-oar of the running ship — Phrontis, son of Onetor, who was better than anyone alive at handling a ship when the storm winds blew hard. So Menelaus stayed there, eager as he was to get on, to bury his companion and pay him the funeral rites. But when he in his turn went on over the wine-dark sea in the hollow ships and came at speed to the steep height of Malea, then Zeus, whose voice carries far, planned a hateful road for him and poured out the blasts of shrieking winds, and the waves swelled up huge, like mountains.

"Then he split the ships in two and brought some of them to Crete, where the Cydonians live along the streams of Iardanus. There is a smooth cliff there, sheer down to the sea, on the boundary of Gortyn in the misty water, where the south-west wind drives the great wave against the headland on the left towards Phaestus, and a little rock holds back a great sea. Some of the ships came there, and the men barely escaped drowning, while the waves smashed the ships to pieces against the reef. The other five dark-prowed ships the wind and the wave carried to Egypt. So Menelaus was wandering there with his ships among people of another language, collecting a great deal of goods and gold — and meanwhile Aegisthus was contriving this miserable business at home.

"Seven years he ruled over Mycenae, rich in gold, after killing the son of Atreus, and the people were held down under him. But in the eighth year his ruin came, noble Orestes, back from Athens, and he killed his father's murderer, sly Aegisthus, for killing his glorious father. When he had killed him he gave the Argives a funeral feast over his hateful mother and the cowardly Aegisthus; and on the very same day Menelaus came to him, good at the war-cry, bringing a great deal of treasure, as much as his ships would carry.

"So do not you wander long away from home, my friend, leaving your wealth behind you and such insolent men in your house — in case they divide it all up and eat it, and you have made your journey for nothing. But I urge you and tell you to go to Menelaus, because he has only lately come from a foreign country, from people that no man would hope in his heart to come back from, once the storms had driven him astray into a sea so vast that the very birds do not cross it in a year, it is so great and so terrible. Go now, with your ship and your companions; or, if you would rather go by land, there is a chariot and horses here for you, and my sons here at your service, who will guide you to lovely Lacedaemon, where fair-haired Menelaus lives. Ask him yourself to tell you the exact truth. He will not lie to you; he is a man of real understanding."

So he spoke, and the sun went down and the dark came on. Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena said to them: "Old man, you have told this properly. But come, cut out the tongues of the victims and mix the wine, so that when we have poured offerings to Poseidon and the other immortals we can think about sleep. It is the hour for it. The light has gone down under the dark already, and it is not right to sit long at the gods' feast; we should be going."

So spoke the daughter of Zeus, and they did as she said. Heralds poured water over their hands, and boys filled the bowls to the brim and served everyone, pouring the first drops into the cups for an offering. Then they threw the tongues on the fire and stood up and poured. And when they had poured the offering and drunk as much as they wanted, Athena and godlike Telemachus were both for going back to the hollow ship. But Nestor tried to stop them, and said:

"Zeus forbid, and the other immortal gods, that you should go from my house to your fast ship as though you were leaving a man with no clothes to his name and no means, who has no cloaks or blankets in his house for himself and his guests to sleep soft on. I have cloaks and fine blankets in my house. The dear son of this man Odysseus is certainly never going to lie down on a ship's deck while I am alive, and while there are children left in my halls after me to look after guests, whoever comes to my house."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "You have spoken well in this, old friend, and it is right for Telemachus to do as you say; it is much better. He shall go with you now and sleep in your halls, and I will go to the black ship to put heart into my companions and tell them everything. I am the only older man among them; the rest are younger, who come with us out of friendship, all of them the same age as great-hearted Telemachus. I will lie down by the hollow black ship for tonight, and in the morning I will go to the great-hearted Cauconians, where a debt is owed to me, and it is neither new nor small. As for this man, since he has come to your house, send him on with a chariot and with your son, and give him horses — the fastest runners you have and the strongest."

With that the goddess grey-eyed Athena was gone, in the likeness of a sea-eagle. Astonishment fell on all of them at the sight, and the old man marvelled when he saw it with his own eyes. He took Telemachus by the hand and said to him:

"My friend, I do not think you will turn out a coward or a weakling, if the gods go with you to guide you when you are as young as this. This is none other of those who live on Olympus but the daughter of Zeus, Tritogeneia, the most glorious of maidens, who honoured your noble father too among the Argives. Queen, be gracious to me, and give a fine name to me and to my sons and to my honoured wife; and in return I will sacrifice to you a broad-browed heifer, a yearling, unbroken, that no man has yet led under the yoke. Her I will sacrifice, and I will lay gold over her horns."

That was his prayer, and Pallas Athena heard him. Then the horseman Nestor of Gerenia led the way, his sons and the husbands of his daughters, to his beautiful palace. And when they reached the king's splendid house they sat down in rows on the chairs and the high seats, and the old man mixed a bowl of sweet wine for them as they came, wine now in its eleventh year, which the housekeeper opened by loosing the string that held the lid. The old man had a bowl of it mixed, and prayed hard as he poured the offering to Athena, daughter of Zeus who holds the aegis.

And when they had poured the offering and drunk as much as they wanted, they went off, each to his own house, to rest. But the horseman Nestor of Gerenia had Telemachus, the dear son of godlike Odysseus, sleep there on a corded bedstead under the echoing portico, and beside him Peisistratus with the good ash spear, a leader of men, who was the one son still unmarried in the palace. Nestor himself slept in the inner room of the high house, with the lady his wife beside him, who had made up the bed.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, the horseman Nestor of Gerenia got up from his bed and went out and sat down on the polished stones in front of his high doors, white and gleaming as if with oil. Neleus used to sit on them once, who was the equal of the gods in counsel; but fate had struck him down before this and he had gone to the house of Hades, and now Nestor of Gerenia sat on them in his turn, the guardian of the Achaeans, holding a sceptre in his hands. His sons gathered round him in a crowd as they came out of their rooms — Echephron and Stratius and Perseus and Aretus and godlike Thrasymedes — and after them, sixth, came lord Peisistratus. They brought godlike Telemachus and sat him down beside them, and the horseman Nestor of Gerenia spoke first among them.

"Quickly, my dear children, do what I want, so that I may win the goodwill of Athena first of all the gods, who came to me openly, in her own presence, at the god's rich feast. Let one of you go to the plain for a heifer, so that she comes quickly, and let the herdsman drive her; and let one go to the black ship of great-hearted Telemachus and bring all his companions, leaving only two men; and let one go and tell the goldsmith Laerces to come here and lay gold over the heifer's horns. The rest of you stay together here, and tell the women inside to get a feast ready through our splendid halls, and to fetch seats, and logs to set on either side of the altar, and to bring clear water."

So he spoke, and they all set busily to work. The heifer came from the plain; the companions of great-hearted Telemachus came from the fast, trim ship; the smith came with his bronze tools in his hands, the equipment of his trade, anvil and hammer and well-made tongs, which he worked the gold with; and Athena came to accept the sacrifice. The old man Nestor, the chariot-driver, gave the gold, and the smith prepared it and laid it over the heifer's horns, so that the goddess might be pleased when she saw the offering. Stratius and noble Echephron led the heifer by the horns, and Aretus came out of the store-room with water for their hands in a basin worked with flowers, and barley grains in a basket in his other hand; and Thrasymedes, steady in a fight, stood by holding a sharp axe to fell the heifer, and Perseus held the bowl for the blood. Then the old man Nestor, the chariot-driver, began the opening rite, washing his hands and scattering the barley, and prayed hard to Athena, cutting hair from the heifer's head as a first offering and throwing it into the fire.

When they had prayed and scattered the barley grains, Nestor's son Thrasymedes, high of heart, stepped up at once and struck. The axe cut through the sinews of the neck and loosened the strength of the heifer. Then the women raised the sacred cry, the daughters and the sons' wives and the honoured wife of Nestor, Eurydice, the eldest of the daughters of Clymenus. The men lifted the heifer's head from the wide-wayed earth and held it, and Peisistratus, a leader of men, cut the throat. When the black blood had run out of her and the life had left the bones, they cut the body up at once, and cut out the thigh-pieces in the proper order, and covered them with a double layer of fat, and laid raw flesh on top. The old man burned them on split wood and poured bright wine over them, and beside him the young men held the five-pronged forks in their hands. And when the thigh-pieces were burned up and they had tasted the inner parts, they cut the rest into pieces and spitted it and roasted it, holding the pointed spits in their hands.

Meanwhile lovely Polycaste, the youngest daughter of Nestor son of Neleus, bathed Telemachus. When she had bathed him and rubbed him richly with oil and put a fine cloak and a tunic on him, he came out of the bath looking like the immortals; and he went and sat down beside Nestor, the shepherd of the people.

Now when they had roasted the outer flesh and drawn it off the spits, they sat down and feasted, and good men waited on them, pouring wine into golden cups. And when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, the horseman Nestor of Gerenia spoke first.

"My sons, up, and yoke horses with beautiful manes under the car for Telemachus, so that he can get on with his journey."

So he spoke, and they listened readily and obeyed, and quickly yoked the fast horses under the car. The housekeeper put bread and wine in the car, and good things such as kings brought up by Zeus are used to eating. Then Telemachus climbed into the beautiful car, and Peisistratus, son of Nestor, a leader of men, got up beside him and took the reins in his hands. He touched the horses with the whip to start them, and the pair went off willingly to the plain and left the steep citadel of Pylos behind. All day long they shook the yoke they carried on their necks.

The sun went down and all the roads grew dark. They came to Pherae, to the house of Diocles, son of Ortilochus, whose father was Alpheus. There they spent the night, and he set out the entertainment that is due to strangers. And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, they yoked the horses and mounted the inlaid car and drove out through the gateway and the echoing portico. Peisistratus touched the horses with the whip to start them, and the pair went on willingly. So they came to the wheat-bearing plain, and after that pressed on towards the end of their journey, so well did the fast horses carry them.

And the sun went down and all the roads grew dark.

Book Four (Part 1 of 2)

They came to the hollow land of Lacedaemon with its many ravines, and drove to the palace of glorious Menelaus. They found him giving a wedding feast in his house for a great many relatives, for his son and his daughter. The girl he was sending to the son of Achilles, who breaks the ranks of men: he had first promised her and agreed to give her in the land of Troy, and now the gods were bringing the marriage about. So he was sending her off with horses and chariots to the famous city of the Myrmidons, whose king her husband was. And for his son he was bringing home from Sparta the daughter of Alector, for sturdy Megapenthes, his well-loved son, whom a slave woman had borne him; for the gods gave Helen no more children after she had borne her lovely daughter Hermione, who had the beauty of golden Aphrodite.

So they were feasting in the great high-roofed hall, the neighbours and the family of glorious Menelaus, enjoying themselves; and among them a divine singer sang to the lyre, and two tumblers whirled up and down through the middle of them as he began his song.

The two of them, prince Telemachus and the fine son of Nestor, stopped at the gateway of the palace, they and their horses. Lord Eteoneus came out and saw them, the busy attendant of glorious Menelaus, and went through the hall to bring the news to the shepherd of the people. He came up beside him and spoke winged words.

"Menelaus, brought up by Zeus, there are two strangers here, two men who look like the seed of great Zeus. Tell me, shall we unyoke their fast horses for them, or send them on to somebody else who will look after them?"

Fair-haired Menelaus was seriously annoyed, and said to him: "You were not a fool before this, Eteoneus, son of Boethous, but now you are talking nonsense like a child. The two of us ate other men's hospitality often enough before we came home here, hoping Zeus would give us some rest from suffering afterwards. Unyoke the strangers' horses, and bring the men inside to the feast."

So he spoke, and the other hurried through the hall and called the other busy attendants to come with him. They loosed the sweating horses from under the yoke and tied them at the horses' stalls and threw down spelt for them with white barley mixed in, and tilted the chariot against the shining wall by the entrance, and brought the men into the marvellous house. They stared as they went through the palace of the king whom Zeus brought up: there was a gleam as of sun or moon over the high-roofed house of glorious Menelaus. And when they had looked their fill they went into the polished baths and washed.

When the women had bathed them and rubbed them with oil and put fleecy cloaks and tunics on them, they sat down on chairs beside Menelaus, son of Atreus. A maid brought water for their hands in a lovely golden jug and poured it over a silver basin for them to wash, and drew a polished table up beside them. The housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it out, and plenty of good things with it, giving freely from her stores. A carver lifted platters of every kind of meat and set them down, and put out golden cups.

Then fair-haired Menelaus greeted the two of them. "Help yourselves to the food, and be glad. When you have eaten we will ask you who you are, since the breeding of your fathers is not lost in you: you are of the stock of men who are sceptred kings, brought up by Zeus. Common men could not father sons like you."

With that he took up roast meat in his hands and set it in front of them, the fat ox chine that had been put before him as his own portion of honour. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them. And when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, Telemachus leaned his head close to the son of Nestor, so that the others would not hear, and said:
