"Son of Nestor, dear to my heart, look at the flash of bronze all through these echoing halls, and the gold, and the electrum, and the silver, and the ivory. The court of Olympian Zeus inside must be something like this. There is wealth here past telling. Amazement takes hold of me looking at it."

Fair-haired Menelaus heard him as he spoke, and said to them with winged words: "Dear children, no mortal man can compete with Zeus, whose halls and possessions are everlasting. Among men, another might compete with me in wealth, or he might not. I brought my wealth home in my ships after a great deal of suffering and wide wandering, and came home in the eighth year. I wandered over Cyprus and Phoenicia and Egypt, and I came to the Ethiopians and the Sidonians and the Erembi, and to Libya, where the lambs are horned from birth — for there the ewes bear three times in the full round of the year, and neither master nor shepherd ever lacks cheese or meat or sweet milk, because the flocks give milk to the milking the whole year through.

"While I was wandering in those countries gathering a great living, another man killed my brother, secretly, when he was off his guard, through the trickery of his accursed wife. So you see I take no joy in being lord of all this wealth. You will have heard about it from your fathers, whoever they may be, because I suffered a great deal and let a stately house fall to ruin, and one stocked with a great deal of fine treasure. I wish I lived in my halls with a third of this wealth and those men were alive who died then in the broad land of Troy, far from horse-pasturing Argos.

"And even so, though I often sit in my halls weeping and grieving for all of them — one moment I ease my heart with weeping, and then I stop, because men soon have enough of the cold comfort of mourning — even so, I do not mourn for all of them as much as I mourn for one, who makes me hate sleep and food both when I think of him. No Achaean worked or endured as much as Odysseus worked and endured. And it seems his portion was to be nothing but grief, and mine to have a sorrow for him that I never forget, because he has been gone so long, and we do not know whether he is alive or dead. The old man Laertes mourns him, I suppose, and steady Penelope, and Telemachus, whom he left a new-born child in his house."

So he spoke, and roused in Telemachus the wish to weep for his father. Tears fell from his eyelids to the ground when he heard his father's name, and he held his purple cloak up in front of his eyes with both hands. Menelaus noticed, and turned it over in his mind and heart, whether to leave him to speak of his father himself, or to question him first and try him on every point.

While he was turning it over in his mind and heart, Helen came out of her fragrant high-roofed room, like Artemis of the golden arrows. Adraste came with her and set a beautifully made chair for her, and Alcippe brought a rug of soft wool, and Phylo a silver basket, which Alcandre had given her, the wife of Polybus, who lived in Thebes of Egypt, where the greatest store of wealth is laid up in men's houses. He gave Menelaus two silver baths and two tripods and ten talents of gold. And besides these, his wife gave Helen beautiful gifts of her own: a golden distaff, and a basket on wheels, of silver, with the rims finished in gold. Phylo the maid brought it and set it beside her, full of finely spun yarn, and the distaff lay across it loaded with violet-dark wool.

So Helen sat down on the chair, with a footstool below for her feet, and at once she questioned her husband about it all.

"Do we know, Menelaus, brought up by Zeus, who these men say they are who have come to our house? Shall I hide what I think, or say it? My heart tells me to say it. I never yet saw anyone so like another, man or woman — amazement takes hold of me looking at him — as this man is like the son of great-hearted Odysseus, Telemachus, whom that fighting man left a new-born child in his house, when for the sake of shameless me you Achaeans came up under the walls of Troy with fierce war in your hearts."

Then fair-haired Menelaus answered her: "I am noticing it myself now, wife, since you point out the likeness. His feet were like that, and his hands, and the glances of his eyes, and his head and the hair on it. And just now, when I was speaking of Odysseus and telling all the misery and the labour he went through for my sake, this young man let a bitter tear fall from under his brows and held his purple cloak up in front of his eyes."

Then Peisistratus, son of Nestor, answered him: "Menelaus, son of Atreus, brought up by Zeus, leader of armies — he is that man's son, as you say. But he is careful in his mind and ashamed in his heart, on his first coming, to make a display of forward talk in front of you, when we both take pleasure in your voice as if it were a god's. The horseman Nestor of Gerenia sent me out to come with him as his guide, because he was eager to see you, so that you might put some word or some deed in his heart. A son has a great deal of trouble in his halls when his father is gone and there is nobody else to help him, which is how it is with Telemachus now: his father is gone, and there is nobody among the people who can keep ruin away."

Then fair-haired Menelaus answered him and said: "Well, well — so the son of a man I loved has come to my house, a man who went through so much for my sake. I always thought that if he came back I would welcome him beyond all the other Argives, if Olympian Zeus, whose voice carries far, had granted the two of us a homecoming over the sea in our fast ships. I would have given him a city in Argos to live in, and built him a house, and brought him over from Ithaca with his goods and his son and all his people, clearing out one of the cities round about that are ruled by me. Then we would have been together often, here, and nothing would have kept us apart, loving each other and taking pleasure in each other, until the black cloud of death wrapped us round. But the god himself must have been jealous of that, I think, who granted no homecoming to that one unlucky man."

So he spoke, and stirred in all of them the wish to mourn. Argive Helen wept, the daughter of Zeus, and Telemachus wept, and Menelaus, son of Atreus; nor could the son of Nestor keep his eyes dry, because he was thinking of faultless Antilochus, whom the glorious son of the shining Dawn had killed. Thinking of him, he spoke winged words.

"Son of Atreus, old Nestor always used to say you were wiser than anyone, whenever we mentioned you in his halls and asked each other about you. So now, if it is at all possible, listen to me. I take no pleasure in weeping at suppertime — and besides, the early dawn will soon be here. Not that I think it any shame to weep for a mortal who has died and met his fate. This is the only due we pay to miserable mortals, to cut our hair and let a tear fall down the cheeks. A brother of mine is dead too, and not the meanest of the Argives; you probably knew him. I never met him or saw him myself, but they say Antilochus was ahead of everyone, quicker on his feet than anyone and a fighter besides."

Then fair-haired Menelaus answered him and said: "My friend, you have said everything a wise man might say or do, even a man older than you. It is the father you come from that makes you speak so sensibly. The seed of the man is easy to recognise for whom the son of Cronos spins good fortune at his marriage and at his birth — as now he has granted Nestor all his days long that he should come to a sleek old age himself in his halls, and that his sons in their turn should be wise and very brave with the spear. Let us stop this weeping that has begun and think about our supper again, and let them pour water over our hands. There will be stories in the morning too for Telemachus and me to tell each other, our fill of them."

So he spoke, and Asphalion poured water over their hands, the busy attendant of glorious Menelaus, and they put out their hands to the good food in front of them.

Then Helen, daughter of Zeus, thought of something else. Straight away she threw into the wine they were drinking a drug that quiets all pain and anger and brings forgetfulness of every trouble. Whoever swallowed it, once it was mixed in the bowl, would not let a tear fall down his cheeks all that day, not even if his mother and father lay there dead, not even if men killed his brother or his own son with the sword in front of him and his own eyes saw it. Such cunning drugs the daughter of Zeus had, and good ones, which Polydamna gave her, the wife of Thon, a woman of Egypt — for there the grain-giving earth bears the greatest store of drugs, many that heal when they are mixed and many that are deadly, and there every man is a doctor, wiser than the rest of mankind, for they are of the race of Paeeon.

Now when she had thrown the drug in and told them to pour the wine, she spoke again and said:

"Menelaus, son of Atreus, brought up by Zeus, and you here, sons of good men — Zeus gives good and bad, now to one and now to another, since he can do anything — so sit here in the halls and eat, and take pleasure in the stories, and I will tell one that suits the hour. I cannot tell or count everything, all the labours of Odysseus, who endures everything. But here is one thing that strong man did and endured in the land of the Trojans, where you Achaeans suffered so much.

"He beat himself with cruel blows, marking his own body, and threw a wretched garment round his shoulders, and in the likeness of a slave he went into the broad-wayed city of the enemy. He hid himself under the appearance of another man, a beggar, when he was nothing of the kind at the Achaean ships. In that likeness he went into the city of the Trojans, and every one of them took him for nothing. I alone recognised him under the disguise, and questioned him, and he was clever and tried to get away from me. But when I was bathing him and rubbing him with oil, and had put clothes on him and sworn a great oath not to make him known among the Trojans as Odysseus before he got back to the fast ships and the huts, then at last he told me the whole plan of the Achaeans. And after he had killed a great many Trojans with his long sword he went back to the Argives and brought back a great deal of information. Then the other Trojan women wailed aloud, but my heart was glad, because it had already turned back towards home, and I groaned for the blindness Aphrodite gave me, when she led me there from my own dear country, leaving my child and my bridal chamber and a husband who lacked nothing, either in mind or in looks."

Then fair-haired Menelaus answered her and said: "Yes, wife, everything you have said is right. I have come to know the mind and the counsel of many fighting men before now, and I have travelled over the wide earth, but I never yet saw with my own eyes a man like Odysseus, who endures everything. Here is another thing that strong man did and endured, in the carved horse, where all the leaders of the Argives were sitting, bringing death and doom to the Trojans. You came out there, and it must have been that some god told you to, who wanted to give glory to the Trojans; and godlike Deiphobus came with you. Three times you walked round the hollow ambush, feeling it with your hands, and you called the leaders of the Danaans aloud by name, making your voice like the voices of the wives of all the Argives. Now I and the son of Tydeus and noble Odysseus were sitting in the middle and heard you calling, and the two of us were desperate to get up and come out, or else to answer at once from inside; but Odysseus held us back and stopped us, however much we wanted to. Then all the other sons of the Achaeans kept quiet, and only Anticlus was determined to answer you; but Odysseus shut his mouth with his strong hands, and saved all the Achaeans, and held him like that until Pallas Athena led you away."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Menelaus, son of Atreus, brought up by Zeus, leader of armies, it is all the harder to bear; because none of that kept miserable destruction away from him, not even though the heart inside him had been made of iron. But come, send us to bed, so that we can lie down now and take our pleasure in sweet sleep."

So he spoke, and Argive Helen told her women to set bedsteads under the portico and to lay fine purple blankets on them and spread covers over the top, and to put fleecy cloaks on those to wear. So the maids went out of the hall with torches in their hands and made up the beds, and a herald led the guests out. So they slept there in the forecourt of the palace, prince Telemachus and the fine son of Nestor; but the son of Atreus slept in the inner room of the high house, and long-robed Helen lay beside him, loveliest of women.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, Menelaus got up from his bed, good at the war-cry, and put on his clothes. He slung his sharp sword over his shoulder, bound the fine sandals under his shining feet, and came out of his room looking like a god. He sat down beside Telemachus and said to him:

"What need has brought you here, prince Telemachus, to lovely Lacedaemon over the broad back of the sea? Is it public business, or your own? Tell me the truth of it."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Menelaus, son of Atreus, brought up by Zeus, leader of armies, I came in case you could tell me some news of my father. My house is being eaten up and my rich lands ruined. My house is full of men who are enemies, who go on killing my crowding sheep and my slow-moving cattle — my mother's suitors, arrogant past bearing. That is why I have come to your knees now: in case you are willing to tell me about his miserable death, whether you happened to see it with your own eyes or heard the story of his wanderings from someone else. His mother bore him to more sorrow than any other man. Do not soften it out of pity for me or out of kindness. Tell me exactly how you came to see him. I beg you, if my father, noble Odysseus, ever promised you anything in word or deed and carried it out in the land of the Trojans, where you Achaeans suffered so much — remember it now, and tell me the truth."

Fair-haired Menelaus was seriously annoyed, and said to him: "The disgrace of it — that men with no courage in them should want to lie in the bed of a man of such a heart. It is as when a hind puts her new-born suckling fawns to sleep in the thicket lair of a strong lion, and goes off over the mountain slopes and the grassy valleys looking for pasture, and then the lion comes back to his lair and lets loose a cruel doom on the two of them. That is how Odysseus will let loose a cruel doom on these men. Father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo — I wish that Odysseus might come among the suitors with the strength he had that time in well-built Lesbos, when he stood up and wrestled a match with Philomeleides and threw him hard, and all the Achaeans were delighted. If he came in that strength, every one of them would find a quick death and a bitter courtship.

"But as for what you are asking me and begging me about, I will not turn aside and talk of other things, or deceive you. Of everything the unerring old man of the sea told me, I will not hide or keep back one word.

"In Egypt the gods held me back, eager as I was to get here, because I did not sacrifice the full hecatombs to them; and the gods always want men to remember what they have ordered. Now there is an island in the surging sea in front of Egypt, and men call it Pharos, as far off as a hollow ship runs in a whole day when a shrill wind blows fair behind her. There is a harbour there with good anchorage, where men launch their trim ships out to sea when they have drawn their supply of dark water. For twenty days the gods kept me there, and the winds that blow over the deep never once got up, the winds that speed men's ships over the broad back of the sea. And by now all my stores would have been spent, and my men's strength with them, if one of the gods had not pitied me and saved me — Eidothea, daughter of mighty Proteus, the old man of the sea, whose heart I had moved above all others.

"She met me wandering alone, away from my companions, who were always roaming about the island fishing with bent hooks, because hunger was pinching their bellies. She came close to me and said:

"'Are you as foolish as this, stranger, and as slack in the wits? Or are you slack on purpose, and enjoying your suffering? You have been shut up on this island so long, and can find no sign of getting out, and your companions' hearts are failing them.'

"So she spoke, and I answered her: 'I will speak out and tell you, whichever of the goddesses you are, that I am not shut up here of my own will. I must have sinned somehow against the immortals who hold the wide sky. But tell me — because the gods know everything — which of the immortals has me in chains here and has blocked my road, and tell me about my homecoming, how I am to go over the teeming water.'

"So I spoke, and the beautiful goddess answered at once: 'Then I will tell you plainly, stranger. The unerring old man of the sea comes here, immortal Proteus of Egypt, who knows the depths of every sea and is the servant of Poseidon. He is my father, they say, who begot me. If you could somehow lie in wait and catch him, he will tell you your road and the length of your journey, and about your homecoming, how you are to go over the teeming water. And he will tell you too, if you like, you whom Zeus brought up, what harm and what good has been done in your halls while you have been away on your long and miserable road.'

"So she spoke, and I answered her: 'Work out yourself, then, how to lie in wait for the divine old man, in case he sees me first and knows my purpose and avoids me. A god is hard for a mortal man to master.'

"So I spoke, and the beautiful goddess answered at once: 'Then I will tell you plainly, stranger. When the sun has reached the middle of the sky, the unerring old man of the sea comes out of the water at the breath of the west wind, hidden under the dark ripple. And when he has come out he lies down to sleep in the hollow caves, and round about him the seals sleep in a herd, the brood of the lovely daughter of the sea, coming up out of the grey water, and bitter is the smell they breathe of the depths of the sea. I will bring you there at daybreak and lay you all in a row; so choose three of your companions carefully, the best you have in your well-benched ships.

"'And I will tell you all the old man's wizard tricks. First he will count the seals and go over them; and when he has told them all off by fives and looked at them, he will lie down in the middle of them, the way a shepherd does among his flocks. As soon as you see him settled, then fill your hearts with strength and courage and hold him there, however hard he struggles and strains to get away. Try he will, and he will turn himself into every shape there is of everything that moves on the earth, and into water, and into blazing fire. Hold him without flinching and grip him all the harder. But when at last he speaks of his own accord and questions you in the shape you saw him lie down in, then stop your force, hero, and let the old man go, and ask him which of the gods is angry with you, and about your homecoming, how you are to go over the teeming water.'

"With that she plunged under the surging sea, and I went to my ships where they stood on the sand, and my heart turned over a great deal darkly as I went. But when I had come down to the ship and the sea, and we had got our supper ready, and immortal night had come on, then we lay down to rest on the shore of the sea. And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, I went along the shore of the broad-wayed sea, praying hard to the gods; and I took three of my companions with me, the ones I trusted most for any adventure.

"Meanwhile she had plunged under the broad breast of the sea and brought up the skins of four seals from the deep, all newly flayed, and she planned a trick against her father. She had scooped out lairs in the sand of the sea and sat waiting; and we came up close to her, and she made us lie down in a row and threw a skin over each of us. Then our ambush would have been the worst thing imaginable, because the deadly stench of the sea-bred seals distressed us horribly — who would lie down beside a beast of the sea? — but she saved us herself and thought of a great remedy. She brought ambrosia of a very sweet smell and put it under each man's nose, and killed the stink of the animal.

"So all morning we waited with steady hearts, and the seals came out of the sea in crowds. They lay down in rows along the shore of the sea, and at noon the old man came out of the sea and found his fat seals, and went over them all and counted their number. He counted us first among the creatures, and his heart never guessed there was a trick; and then he lay down himself. At that we rushed on him with a shout and threw our arms round him, and the old man did not forget his crafty tricks. First he turned into a bearded lion, and then into a snake, and a leopard, and a huge boar; then he turned into running water, and into a tall leafy tree. But we held on without flinching, with steady hearts.

"And when at last the old man grew tired, skilled in wizardry as he was, he questioned me and said: 'Which of the gods took counsel with you, son of Atreus, so that you could lie in wait for me and take me against my will? What do you need?'

"So he spoke, and I answered him: 'You know, old man — why do you try to put me off with that question? — how long I have been shut up on this island, and can find no sign of getting out, and my heart is failing inside me. Tell me — because the gods know everything — which of the immortals has me in chains here and has blocked my road, and tell me about my homecoming, how I am to go over the teeming water.'

"So I spoke, and he answered at once: 'You ought to have made fine offerings to Zeus and the other gods before you embarked, so that you might come to your own country as quickly as possible, sailing over the wine-dark sea. It is not your fate to see your friends and reach your well-built house and your own country before you have gone once more to the waters of the Aegyptus, the heaven-fed river, and made holy sacrifices to the immortal gods who hold the wide sky. Then at last the gods will grant you the journey you want.'

"So he spoke, and my spirit broke inside me, because he was telling me to go back over the misty water to Egypt, a long and weary way. Even so I answered him: 'I will do all of it, old man, exactly as you tell me. But tell me this now, and tell me the truth. Did all the Achaeans come home unhurt in their ships, all the ones Nestor and I left behind when we set out from Troy? Or did any of them die a cruel death on board his ship, or in the arms of his friends, when he had wound up the whole skein of the war?'

"So I spoke, and he answered at once: 'Son of Atreus, why do you ask me this? You have no business knowing it, or learning what is in my mind; and I do not think you will be long without tears once you have heard it all. Many of them were killed and many were left. Two leaders only of the bronze-coated Achaeans died on the way home — as for the fighting, you were there yourself — and one, I think, is still alive, and held back on the wide sea.

"'Ajax was lost among his long-oared ships. Poseidon drove him first onto the great rocks of Gyrae, and saved him out of the sea; and he would have escaped his doom, hated as he was by Athena, if he had not spoken a boastful word in the great blindness of his heart. He declared that he had got clear of the great gulf of the sea in spite of the gods. Poseidon heard him boasting, and at once took his trident in his mighty hands and struck the rock of Gyrae and split it apart. One part stayed where it was, and the broken part fell into the sea — the part Ajax had been sitting on when his heart was so blinded — and it carried him down into the boundless surging deep. So he died there, when he had drunk the salt water.

"'Your brother escaped the fates, indeed, and got clear of them in his hollow ships, because queenly Hera saved him. But when he was about to reach the steep height of Malea, then the storm wind caught him up and carried him over the teeming water, groaning heavily, to the edge of the country where Thyestes once lived, and where now his son Aegisthus lived. But when a safe homecoming showed itself even from there, and the gods turned the wind round so that it blew fair, and they reached home, then Agamemnon set foot on his own land rejoicing, and took hold of his land and kissed it, and the hot tears streamed from his eyes, because the sight of his country was welcome to him.

"'Now a watchman saw him from his post, whom sly Aegisthus had taken and set there, promising him two talents of gold as a reward; and he had been keeping guard for a year, in case Agamemnon should get past him unseen and remember his furious strength. He went to the palace to bring the news to the shepherd of the people, and Aegisthus at once planned a treacherous stroke. He picked twenty men, the best in the country, and set them in ambush, and on the other side of the hall he gave orders for a feast to be got ready. Then he went with chariot and horses to fetch Agamemnon, shepherd of the people, with a cowardly thing in his mind. He brought him up knowing nothing of his doom, and when he had feasted him he killed him, the way a man kills an ox at the stall. And not one of the companions of the son of Atreus was left, of all who followed him, and not one of Aegisthus's men: they were all killed in the halls.'

"So he spoke, and my spirit broke inside me, and I sat down on the sand and wept, and my heart had no more wish to live and see the light of the sun. But when I had had my fill of weeping and writhing on the ground, the unerring old man of the sea said to me: 'Do not weep any longer, son of Atreus, for so long a time without stopping; we shall find no help in it. Rather, try with all the speed you have to get to your own country. Either you will find Aegisthus alive, or Orestes may have got in ahead of you and killed him, and you may come in on his funeral feast.'

"So he spoke, and my heart and spirit were warmed with comfort again inside me, in spite of my grief, and I spoke to him with winged words: 'These two I know of now. Name the third, the one who is still alive and held back on the wide sea — or dead, it may be. I want to hear it, in spite of my grief.'

"So I spoke, and he answered at once: 'It is the son of Laertes, whose home is in Ithaca. I saw him on an island, shedding big tears, in the halls of the nymph Calypso, who keeps him there by force; and he cannot come to his own country, because he has no ships with oars at hand and no companions to send him on his way over the broad back of the sea. But as for you, Menelaus, brought up by Zeus, it is not ordained that you should die and meet your fate in horse-pasturing Argos. The immortals will carry you to the Elysian plain at the ends of the earth, where fair-haired Rhadamanthus lives, and where living is easiest for men. There is no snow there, and no heavy storm, and it never rains, but Ocean always sends up the blasts of the shrill west wind to cool men; because you have Helen for a wife, and in their eyes you are the husband of the daughter of Zeus.'

"With that he plunged under the surging sea, and I went to my ships with my godlike companions, and my heart turned over a great deal darkly as I went. But when I had come down to the ship and the sea, and we had got our supper ready, and immortal night had come on, then we lay down to rest on the shore of the sea. And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, first of all we drew our ships down to the bright sea, and set the masts and the sails in the trim ships, and the men went aboard and sat down at the benches, and sitting in their order struck the grey sea with their oars.

"So I sailed back to the waters of the Aegyptus, the heaven-fed river, and moored my ships there and made the full sacrifices. And when I had stopped the anger of the gods who live for ever, I heaped up a mound for Agamemnon, so that his fame might never be put out. When I had finished that I set out for home, and the immortals gave me a fair wind and brought me quickly to my own dear country.

"But come now, stay in my halls until the eleventh or the twelfth day. Then I will send you off with honour and give you splendid gifts: three horses and a well-polished car. And besides that I will give you a beautiful cup, so that you can pour offerings to the immortal gods and remember me all your days."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Son of Atreus, do not keep me here a long time. I would be content to sit in your house for a year without any longing for home or parents coming on me — the pleasure I take in listening to your stories and your talk is extraordinary. But my companions are already fretting in sacred Pylos, and you are keeping me here a long while. And whatever gift you would give me, let it be some treasure. I will not take horses to Ithaca; I will leave them here for you to enjoy, since you are lord of a wide plain where there is lotus in plenty, and galingale, and wheat and spelt, and broad-eared white barley. In Ithaca there are no wide tracks and no meadowland at all. It is a pasture for goats, and pleasanter than one that pastures horses. Not one of the islands that lean on the sea is fit for driving horses or rich in meadows, and Ithaca least of all."

So he spoke, and Menelaus smiled, good at the war-cry, and stroked him with his hand and said to him: "You are of good blood, dear child, to talk like that. So I will change the gifts; I can well enough. Of all the gifts stored as treasure in my house, I will give you the finest and the most valuable. I will give you a well-made mixing bowl. It is all of silver, with the rims finished in gold, the work of Hephaestus; and the warrior Phaedimus, king of the Sidonians, gave it to me when his house sheltered me on my way there, and now I have a mind to give it to you."

So they talked to each other, and meanwhile the banqueters were coming to the palace of the king. They drove up sheep and brought strengthening wine, and their wives with their beautiful veils sent them bread. So they were busy about the feast in the halls.

Meanwhile the suitors in front of the palace of Odysseus were amusing themselves, throwing the discus and the javelin on the levelled ground, in their usual insolence of heart. Antinous and godlike Eurymachus were sitting there, the leaders of the suitors, who were far the best of them in ability. Noemon, son of Phronius, came up to them and put a question to Antinous.

"Antinous, do we know at all, or not, when Telemachus is coming back from sandy Pylos? He has gone off with a ship of mine, and I need her to cross over to spacious Elis, where I have twelve brood mares with sturdy mules still unbroken at the teat. I want to drive one of them off and break him in."

So he spoke, and their hearts were astonished, because they had not supposed Telemachus had gone to Neleian Pylos, but that he was out there somewhere on his lands, among the flocks or with the swineherd. Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, said to him:

"Tell me the truth: when did he go, and which young men went with him? Were they picked men of Ithaca, or his own hired men and slaves? He would be capable of managing even that. And tell me this truly, so that I know for certain. Did he take the black ship from you by force and against your will, or did you give it him freely of your own accord, because he asked you?"

Then Noemon, son of Phronius, answered him: "I gave it him freely myself. What else could any man do, when a man like that, with his heart full of trouble, comes asking? It would be hard to refuse the gift. The young men who went with him are the best in the country after ourselves; and I noticed one going aboard as their leader — Mentor, or a god who was in every way like Mentor. But that is what puzzles me. I saw noble Mentor here yesterday at daybreak, and yet he had embarked for Pylos before that."

With that he went off to his father's house, and the proud hearts of those two were angry. They made the suitors sit down and leave off their games, and Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them, in a rage. His black heart was full of it, and his eyes were like blazing fire.

"The insolence of it — a great piece of work has been carried through, this journey of Telemachus, and we thought he would never get it done. Off the boy goes, in spite of all of us here, without a word — launching a ship and picking the best men in the country. He will be starting to be our ruin before long. But may Zeus destroy his strength, to his own undoing, before he reaches the full measure of manhood. Come, give me a fast ship and twenty men, so that I can lie in wait for him and watch as he passes in the strait between Ithaca and rugged Samos. This voyage of his after his father will come to a sorry end."

So he spoke, and they all praised his words and told him to get on with it; and they got up at once and went to the house of Odysseus.

Now Penelope was not long without knowledge of the plans the suitors were plotting in the depths of their hearts, because the herald Medon told her. He had heard what they were deciding, standing outside the court while they were weaving their plot inside. He went through the hall to bring the news to Penelope, and as he stepped over the threshold she spoke to him and said:

"Herald, why have the lordly suitors sent you out? To tell the women of godlike Odysseus to stop their work and get a feast ready for them? I wish they would stop courting and stop gathering here, and that this were the last meal they ever ate — you men who are always crowding in here and wasting so much of the living, the wealth of clear-headed Telemachus. Surely you never listened, in the old days when you were children, when your fathers told you what kind of man Odysseus was among the men who fathered you: how he did no wrong to any man in the country, in word or in deed, though that is the way with kings, who hate one man and love another. He never did anything unjust to anyone at all. But your minds and your unseemly behaviour are there for anyone to see, and there is no gratitude afterwards for good deeds done."

Then Medon, who had good sense, answered her: "I wish, my queen, that this were the worst of it. But the suitors are planning something far greater and far more terrible, which I pray the son of Cronos never brings about. They intend to kill Telemachus with the sharp sword on his way home. He has gone after news of his father to sacred Pylos and to lovely Lacedaemon."

So he spoke, and her knees went slack where she sat and her heart melted. For a long time she could not speak; her eyes filled with tears and the flow of her voice was checked. At last she answered him and said:

"Herald, why has my son gone? He had no need to go aboard the fast-going ships, which serve men as horses of the deep and cross the wide waters of the sea. Was it so that not even his name should be left among men?"

Then Medon, who had good sense, answered her: "I do not know whether some god drove him to it, or whether his own heart moved him to go to Pylos, to learn either of his father's homecoming or what fate he met."

So he spoke, and went off through the house of Odysseus; and a cloud of soul-consuming grief came down on her. She had no heart left to sit on any of the many seats in the room, but sank down on the threshold of her beautifully made chamber, moaning pitifully, and all round her the women wailed, all of them in the house, young and old alike. Penelope spoke among them, sobbing.

"Hear me, my friends. The Olympian has given me sorrow beyond all the women who were born and brought up with me. Long ago I lost my noble husband with the lion heart, who stood out among the Danaans in every kind of excellence, my noble husband, whose fame goes wide through Hellas and the middle of Argos. And now the storm winds have swept my well-loved son away out of the halls without a word, and I never heard he was going. Cruel, all of you — not one of you thought to rouse me out of my bed, though in your hearts you knew perfectly well when he went aboard the hollow black ship. If I had known he was thinking of this journey, he would have stayed here, however eager he was to be gone, or he would have left me dead in the halls.

"Now let one of you hurry and call old Dolius here, my servant, whom my father gave me before I came here, and who keeps my garden with all its trees. Let him go at once and sit down beside Laertes and tell him all this. It may be Laertes can weave some plan in his heart, and come out and make his case with tears to the people, who mean to destroy his line and the line of godlike Odysseus."

Then the good nurse Eurycleia answered her: "Dear lady, you can kill me with the pitiless bronze or let me live on in the house, but I am not going to keep it from you. I knew all this and gave him what he told me to, bread and sweet wine. But he made me swear a great oath not to tell you until at least the twelfth day came, or you missed him yourself and heard he was gone, so that you should not spoil your lovely face with weeping. Now wash, and put clean clothes on, and go up to your room with your women and pray to Athena, the daughter of Zeus who holds the aegis; she may save him even from death. And do not trouble an old man who has trouble enough. I do not think the family of the son of Arceisius is altogether hated by the blessed gods. There will still be one of them, I think, to hold the high-roofed halls and the rich fields lying far off."

So she spoke, and quieted Penelope's laments and stopped her eyes from weeping. She washed and put clean clothes on and went up to her room with her women, and put barley grains in a basket, and prayed to Athena.

"Hear me, child of Zeus who holds the aegis, unwearied one. If ever Odysseus of the many tricks burned fat thigh-pieces of heifer or ewe to you in his halls, remember them now, I beg you, and save my dear son, and keep the suitors off him in their wicked insolence."

With that she raised the sacred cry, and the goddess heard her prayer.

But the suitors broke into uproar through the shadowy halls, and one of the arrogant young men would say: "Our queen, courted by so many, is certainly getting our marriage ready — and she does not know at all that her son has been marked for death."

That is how one of them talked; but they did not know how these things were going to go. Then Antinous spoke to the company and said: "You fools, keep off this high talk, all of you alike, in case somebody reports what you say inside the house. Come, let us get up quietly like this and put into effect the plan that pleased every one of us."

So he spoke, and chose the twenty best men, and they went off to the fast ship and the shore of the sea. First they drew the ship down to deep water and set the mast and sail in the black ship, and fitted the oars in the leather thole-straps, all in their order, and spread the white sail; and proud attendants brought them their weapons. They moored the ship well out in the roadstead and went ashore themselves, and there they took their supper and waited for the evening to come.

But wise Penelope lay up in her room, touching no food, tasting neither meat nor drink, turning over whether her faultless son would escape death or be killed by the insolent suitors. As a lion is seized with fear and broods in the middle of a crowd of men when they are drawing their cunning ring about him, so she was turning it over when sweet sleep came on her. She sank back and slept, and every joint in her went slack.

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena thought of something else. She made a phantom and gave it the form of a woman, Iphthime, the daughter of great-hearted Icarius, whom Eumelus had married, whose home was in Pherae. And she sent it to the house of godlike Odysseus, to Penelope in the middle of her wailing and crying, to tell her to stop weeping and tearful lamenting. It passed into the room by the thong of the bolt and stood over her head and said to her:

"Are you asleep, Penelope, with your heart so stricken? The gods who live at their ease do not allow you to weep or be distressed, since your son is going to come back after all. He is no sinner in the eyes of the gods."

Then wise Penelope answered her, slumbering very sweetly at the gates of dreams: "Why have you come here, sister? You never used to come before, because your home is a long way off. And you tell me to stop grieving and to stop the many pains that distress me in my mind and heart. Long ago I lost my noble husband with the lion heart, who stood out among the Danaans in every kind of excellence, my noble husband, whose fame goes wide through Hellas and the middle of Argos. And now my well-loved son has gone off in a hollow ship, a mere child, who knows nothing of hardship or of the gatherings of men. I grieve for him even more than for the other one, and I tremble for him, and I am afraid something will happen to him, either in the country he has gone to or on the sea. There are a great many enemies plotting against him, eager to kill him before he comes back to his own country."

Then the dim phantom answered her and said: "Take heart, and do not be too much afraid in your mind. There is such a guide going with him as other men have often begged to have stand beside them, because she has the power — Pallas Athena. And she pities you in your grief. It is she who has sent me out to tell you this."

Then wise Penelope answered her again: "If you are indeed a god, and have heard the voice of a god, come, tell me about that unlucky man too — whether he is still alive and sees the light of the sun, or whether he is dead already and in the house of Hades."

And the dim phantom answered her and said: "About him I cannot speak at length, whether he is alive or dead. It is a bad thing to speak words as empty as wind."

With that the phantom slipped away by the bolt of the door into the breath of the winds. And the daughter of Icarius started up out of her sleep, and her heart was warmed with comfort that so clear a vision had come speeding to her in the dark of the night.

But the suitors embarked and sailed over the watery roads, with utter murder for Telemachus in their hearts. There is a rocky island in the middle of the sea, halfway between Ithaca and rugged Samos — Asteris, not large, but there is a harbour in it where ships can lie, with an entrance on either side. That is where the Achaeans waited, lying in ambush for Telemachus.

Book Five

Dawn rose from her bed beside lordly Tithonus, to bring light to the immortals and to mortal men. And the gods were sitting down to council, and among them Zeus who thunders on high, whose strength is greatest. Athena was telling them the many troubles of Odysseus, calling them to mind, because it disturbed her that he was staying in the nymph's house.

"Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live for ever — from now on let no sceptred king be deliberately kind and gentle, or take any care for what is right in his mind. Let him be harsh, always, and do injustice, seeing that nobody remembers godlike Odysseus, among the people he was lord over, and he was gentle as a father. He is on an island suffering terribly, in the halls of the nymph Calypso, who keeps him there by force; and he cannot get back to his own land, because he has no ships with oars at hand and no companions to send him on his way over the broad back of the sea. And now on top of that they mean to kill his well-loved son on his way home; for he has gone after news of his father to sacred Pylos and to lovely Lacedaemon."

Then Zeus who gathers the clouds answered her and said: "My child, what a thing to let past your teeth. Did you not plan this yourself — that Odysseus should take his revenge on those men when he came? As for Telemachus, guide him with your own skill, since you can, so that he reaches his own country entirely unhurt, and the suitors come back in their ship with nothing accomplished."

He spoke, and said to Hermes, his dear son: "Hermes, since you are our messenger at other times, go now and tell the nymph with the lovely hair what we have firmly decided: the homecoming of Odysseus, who endures everything. He is to go back with no guidance from gods or from mortal men, but on a raft bound firm, suffering as he goes, and on the twentieth day he is to come to deep-soiled Scheria, the land of the Phaeacians, who are close kin to the gods. They will honour him heartily, as if he were a god, and send him in a ship to his own dear country, after giving him stores of bronze and gold and clothing, more than Odysseus would ever have brought back from Troy if he had come home unhurt with his fair share of the plunder. That is how it is his fate to see his friends and reach his high-roofed house and his own country."

So he spoke, and the messenger, the slayer of Argus, did not fail to listen. Straight away he bound the beautiful sandals under his feet, immortal and golden, which carry him over the water and over the endless land as fast as a gust of wind. And he took up the wand with which he lulls the eyes of whoever he likes to sleep, and wakes others again out of their slumber. With this in his hand the strong slayer of Argus flew.

He stepped onto Pieria out of the upper air and swooped down on the sea, and then went skimming over the wave like a bird, the cormorant, which hunts fish over the dreadful gulfs of the restless sea and wets its thick feathers in the salt water. That was how Hermes rode over the crowding waves.

But when he reached the island, which lay far off, he came out of the violet sea onto the land, and went on until he came to a great cave, where the nymph with the lovely hair lived; and he found her inside. A great fire was burning on the hearth, and the smell of split cedar and juniper burning carried far over the island; and inside she was singing in a sweet voice as she went to and fro at the loom, weaving with a golden shuttle.

Round the cave a thick wood was growing, alder and poplar and sweet-smelling cypress, where long-winged birds nested, owls and falcons and chattering sea-crows, who do their business on the sea. And right there about the hollow cave a garden vine ran trailing, in the pride of its season, thick with clusters. And four springs in a row were flowing with bright water, close beside one another, turned one this way and one that. And soft meadows of violets and parsley were in bloom all round. Even an immortal coming there might stand and stare and take delight in it; and the messenger, the slayer of Argus, stood there and stared.

But when he had marvelled at it all in his heart, he went into the wide cave. Calypso, the beautiful goddess, knew him at once when she saw him face to face; the immortal gods are not strangers to one another, even when one of them lives far away. But he did not find great-hearted Odysseus inside, because he was sitting on the shore, as he always did, wearing out his soul with tears and groans and grief, looking out over the restless sea and weeping.

And Calypso, the beautiful goddess, questioned Hermes, after she had made him sit down on a bright shining chair.

"Why have you come here, Hermes of the golden wand, an honoured guest and a welcome one? You have not been in the habit of coming before. Say what is in your mind. My heart tells me to do it, if I can do it, and if it is a thing that can be done. But come further in and let me set something before you."

With that the goddess set a table beside him loaded with ambrosia, and mixed the red nectar. So the messenger, the slayer of Argus, drank and ate. And when he had dined and satisfied himself with food, he answered her and said:

"You question me on my coming, a goddess questioning a god, and I will tell you the truth, since you ask me. It was Zeus who told me to come here, against my will. Who would choose to run across such a stretch of salt water, past all telling? And there is no city of mortals near who make sacrifice to the gods and choice hecatombs. But there is no possible way for any other god to get round the will of Zeus who holds the aegis, or make it come to nothing.

"He says there is a man here with you who is the most wretched of all those fighters who fought round Priam's city for nine years, and in the tenth year sacked the city and set out for home. On the way they sinned against Athena, and she sent an evil wind and long waves against them. There all the rest of his fine companions died, and as for him, the wind and the wave carried him and brought him here. Zeus now tells you to send him on his way as quickly as possible. It is not his fate to die here, far from his friends. It is still his lot to see his friends and to reach his high-roofed house and his own country."

So he spoke, and Calypso, the beautiful goddess, shuddered, and spoke to him with winged words.

"You are cruel, you gods, and jealous beyond anything, since you grudge it to goddesses to sleep with men openly, if any of us takes a mortal for a lover. So when rosy-fingered Dawn took Orion for herself, you gods who live at your ease grudged it to her, until in Ortygia chaste Artemis of the golden throne attacked him with her gentle arrows and killed him. So too, when Demeter with the lovely hair gave way to her feeling and lay in love with Iasion in the thrice-ploughed fallow field, Zeus did not stay ignorant of it long, but struck him with his bright thunderbolt and killed him. And now you grudge it to me in the same way, you gods, that a mortal man should stay with me.

"I saved him when he was astride the keel and all alone, because Zeus had struck his fast ship with his bright thunderbolt and smashed it in the middle of the wine-dark sea. There all the rest of his fine companions died, and as for him, the wind and the wave carried him and brought him here. I welcomed him kindly and gave him food, and said I would make him immortal and ageless all his days. But since there is no possible way for any other god to get round the will of Zeus who holds the aegis, or make it come to nothing, let him go, if Zeus orders it and commands it, over the restless sea. But I will not be the one to see him off, because I have no ships with oars at hand and no men to send him on his way over the broad back of the sea. Still, I will advise him willingly and hide nothing, so that he can get back to his own country entirely unhurt."

Then the messenger, the slayer of Argus, answered her: "Send him off now, then, and beware of the anger of Zeus, in case he grows angry with you and takes it out on you afterwards."

With that the strong slayer of Argus went away, and the queenly nymph went to great-hearted Odysseus, now that she had heard the message of Zeus. She found him sitting on the shore, and his eyes were never dry of tears, and his sweet life was ebbing away as he mourned for his homecoming, since the nymph no longer pleased him. At night he did sleep beside her in the hollow caves, because he had to, unwilling beside a willing woman; but by day he would sit on the rocks and the sands, wearing out his soul with tears and groans and grief, and look out over the restless sea and weep.

Coming up close to him, the beautiful goddess spoke to him. "Unhappy man, do not go on grieving here, and do not let your life waste away. I am ready now to send you off. Come, cut long beams with the axe and make a broad raft, and fix cross-planks on it for a deck well up above, to carry you over the misty water. And I will put bread and water and red wine in it to please your heart and keep hunger off you. And I will put clothes on you and send a fair wind behind you, so that you get back to your own country entirely unhurt — if it is the will of the gods who hold the wide sky, who are stronger than I am both to plan and to bring about."

So she spoke, and much-enduring noble Odysseus shuddered, and spoke to her with winged words. "You are planning something else here, goddess, and not my sending off, when you tell me to cross the great gulf of the sea on a raft — a dreadful and hard crossing, which not even the trim fast-running ships get over, glad as they are of a wind from Zeus. I will not set foot on a raft against your will, unless you will bring yourself, goddess, to swear a great oath that you are not plotting some fresh mischief against me to my harm."

So he spoke, and Calypso, the beautiful goddess, smiled and stroked him with her hand and said to him: "You are a rogue, and no fool either, to have thought of saying such a thing. Let the earth be my witness in this, then, and the wide sky above, and the down-flowing water of the Styx, which is the greatest and most dreadful oath for the blessed gods, that I am not plotting any fresh mischief against you to your harm. What I have in mind, and what I mean to advise, is what I would work out for myself if such a need came on me. My mind is a fair one, and the heart in this breast of mine is not made of iron: it feels pity."

With that the beautiful goddess led the way quickly, and he followed in the goddess's footsteps. And they came to the hollow cave, the goddess and the man, and he sat down on the chair Hermes had got up from, and the nymph set out every kind of food for him to eat and drink, the kind mortal men eat. She herself sat down facing godlike Odysseus, and the women set ambrosia and nectar in front of her. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them. And when they had had their fill of food and drink, Calypso, the beautiful goddess, spoke first.

"Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks — is it home to your own dear country that you want to go, straight away, now? Well, even so, I wish you well. But if you knew in your heart the full measure of suffering it is your fate to fill up before you reach your own country, you would stay here and keep this house with me and be immortal — for all your longing to see your wife, whom you want day after day. I do not think I am the lesser of us in looks or in build. It is not seemly for mortal women to compete with immortals in shape or beauty."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Great goddess, do not be angry with me over this. I know perfectly well myself that wise Penelope is less to look at than you, in beauty and in build. She is mortal, and you are immortal and ageless. But even so I want, and I long day after day, to get home and see the day of my return. And if some god strikes me again on the wine-dark sea, I will endure it; I have a heart in my breast that endures affliction. I have suffered a great deal already and worked hard, on the waves and in the war. Let this be added to that."

So he spoke, and the sun went down and the dark came on. And the two of them went into the innermost recess of the hollow cave and took their pleasure in love, staying beside one another.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, Odysseus put on a cloak and a tunic, and the nymph dressed herself in a long white robe, finely woven and beautiful, and put a fine golden girdle round her waist and a veil on her head. Then she set about planning the sending-off of great-hearted Odysseus. She gave him a great axe that fitted his hands, an axe of bronze, sharpened on both sides, with a beautiful olive-wood handle fastened firmly in it; and after that she gave him a polished adze. Then she led the way to the far edge of the island where tall trees were standing, alder and poplar and fir, reaching up to the sky, long dead and well seasoned, which would float lightly for him.

And when she had shown him where the tall trees grew, Calypso, the beautiful goddess, went home, and he set to cutting timber, and the work went fast. He felled twenty trees in all and trimmed them with the axe, and then smoothed them all skilfully and made them straight to the line. Meanwhile Calypso, the beautiful goddess, brought him augers, and he bored all the pieces and fitted them to one another and hammered it together with pegs and joints. As wide as a man well skilled in carpentry marks out the curve of the hull of a broad-beamed freighter, that wide did Odysseus make his raft. He set up the deck-beams, bolting them to the close-set ribs, and worked on, and finished the raft with long gunwales. He set a mast in it and a yard-arm fitted to it, and made himself a steering-oar besides, to steer with. Then he fenced the whole thing in from stem to stern with willow withes, to keep the waves off, and piled brush on top. Meanwhile Calypso, the beautiful goddess, brought him cloth to make a sail, and he made that skilfully too, and made the braces and halyards and sheets fast in the raft, and then forced it down into the bright sea with levers.

The fourth day came and all his work was done, and on the fifth beautiful Calypso sent him off from the island, after she had bathed him and dressed him in sweet-smelling clothes. The goddess put a skin of dark wine on the raft, and another, a big one, of water, and provisions in a bag, and plenty of good things in it to please his heart, and she sent out a gentle warm wind. Noble Odysseus spread his sail to the breeze gladly, and sat and steered his raft skilfully with the steering-oar, and no sleep fell on his eyelids as he watched the Pleiades, and late-setting Bootes, and the Bear, which men also call the Wain, which always turns where it is and keeps watch on Orion, and alone has no share in the baths of Ocean. Calypso, the beautiful goddess, had told him to keep that star on his left as he sailed over the sea.

So for seventeen days he sailed over the sea, and on the eighteenth the shadowy mountains of the land of the Phaeacians appeared, at the point where it lay nearest to him; and it looked like a shield in the misty water.

But the glorious earth-shaker, coming back from the Ethiopians, saw him far off from the mountains of the Solymi, because Odysseus came into his sight sailing over the sea. He grew angrier still in his heart, and shook his head, and said to himself:

"So the gods have changed their minds about Odysseus while I was among the Ethiopians. And there he is, close to the land of the Phaeacians, where it is his fate to get clear of the great chain of misery that has come on him. But even yet, I think, I shall drive him to his fill of trouble."

With that he gathered the clouds, and took his trident in his hands and stirred up the sea, and roused the blasts of every kind of wind, and hid land and sea alike in cloud; and night rushed down out of the sky. The east wind and the south wind clashed together, and the hard-blowing west wind and the north wind, born in the bright sky, rolling a huge wave before it.

Then the knees of Odysseus went slack and his heart melted, and in his distress he said to his own great spirit: "Wretch that I am — what is going to happen to me at the end of it? I am afraid everything the goddess said was true, when she told me that on the sea, before I reached my own country, I should fill up my measure of suffering. All of it is happening now. Look how Zeus is covering the wide sky with clouds and has stirred up the sea, and the blasts of every kind of wind sweep down on me. Now my destruction is certain.

"Three times blessed those Danaans, four times blessed, who died long ago in the wide land of Troy doing what the sons of Atreus wanted. I wish I had died and met my fate that day when the crowds of Trojans were throwing bronze-tipped spears at me, fighting over the dead body of the son of Peleus. Then I would have had funeral rites, and the Achaeans would have spread my fame; but now it was appointed for me to be cut off by a miserable death."

Even as he spoke the great wave struck him from above, rushing on him with terrible force, and whirled his raft round. He fell far from the raft and let the steering-oar go out of his hand, and the mast was broken in the middle by the fierce blast of the tumbling winds that came on it, and the sail and yard-arm fell far out in the sea. The wave held him under a long time, and he could not come up at once from under the rush of the mighty water, because the clothes beautiful Calypso had given him weighed him down. At last he came up and spat the bitter brine out of his mouth, which ran in streams off his head. Even so he did not forget his raft, badly off as he was, but went after it through the waves and got hold of it and sat down in the middle of it, trying to keep clear of the doom of death; and a great wave kept carrying him this way and that along its course.

As when in autumn the north wind carries the thistle-tufts over the plain and they cling close to one another, so the winds carried the raft this way and that over the sea. Now the south wind would fling it to the north wind to drive, and now the east wind would give it up to the west wind to chase.

But the daughter of Cadmus saw him, Ino of the lovely ankles, Leucothea, who was once a mortal with human speech and now has her share of honour from the gods in the depths of the sea. She was touched with pity for Odysseus as he drifted in his misery, and she rose up out of the deep like a sea-mew on the wing, and sat on the firmly-bound raft, and said:

"Unhappy man, why has Poseidon the earth-shaker taken such a furious anger against you, that he is sowing so many troubles for you? Yet he shall not destroy you altogether, for all his rage. Do this — and I do not think you lack understanding. Strip off these clothes and leave your raft for the winds to drive, and swim with your hands and try to reach the land of the Phaeacians, where it is your fate to escape. Here, take this veil and stretch it under your breast. It is immortal; there is no fear that you will suffer anything or drown. But when you have got your hands on the land, untie it and throw it into the wine-dark sea, well away from the land, and turn your own face away."

With that the goddess gave him the veil, and plunged back into the surging deep herself like a sea-mew, and the dark wave hid her.

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus turned it over, and in his distress he said to his own great spirit: "Oh no. Let it not be that one of the immortals is weaving another snare for me, telling me to leave my raft. I will not obey yet, because with my own eyes I saw that the land is a long way off, where she said I was to escape. This is what I will do, and it seems best: as long as the timbers hold firm in their fastenings, I will stay here and endure the suffering; and when the wave has smashed the raft to pieces, I will swim, since there is nothing better to think of."

While he was turning it over in his mind and heart, Poseidon the earth-shaker raised up a great wave, dreadful and hard, arching over from above, and drove it at him. As a strong wind tosses a heap of dry straw and scatters some this way and some that, so the wave scattered the long timbers of the raft. But Odysseus got astride one plank as if he were riding a horse, and stripped off the clothes beautiful Calypso had given him. Then he stretched the veil under his breast at once and threw himself headlong into the sea with his hands spread, ready to swim.

And the lord, the earth-shaker, saw him, and shook his head, and said to himself: "So now go wandering over the deep, after all the trouble you have suffered, until you come among the people whom Zeus brought up. Even so, I do not think you will make light of your suffering."

With that he whipped up his fine-maned horses and came to Aegae, where his glorious palace is.

But Athena, daughter of Zeus, thought of something else. She blocked the paths of the other winds and told them all to stop and be lulled to rest; but she roused the swift north wind and broke the waves down in front of him, so that Odysseus, born of Zeus, might come among the Phaeacians, who love the oar, and get clear of death and the fates.

So for two nights and two days he was driven about on the swollen waves, and again and again his heart foresaw destruction. But when Dawn with the lovely hair brought the third day to its birth, then the wind dropped and there was a windless calm, and he caught sight of the shore close at hand, throwing a quick glance forward as a great wave lifted him. And as a father's life is most welcome to his children when he has been lying ill, suffering hard pains, wasting away a long while, with some cruel god attacking him, and then to their joy the gods free him from his trouble — so welcome the land and the woods looked to Odysseus, and he swam on, eager to set foot on the ground.

But when he was as far off as a man's voice carries when he shouts, and heard the boom of the sea on the reefs — for the great wave was thundering against the dry land, belching over it terribly, and everything was wrapped in the foam of the sea, since there were no harbours there where ships could ride, and no roadsteads, only jutting headlands and reefs and cliffs — then the knees of Odysseus went slack and his heart melted, and in his distress he said to his own great spirit:

"Oh no. Zeus has granted me to see land at last, beyond all my hoping, and I have managed to cut my way across this gulf, and now nowhere is there a way out of the grey sea. Outside there are sharp crags, and the wave roars foaming round them, and the rock runs up sheer, and the water is deep close in, so that there is no way at all to plant both feet firmly and get clear. If I try to land, a great wave may seize me and dash me against the jagged rock, and my struggle will have been for nothing. But if I swim on further, hoping to find shelving beaches and harbours of the sea, I am afraid the storm wind may catch me up again and carry me groaning heavily over the teeming deep — or a god may send some great monster out of the sea at me, and glorious Amphitrite breeds a great many of them. For I know the glorious earth-shaker is full of anger against me."

While he was turning it over in his mind and heart, a great wave carried him against the rugged shore. There his skin would have been stripped off and his bones broken, if the goddess grey-eyed Athena had not put a thought in his mind. He rushed on and seized the rock with both hands and clung to it, groaning, until the great wave went past. So he got clear of that wave; but in its backward rush it came at him again and struck him and flung him far out to sea. As when a cuttlefish is dragged out of its hole and a great many pebbles cling to its suckers, so bits of skin were stripped off his strong hands against the rocks; and the great wave covered him.

Then unlucky Odysseus would have died before his time, if grey-eyed Athena had not given him presence of mind. He made his way out of the surge where it belched on the shore, and swam along outside, keeping his eyes on the land all the time, hoping to find shelving beaches and harbours of the sea. But when, swimming on, he came to the mouth of a fair-flowing river, the place looked best to him: it was smooth of stones, and there was shelter from the wind besides. He recognised the river as it flowed out, and prayed to him in his heart:

"Hear me, king, whoever you are. I come to you as to one greatly longed for, trying to get out of the sea, away from the threats of Poseidon. Even in the eyes of the immortal gods that man deserves respect who comes as a wanderer, as I have come now to your stream and to your knees, after so much hard labour. Pity me, king. I declare that I am your suppliant."

So he spoke, and the god stopped his stream at once, and checked the waves, and made a calm in front of him, and brought him safely to the river mouth. He let his two knees bend and his strong hands fall, because the sea had crushed his spirit. All his flesh was swollen, and sea water ran in streams up through his mouth and his nostrils. So he lay there breathless and speechless, with hardly the strength to move; a terrible weariness had come over him.

But when he came round, and his spirit came back into his breast, he loosed the goddess's veil from him and let it fall into the river that murmured seaward. The great wave carried it back down the stream, and Ino caught it at once in her hands. Then Odysseus went back from the river and sank down in the reeds and kissed the earth, the giver of grain; and in his distress he said to his own great spirit:

"What is to become of me? What is going to happen to me at the end of it? If I keep watch here in the river bed through the miserable night, I am afraid the bitter frost and the fresh dew together will finish me, when I have breathed out my spirit from weakness; and the breeze off the river blows cold in the early morning. But if I climb the slope to the shady wood and lie down to rest in the thick brushwood, hoping the cold and the weariness will leave me and sweet sleep come over me, then I am afraid I shall be a prey and a spoil for wild animals."

As he turned it over, this seemed the better course. He went off to the wood and found it near the water in an open space, and crept under two bushes that grew from the same spot, one of thorn and one of olive. The strength of the wet winds could never blow through them, nor could the rays of the bright sun beat in, nor the rain get through, so closely they grew, twisted into one another. Odysseus crept in under these and at once gathered a wide bed with his hands, for there were fallen leaves there in plenty, enough to shelter two men or three in winter, however bitter the weather. Much-enduring noble Odysseus saw it and was glad, and lay down in the middle and heaped the fallen leaves over himself.

And as a man hides a brand under the dark embers on an outlying farm, a man who has no neighbours, and so saves a seed of fire and does not have to kindle it from somewhere else, so Odysseus covered himself with leaves. And Athena poured sleep over his eyes, to close his eyelids and free him quickly from his wearing exhaustion.

Book Six

So he lay there asleep, much-enduring noble Odysseus, overcome with sleep and weariness. But Athena went to the country and the city of the Phaeacians. They had lived once in spacious Hypereia, close to the Cyclopes, men overbearing in their pride, who kept plundering them and were stronger than they were. Godlike Nausithous had moved them from there and settled them in Scheria, far from men who live by their labour. He had drawn a wall round the city and built houses and made temples for the gods and divided up the ploughland. But fate had struck him down before this and he had gone to the house of Hades, and Alcinous was king now, made wise in counsel by the gods.

The goddess grey-eyed Athena went to his house, planning the homecoming of great-hearted Odysseus. She went to a richly worked room where a girl was sleeping who was like the immortal goddesses in shape and beauty, Nausicaa, the daughter of great-hearted Alcinous. Two maids slept close by, given their beauty by the Graces, one on either side of the doorposts, and the shining doors were shut. But the goddess sped to the girl's bed like a breath of air and stood over her head and spoke to her, taking the form of the daughter of Dymas, who was famous for his ships, a girl of the same age as Nausicaa and dear to her heart. Looking like her, grey-eyed Athena said:

"Nausicaa, how did your mother come to bear such a careless daughter? Your bright clothes are lying about uncared for, and your marriage is close, when you will have to be dressed in fine things yourself and give the same to the people who escort you. It is out of things like these that a good name goes up among men, and a father and an honoured mother are glad. Come, let us go and wash them at daybreak. I will come along and help you, so that you can get ready quickly; you are not going to stay unmarried much longer. You have suitors in the country already, the noblest of all the Phaeacians, where your own family comes from. Come, stir your noble father up early this morning to get mules and a wagon ready for you, to carry the sashes and the robes and the bright coverlets. And it is much more fitting for you to go like that than on foot; the washing tanks are a long way from the city."

With that the goddess grey-eyed Athena went off to Olympus, where they say the home of the gods stands firm for ever. It is not shaken by winds, nor ever wet with rain, nor does snow fall on it, but the air is spread out clear and cloudless, and a radiant whiteness plays over it. There the blessed gods take their pleasure all their days; and there the grey-eyed one went, when she had said everything to the girl.

At once fair-throned Dawn came and woke Nausicaa of the beautiful robes, and she was astonished at once by her dream and went through the house to tell her parents, her dear father and her mother; and she found them both inside. Her mother was sitting at the hearth with her women, spinning the purple-dyed yarn, and she met her father as he was going out to join the famous kings at the council, where the lordly Phaeacians had called him. She came up close to her dear father and said:

"Papa dear, will you not get a wagon ready for me, a high one with strong wheels, so that I can take the good clothes of mine that are lying here dirty down to the river to wash? Besides, it is only fitting that you should have clean clothes on when you are at the council with the princes. And you have five sons living in your halls — two are married and three are sturdy bachelors — and they always want freshly washed clothes to put on when they go to the dance. All this is on my mind."

That is what she said, because she was ashamed to speak of a joyful marriage to her father; but he understood it all, and answered: "I do not grudge you the mules, my child, or anything else. Go. The slaves will get the wagon ready for you, a high one with strong wheels and a box fitted on top."

With that he called to the slaves, and they listened. They got the light-running mule wagon ready outside the palace, and led the mules up and yoked them to it; and the girl brought the bright clothes out of her room and put them on the polished car, while her mother packed food of every kind in a chest to please the heart, and put good things in it, and poured wine into a goatskin flask. The girl climbed onto the wagon, and her mother gave her soft olive oil in a golden flask as well, so that she and her maids could use it after bathing.

Then Nausicaa took the whip and the bright reins and struck the mules to start them, and the mules went clattering off at speed, carrying the clothes and the girl. She did not go alone; her women went with her.

Now when they came to the beautiful streams of the river, where the washing tanks were that never ran dry — plenty of clear water welled up from below and flowed over, enough to clean clothes however dirty — they unyoked the mules from the wagon and drove them along the eddying river to graze on the honey-sweet grass by the water, and took the clothes out of the wagon in their arms and carried them into the dark water and trampled them in the trenches, working busily and racing each other. And when they had washed the clothes and got all the stains out, they spread them in rows on the shore of the sea, where the waves beating against the land washed the pebbles cleanest. Then they bathed and rubbed themselves richly with oil, and had their meal on the river banks, and waited for the clothes to dry in the bright sun.

And when they had enjoyed their food, she and her women threw off their headscarves and started playing at ball, and white-armed Nausicaa led the singing. As Artemis the archer ranges over the mountains, along the ridges of high Taygetus or Erymanthus, enjoying the chase of boars and swift deer, and the wood-nymphs play with her, the daughters of Zeus who holds the aegis, and Leto is glad in her heart — Artemis holds her head and her brows above them all, and is easy to pick out, though every one of them is lovely — so the unmarried girl shone among her women.

But when she was about to yoke the mules and fold the fine clothes to go home, then the goddess grey-eyed Athena thought of something else, so that Odysseus would wake and see the lovely girl, and she would lead him to the city of the Phaeacians. So the princess threw the ball to one of her maids, and missed the maid and threw it into a deep eddy; and they all cried out at it, and noble Odysseus woke and sat up, and turned it over in his mind and heart.

"What country have I come to now? What kind of people? Are they cruel and wild and unjust, or do they welcome strangers, and do their minds fear the gods? A cry rang in my ears like the cry of girls — nymphs, who haunt the high peaks of the mountains and the springs that feed the rivers and the grassy meadows. Can it be that I am somewhere near men with human speech? I will go and see for myself."

With that noble Odysseus came out from under the bushes, and broke a leafy branch off the thick wood with his strong hand, to hold in front of him and hide his nakedness with. He came out like a mountain-bred lion trusting his own strength, who goes out beaten by rain and wind with his two eyes blazing, and goes in among the cattle or the sheep or after the wild deer, and his belly drives him to try the flocks even in a close-built fold. That is how Odysseus was about to go among the girls with the lovely hair, naked as he was, because need had come on him.

He looked terrible to them, all fouled with brine, and they shrank away in fear, one here, one there, along the jutting sandspits. Only the daughter of Alcinous stood her ground, because Athena put courage in her heart and took the fear out of her limbs. She did not run, but stood and faced him. And Odysseus turned over whether he should take the lovely girl by the knees and make his plea, or whether he should keep his distance and beg her with gentle words, hoping she would show him the city and give him clothes. And as he turned it over it seemed better to keep his distance and beg her with gentle words, in case the girl's heart should be angry with him for taking hold of her knees. So he spoke at once, gently and with cunning in it.

"I beg you, my queen. Are you a goddess, or a mortal? If you are a goddess, one of those who hold the wide sky, then it is Artemis, the daughter of great Zeus, that I would compare you to most closely, in beauty and in height and in build. And if you are one of the mortals who live on the earth, then three times blessed are your father and your honoured mother, and three times blessed your brothers. Their hearts must be warmed with pleasure over and over because of you, when they see a plant as lovely as you going into the dance. But the man who is blessed in his heart above all the others is the one who wins you with his wedding gifts and takes you home. I never yet set eyes on a mortal like you, man or woman. Amazement takes hold of me looking at you.

"Once in Delos I saw such a thing, a young shoot of a palm springing up beside the altar of Apollo.
