I went there too, and a great many people followed me, on the journey that was to bring me so much misery. And when I saw that, I marvelled at it a long while in my heart, because no such tree had ever sprung up out of the earth. In the same way, lady, I marvel at you and am amazed, and I am very much afraid to touch your knees. But hard grief has come on me. Yesterday, on the twentieth day, I got clear of the wine-dark sea; until then the wave and the swift winds carried me from the island of Ogygia, and now fate has thrown me ashore here, so that here too, I suppose, I may suffer something. I do not think my troubles will stop yet; the gods will bring on a good many more before that.

"Have pity, my queen. It is you I have come to first, after all this hard suffering, and I know nobody else of all the people who hold this city and this land. Show me the city, and give me some rag to throw round me, if you had any wrapping for the clothes when you came here. And for yourself, may the gods grant you everything your heart desires — a husband and a home may they grant you, and one mind between you, which is a good gift. There is nothing greater or better than this, when a man and a woman keep house together with one mind: a great grief to their enemies and a joy to their friends. But they know it best themselves."

Then white-armed Nausicaa answered him: "Stranger, since you seem to be neither a bad man nor a fool — and it is Zeus himself, the Olympian, who gives good fortune to men, to the good and the bad alike, to each man as he pleases — he must have given you this lot, and you have to endure it. But now, since you have come to our city and our country, you shall not go short of clothes or of anything else that is right for a hard-pressed suppliant who comes our way. I will show you the city and tell you the name of the people. The Phaeacians hold this city and this land, and I am the daughter of great-hearted Alcinous, on whom the strength and the power of the Phaeacians depend."

She spoke, and called to her women with the lovely hair. "Stand still, my girls. Where are you running to at the sight of a man? You do not think, surely, that he is an enemy? The mortal man is not alive, and does not exist, and will never be born, who could come to the land of the Phaeacians as an enemy, because we are very dear to the immortals. We live far off in the surging sea, the furthest of men, and no other mortals have dealings with us. This is some unlucky wanderer who has come here, and we have to look after him now. All strangers and beggars come from Zeus, and a gift is welcome even when it is small. Come, my girls, give the stranger food and drink, and bathe him in the river where there is shelter from the wind."

So she spoke, and they stopped and called to each other, and set Odysseus in a sheltered place, as Nausicaa, the daughter of great-hearted Alcinous, told them, and put a cloak and a tunic down beside him for clothes, and gave him the soft olive oil in the golden flask, and told him to bathe in the streams of the river. Then noble Odysseus spoke among the girls.

"Girls, stand over there, away from me, so that I can wash the brine off my shoulders myself and rub myself with oil. It is a long time since oil came near my skin. I will not bathe in front of you. I am ashamed to be naked among girls with such lovely hair."

So he said, and they went off and told the princess. Then noble Odysseus washed the brine from his skin with water from the river, the brine that clothed his back and his broad shoulders, and wiped the scurf of the restless sea off his head. And when he had washed his whole body and rubbed himself with oil and put on the clothes the unmarried girl had given him, then Athena, daughter of Zeus, made him taller to look at and stronger, and made the hair flow down from his head in curls like the hyacinth flower. As when a man lays gold over silver, a skilled workman whom Hephaestus and Pallas Athena have taught every kind of craft, and the work he turns out is full of grace, so the goddess poured grace over his head and shoulders. Then he went off and sat down on the shore of the sea, gleaming with beauty and grace.

The girl looked at him and marvelled, and said to her women with the lovely hair: "Listen, white-armed girls, and let me say something. It is not against the will of all the gods who hold Olympus that this man has come among the godlike Phaeacians. Before, he seemed to me a rough-looking creature; now he is like the gods who hold the wide sky. I wish a man like him might be called my husband, living here, and that it might please him to stay. But come, my girls, give the stranger food and drink."

So she spoke, and they listened readily and obeyed, and set food and drink in front of Odysseus. And much-enduring noble Odysseus drank and ate ravenously, because it was a long time since he had tasted food.

Then white-armed Nausicaa thought of something else. She folded the clothes and put them in the fine wagon and yoked the strong-hoofed mules and got up on the car herself. Then she called Odysseus and said to him:

"Get up now, stranger, and come to the city, so that I can send you on to the house of my wise father, where I promise you will get to know all the best of the Phaeacians. Only do it like this — and I do not think you lack understanding. As long as we are going through the country and the ploughed fields of men, walk quickly with the women behind the mules and the wagon, and I will lead the way. But when we are about to go into the city, which has a high wall running round it — there is a fine harbour on either side of the city and the entrance is narrow, and the curved ships are drawn up along the road, because every man has a berth of his own for his ship. Their place of assembly is there too, round the fine temple of Poseidon, made of huge stones set deep in the earth. There the men busy themselves with the tackle of their black ships, with cables and sails, and shape the thin oar-blades. The Phaeacians care nothing for the bow or the quiver, but for masts and ships' oars and the trim ships they take such pleasure in crossing the grey sea in.

"It is their rough talk I want to avoid, in case somebody sneers at me afterwards — there are insolent people in this country — and some low fellow, meeting us, might say: 'Who is this following Nausicaa, this tall good-looking stranger? Where did she find him? He will be a husband for her, no doubt. Perhaps she has picked up some wanderer off his ship, from a people who live far away — there is nobody near us — or perhaps a god has come down from heaven in answer to her prayers, after all this praying, and she will have him for a husband all her days. Better so, if she has gone out and found herself a husband from somewhere else; she certainly looks down on the Phaeacians here in her own country, where she has plenty of noble suitors.' That is what they would say, and it would be thrown up at me. And I would blame another girl myself who did such a thing, and went about with men before the day of her open marriage, in spite of her dear father and mother, while they were still alive.

"So listen to what I say, stranger, and be quick about it, so that you get an escort from my father as soon as possible, and your journey home. You will find a fine grove of Athena close to the road, a grove of poplars. A spring wells up in it, and there is a meadow round about. That is my father's park and his fruitful vineyard, as far from the city as a man's voice carries when he shouts. Sit down there and wait a while, until we get to the city and reach my father's house. And when you think we have reached the house, then go into the city of the Phaeacians and ask for the house of my father, great-hearted Alcinous. It is easy to recognise, and a child could show you the way, a mere baby; the Phaeacians' houses are not built at all like the palace of the lord Alcinous.

"But when the house and the courtyard have taken you in, go quickly through the great hall until you come to my mother. She sits at the hearth in the firelight, spinning the purple yarn, a wonder to look at, leaning against a pillar, with her women sitting behind her. My father's throne is set against the same pillar, and he sits on it drinking his wine like an immortal. Go past him and put your arms round my mother's knees, so that you may see the day of your homecoming quickly and with joy, however far away you have come from. If she thinks well of you, then there is hope that you will see your friends and get back to your well-built house and your own country."

With that she struck the mules with the shining whip, and they quickly left the streams of the river behind. They trotted well and worked their ambling feet well, and she drove carefully, so that the girls and Odysseus could follow on foot, and used the lash with judgment.

Then the sun set, and they came to the famous grove sacred to Athena. Odysseus sat down there and prayed at once to the daughter of great Zeus.

"Hear me, child of Zeus who holds the aegis, unwearied one. Listen to me now, since you did not listen before, when I was being battered, when the glorious earth-shaker was battering me. Grant that I come to the Phaeacians as a man to be welcomed and pitied."

So he spoke in prayer, and Pallas Athena heard him. But she did not yet appear to him face to face, because she was wary of her father's brother; and he went on raging furiously against godlike Odysseus, until at last he reached his own land.

Book Seven

So he prayed there, much-enduring noble Odysseus, while the two strong mules carried the girl to the city. And when she came to her father's splendid palace she stopped the mules at the outer gate, and her brothers came crowding round her, men like the immortals, and unyoked the mules from the wagon and carried the clothes inside; and she went to her room. There a fire was lit for her by her attendant, Eurymedusa, an old woman from Apeire. The curved ships had brought her from Apeire long ago, and the men had picked her out of the plunder as a gift of honour for Alcinous, because he was king over all the Phaeacians and the people listened to him as to a god. It was she who had brought up white-armed Nausicaa in the palace, and she who lit the fire for her and got her supper ready in her room.

Then Odysseus roused himself to go to the city, and Athena, meaning kindly, poured a thick mist round him, so that none of the great-hearted Phaeacians should meet him and speak mockingly to him and ask him who he was. But when he was about to go into the lovely city, the goddess grey-eyed Athena met him, in the likeness of a young girl carrying a jug, and stood in front of him; and noble Odysseus asked her:

"My child, could you show me the way to the house of the man they call Alcinous, who is lord among the people here? I have come as a stranger who has suffered a great deal, from far away, from a distant country, and so I know nobody among the people who hold this city and this land."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Then I will show you the palace, sir, since you ask me. It stands close to the house of my own noble father. Only go quietly, and I will lead the way. Do not look at any man, and do not question anybody. The men here have no patience with strangers, and give no kindly welcome to anyone who comes from another country. They trust in their fast ships and cross the great gulf of the sea, because the earth-shaker granted them that; and their ships are as swift as a bird on the wing, or as a thought."

So speaking, Pallas Athena led the way quickly, and he followed in the goddess's footsteps. And as he went through the city in the middle of them, the Phaeacians, who are famous for their ships, took no notice of him, because fair-haired Athena, the dread goddess, would not allow it, and poured a marvellous mist round him out of the kindness of her heart. And Odysseus marvelled at the harbours and the stately ships, and at the meeting-places where the heroes themselves gathered, and at the walls, long and high and crowned with palisades, a wonder to look at.

But when they had come to the king's splendid palace, the goddess grey-eyed Athena spoke first.

"Here, sir, is the house you told me to show you, and you will find the kings, brought up by Zeus, feasting at the banquet. Go in, and let your heart be afraid of nothing. A bold man does better in everything, even a stranger from another country. It is the queen you must go to first in the palace. Arete is her name, and she comes from the same line as king Alcinous does. Nausithous was first born to the earth-shaker Poseidon and to Periboea, the loveliest of women, the youngest daughter of great-hearted Eurymedon, who was once king over the insolent Giants. But he brought ruin on his stubborn people, and was ruined himself. Poseidon lay with Periboea and fathered a son, great-hearted Nausithous, who ruled over the Phaeacians; and Nausithous fathered Rhexenor and Alcinous. Rhexenor, when he had no son yet, was struck down in his hall by Apollo of the silver bow, a bridegroom as he was, and left only one daughter, Arete. Alcinous made her his wife and honours her as no other woman on earth is honoured of all those who keep house nowadays under their husbands. That is how heartily she is honoured, and always has been, by her children and by Alcinous himself and by the people, who look on her as on a goddess and greet her as she goes through the city. She has no lack of good understanding herself, and for the women she wishes well to she puts an end to quarrels even among their husbands. If she thinks well of you, then there is hope that you will see your friends and get back to your high-roofed house and your own country."

With that grey-eyed Athena went away over the restless sea and left lovely Scheria. She came to Marathon and broad-wayed Athens, and went into the well-built house of Erechtheus; and Odysseus went to the splendid palace of Alcinous. He stood there and his heart turned over a great deal before he reached the bronze threshold; because there was a gleam as of sun or moon over the high-roofed house of great-hearted Alcinous.

The walls were of bronze, running this way and that from the threshold to the innermost room, with a cornice of blue enamel round them. Golden doors closed the well-built house in, and silver doorposts stood in a bronze threshold, with a silver lintel above and a golden handle. On either side of the door stood gold and silver dogs, which Hephaestus had made with cunning skill to guard the palace of great-hearted Alcinous; and they were immortal and ageless all their days. Inside, seats were fixed along the wall on either hand from the threshold to the innermost room, and robes of soft cloth were thrown over them, finely woven, the work of women. On these the leaders of the Phaeacians used to sit drinking and eating, because they had unfailing supplies. And golden youths stood on well-built pedestals holding lighted torches in their hands, to give light by night to the banqueters in the hall.

He had fifty slave women in the house. Some of them grind the yellow grain on the millstone, and others weave cloth or sit twirling the yarn, like the leaves of a tall poplar tree; and the soft olive oil drips off the closely woven linen. For as the Phaeacian men are more skilled than anyone else at driving a fast ship over the sea, so the women are cunning workers at the loom, since Athena has given them, above all others, skill in fine work and an understanding mind.

Outside the courtyard, close to the door, is a great orchard of four acres, with a hedge running round it on either side. Tall thriving trees grow there, pears and pomegranates and apple trees with their bright fruit, and sweet figs and thriving olives. Their fruit never fails or gives out, winter or summer, but lasts the whole year through; and the west wind, blowing steadily, is always bringing some to life and ripening others: pear ripens on pear, apple on apple, cluster on cluster and fig on fig. And his fruitful vineyard is planted there, one part of it a warm patch on level ground being dried in the sun, while other grapes are being gathered and others trodden; and in front are unripe grapes just shedding the blossom, and others turning purple. And there, along the last row of vines, grow trim garden beds of every kind, in bloom the year round. There are two springs in it, one of which sends its water all through the garden, while the other, opposite it, runs under the threshold of the court towards the high house, and the townspeople draw their water from that one. Such were the splendid gifts of the gods in the palace of Alcinous.

Much-enduring noble Odysseus stood there and gazed. And when he had marvelled at it all in his heart, he went quickly over the threshold into the house. There he found the leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians pouring offerings from their cups to the sharp-eyed slayer of Argus, to whom they always poured the wine last of all, when they were ready to go to bed. And much-enduring noble Odysseus went through the hall, wrapped in the thick mist Athena had poured round him, until he came to Arete and to Alcinous the king. He threw his arms round Arete's knees, and at that moment the marvellous mist melted off him. A hush fell on everyone in the room at the sight of the man, and they stared at him in astonishment. And Odysseus made his plea.

"Arete, daughter of godlike Rhexenor — after all my hard labours I have come to your husband and to your knees, and to these banqueters too, to whom may the gods grant happiness in life, and may each of them hand down to his children the wealth in his halls and the honours the people have given him. But send me on my way, so that I may come to my own country, and quickly. I have been suffering a long time, far from my friends."

With that he sat down on the hearth in the ashes by the fire, and they were all hushed and silent. At last the old lord Echeneus spoke among them, who was an elder among the Phaeacians, skilled in speech and knowing all the wisdom of the old days. He meant it kindly, and spoke to the gathering.

"Alcinous, this is not the better way and it is not seemly, that a stranger should sit on the ground on the hearth in the ashes, while the rest of us hold back waiting for your word. Come, get the stranger up and set him on a silver-studded chair, and tell the heralds to mix wine, so that we can pour an offering to Zeus who hurls the thunderbolt, since he always attends on suppliants who deserve respect. And let the housekeeper give the stranger supper out of the store in the house."

When the strong and mighty Alcinous heard this, he took Odysseus by the hand — Odysseus, the wise and cunning-minded — and raised him from the hearth and set him on a bright chair, telling his son, the good Laodamas, to get up from it; for he sat next to him and was his best loved. A maid brought water for his hands in a lovely golden jug and poured it over a silver basin for him to wash, and drew a polished table up beside him. The housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it out, and plenty of good things with it, giving freely from her stores. So much-enduring noble Odysseus drank and ate.

Then mighty Alcinous spoke to the herald. "Pontonous, mix the bowl and serve wine to everyone in the hall, so that we can pour an offering to Zeus who hurls the thunderbolt, since he always attends on suppliants who deserve respect."

He spoke, and Pontonous mixed the honey-hearted wine and served it out to everyone, pouring the first drops into the cups for an offering. And when they had poured the offering and drunk as much as they wanted, Alcinous spoke to the gathering.

"Listen to me, leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians, and let me say what the heart in my breast tells me. Now that you have finished your feast, go home, each of you, and rest. In the morning we will call more of the elders together and entertain the stranger in our halls and make fine offerings to the gods. After that we will see about his sending, so that this stranger may come to his own country under our escort, without labour or pain, quickly and rejoicing, however far away he has come from. And he shall suffer no harm or trouble on the way, until he sets foot on his own land; after that he must suffer whatever Fate and the dread Spinners spun into his thread at his birth, when his mother bore him.

"But if he is one of the immortals come down from heaven, then this is some new thing the gods are planning. Up to now they have always appeared to us plainly, when we sacrifice them splendid hecatombs, and they feast among us sitting where we sit. And if one of us meets them travelling alone, they use no disguise, because we are close kin to them, as the Cyclopes are and the wild tribes of the Giants."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Alcinous, put that thought away. I am not like the immortals who hold the wide sky, either in height or in build, but like mortal men. Whoever you know among men who carries the heaviest load of trouble, those are the men I would compare myself to in my sorrows. Yes, and I could tell a longer tale still of all the evils I have suffered by the will of the gods.

"But let me eat now, in spite of my grief. There is nothing more shameless than a hateful belly, which insists that a man take notice of it however badly off he is and however weighed down with grief he is — as I am weighed down with grief, and yet my belly keeps ordering me to eat and drink, and makes me forget everything I have suffered, and commands me to eat my fill. But be quick at daybreak, so that you set me, unlucky as I am, on the soil of my own country, after all my troubles. And let life leave me, once I have seen my possessions again, and my slaves, and my great high-roofed house."

So he spoke, and they all praised his words and said the stranger should be sent on his way, since he had spoken properly. And when they had poured the offering and drunk as much as they wanted, they went off, each man to his own house, to rest.

Noble Odysseus was left behind in the hall, and Arete and godlike Alcinous sat beside him, while the women cleared away the dishes from the feast. Then white-armed Arete spoke first; for she had recognised his fine clothes when she saw them, the cloak and the tunic, which she had made herself with her women. And she spoke to him with winged words.

"Stranger, this is the question I will ask you myself, first. Who are you among men, and where from? Who gave you these clothes? Did you not say you came here wandering over the sea?"

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "It would be hard, my queen, to tell the whole tale of my troubles to the end, because the heavenly gods have given me a great many. But I will tell you this, which you ask me about. There is an island, Ogygia, lying far off in the sea. The daughter of Atlas lives there, Calypso of the lovely hair, full of tricks, a dread goddess, and nobody has anything to do with her, god or mortal. But fate brought me to her hearth alone, wretch that I am, because Zeus had struck my fast ship with his bright thunderbolt and smashed it in the middle of the wine-dark sea. There all the rest of my trusty companions died, and I got my arms round the keel of my curved ship and was carried adrift nine days, and on the tenth black night the gods brought me to the island of Ogygia, where Calypso of the lovely hair lives, a dread goddess.

"She took me into her house with a kind welcome, and gave me food, and said she would make me immortal and ageless all my days; but she could never persuade the heart in my breast. I stayed there without a break for seven years, and all that time I wetted with my tears the immortal clothes Calypso gave me. But when the eighth year came round, then she roused me and told me to go — either because of some message from Zeus, or because her own mind had changed. And she sent me off on a raft bound firm, and gave me plenty of bread and sweet wine, and dressed me in immortal clothes, and sent out a gentle warm wind.

"So for seventeen days I sailed over the sea, and on the eighteenth the shadowy mountains of your country appeared, and my heart was glad — ill-starred as I am, since I still had a great deal of misery to keep company with, which Poseidon the earth-shaker sent on me. He stirred the winds up against me and blocked my way, and roused the sea to an extraordinary pitch, and the wave would not let me stay on my raft while I groaned without stopping. The storm smashed the raft, but I swam and cut my way through that gulf of the sea, until the wind and the waves carried me and brought me to your shores. There, if I had tried to land, the waves would have flung me on the shore and dashed me against the great crags in a cheerless place; so I gave way and swam back until I came to a river, where the place looked best to me, since it was smooth of rocks and there was shelter from the wind besides. I struggled out and sank down, gasping for breath, and immortal night came on. Then I went away from the heaven-fed river and lay down to sleep in the bushes, gathering leaves round me; and a god poured endless sleep over me.

"So I slept there among the leaves, with my heart badly stricken, the whole night through and until morning and past midday, and the sun was turning towards his setting before sweet sleep let me go. Then I saw your daughter's women playing on the shore, and she was among them, lovely as the goddesses. I made my plea to her; and she showed no lack of good understanding, and behaved as you would not expect a younger person to behave on meeting you, since young people are always thoughtless. She gave me bread in plenty and bright wine, and bathed me in the river, and gave me these clothes. That is the truth, for all my sorrows."

Then Alcinous answered him and said: "Stranger, my daughter did not judge this rightly, in not bringing you to our house with her women. It was to her that you made your plea first."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Prince, do not blame your blameless daughter for that. She did tell me to follow with her women, but I would not, out of fear and shame, in case your heart should darken with anger when you saw it. We are quick to anger, we tribes of men on the earth."

And Alcinous answered him again: "Stranger, the heart in my breast is not the kind to fill with anger over nothing. Due measure is better in everything. Father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo — I wish that a man as fine as you, and of one mind with me, would take my daughter for a wife and be called my son and stay here. I would give you a house and possessions, if you chose to remain; but no Phaeacian will keep you here against your will. Let that never be the will of father Zeus.

"As for your sending, so that you may be sure of it, I fix the time for it: tomorrow. Then you shall lie down, overcome with sleep, and they will row you over the calm sea until you come to your country and your house, or wherever you like — even far beyond Euboea, which those of our people who saw it say is the furthest of lands, when they carried fair-haired Rhadamanthus to visit Tityus, the son of Gaea. They went there and finished the journey without labour, and came back home on the same day. So you shall find out for yourself how far my ships are the best, and my young men at tossing the salt water with the oar-blade."

So he spoke, and much-enduring noble Odysseus was glad, and spoke in prayer and said: "Father Zeus, grant that Alcinous brings about everything he has said. Then his fame will never be put out over the grain-giving earth, and I shall reach my own country."

So they spoke to one another; and white-armed Arete told her women to set a bedstead under cover of the portico and to lay fine purple blankets on it and spread covers over the top, and put fleecy cloaks on those to wear. So they went out of the hall with torches in their hands, and when they had busily made up the strong-built bed they came to Odysseus and called him and said: "Get up now, stranger, and come to bed. Your bed is made."

So they spoke, and it seemed welcome to him to lie down and sleep. So he slept there, much-enduring noble Odysseus, on the corded bedstead under the echoing portico; and Alcinous lay down in the inner room of the high house, with the lady his wife beside him, who had made up the bed.

Book Eight

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, the strong and mighty Alcinous got up from his bed, and Odysseus got up too, born of Zeus, the sacker of cities. And the strong and mighty Alcinous led the way to the meeting place of the Phaeacians, which was built for them close by their ships. They came there and sat down on the polished stones beside one another; and Pallas Athena went through the city in the likeness of the herald of wise Alcinous, planning a homecoming for great-hearted Odysseus. She came up to each man and said:

"Come now, leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians, come to the meeting place, and hear about the stranger who has just come to the palace of wise Alcinous after his wanderings over the sea, and who is like the immortals in build."

With that she stirred up the spirit and heart of every man, and the meeting place and the seats were quickly filled with the men who gathered. A great many marvelled at the sight of the wise son of Laertes, because Athena poured a marvellous grace over his head and shoulders, and made him taller and sturdier to look at, so that he would be welcomed by all the Phaeacians and win awe and respect, and might carry off the many feats the Phaeacians would test him with.

And when they had come together, Alcinous spoke to the gathering.

"Listen to me, leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians, and let me say what the heart in my breast tells me. This stranger — I do not know who he is — has come to my house in his wanderings, whether from the men of the east or of the west. He is pressing to be sent on his way, and asks for a promise of it. Let us speed his sending, as we have always done. No man at all who comes to my house sits here long in distress for want of a sending. Come, let us draw a black ship down to the bright sea for her first voyage, and let the people pick fifty-two young men, the best there have been up to now. And when you have all lashed the oars properly to the thole-pins, go ashore and come to my house, and get a feast ready quickly; I will provide generously for everyone. That is my order to the young men. But the rest of you, the sceptred kings, come to my fine palace so that we can entertain the stranger in the halls; and let nobody refuse me. And call the divine singer here, Demodocus, since the god has granted him skill in song beyond anyone else, to give delight in whatever way his spirit prompts him to sing."

With that he led the way, and the sceptred kings followed him, while a herald went for the divine singer. And fifty-two picked young men went, as he ordered, to the shore of the restless sea. And when they had come down to the ship and the sea, they drew the black ship down to deep water and set the mast and sail in the black ship and fitted the oars in the leather thole-straps, all in their order, and spread the white sail. They moored the ship well out in the roadstead, and then went off to the great palace of wise Alcinous.

The porticoes and the courts and the rooms were full of the men who gathered, a great many of them, young and old. Alcinous slaughtered twelve sheep for them, and eight white-tusked boars, and two slow-moving oxen. They skinned them and dressed them and got a splendid feast ready.

Then the herald came up, leading the good singer, whom the Muse loved above all other men, and gave both good and evil: she took away his sight and gave him the gift of sweet song. Pontonous the herald set a silver-studded chair for him in the middle of the banqueters, leaning it against a tall pillar, and hung the clear-toned lyre from a peg just above his head and showed him how to reach it with his hands. And beside him he put a basket and a beautiful table and a cup of wine, to drink when his heart told him to. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them.

But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, the Muse moved the singer to sing of the famous deeds of fighting men, from a song whose fame had reached the wide sky in those days: the quarrel of Odysseus and Achilles, son of Peleus, how once they fell out with furious words at a rich feast of the gods, and Agamemnon, king of men, was glad in his heart that the best of the Achaeans were quarrelling — because Phoebus Apollo had told him in his answer that it would be so, in sacred Pytho, when he crossed the threshold of stone to consult the oracle. For then the beginning of the misery was rolling down on Trojans and Danaans alike, through the will of great Zeus.

That was the song the famous singer sang. But Odysseus took hold of his great purple cloak with his strong hands and drew it down over his head and hid his handsome face, because he was ashamed to let the Phaeacians see the tears falling from under his eyebrows. And whenever the divine singer stopped singing, Odysseus would wipe away his tears and pull the cloak off his head and take the two-handled cup and pour an offering to the gods. But every time he began again, and the Phaeacian nobles told him to sing because they enjoyed his song, Odysseus would cover his head again and groan.

He hid his weeping from all the rest of them, but Alcinous alone noticed him and took it in, because he sat beside him and heard him groaning heavily. And at once he spoke among the Phaeacians, who love the oar.

"Listen to me, leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians. We have satisfied our hearts already with the shared banquet and with the lyre, which is the companion of a rich feast. Now let us go out and try our hands at every kind of game, so that the stranger can tell his friends, when he gets home, how far we are ahead of other men at boxing and wrestling and jumping and running."

With that he led the way and they followed him. The herald hung the clear-toned lyre on its peg and took Demodocus by the hand and led him out of the hall, guiding him by the same road the others had gone, the Phaeacian nobles, to watch the games. They went off to the meeting place, and a huge crowd went with them, past counting; and a good many noble young men stood up. Acroneus stood up, and Ocyalus, and Elatreus, and Nauteus, and Prymneus, and Anchialus, and Eretmeus, and Ponteus, and Proreus, Thoon and Anabesineus, and Amphialus, son of Polyneus, son of Tecton; and Euryalus stood up too, the equal of man-destroying Ares, the son of Naubolus, who was the best-looking and best-built of all the Phaeacians after faultless Laodamas. And the three sons of noble Alcinous stood up, Laodamas and Halius and godlike Clytoneus.

These made trial first in the foot race. A course was marked out for them from the turning point, and they all ran fast, raising the dust of the plain; but noble Clytoneus was far the best of them at running, and by as much as the range of a team of mules in fallow land he shot ahead and reached the crowd, and left the others behind. Then they tried the hard work of wrestling, and there Euryalus was better than all the princes. At jumping Amphialus was best of all, and with the discus Elatreus was far the best again, and at boxing Laodamas, the good son of Alcinous.

But when they had all taken their pleasure in the contests, Laodamas, son of Alcinous, spoke among them. "Come, friends, let us ask the stranger whether he knows any contests, or has learned any. He is certainly no small man in build — look at his thighs and his calves, and his two arms above, and his strong neck, and how powerful he is. He lacks nothing of the strength of youth; he has only been broken by a great deal of trouble. To my mind there is nothing worse than the sea for wrecking a man, however strong he is."

And Euryalus answered him: "Laodamas, that is very properly said. Go and challenge him yourself, and make your point."

When the good son of Alcinous heard this, he went and took his stand in the middle and said to Odysseus: "Come, sir, you try the contests too, if you know any. And you must know some, since there is no greater glory for a man as long as he lives than what he wins with his own hands and feet. Come and try, and throw the care out of your heart. Your journey will not be put off much longer; your ship is launched already and the crew is ready."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him: "Laodamas, why do you mock me with this challenge? There is far more sorrow in my mind than there are contests. I have suffered a great deal and worked hard in the past, and now I sit in the middle of your assembly longing for my homecoming, making my plea to the king and to all the people."

Then Euryalus answered him and taunted him to his face: "No, stranger, I would not compare you to a man skilled in the contests, of which there are plenty among men. I would compare you to a man who goes to and fro with his benched ship as the captain of sailors who are traders, a man with his mind on his freight, keeping an eye on his cargo and the profits of his greed. You do not look like an athlete."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave him a black look from under his brows and answered him: "Stranger, that was not well said. You are like a man blinded by his own foolishness. It is true, then, that the gods do not give gracious gifts to everyone alike, not looks or mind or eloquence. One man is poor in appearance, but the god sets a crown of beauty on his words, and men look at him with delight, and he speaks on steadily with sweet modesty and stands out among the gathered people, and as he goes through the city they gaze at him as at a god. Another is like the immortals to look at, and there is no crown of grace set about his words. So with you: your looks are outstanding, and a god himself could not improve them, but in mind you are stunted.

"You have stirred up the spirit in my breast by speaking so rudely. I am not unskilled in sports, as you chatter. I think I was among the first of them, as long as I could trust my youth and my hands. But now I am held down by suffering and pain; I have endured a great deal, passing through the wars of men and the grievous waves. Even so, and though I have suffered so much, I will try the contests. Your words stung me to the heart, and you provoked me with what you said."

He spoke, and jumped up with his cloak still round him as it was, and seized a discus larger than the rest and thick, a good deal heavier than the ones the Phaeacians used against one another. He whirled it and sent it from his strong hand, and the stone hummed as it flew; and down they crouched to the ground, the long-oared Phaeacians, men famous for their ships, under the rush of the stone. It flew past the marks of all of them, running lightly from his hand, and Athena, in the likeness of a man, set the mark, and spoke to him.

"Even a blind man could pick this mark out, stranger, groping for it with his hands, because it is not muddled up in the crowd of the others but far the first. Take heart about this bout at any rate: no Phaeacian will reach this or throw past it."

So she spoke, and much-enduring noble Odysseus was glad, delighted to see a true friend among the contestants. Then he spoke among the Phaeacians with a lighter heart.

"Reach that now, young men. And soon, I think, I shall send another after it, as far or even further. And of the rest of you, if any man's heart and spirit tell him to, let him come here and try me — since you have made me thoroughly angry — at boxing or at wrestling or even at running, I do not mind. Let any of the Phaeacians come, except Laodamas alone. He is my host, and who would quarrel with the man who is entertaining him? That man is a fool and worth nothing who challenges his host to a contest in a strange country; he only spoils his own chances. Of all the rest I refuse nobody and think little of nobody, but I am willing to know them and try them man to man. I am no weakling at anything, at any of the contests men practise. I know well enough how to handle a polished bow, and I would always be the first to shoot and hit my man in a crowd of the enemy, even with a great many companions standing by me shooting at them. Philoctetes alone beat me with the bow in the land of the Trojans, when we Achaeans shot. Of all the others I say I am far the best, of all the mortals now alive on the earth who eat bread. But I will not try to compete with the men of the old days, with Heracles or Eurytus of Oechalia, who competed even with the immortals in archery. That is why great Eurytus died soon, and old age never came on him in his halls, because Apollo grew angry and killed him for challenging him to a contest with the bow. And with the spear I throw further than any other man can shoot with an arrow. It is only in the foot race that I am afraid some Phaeacian may outrun me. I have been cruelly broken among all those waves, since there was no lasting store of provisions in my ship, and my limbs are loosened."

So he spoke, and they were all hushed and silent; and only Alcinous answered him and said: "Stranger, since you speak among us in no ungracious way, but mean to show the ability that goes with you, in anger that this man came up to you among the contestants and taunted you in a way that no mortal would make light of your ability who knew in his heart how to speak properly — come, listen to me now, so that you can tell another hero, when you are feasting in your halls with your wife and children and remembering our skill, what feats Zeus has granted us from our fathers' days until now. We are not faultless boxers or wrestlers, but we run swiftly in the foot race, and we are the best seamen; and the banquet is always dear to us, and the lyre, and the dance, and changes of clothes, and warm baths, and bed.

"But come now, all you who are the best dancers of the Phaeacians, make sport, so that the stranger can tell his friends when he gets home how far we are ahead of others in seamanship and in speed of foot, and in the dance and in song. And let somebody go straight away and fetch the clear-toned lyre for Demodocus, which is lying somewhere in our halls."

So spoke godlike Alcinous, and the herald got up to fetch the hollow lyre from the king's palace. Then the masters of the lists stood up, nine of them, men chosen from the people, who used to put everything in order at their gatherings. They levelled a place for the dance and marked out a fine wide ring, and the herald came up carrying the clear-toned lyre for Demodocus. He moved into the middle, and boys stood round him in the first bloom of youth, skilled in the dance, and they beat the good dancing floor with their feet; and Odysseus watched the twinkling of their feet and marvelled in his spirit.

But the singer struck the chords and began his sweet song, and sang of the love of Ares and Aphrodite of the fair crown: how they first lay together in the house of Hephaestus, secretly, and Ares gave her many gifts, and shamed the bed of the lord Hephaestus. And someone came to him at once with the news, Helios, who had noticed them lying together in love. And when Hephaestus heard the bitter tale, he went off to his smithy turning evil over in the depths of his heart, and set the great anvil on the anvil block and forged bonds that could not be broken or loosed, so that the lovers should stay fast where they were.

And when he had made the snare in his anger against Ares, he went to his room, where his bed was, and spread the bonds all round the bedposts, and hung many of them from above, from the roof-beams, fine as spiders' webs, so that nobody could see them, not even one of the blessed gods; they were made so extraordinarily cunningly. And when he had spread the whole snare round the bed, he pretended he was going to Lemnos, that well-built city, which is far the dearest of all lands in his eyes.

And Ares of the golden rein was keeping no blind watch, and when he saw Hephaestus, famous for his craft, going off, he went to the house of famous Hephaestus, eager for the love of Cytherea of the fair crown. She had only just come from the presence of her father, the mighty son of Cronos, and had sat down. And Ares came into the house and took her hand and spoke to her.

"Come, my love, let us go to bed and take our pleasure lying together. Hephaestus is not in the country any longer; I imagine he has gone off to Lemnos by now, to visit the Sintians with their savage speech."

So he spoke, and it seemed a welcome thing to her to lie with him. So the two of them went to the bed and lay down to sleep, and the cunning bonds of clever Hephaestus closed round them, and they could not stir a limb or lift one. Then at last they understood there was no getting away.

And the famous god of the two strong arms came up close to them, having turned back before he reached the land of Lemnos, because Helios had kept watch for him and brought him word. He went to his house with a heavy heart and stood at the gateway, and fierce anger took hold of him, and he cried out terribly and called to all the gods:

"Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live for ever, come here and see something laughable and monstrous — how Aphrodite, daughter of Zeus, despises me for being lame, and loves destructive Ares because he is handsome and sound of limb, while I was born misshapen. And there is nobody to blame for that but my own two parents — I wish they had never had me. But look where the two of them have gone up into my bed and are sleeping together in love; and I am sick at the sight. Still, I do not think they will want to lie like that any longer, not for a moment, however much in love they are. Soon they will both lose their appetite for sleeping. But the snare and the bonds will hold them until her father pays me back every one of the wedding gifts I gave him for the sake of his shameless girl. His daughter is beautiful, but she cannot govern her passion."

So he spoke, and the gods gathered at the house of the bronze floor. Poseidon came, who holds the earth, and the helper Hermes came, and the lord Apollo the archer. The goddesses stayed at home, each of them, out of modesty; but the gods, the givers of good things, stood in the gateway; and unquenchable laughter rose up among the blessed gods when they saw the craft of clever Hephaestus. And one of them would say, with a glance at his neighbour:

"Bad deeds do not prosper. The slow one catches the swift — look how Hephaestus, slow as he is, has caught Ares, though Ares is the fastest of all the gods who hold Olympus. Lame as he is, he has caught him by craft. So Ares owes the adulterer's fine."

That is how they talked to one another. But the lord Apollo, son of Zeus, said to Hermes: "Hermes, son of Zeus, messenger, giver of good things — would you really be willing to lie on a bed beside golden Aphrodite, even tangled in strong bonds?"

Then the messenger, the slayer of Argus, answered him: "I only wish it might happen, lord Apollo, you archer god — that three times as many bonds might hold me fast past untying, and you gods, and all the goddesses too, might be looking on, so long as I slept beside golden Aphrodite."

So he spoke, and laughter rose up among the immortal gods. Only Poseidon did not laugh, but kept begging Hephaestus, the famous craftsman, to set Ares free; and he spoke to him with winged words: "Loose him, and I promise, as you tell me, that he will pay you everything that is right himself, in the presence of the immortal gods."

Then the famous god of the two strong arms answered him: "Do not ask this of me, Poseidon, you who hold the earth. Standing surety for a worthless man is a poor sort of security. How could I put you in bonds among the immortal gods, if Ares got out of both the debt and the bonds and went off?"

Then Poseidon the earth-shaker answered him again: "Hephaestus, even if Ares does get out of the debt and run away, I will pay you this myself."

Then the famous god of the two strong arms answered him: "It is not possible for me to refuse you, and it would not be right."

With that mighty Hephaestus loosed the bonds, and the two of them, when they were free of that bond so strong, sprang up at once. Ares went off to Thrace, and she, laughter-loving Aphrodite, went to Cyprus, to Paphos, where her precinct is and her fragrant altar. There the Graces bathed her and rubbed her with immortal oil, the kind that gleams on the gods who live for ever, and dressed her in lovely clothes, a wonder to look at.

That was the song the famous singer sang. And Odysseus was glad in his heart as he listened, and so were the long-oared Phaeacians, men famous for their ships.

Then Alcinous told Halius and Laodamas to dance alone, since nobody could compete with them. And when they had taken in their hands the beautiful purple ball, which clever Polybus had made for them, one of them would lean back and toss it towards the shadowy clouds, and the other would leap up off the ground and catch it skilfully before his feet touched down again. And when they had tried their skill at throwing the ball straight up, the two of them fell to dancing on the bountiful earth, tossing the ball to and fro all the while, and the other young men stood in the ring and beat time, and a great din rose up from it.

Then noble Odysseus said to Alcinous: "Lord Alcinous, renowned above all men, you boasted that your dancers were the best, and your words are made good. Amazement takes hold of me looking at them."

So he spoke, and the strong and mighty Alcinous was glad, and at once he spoke among the Phaeacians, who love the oar: "Listen to me, leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians. This stranger seems to me a man of understanding. Come, then, let us give him a friendship gift, as is fitting. Twelve glorious kings hold power in our country as rulers, and I am the thirteenth myself. Let each of the twelve of you bring a newly washed cloak and a tunic and a talent of precious gold, and let us bring it all together quickly, so that the stranger may go to his supper with our gifts in his hands and a glad heart. And let Euryalus make amends to the stranger himself, with words and with a gift, since what he said was not at all seemly."

So he spoke, and they all praised his words and said it should be so, and every man sent a herald to fetch the gifts. And Euryalus answered and said: "Lord Alcinous, renowned above all men, I will indeed make amends to the stranger, as you tell me. I will give him this sword, all of bronze, with a silver hilt on it and a scabbard of newly sawn ivory worked round it; and it will be a thing of great worth to him."

With that he put the silver-studded sword into his hands, and spoke to him with winged words: "Greetings, sir stranger. If any word has been spoken that was harsh, may the storm winds snatch it up at once and carry it away. And for yourself, may the gods grant you to see your wife and to come to your own country; you have been suffering a long time, far from your friends."

And Odysseus of the many tricks answered him: "Greetings to you too, my friend, and may the gods grant you happiness; and may you never afterwards miss this sword you have given me, making amends with gentle words."

He spoke, and hung the silver-studded sword over his shoulders. And the sun set, and the splendid gifts were brought to him. The lordly heralds carried them to the palace of Alcinous, and the sons of faultless Alcinous took the beautiful gifts and set them down before their honoured mother. And the strong and mighty Alcinous led the way, and they came in and sat down on the high seats.

Then mighty Alcinous said to Arete: "Bring a fine chest here, wife, the best you have, and put a newly washed cloak and tunic in it yourself. And heat a cauldron on the fire for the stranger and warm some water, so that when he has bathed and seen all the gifts the noble Phaeacians have brought here safely stowed, he can take pleasure in the feast and in hearing the strains of the song. And I will give him this beautiful cup of mine, made of gold, so that he may remember me all his days as he pours offerings in his halls to Zeus and to the other gods."

So he spoke, and Arete told her women to set a great cauldron on the fire as fast as they could. They set the cauldron for the bath water on the blazing fire and poured water in and took billets of wood and kindled them underneath. The fire played round the belly of the cauldron and the water grew warm; and meanwhile Arete brought a beautiful chest out of the treasure chamber for the stranger and put the fine gifts in it, the clothes and the gold the Phaeacians had given. And she put a cloak and a fine tunic in it herself, and spoke to Odysseus with winged words.

"See to the lid yourself now, and throw a cord round it quickly, in case somebody robs you of your goods on the way, when you are lying in sweet sleep later on as you travel in the black ship."

When much-enduring noble Odysseus heard this he fitted the lid on at once and quickly threw a cord round it, in a cunning knot which queenly Circe had once taught him. Then the housekeeper told him to go to the bath and wash; and his heart was glad when he saw the warm bath, because he had not been used to such attention since he left the house of fair-haired Calypso, though up to then he had been attended constantly, like a god.

And when the women had bathed him and rubbed him with oil and put a fine cloak and a tunic on him, he came out of the bath and went to join the men at their wine. And Nausicaa, given her beauty by the gods, stood by the doorpost of the well-built hall, and marvelled at Odysseus as her eyes took him in, and spoke to him with winged words.

"Farewell, stranger. And afterwards, even in your own country, remember me, because it is to me first that you owe your life."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her: "Nausicaa, daughter of great-hearted Alcinous, may Zeus grant it so, the loud-thundering lord of Hera, that I reach my home and see the day of my return. Then I will pray to you there as to a god, all my days; because you gave me my life, girl."

He spoke, and sat down on a chair beside king Alcinous. And now they were serving out the portions and mixing the wine. Then the herald came up leading the good singer, Demodocus, honoured by the people, and seated him in the middle of the banqueters, leaning his chair against a high pillar.

Then Odysseus of the many tricks said to the herald, cutting off a portion of the chine of a white-tusked boar, with more still left on it and rich fat on either side: "Herald, take this portion and give it to Demodocus to eat, and let me greet him, in spite of my grief. Among all the men on earth singers win honour and respect, because the Muse has taught them the paths of song and loves the tribe of singers."

So he spoke, and the herald carried the portion and put it in the hands of the lord Demodocus, and he took it and was glad in his heart. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them. And when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, Odysseus of the many tricks said to Demodocus:

"Demodocus, I praise you above all mortal men, whether it was the Muse who taught you, the daughter of Zeus, or Apollo. You sing the fate of the Achaeans truly and well, everything they did and suffered and all the labour they endured, as though you had been there yourself or heard it from someone who was. But come now, change your theme and sing of the building of the wooden horse, which Epeius made with Athena's help, the horse Odysseus once led up into the citadel as a thing of guile, when he had filled it with the men who sacked Ilios. If you tell me this tale properly, I will declare to all mankind that the god has granted you the gift of divine song with a ready heart."

So he spoke, and the singer, moved by the god, began and let his song be heard, taking up the tale where the Argives had gone aboard their benched ships and were sailing away, after throwing fire into their huts, while the others, led by glorious Odysseus, were sitting by then in the meeting place of the Trojans, hidden in the horse; because the Trojans had dragged it up to the citadel themselves. So it stood there, while the people talked on and on sitting round it, and could reach no decision. Three courses found favour with them: to split the hollow timber with the pitiless bronze, or to drag it to the height and throw it down the rocks, or to let it stand as a great offering to win the goodwill of the gods — which is what was to be done in the end, because it was their fate to die when their city took in the great wooden horse, with all the best of the Argives sitting inside it, bringing death and doom to the Trojans.

And he sang how the sons of the Achaeans poured out of the horse and left their hollow ambush and sacked the city; how the others laid waste the high city this way and that, and how Odysseus went like Ares to the house of Deiphobus together with godlike Menelaus. That, he said, was where Odysseus faced the most terrible fighting, and in the end conquered with the help of great-hearted Athena.

That was the song the famous singer sang. But the heart of Odysseus melted and the tears wet his cheeks under his eyelids. As a woman wails and flings herself over her dear husband, who has fallen in front of his city and his people, trying to keep the pitiless day away from his city and his children; and as she sees him dying and gasping for breath she clings to him and shrieks aloud, while behind her they strike her back and shoulders with their spears and lead her off into slavery, to hard labour and misery, and her cheeks waste away with the most pitiful grief — so Odysseus let the pitiful tears fall from under his brows.

He hid his weeping from all the rest of them, but Alcinous alone noticed him and took it in, because he sat beside him and heard him groaning heavily. And at once he spoke among the Phaeacians, who love the oar.

"Listen to me, leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians, and let Demodocus stop his clear-toned lyre now, since this song does not give pleasure to everyone alike. From the moment we began to eat and the divine singer was moved to sing, the stranger there has never stopped his sorrowful lamenting. Grief has taken hold of his heart, I am sure of it. Let the singer stop, so that we can all enjoy ourselves, hosts and guest alike, since that is better. All this has been made ready for the sake of our honoured guest, his sending and the friendship gifts we give him out of our affection. A stranger and a suppliant is as dear as a brother to any man whose wits reach even a little way.

"So do not hide any longer with crafty thoughts whatever I ask you. Speaking out plainly is the better course. Tell me the name they called you by at home, your mother and your father and the others besides, your townsmen and the people living round about. There is nobody among all mankind who has no name, base man or noble, once he has been born; parents give names to all of them when they are born. And tell me your country, your people, and your city, so that our ships can carry you there, working out the course by their own wits. The Phaeacians have no pilots and no steering-oars such as other ships have; their ships understand the thoughts and minds of men by themselves, and they know the cities and the rich fields of every people, and cross the gulf of the sea most swiftly, hidden in mist and cloud, and never have any fear of damage or ruin.

"Yet I once heard this story told by my father Nausithous, who used to say that Poseidon was angry with us because we give safe convoy to everyone. He said that one day, as a well-built ship of the Phaeacians was coming home from a convoy over the misty water, Poseidon would strike her, and would throw a great mountain round our city. That is what the old man said, and the god may bring it about or leave it unfulfilled, whichever pleases him.

"But come, tell me this and tell me the truth. Where have you wandered, and what countries of men have you come to? Tell me about the people and their well-built cities, both those who are cruel and wild and unjust, and those who welcome strangers and whose minds fear the gods. And tell me why you weep and mourn in your spirit when you hear the doom of the Argive Danaans and of Ilios. The gods brought that about, and spun the thread of ruin for men, so that there might be a song for those yet to be born. Did some relative of yours fall before Ilios, some good true man, your daughter's husband or your wife's father, who are the nearest to a man after his own kin and blood? Or was it a companion who was dear to your heart, some good true man? A companion who has an understanding heart is no worse than a brother."

Book Nine

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him.

Lord Alcinous, renowned above all men, this is a good thing indeed, to listen to a singer as fine as this man, whose voice is like a god's. For myself, I say there is no better fulfilment of delight than when joy holds a whole people, and banqueters in the halls listen to a singer as they sit in their order, and the tables beside them are loaded with bread and meat, and the wine-steward draws wine from the bowl and carries it round and pours it into the cups. That seems to my mind the fairest thing there is.

But your heart has turned to asking about my miserable troubles, so that I may weep and groan the more. What shall I tell you first, then, and what last? The heavenly gods have given me a great many.

First I will tell you my name, so that you know it too, and so that afterwards, when I have got clear of the pitiless day of doom, I may be your host, though my home is far off. I am Odysseus, son of Laertes, known among men for every kind of trick, and my fame reaches to heaven. I live in clear-seen Ithaca, where there is a mountain, Neriton, covered with waving forests, that can be seen from far off; and round it lie a good many islands close to one another, Dulichium and Same and wooded Zacynthus. Ithaca itself lies low, furthest out towards the dark, and the others lie apart towards the Dawn and the sun — a rugged island, but a good nurse of young men; and for myself I can see nothing sweeter than one's own country.

Calypso, the beautiful goddess, tried to keep me with her in her hollow caves, wanting me for a husband; and in the same way Circe held me back in her halls, the cunning lady of Aeaea, wanting me for a husband. But neither of them could ever persuade the heart in my breast. So true is it that nothing is sweeter than a man's own country and his parents, even if he is living in a rich house far away in a foreign land, away from his parents.

But come, let me tell you about my miserable homecoming too, which Zeus laid on me as I came from Troy.

The wind carried me from Ilios and brought me to the Cicones, to Ismarus. There I sacked the city and killed the men; and we took their wives out of the city, and a great store of treasure, and divided it among us, so that as far as it lay with me no man should go cheated of an equal share. Then I gave the order that we should get away as fast as we could; but the others in their great folly would not listen. There was a great deal of wine drunk, and they killed a great many sheep by the shore, and slow-moving cattle.

Meanwhile the Cicones went and called on other Cicones, their neighbours, more numerous than they were and braver — men who lived inland and were skilled at fighting from chariots, and on foot if they had to. They came in the morning, as thick as the leaves or the flowers come up in their season; and then an evil fate from Zeus fell on us, poor wretches, so that we should suffer a great deal. They drew up their line and fought beside the fast ships, and both sides threw bronze-tipped spears at each other. As long as it was morning and the sacred day was growing, so long we held our ground and beat them off, though they outnumbered us. But when the sun turned towards the hour for unyoking oxen, then the Cicones got the upper hand and routed the Achaeans, and six of my well-greaved companions died from each ship; the rest of us got clear of death and fate.

From there we sailed on, grieving in our hearts, glad to have escaped death, though we had lost our dear companions. And I did not let my curved ships go on until we had called three times on each of those unlucky companions of ours who died on the plain, cut down by the Cicones. But Zeus the cloud-gatherer roused the north wind against our ships with an extraordinary storm, and hid land and sea alike in cloud, and night rushed down out of the sky. The ships were driven headlong, and their sails were torn to shreds by the violence of the wind. So we lowered the sails and stowed them aboard, in fear of death, and rowed the ships hard towards the land. We lay there two nights and two days without a break, eating our hearts out with weariness and grief. But when fair-haired Dawn brought the third day to its birth, we set up the masts and hoisted the white sails and sat down, and the wind and the helmsmen steered the ships.

And now I would have come to my own country unhurt; but the wave and the current and the north wind beat me back as I was rounding Malea, and drove me off my course past Cythera.

From there I was carried for nine days by deadly winds over the teeming water; and on the tenth we set foot in the country of the Lotus-eaters, who eat a flowery food. There we went ashore and drew water, and my companions took their meal at once beside the fast ships. And when we had tasted food and drink, I sent some of my companions to go and find out who the men were who ate bread here on the earth. I chose two, and sent a third with them as a herald. They went off and mixed with the Lotus-eaters, and the Lotus-eaters did not plan any death for my companions, but gave them lotus to taste. And whichever of them ate the honey-sweet fruit of the lotus had no more wish to bring back word or to come home, but wanted to stay there among the Lotus-eaters, feeding on lotus, and forgot the way home. So I brought these men back to the ships by force, weeping, and dragged them under the benches and tied them fast in the hollow ships; and I told the rest of my trusty companions to embark quickly on the fast ships, in case anyone should eat the lotus and forget the way home. So they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches, and sitting in their order struck the grey sea with their oars.

From there we sailed on, grieving in our hearts, and we came to the country of the Cyclopes, an overbearing and lawless people, who trust in the immortal gods and plant nothing with their hands and do no ploughing; but everything comes up for them without sowing or ploughing — wheat and barley and vines that carry the rich clusters of wine — and the rain of Zeus makes them grow. They have no assemblies for council and no established laws, but live on the peaks of high mountains in hollow caves, and each of them lays down the law to his own children and wives, and they take no notice of one another.

Now there is a level island stretching aslant outside the harbour, not close to the shore of the country of the Cyclopes and not far off either, a wooded island. Wild goats live there past counting, because the tread of men does not frighten them off, and hunters do not go there, the men who endure hardship in the woodland ranging over the mountain peaks. It is not held with flocks or with ploughland; unsown and untilled all its days, it knows nothing of men and feeds the bleating goats. For the Cyclopes have no ships with vermilion cheeks, and there are no shipwrights in their country who might build them well-benched ships to do everything they need, crossing to the cities of other peoples as men often cross the sea in ships to visit one another — craftsmen, who would have made a fine settlement of this island too. It is not a poor island; it would bear everything in season. There are meadows by the shores of the grey sea, well-watered and soft, where vines would never fail, and level ploughland in it, where they could reap harvests season after season, extraordinarily deep, so rich is the soil underneath. And there is a harbour in it with safe anchorage, where there is no need of moorings, either to throw out anchor-stones or to make fast stern cables; a man can beach his ship and wait until the sailors' minds tell them to put out and the breezes blow fair. And at the head of the harbour a spring of bright water flows out from under a cave, and poplars grow round about it.

That is where we sailed in, and some god guided us through the murky night; there was no light to see by, but a mist lay deep about the ships and the moon gave no light from the sky, because it was shut in by clouds. So nobody's eyes made out that island, and we did not see the long waves rolling in on the beach until we had run our well-benched ships ashore. And when we had beached the ships we lowered all the sails and went out ourselves onto the shore of the sea, and there we fell asleep and waited for the bright Dawn.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, we wandered about the island, marvelling at it; and the nymphs, the daughters of Zeus who holds the aegis, roused the mountain goats, so that my companions might have something to make a meal of. Straight away we took our curved bows and long javelins out of the ships and, drawing up in three bands, started shooting; and the god soon gave us game enough to satisfy us. Twelve ships followed me, and nine goats fell to each by lot, but for me alone they picked out ten.

So all day long until sunset we sat feasting on plenty of meat and sweet wine. The red wine was not used up out of our ships; some was still left, because we had drawn plenty of it in jars for each crew when we took the sacred citadel of the Cicones. And we looked across at the land of the Cyclopes, who lived close by, and noticed the smoke, and the voices of men, and of the sheep and the goats. But when the sun set and the dark came on, we lay down to rest on the shore of the sea.

And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, I called my men together and spoke to them all.

"Stay here now, the rest of you, my trusty companions. I will go with my own ship and my own crew and find out about those men over there — whether they are cruel and wild and unjust, or whether they welcome strangers, and whether their minds fear the gods."

With that I went aboard the ship and told my companions to embark themselves and loose the stern cables. So they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches, and sitting in their order struck the grey sea with their oars.

But when we reached the place, which lay close by, we saw a high cave right at the edge of the land, close to the sea, roofed over with laurels, and a great many flocks, sheep and goats alike, used to sleep there. Round about it a high court was built with stones set deep in the earth, and with tall pines and high-crested oaks. A monstrous man slept there, who pastured his flocks alone and far off, and did not mix with the others but lived apart, with his heart set on lawlessness. He was made a marvellous monster, and was not like a man who lives on bread, but like a wooded peak of a high mountain that stands out alone, apart from the rest.

Then I told the rest of my trusty companions to stay there by the ship and guard the ship, and I picked twelve of my best companions and went off. I took with me a goatskin of the dark sweet wine which Maro, son of Euanthes, had given me, the priest of Apollo, the god who watched over Ismarus. He had given it me because we had protected him and his child and his wife out of respect; for he lived in a wooded grove of Phoebus Apollo. And he gave me splendid gifts: seven talents of well-worked gold, and a mixing bowl all of silver; and besides these, wine, which he filled twelve jars with in all, sweet and unmixed, a divine drink. Not one of his slaves or of the maids in his halls knew about it, only himself and his dear wife and one housekeeper. And whenever they drank that honey-sweet red wine, he would fill one cup and pour it into twenty measures of water, and a smell would rise from the mixing bowl marvellously sweet; and then you would not want to hold back. I filled a great skin with this wine and took it with me, and provisions in a bag as well; because my proud spirit had a feeling that a man would come at me before long clothed in great strength, a savage who knew nothing of justice or of law.

We reached the cave quickly, and did not find him inside; he was pasturing his fat flocks in the fields. So we went into the cave and stared at everything in it. The crates were loaded with cheeses, and the pens were crowded with lambs and kids. Each kind was penned separately: the firstlings by themselves, the later lambs by themselves, and the newly weaned by themselves again. And all the well-made vessels were swimming with whey, the milk-pails and the bowls he milked into.

Then my companions spoke and begged me first of all to take some of the cheeses and go, and after that to drive the kids and lambs quickly out of the pens to the fast ship, and sail over the salt water. But I would not listen to them — it would have been far better if I had — because I wanted to see the man himself, and whether he would give me guest-gifts. As it turned out, his appearing was not going to be a joy to my companions.

Then we kindled a fire and made an offering, and took some of the cheeses ourselves and ate, and sat in the cave and waited for him until he came back with his flocks. He was carrying a huge weight of dry wood to serve him at suppertime, and he flung it down with a crash inside the cave, and we shrank back into a recess of the cave in terror. He drove his fat flocks into the wide cavern, all the ones he milked, but the males, the rams and the goats, he left outside in the deep court. Then he lifted the great doorstone and set it in place, a mighty rock; twenty-two strong four-wheeled wagons could not have lifted it off the ground, such a towering mass of rock he set in the doorway. Then he sat down and milked the ewes and the bleating goats, all in turn, and put her young under each mother. Then he curdled half the white milk and gathered it into wicker baskets and put it away, and the other half he set in vessels, so that he could take it and drink it, and it would do for his supper.

But when he had busily got through his work, he rekindled the fire, and caught sight of us, and asked:

"Strangers, who are you? Where do you sail from over the watery roads? Is it on some business, or are you wandering at random over the sea like pirates, who wander with their lives in their hands and bring harm to people in other countries?"

So he spoke, and our spirits broke in our breasts at the terror of his deep voice and his monstrous self; but even so I answered him and said:

"We are Achaeans from Troy, driven wandering by all kinds of winds over the great gulf of the sea. Trying to get home, we have come by another way, by other roads; that is how Zeus was pleased to arrange it, I suppose. And we say we are the men of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, whose fame is now the greatest under heaven, so great a city did he sack, and killed so many people. But we, coming to you like this, have come as suppliants to your knees, hoping you will give us hospitality, or make us some present of another kind, which is what is due to strangers. Respect the gods, mightiest one; we are your suppliants. Zeus is the avenger of suppliants and strangers — Zeus the god of strangers — who always attends on strangers who deserve respect."

So I spoke, and he answered me straight away out of a pitiless heart: "You are a fool, stranger, or you have come from a long way off, if you tell me to fear the gods or avoid them. The Cyclopes take no notice of Zeus who holds the aegis, or of the blessed gods, since we are far stronger than they are. I would not spare you or your companions to avoid the anger of Zeus, not unless my own heart told me to. But tell me where you moored your well-made ship when you came. Was it somewhere at the far end of the country, or close by? I should like to know."

So he spoke, testing me; but he did not trap me, because I know too much. I answered him with crafty words: "Poseidon the earth-shaker smashed my ship, throwing it on the rocks at the edge of your country. He brought it close to the headland and the wind drove it in from the sea. But I and these men here got clear of destruction."

So I spoke, and out of his pitiless heart he made no answer at all, but sprang up and laid his hands on my companions. He seized two of them at once and dashed them on the ground like puppies, and their brains ran out on the ground and wetted the earth. Then he cut them limb from limb and got his supper ready, and ate them like a mountain-bred lion, leaving nothing — ate the entrails and the flesh and the marrowy bones. And we held our hands up to Zeus, wailing, seeing his cruel work; and helplessness took hold of our souls.

But when the Cyclops had filled his huge belly by eating human flesh and drinking unmixed milk afterwards, he lay down in the cave, stretched out among the sheep. And I made a plan in my great heart to steal up to him and draw my sharp sword from beside my thigh and stab him in the chest, where the midriff holds the liver, feeling for the place with my hand. But a second thought stopped me, because we too would have died right there in utter ruin: we could not have pushed the mighty stone he had set there away from the high door with our hands. So we waited, wailing, for the bright Dawn.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, he rekindled the fire and milked his fine flocks, all in turn, and put her young under each mother. And when he had busily got through his work he seized two men again at once and got his meal ready. And when he had eaten he drove his fat flocks out of the cave, moving the great doorstone away easily; and then he put it back in place, the way a man puts the lid on a quiver. Then, whistling loudly, the Cyclops turned his fat flocks towards the mountain, and I was left there, planning evil in the depths of my heart, hoping to take some revenge on him, and that Athena would grant me the glory.

And this seemed to my mind the best plan. There was a great club of the Cyclops lying beside a sheep-pen, a staff of green olive wood, which he had cut to carry when it was dry; and looking at it we thought it was as big as the mast of a black twenty-oared ship, a broad-beamed merchantman that crosses the great gulf: that was how huge it looked in length and thickness. I went to it and cut off about a fathom's length and handed it to my companions and told them to dress it down; and they made it smooth, and I stood by and sharpened it to a point, and then took it and hardened it in the blazing fire. Then I laid it carefully away, hiding it under the dung, which lay in great heaps all through the cave.
