And I told my companions to cast lots for which of them should have the nerve to lift the stake with me and grind it into his eye when sweet sleep came on him. And the lot fell on the very ones I would have chosen myself: there were four of them, and I made the fifth.

He came at evening, herding his flocks with their fine fleeces, and drove his fat flocks into the wide cave straight away, every one of them, and did not leave one outside in the deep court, either from some foreboding or because a god told him to. Then he lifted the great doorstone and set it in place, and sat down and milked the ewes and the bleating goats, all in turn, and put her young under each mother. And when he had busily got through his work, again he seized two men at once and got his supper ready.

Then I went up to the Cyclops and spoke to him, holding an ivy-wood bowl of the dark wine in my hands:

"Cyclops, here, drink wine after your meal of human flesh, so that you know what kind of drink our ship held. I was bringing it to you as an offering, hoping you might take pity and send me on my way home; but you rage past all bearing. Cruel man — how is anyone of all the crowds of men ever going to come to you again, when what you do is lawless?"

So I spoke, and he took the cup and drained it, and was marvellously pleased as he drank the sweet draught, and asked me for it a second time:

"Give me more, and readily, and tell me your name straight away, so that I can give you a stranger's gift to be glad of. The grain-giving earth bears the rich clusters of wine for the Cyclopes too, and the rain of Zeus makes them grow; but this is a stream of ambrosia and nectar."

So he spoke, and I handed him the flaming wine again. Three times I brought it and gave it him, and three times he drained it in his folly. But when the wine had got round the wits of the Cyclops, then I spoke to him with gentle words:

"Cyclops, you ask my famous name, and I will tell it you; and give me the stranger's gift, as you promised. Nobody is my name. Nobody they call me — my mother and my father and all my companions too."

So I spoke, and he answered me straight away out of a pitiless heart: "Nobody I will eat last among his companions, and the others before him. That shall be your gift."

He spoke, and reeled and fell on his back, and lay there with his thick neck bent aslant, and sleep that conquers everything took hold of him. And wine came up out of his gullet, and bits of human flesh, and he vomited in his drunken sleep.

Then I thrust the stake in under the deep ashes until it should get hot, and heartened all my companions with cheering words, so that no man should flinch from fear. But when the olive stake was about to catch fire, green as it was, and began to glow terribly, then I came close, bringing the stake out of the fire, and my companions stood round me, and a god breathed great courage into us. They took the olive stake, sharp at the point, and drove it into his eye, while I threw my weight on it from above and whirled it round, as a man bores a ship's timber with a drill while the men below keep it spinning with a thong, holding it by either end, and the drill runs round without stopping. So we took the fiery-pointed stake and whirled it round in his eye, and the blood flowed round the hot thing. The flame singed his eyelids and his brows all round as the eyeball burned, and its roots crackled in the fire. As when a smith dips a great axe or an adze in cold water with a loud hissing, to temper it — because that is where the strength of iron comes from — so his eye hissed round the olive stake.

Then he cried out terribly, and the rock rang round us, and we shrank back in terror, while he wrenched the stake out of his eye, all fouled with blood, and flung it away from him, waving his arms wildly. Then he called aloud to the Cyclopes who lived round about him in caves among the windy heights; and they heard his cry and came crowding from every side, and stood round the cave and asked him what was wrong.

"What is so badly wrong with you, Polyphemus, that you shout out like this through the immortal night and keep us from sleeping? Can it be that some mortal man is driving off your flocks against your will, or killing you himself by guile or by force?"

Then mighty Polyphemus answered them from inside the cave: "My friends, it is Nobody who is killing me by guile, and not by force."

And they answered him with winged words: "If nobody is doing you violence when you are alone, then it is a sickness from great Zeus and you cannot escape it. Pray to our father, the lord Poseidon."

So they spoke and went away; and my heart laughed inside me, that my name and my clever trick had taken them in so.

But the Cyclops, groaning and labouring in his pain, groped with his hands and took the stone away from the door and sat down in the doorway himself with his arms stretched out, hoping to catch anyone who tried to go out with the sheep: that was how witless he thought I was in his heart. But I was thinking how it might all turn out for the best, if I could find some way of escaping death for my companions and for myself. And I wove every kind of trick and plan, as a man will when it is a matter of life and death; for the danger was close on us. And this seemed to my mind the best plan.

There were rams, well fed and thick of fleece, fine big beasts with wool dark as the violet. These I bound together silently with the twisted withes the Cyclops used to sleep on, that monster with his heart set on lawlessness. I took them three at a time: the one in the middle carried a man each time, and the other two went one on either side, keeping my companions safe. So every three sheep carried one man. But for myself — there was a ram, far the best of the whole flock. I took hold of him by the back and curled up under his shaggy belly and lay there face upwards, holding on to his marvellous fleece with my hands, with a steady heart. So we waited, wailing, for the bright dawn.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, the males of the flock hurried out to pasture, and the females bleated unmilked round the pens, because their udders were bursting. And their master, in terrible pain, felt along the backs of all the sheep as they stood up in front of him; but in his folly he did not notice this, that my men were tied under the breasts of his fleecy sheep. Last of all the flock the ram went out, weighed down by his fleece and by my cunning self. And mighty Polyphemus felt along his back and said to him:

"Good ram, why are you going out through the cave like this, the last of the flock? You never used to lag behind the sheep before; you were always far the first to feed on the tender bloom of the grass, moving with long strides, and always the first to reach the streams of the river, and the first to want to come back to the fold in the evening. But now you are the last of all. Surely you are grieving for your master's eye, which an evil man blinded, with his miserable companions, when he had overpowered my wits with wine — Nobody, who I tell you has not yet escaped destruction. If only you could feel as I do, and could get the power of speech to tell me where he is skulking away from my anger, then his brains should be dashed on the ground here and there through the cave when I had struck him, and my heart should be lightened of the troubles that good-for-nothing Nobody has brought me."

With that he sent the ram out away from him. And when we had got a little way from the cave and the court, I first loosed myself from under the ram and then set my companions free. Quickly we drove off those long-shanked sheep, rich with fat, turning round again and again to look until we reached the ship. And the sight of us who had escaped death was welcome to our dear companions; but for the others they wept and wailed. I would not let them weep, though, but forbade each man with a frown. I told them instead to fling the many fine-fleeced sheep aboard quickly and sail over the salt water. So they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches, and sitting in their order struck the grey sea with their oars.

But when I was as far off as a man's voice carries when he shouts, then I spoke to the Cyclops with mocking words:

"Cyclops, that man was no weakling, it seems, whose companions you meant to eat by brute strength in your hollow cave. Your evil deeds were bound to fall on your own head, you cruel wretch, who did not shrink from eating your guests in your own house. So Zeus has taken his revenge on you, and the other gods."

So I spoke, and he grew angrier still in his heart, and broke off the peak of a high mountain and hurled it at us, and threw it in front of the dark-prowed ship. And the sea surged under the stone as it fell, and the backward flow, like a flood out of the deep, carried the ship quickly landward and drove it onto the shore. But I seized a long pole in my hands and shoved the ship off and along the shore, and roused my companions with a nod of my head and told them to fall to their oars so that we could get out of trouble. And they bent to the oars and rowed.

But when we had gone twice as far over the sea, then I wanted to call to the Cyclops, though my companions, one after another, tried to check me with gentle words: "Reckless man, why do you want to provoke a savage to anger? He has just thrown his missile into the sea and driven our ship back to the land, and we really thought we had died there. If he had heard one of us making a sound or speaking, he would have hurled a jagged rock and crushed our heads and the ship's timbers, he throws so hard."

So they spoke, but they could not persuade my great-hearted spirit, and I answered him again in anger:

"Cyclops, if any mortal man asks you about the shameful blinding of your eye, say that Odysseus blinded it, the sacker of cities, the son of Laertes, whose home is in Ithaca."

So I spoke, and he groaned and answered: "Now the prophecy spoken long ago has come on me. There was a seer here, a good tall man, Telemus, son of Eurymus, who was better than anyone at prophecy and grew old as a seer among the Cyclopes. He told me all this would come to pass in days to come, that I should lose my sight at the hands of Odysseus. But I always looked for some tall handsome man to come here, clothed in great strength; and now a puny thing, a man of no account and a weakling, has blinded me of my eye, after overpowering me with wine. But come here, Odysseus, so that I can set guest-gifts in front of you and speed your sending, and that the glorious earth-shaker may grant it you. I am his son, and he declares himself my father; and he himself will heal me, if he pleases, and nobody else, not one of the blessed gods and not any mortal man."

So he spoke, and I answered him: "I only wish I could rob you of soul and life and send you to the house of Hades, as surely as not even the earth-shaker will heal your eye."

So I spoke, and then he prayed to the lord Poseidon, stretching both his hands out to the starry sky: "Hear me, Poseidon, holder of the earth, dark-haired god, if I really am your son and you declare yourself my father. Grant that Odysseus, sacker of cities, may never reach his home, the son of Laertes, whose home is in Ithaca. But if it is his fate to see his friends and reach his well-built house and his own country, let him come late and in a bad way, after losing all his companions, in another man's ship; and let him find trouble in his house."

So he spoke in prayer, and the dark-haired god heard him. But the Cyclops lifted a far greater stone again and swung and hurled it, putting measureless strength into the throw. He threw it a little behind the dark-prowed ship, and barely missed the end of the steering-oar. And the sea surged under the stone as it fell, and the wave carried the ship on and drove it to the shore.

Now when we had come to the island where our other well-benched ships all lay together, and our companions sat round them weeping, always expecting us, we beached our ship on the sand when we got there, and went out ourselves onto the shore of the sea. Then we took the Cyclops's flocks out of the hollow ship and divided them, so that as far as it lay with me no man should go cheated of an equal share. But my well-greaved companions gave the ram to me alone, as a gift apart, when the flocks were divided; and I sacrificed him on the shore to Zeus, son of Cronos, the god of the dark clouds, who is lord of all, and burned the thigh-pieces. But he took no notice of my offering; he was planning how all my well-benched ships and my trusty companions might be destroyed.

So all day long until sunset we sat feasting on plenty of meat and sweet wine; and when the sun set and the dark came on we lay down to rest on the shore of the sea. And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, I roused my companions and told them to embark themselves and loose the stern cables. So they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches, and sitting in their order struck the grey sea with their oars.

From there we sailed on, grieving in our hearts, glad to have escaped death, though we had lost our dear companions.

Book Ten

Then we came to the Aeolian island, where Aeolus lived, son of Hippotas, dear to the immortal gods, on a floating island, with a wall of unbreakable bronze all round it and the cliff running up sheer. Twelve children of his live in the halls, six daughters and six sturdy sons, and he gave his daughters to his sons as wives. So they feast continually beside their dear father and their good mother, with boundless good things laid out in front of them; and the house, full of the savour of the feasting, rings all round it even in the outer court by day, and by night they sleep again beside their chaste wives on blankets and on corded bedsteads.

So we came to their city and their fine palace, and for a whole month he made me welcome and questioned me about everything, about Ilios and the ships of the Argives and the homecoming of the Achaeans; and I told him the whole story in its order. But when I asked him in my turn if I might go, and told him to send me on my way, he refused me nothing, and helped my sending. He gave me a bag made of the hide of a nine-year-old ox, which he skinned, and in it he bound the ways of the blustering winds; because the son of Cronos had made him keeper of the winds, to still or to rouse whichever one he pleases. And he made it fast in my hollow ship with a bright silver cord, so that not the slightest breath could escape. But he sent the breath of the west wind out to blow for my sending, so that it should carry ships and men on their way. And this he was not to bring off, because we were lost through our own folly.

For nine days we sailed, night and day alike, and now on the tenth our own country came in sight, and we were so close that we could see men tending the beacon fires. Then sweet sleep came over me, in my weariness; because I had kept the ship's sheet in my own hand the whole time and given it to none of my companions, so that we might get home sooner. And meanwhile my companions started talking to one another, and saying that I was bringing gold and silver home for myself as gifts from Aeolus, the great-hearted son of Hippotas. And one of them would say, with a glance at his neighbour:

"How loved and honoured this man is, wherever he comes, in whatever city and country. He is carrying a great deal of fine treasure away with him from the land of Troy out of the plunder, while we, who have made the same journey he has, are going home with empty hands. And now Aeolus has given him these gifts, granting them freely out of affection. Come, let us have a quick look at what is here, and see how much gold and silver is in the bag."

So they spoke, and my companions' bad advice won the day. They loosed the bag, and all the winds leapt out, and the storm wind seized them at once and carried them out to sea, weeping, away from their own country. And I woke, and turned it over in my good heart whether to throw myself out of the ship and die in the sea, or to endure in silence and stay among the living. I endured and stayed, and covered my head and lay down in the ship. And the ships were carried by an evil blast of wind back to the Aeolian island, and my companions groaned.

There we went ashore and drew water, and my companions took their meal at once beside the fast ships. And when we had tasted food and drink, I took a herald and one companion with me and went to the splendid palace of Aeolus, and found him feasting beside his wife and his children. So we went into the house and sat down by the doorposts on the threshold, and their hearts were amazed, and they asked us:

"How have you come here, Odysseus? What cruel god attacked you? We sent you off with every care, so that you should reach your own country and your home, and any place you wanted."

So they spoke, and I answered them with a sorrowing heart: "My wicked companions did me this harm, and an accursed sleep along with them. But heal it, my friends; the power is with you."

So I spoke, addressing them with gentle words, and they said nothing. Then their father answered and said: "Get off our island as fast as you can, you vilest of all living things. There is no way I can help or send on his way a man who is hated by the blessed gods. Get out, since you come here hated by the immortals."

With that he sent me out of the house, groaning heavily.

From there we sailed on, grieving in our hearts. And the men's spirits were worn down by the hard rowing, through our own folly, since there was no breeze any more to carry us on our way. So for six days we sailed, night and day alike, and on the seventh we came to the high citadel of Lamus, to Telepylus of the Laestrygonians, where one herdsman calls to another as he drives his flock in, and the other answers as he drives his out. There a man who never slept could have earned a double wage, one for herding cattle and one for pasturing white sheep; because the outgoings of the night and of the day are close together there.

When we had come in there to the fine harbour, which has a sheer cliff running unbroken along both sides of it, with jutting headlands opposite each other stretching out at the mouth and a narrow entrance, all the rest steered their curved ships in, and the ships were moored inside the hollow harbour close together; because no wave ever swelled in there, great or small, but there was a bright calm all round. But I alone moored my black ship outside, there at the edge of the land, making the cables fast to the rock. Then I climbed a rugged height, a point of outlook, and took my stand there. From there no works of oxen or of men could be seen; we saw only smoke going up from the land.

So I sent some of my companions to go and find out who the men were who ate bread here on the earth. I chose two, and sent a third with them as a herald. When they had gone ashore they went along a smooth road by which wagons used to bring wood down to the city from the high mountains. And in front of the city they met a girl drawing water, the fine daughter of Antiphates the Laestrygonian, who had come down to the fair-flowing spring Artacia, where they used to carry water to the town from. So they came up to her and spoke to her and asked her who was king of these people, and who they were that he was lord over. And she showed them at once the high-roofed house of her father.

But when they had gone into the splendid house, they found his wife there, huge as a mountain peak, and they were appalled at her. She called glorious Antiphates at once from the meeting place, her husband, and he contrived a miserable destruction for them. He seized one of my companions straight away and got his meal ready, and the other two sprang up and came running back to the ships. Then he raised a cry through the city, and as they heard it the mighty Laestrygonians came crowding from all sides, a host past counting, not like men but like the Giants. They pelted us from the cliffs with rocks as big as a man could lift, and at once a dreadful din went up through the ships, of men dying and of ships being crushed. And they speared them like fish and carried them home, a loathsome meal.

Now while they were killing the men inside the deep harbour, I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and cut the cables of my dark-prowed ship with it, and called quickly to my companions and told them to fall to their oars so that we could get out of trouble. And they all tossed the sea with their oar-blades in fear of death, and gladly my ship ran out to sea, away from the beetling cliffs; but all those other ships were lost together there.

From there we sailed on, grieving in our hearts, glad to have escaped death, though we had lost our dear companions; and we came to the island of Aeaea, where Circe of the lovely hair lived, a dread goddess with human speech, own sister of Aeetes of the deadly mind. Both of them are the children of Helios, who gives light to mortals, and of Perse their mother, whom Oceanus fathered.

Here we put in to shore with our ship in silence, into a harbour where ships may lie, and some god guided us. Then we disembarked and lay there two days and two nights, eating our hearts out with weariness and grief. But when Dawn of the lovely hair brought the third day to its birth, I took my spear and my sharp sword and went up quickly from the ship to a place with a wide view, hoping to see the works of men and hear their voices. So I climbed a rugged height, a place of outlook, and took my stand there, and I saw smoke rising from the broad-wayed earth in the halls of Circe, through the thick brush and the wood.

And I turned over in my mind and heart whether to go and look into it, since I had seen the flaming smoke. And as I turned it over, this seemed to me the better way: to go first to the fast ship and the shore of the sea and give my companions their meal, and then send them out to look. But when, as I went, I was near the curved ship, some god took pity on me in my loneliness, and sent a great high-antlered stag right into my path. He was coming down from his pasture in the wood to the river to drink, because the strength of the sun was oppressing him; and as he came out I struck him on the spine in the middle of the back, and the bronze spear went right through him, and down he fell in the dust with a moan, and his spirit flew from him.

Then I planted my foot on him and drew the bronze spear out of the wound and left it lying on the ground; and I pulled up twigs and osiers and wove a rope about a fathom long, well twisted from end to end, and bound the feet of the monstrous beast together and went off to the black ship carrying him across my back, leaning on my spear — because there was no way I could hold him on my shoulder with one hand; he was a very big beast. I flung him down in front of the ship and heartened my companions with gentle words, going up to each man in turn:

"Friends, we are not going down to the house of Hades yet, for all our sorrows, before the day of our fate comes. Come, while there is still food and drink in our fast ship, let us think about eating, so that we do not waste away with hunger."

So I spoke, and they quickly did as I said. They drew their cloaks off their faces and stared at the stag on the shore of the restless sea, because he was a very big beast. And when they had had their fill of looking they washed their hands and got a splendid feast ready. So all day long until sunset we sat feasting on plenty of meat and sweet wine. But when the sun set and the dark came on we lay down to rest on the shore of the sea.

And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, I called my men together and spoke to them all:

"Listen to me, companions, for all the trouble you are in. My friends, we do not know where the dark is or where the dawn, nor where the sun that gives light to mortals goes down under the earth, nor where he rises. Let us think at once whether there is any plan left to us. I do not think there is. I climbed a rugged point of outlook and saw the island, with the boundless deep set round it like a crown. The island itself lies low, and in the middle of it my eyes saw smoke through the thick brush and the wood."

So I spoke, and their spirits broke inside them as they remembered the doings of Antiphates the Laestrygonian, and the violence of the great-hearted Cyclops, the man-eater. And they wailed aloud and shed big tears. But nothing came of their mourning.

Then I divided all my well-greaved companions into two bands and appointed a leader for each. I took command of one, and godlike Eurylochus of the other. Then we quickly shook lots in a bronze helmet, and out leapt the lot of great-hearted Eurylochus. So he set out, and twenty-two companions went with him, all weeping; and they left us behind, lamenting.

In the forest glades they found the house of Circe, built of polished stone in a place with a wide view; and round about it were mountain wolves and lions, whom Circe had bewitched herself, because she gave them evil drugs. These beasts did not rush at my men, but pranced round them fawning, wagging their long tails. And as hounds fawn round their master when he comes from a feast, because he always brings them scraps to soothe their temper, so the stout-clawed wolves and lions fawned round them. But the men were seized with fear when they saw the dreadful creatures.

So they stood in the gateway of the goddess with the lovely hair, and inside they heard Circe singing in a sweet voice as she went to and fro at a great imperishable web, the kind of work that goddesses do, finely woven and beautiful and splendid. Then Polites spoke among them, a leader of men, the dearest and most trustworthy of my companions:

"Friends, somebody inside is going to and fro at a great web, singing sweetly, so that the whole floor echoes with it. Some goddess it is, or some woman. Come, let us call to her quickly."

So he spoke, and they called aloud to her. And she came out at once and opened the shining doors and asked them in; and they all went with her in their folly. Only Eurylochus stayed behind, because he suspected there was a trap.

She brought them in and made them sit on chairs and seats, and made them a potion of cheese and barley meal and yellow honey with Pramnian wine; but she mixed deadly drugs into the food, so that they should forget their own country altogether. And when she had given them the potion and they had drunk it off, she struck them at once with her wand and penned them in the sties. And they had the heads and the voices and the bristles and the shape of pigs, but their minds stayed unchanged, just as before. So they were penned there weeping, and Circe threw down mast and acorns for them, and the fruit of the cornel tree, to eat, the kind of thing wallowing pigs feed on.

But Eurylochus came straight back to the fast black ship to bring news of his companions and their shameful fate. He could not utter a word, for all he wanted to, so stricken to the heart was he with great distress; his eyes were full of tears and his spirit was set on lamenting. But when we questioned him in astonishment, then he told us what had happened to the others, his companions:

"We went through the thickets, as you told us, noble Odysseus. We found a fine palace in the forest glades, built of polished stone, in a place with a wide view. Somebody was going to and fro there at a great web, singing in a clear voice, some goddess or some woman; and they called aloud to her. And she came out at once and opened the shining doors and asked them in, and they all went with her in their folly. But I stayed behind, because I suspected there was a trap. And then they all vanished together, and not one of them appeared again, though I sat a long while and watched."

So he spoke, and I slung my silver-studded sword over my shoulders, a great sword of bronze, and slung my bow round me, and told him to lead me back by the same road. But he took hold of me with both hands and begged me by my knees, and spoke to me with winged words, wailing:

"Do not take me there against my will, you whom Zeus brought up; leave me here. I know that you will not come back yourself, and will not bring any of your companions back either. Let us get away as fast as we can with the men we have here; we may still escape the evil day."

So he spoke, but I answered him: "Eurylochus, you stay here in this place, eating and drinking beside the hollow black ship; but I am going, because a hard necessity is laid on me."

With that I went up from the ship and the sea. But when, as I went through the sacred glades, I was about to come to the great house of the sorceress Circe, then Hermes of the golden wand met me as I went towards the house, in the likeness of a young man with the first down on his lip, at the age when the charm of youth is loveliest. He took my hand and said to me:

"Where are you going now, again, unlucky man, alone through the hills, knowing nothing of the country? Your companions over there in the house of Circe are penned like pigs in close-barred sties. And have you come to set them free? I tell you, you will not get back yourself; you will stay there with the others. But come, I will save you from harm and get you out. Take this potent herb and go to the house of Circe, and it will keep the evil day off your head. And I will tell you all the deadly tricks of Circe.

"She will mix you a potion and throw drugs into the food; but even so she will not be able to bewitch you, because the potent herb I am going to give you will not allow it. And I will tell you the rest. When Circe strikes you with her long wand, then draw your sharp sword from beside your thigh and rush at Circe as if you meant to kill her. And she will be seized with fear and will tell you to lie with her. Then do not refuse the goddess's bed after that, so that she sets your companions free and gives you hospitality. But make her swear a great oath by the blessed gods that she is not plotting some fresh mischief against you to your harm, in case, when she has you stripped, she makes you a weakling and unmanned."

With that the slayer of Argus gave me the herb, pulling it out of the ground, and showed me what it was like. It was black at the root, and its flower was like milk. The gods call it moly, and it is hard for mortal men to dig; but with the gods everything is possible.

Then Hermes went off to high Olympus through the wooded island, and I went on to the house of Circe, and my heart turned over a great deal darkly as I went. So I stood at the gates of the goddess with the lovely hair. I stood there and called, and the goddess heard my voice. She came out at once and opened the shining doors and asked me in; and I went with her, with a badly troubled heart.

She brought me in and made me sit on a silver-studded chair, a beautiful chair, finely made, with a footstool below for the feet. And she made me a potion in a golden cup for me to drink, and put a drug into it, with evil purpose in her heart. But when she had given it to me and I had drunk it off, and was not bewitched, she struck me with her wand and said to me:

"Off with you to the sty now, and lie down with the rest of your companions."

So she spoke; but I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and rushed at Circe as though I meant to kill her. And she gave a loud cry and ran in under the blow and took hold of my knees, and spoke to me with winged words, wailing:

"Who are you among men, and where from? Where is your city, and where are your parents? Amazement takes hold of me that you have drunk this charm and were not bewitched at all. No other man has ever withstood this charm once he has drunk it and it has passed the barrier of his teeth. But the mind in your breast is not one that can be beguiled. You must be Odysseus, the man of ready invention, whom the slayer of Argus with the golden wand always told me would come here on his way home from Troy with his fast black ship. Come, put your sword back in its sheath, and let the two of us go up into my bed, so that we may lie together in love and trust each other."

So she spoke; but I answered her: "Circe, how can you tell me to be gentle to you, when you have turned my companions into pigs in your halls, and are keeping me here now and telling me with a guileful purpose to go to your chamber and go up into your bed, so that when you have me stripped you can make me a weakling and unmanned? No, I will not be willing to go up into your bed unless you, goddess, will consent to swear a great oath that you are not plotting some fresh mischief against me to my harm."

So I spoke, and she swore the oath at once to do me no harm, as I told her. And when she had sworn and finished the oath, then I went up into Circe's beautiful bed.

Meanwhile her women were busy in the halls, four girls who are her serving-women in the house. They are the children of the springs and the groves and the sacred rivers that flow out to the sea. One of them threw fine purple rugs over the chairs and spread a linen cloth underneath them; another drew silver tables up in front of the chairs and set golden baskets on them; and the third mixed sweet honey-hearted wine in a silver bowl and served out golden cups; and the fourth brought water and kindled a great fire under a big cauldron, and the water grew warm. And when the water boiled in the bright bronze she set me in a bath and bathed me with water from the great cauldron, mixing it to my liking and pouring it over my head and shoulders, until she took the soul-consuming weariness out of my limbs.

And when she had bathed me and rubbed me richly with oil and put a fine cloak and a tunic on me, she brought me into the hall and made me sit on a silver-studded chair — a beautiful chair, finely made, with a footstool below for the feet. Then a maid brought water for my hands in a lovely golden jug and poured it over a silver basin for me to wash, and drew a polished table up beside me. And the housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it in front of me, and plenty of meat with it, giving freely from her stores. Then she told me to eat, but my heart was not inclined to it. I sat there with other thoughts, and my spirit foreboded trouble.

Now when Circe noticed that I sat like that and did not put out my hands to the food, but was weighed down by hard grief, she came close to me and spoke winged words:

"Why do you sit like this, Odysseus, like a man struck dumb, eating your heart out, and touch neither food nor drink? Do you suspect some other trick? You need not be afraid at all; I have sworn a great oath already to do you no harm."

So she spoke, but I answered her: "Circe, what right-minded man could bring himself to taste food or drink before he had won his companions' freedom and seen them in front of his own face? If you are telling me to eat and drink with a ready heart, set them free, so that my eyes may see my trusty companions."

So I spoke, and Circe went out through the hall holding her wand in her hand, and opened the doors of the sty and drove them out in the shape of nine-year-old pigs. So they stood there in front of her, and she went through the middle of them and rubbed each man with another charm. Then the bristles fell away from their limbs, which the deadly drug queenly Circe had given them had made grow before, and they became men again, younger than they had been, and far handsomer and taller to look at. They knew me, and every one of them clung to my hands, and a passionate sobbing came over them all, and the house rang marvellously round them; and the goddess herself was moved to pity.

Then the beautiful goddess came up to me and said: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, go now to your fast ship and the shore of the sea. First of all draw the ship up on the land, and store your goods and all the tackle in the caves; and then come back yourself, and bring your trusty companions."

So she spoke, and my proud heart agreed. I went off to the fast ship and the shore of the sea, and there I found my trusty companions beside the fast ship, wailing pitifully, shedding big tears. As calves in a farmyard frisk about the droves of cows coming back to the yard when they have had their fill of grazing — they all frisk together in front of them, and the pens do not hold them any longer, but they run round their mothers lowing continually — so those men, when their eyes took me in, crowded round me weeping; and it seemed to their hearts as though they had got home to their own country and the very city of rugged Ithaca, where they were bred and born. And they spoke to me with winged words, wailing:

"We are as glad at your coming back, you whom Zeus brought up, as though we had got home to Ithaca, our own country. But come, tell us what happened to the others, our companions."

So they spoke, and I answered them with gentle words: "First of all let us draw the ship up on the land, and store our goods and all the tackle in the caves. Then hurry, every one of you, and come with me, so that you can see your companions in the sacred halls of Circe, eating and drinking; they have unfailing supplies."

So I spoke, and they quickly did as I said. Only Eurylochus tried to hold all my companions back, and he spoke to them with winged words:

"Poor wretches, where are we going? Why are you so in love with trouble, that you would go down to the house of Circe, who will turn us all into pigs or wolves or lions, so that we have to guard her great house whether we like it or not? It was the same with the Cyclops, when our companions went to his fold, and this reckless Odysseus went with them. It was through this man's folly that they died too."

So he spoke, and I turned it over in my heart whether to draw the long sword from beside my strong thigh and strike his head off with it and bring it to the ground, close kinsman of mine by marriage though he was. But my companions, one after another, tried to check me with gentle words:

"You who are sprung from Zeus, as for this man, we will leave him here beside the ship, if you say so, to guard the ship; but lead us to the sacred house of Circe."

With that they went up from the ship and the sea. And Eurylochus was not left beside the hollow ship either, but came with us, because he was afraid of my terrible rebuke.

Meanwhile in her halls Circe bathed the rest of my companions with kindly care, and rubbed them richly with oil, and put fleecy cloaks and tunics on them; and we found them all feasting generously in the halls. And when they saw and recognised each other face to face, they wept and wailed, and the house rang round them.

Then the beautiful goddess came up to me and said: "Do not rouse this great lamenting any longer. I know myself both all the troubles you have suffered on the teeming water and all the wrong cruel men have done you on land. Come, eat food and drink wine, until you get the spirit back in your breasts that you had when you first left your own country of rugged Ithaca. As it is you are withered and spiritless, always thinking of your weary wanderings, and your hearts are never glad, because you have suffered a great deal."

So she spoke, and our proud hearts agreed. So we stayed there day after day for a whole year, feasting on plenty of meat and sweet wine. But when a year had gone and the seasons turned, as the months waned and the long days came round in their course, then my trusty companions called me out and said:

"Strange man, think now at last of your own country, if it is fated for you to be saved and to reach your high-roofed house and your own country."

So they spoke, and my proud heart agreed. So all day long until sunset we sat feasting on plenty of meat and sweet wine. But when the sun set and the dark came on they lay down to sleep through the shadowy halls, and I went up into the beautiful bed of Circe and begged her by her knees; and the goddess heard my voice, and I spoke to her with winged words:

"Circe, keep the promise you made me, to send me home. My spirit is eager to be gone now, and so is the spirit of my companions, who wear my heart away sitting round me mourning, whenever you happen not to be there."

So I spoke, and the beautiful goddess answered at once: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, do not stay in my house any longer against your will. But you must finish another journey first, and come to the house of Hades and dread Persephone, to consult the spirit of Theban Teiresias, the blind seer, whose mind stays steady. To him, even in death, Persephone has granted reason, so that he alone has understanding; the others flit about as shadows."

So she spoke, and my spirit broke inside me, and I wept as I sat on the bed, and my heart had no more wish to live and see the light of the sun. But when I had had my fill of weeping and writhing, I answered her and said:

"Circe, who will guide us on this journey? No man has ever yet gone to Hades in a black ship."

So I spoke, and the beautiful goddess answered at once: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, let there be no concern in your mind for a pilot to guide your ship. Set up your mast and spread the white sail and sit down; and the breath of the north wind will carry her on. But when you have crossed the stream of Oceanus in your ship, where there is a level shore and the groves of Persephone — tall poplars and willows that shed their fruit — beach your ship there by the deep eddying Oceanus, and go yourself to the dank house of Hades.

"There Periphlegethon and Cocytus flow into Acheron, and Cocytus is a branch of the water of the Styx; and there is a rock, and the meeting place of the two roaring rivers. Go up close to that, prince, as I tell you, and dig a pit a cubit's length this way and that, and pour an offering round it to all the dead, first with milk and honey, then with sweet wine, and in the third place with water, and sprinkle white barley meal over it. And make an earnest plea to the powerless heads of the dead, promising that when you come to Ithaca you will sacrifice in your halls a barren heifer, the best you have, and will fill the altar with rich gifts; and that to Teiresias alone you will sacrifice separately a ram, wholly black, the finest in your flock.

"But when you have made your plea in prayer to the glorious tribes of the dead, then sacrifice a ram and a black ewe, turning their heads towards Erebus but turning yourself away and setting your face towards the streams of the river. Then many ghosts of men who are dead will come out. Then call to your companions and tell them to flay and burn the sheep lying there, killed by the pitiless bronze, and to pray to the gods, to mighty Hades and to dread Persephone. And draw your own sharp sword from beside your thigh, and sit there, and do not let the powerless heads of the dead come near the blood until you have questioned Teiresias. Then the seer will come to you soon, leader of men, and will tell you your road and the measure of your journey, and about your homecoming, how you are to go over the teeming water."

So she spoke, and golden-throned Dawn came at once. She put a cloak and a tunic on me for clothes, and the nymph dressed herself in a long white robe, finely woven and beautiful, and put a fine golden girdle round her waist and a veil on her head.

But I went through the halls and roused my men with gentle words, going up to each man in turn: "Do not sleep any longer now, dozing in sweet slumber. Let us go; queenly Circe has told me everything."

So I spoke, and their proud hearts agreed. But not even from there could I bring my men away unhurt. There was one, Elpenor, the youngest of them all, not very brave in war and not sound in his understanding, who had lain down apart from his companions in the sacred house of Circe, looking for the cool air, because he was heavy with wine. He heard the noise and the bustle of his companions moving about and sprang up suddenly, and forgot to go to the long ladder to come down again, but fell headlong from the roof, and his neck was broken away from the spine, and his spirit went down to the house of Hades.

But as my men were going on their way I spoke among them and said: "You think, I suppose, that you are going to your own dear country. But Circe has marked out another journey for us, to the house of Hades and dread Persephone, to consult the spirit of Theban Teiresias."

So I spoke, and their spirits broke inside them, and they sat down right where they were and wept and tore their hair. But nothing came of their lamenting.

But as we were going on our way to the fast ship and the shore of the sea, sorrowing and shedding big tears, Circe had gone ahead meanwhile and made fast a ram and a black ewe beside the black ship; she had passed us easily. Who could see a god with his own eyes against the god's will, coming or going?

Book Eleven

But when we had come down to the ship and the sea, first of all we drew the ship down to the bright sea and set the mast and sail in the black ship, and took the sheep and put them aboard, and got in ourselves, sorrowing and shedding big tears. And Circe of the lovely hair, dread goddess with human speech, sent a fair wind after our dark-prowed ship to help us, filling the sail, a good companion. So when we had made all the tackle fast through the ship we sat down, and the wind and the helmsman kept her straight on her course. All day long her sail was stretched as she ran over the sea; and the sun set and all the ways grew dark.

She came to the deep-flowing Oceanus, which bounds the earth, where the country and the city of the Cimmerians are, wrapped in mist and cloud. The bright sun never looks down on them with his rays, either when he climbs the starry sky or when he turns back to earth from the sky, but deadly night is spread over those wretched people. We came there and beached our ship and took the sheep out, and went ourselves along the stream of Oceanus until we came to the place Circe had told us of.

Here Perimedes and Eurylochus held the victims, while I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh and dug a pit a cubit's length this way and that, and poured an offering round it to all the dead, first with milk and honey, then with sweet wine, and in the third place with water, and sprinkled white barley meal over it. And I made an earnest plea to the powerless heads of the dead, promising that when I came to Ithaca I would sacrifice in my halls a barren heifer, the best I had, and pile the altar with fine gifts, and that to Teiresias alone I would sacrifice separately a ram, wholly black, the finest in my flocks.

But when I had made my plea to the tribes of the dead with vows and prayers, I took the sheep and cut their throats over the pit, and the dark blood ran out. Then out of Erebus the spirits of the dead gathered — brides, and unmarried young men, and old men worn out with labour, and tender girls with hearts still new to sorrow, and many who had been wounded with bronze-tipped spears, men killed in fighting, still wearing their blood-stained armour. They came crowding round the pit from every side with an extraordinary cry, and pale fear took hold of me.

Then I called to my companions and told them to flay and burn the sheep that lay there killed by the pitiless bronze, and to pray to the gods, to mighty Hades and dread Persephone. And I drew my sharp sword from beside my thigh myself and sat there, and would not let the powerless heads of the dead come near the blood until I had questioned Teiresias.

The first to come was the spirit of my companion Elpenor. He had not yet been buried under the broad-wayed earth; we had left his body behind in the hall of Circe, unwept and unburied, because another task was pressing us on. When I saw him I wept, and my heart was full of pity for him, and I spoke to him with winged words:

"Elpenor, how did you come here, under the murky dark? You have come on foot and outstripped me in my black ship."

So I spoke, and he answered me with a groan: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, an evil doom from some god was my undoing, and measureless wine. When I had lain down to sleep in the house of Circe I did not think to go to the long ladder to come down again, but fell headlong from the roof, and my neck was broken away from the spine, and my spirit went down to the house of Hades. Now I beg you by those we left behind, who are not here with us, by your wife and by your father who brought you up when you were a baby, and by Telemachus, whom you left an only son in your halls — because I know that when you go from here, from the house of Hades, you will put in at the island of Aeaea with your well-built ship. There, prince, I ask you to remember me. Do not leave me behind unwept and unburied when you go, and do not turn away from me, in case I bring the anger of the gods on you. Burn me with my armour, everything that is mine, and heap a mound up for me on the shore of the grey sea, in memory of an unhappy man, so that men still to come may learn of me. Do this for me, and fix on the mound the oar I rowed with in life when I was among my companions."

So he spoke, and I answered him: "I will carry out all of it, unhappy man, and do it."

So the two of us sat and talked sadly to each other, I on one side holding my sword over the blood, while on the other side the phantom of my companion spoke at length.

Then the spirit of my dead mother came up, Anticleia, the daughter of great-hearted Autolycus, whom I had left alive when I went to sacred Ilios. At the sight of her I wept, and my heart was full of pity for her; but even so I would not let her come near the blood, for all my great sorrow, until I had questioned Teiresias.

Then the spirit of Theban Teiresias came up, carrying his golden staff in his hand, and he knew me and said to me: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, what is it now, unlucky man? Why have you left the light of the sun and come here to look at the dead and a country where there is no joy? Give way from the pit and draw back your sharp sword, so that I can drink the blood and tell you the truth."

So he spoke, and I gave way and thrust my silver-studded sword into its sheath; and when he had drunk the dark blood, then the blameless seer spoke to me and said:

"You are asking about your honey-sweet homecoming, glorious Odysseus; but a god will make it hard for you. I do not think you will get past the earth-shaker, who has stored up anger in his heart against you, furious that you blinded his dear son. Even so you may reach home, though in a bad way, if you will curb your own spirit and your companions', as soon as you bring your well-built ship to the island of Thrinacia, escaping from the violet sea, and find the cattle and fine flocks of Helios grazing there, who oversees and overhears everything. If you leave them unharmed and keep your mind on your homecoming, you may still reach Ithaca, though in a bad way. But if you harm them, then I foresee ruin for your ship and your companions; and even if you get clear yourself, you will come home late and badly, after losing all your companions, in another man's ship, and you will find trouble in your house — proud men eating up your living, courting your godlike wife and offering wedding gifts. But you will take your revenge on their violence when you come.

"And when you have killed the suitors in your halls, whether by trickery or openly with the sharp sword, then go out, taking a well-shaped oar, until you come to men who know nothing of the sea and do not eat food mixed with salt, and know nothing of ships with purple cheeks or of the well-shaped oars that are the wings of ships. And I will tell you a sign, quite unmistakable, which will not escape you. When another traveller meets you and says you have a winnowing-fan on your broad shoulder, then plant your well-shaped oar in the ground and make fine offerings to lord Poseidon — a ram and a bull and a boar that mates with sows — and go home and offer sacred hecatombs to the immortal gods who hold the wide sky, to each in his order. And death will come to you yourself far from the sea, a death so gentle that it will lay you low when you are worn out with a sleek old age, and your people will live in prosperity around you. This is the truth I have told you."

So he spoke, and I answered him: "Teiresias, the gods themselves must have spun the thread of all this. But tell me this, and tell me the truth. I see the spirit of my dead mother here. She sits in silence near the blood and does not think fit to look at her own son's face or to speak to him. Tell me, prince, how can she know that I am he?"

So I spoke, and he answered at once: "The word I shall say is an easy one, and I will put it in your mind. Whichever of the dead and gone you let come near the blood will tell you the truth; whichever you refuse will go back again."

With that the spirit of the prince Teiresias went back into the house of Hades, when he had delivered his prophecies; but I stayed there steadily until my mother came up and drank the dark blood. And at once she knew me, and spoke to me with winged words, wailing:

"My child, how did you come here under the murky dark, still alive? It is hard for the living to look on these regions, because there are great rivers between, and dreadful streams; Oceanus first, which nobody can cross on foot at all, but only with a well-built ship. Have you only now come here from Troy, after long wandering with your ship and your companions? And have you not reached Ithaca yet, or seen your wife in your halls?"

So she spoke, and I answered her: "Mother, necessity brought me down to the house of Hades, to consult the spirit of Theban Teiresias. I have not come near the shore of Achaea yet, nor set foot on my own land; I have been wandering all this time, loaded with trouble, since the day I first went with noble Agamemnon to Ilios, famous for horses, to fight the Trojans. But tell me this, and tell me the truth. What fate of miserable death overcame you? Was it a long illness, or did Artemis the archer attack you with her gentle arrows and kill you? And tell me about my father and my son, whom I left behind. Does the honour that was mine still rest with them, or does some other man hold it now, and do they say I shall never come back? And tell me about my wedded wife, what she means to do and what is in her mind. Does she stay with her son and keep everything safe? Or has one of the Achaeans married her already, whoever is best of them?"

So I spoke, and my honoured mother answered at once: "She does stay in your halls, with a steady heart; and the nights and the days waste away for her sorrowfully as she weeps. Nobody holds your fine honour yet; Telemachus keeps your estate undisturbed, and feasts at the shared banquets that a man who deals judgment should share in, because everybody invites him. But your father stays out there in the country and does not come to the city; and he has no bed for bedding, and no cloaks and bright coverlets, but through the winter he sleeps in the house where the slaves sleep, in the ashes by the fire, and wears poor clothes on his body. And when summer comes, and rich autumn, then all over the slope of his vineyard his low beds of fallen leaves are strewn. There he lies grieving, and nurses his great sorrow in his heart, longing for you to come back; and a heavy old age has come on him.

"And that is how I died too, and met my fate. The keen-sighted archer goddess did not attack me in my halls with her gentle arrows and kill me, and no illness came on me, the kind that most often takes the spirit out of the limbs by a miserable wasting. It was longing for you, and for your counsel, glorious Odysseus, and for your tenderness, that robbed me of honey-sweet life."

So she spoke, and I turned it over in my heart and wanted to take hold of the spirit of my dead mother. Three times I sprang towards her, and my heart told me to hold her, and three times she flitted out of my arms like a shadow or a dream, and the pain grew sharper at my heart. And I spoke to her with winged words:

"Mother, why do you not stay for me, when I want to hold you, so that even in the house of Hades the two of us might put our arms round each other and take our fill of cold lamenting? Is this only a phantom that august Persephone has sent me, so that I may lament and groan the more?"

So I spoke, and my honoured mother answered at once: "Oh, my child, unluckiest of all men, Persephone, the daughter of Zeus, is not deceiving you at all. This is the way it is with mortals when a man dies. The sinews no longer hold the flesh and the bones together, but the strong force of the blazing fire destroys them, as soon as the life leaves the white bones, and the spirit flits away like a dream and hovers to and fro. But make your way to the light as fast as you can, and keep all this in mind, so that you can tell it to your wife afterwards."

So the two of us talked to each other; and the women came, because august Persephone sent them out, all those who had been the wives and daughters of chieftains. They flocked in crowds about the dark blood, and I considered how I might question each of them; and this seemed to my mind the best plan. I drew my long sword from beside my strong thigh and would not let them all drink the dark blood at once. So they came up one after another, and each declared her birth, and I questioned them all.

The first I saw was high-born Tyro, who said she was the daughter of noble Salmoneus and declared herself the wife of Cretheus, son of Aeolus. She fell in love with the river, divine Enipeus, who is far the loveliest of the rivers that send their streams over the earth, and she used to go to the fair waters of Enipeus. But the Enfolder and Shaker of the earth took his shape and lay with her at the mouths of the eddying river. And the dark wave stood round them like a mountain, arched over, and hid the god and the mortal woman. And he loosed her maiden girdle and poured sleep over her. But when the god had finished his work of love, he took her hand and said to her:

"Be glad, woman, in our love. As the year goes round you will bear glorious children, because the embraces of a god are not feeble. Tend them and bring them up. But go home now, and hold your tongue, and tell nobody; and know that I am Poseidon, the shaker of the earth."

With that he plunged under the surging sea. And she conceived and bore Pelias and Neleus, who both became strong servants of great Zeus; and Pelias lived in spacious Iolcus and was rich in flocks, and the other lived in sandy Pylos. And her other children she bore to Cretheus, queenly among women as she was — Aeson, and Pheres, and Amythaon, who fought from chariots.

And after her I saw Antiope, the daughter of Asopus, who boasted that she had slept in the arms of Zeus himself; and she bore two sons, Amphion and Zethus, who first founded the seat of seven-gated Thebe and fenced it round with walls, because they could not live in spacious Thebe unfenced, however mighty they were.

And after her I saw Alcmene, the wife of Amphitryon, who lay in the arms of great Zeus and bore Heracles, steady in a fight, the lion-hearted. And I saw Megara, the daughter of high-hearted Creon, whom the son of Amphitryon had for a wife, always stubborn in his strength.

And I saw the mother of Oedipodes, lovely Epicaste, who did a monstrous thing in the ignorance of her mind, in marrying her own son; and he married her when he had killed his own father, and the gods made it known among men straight away. He stayed on as lord of the Cadmeans in lovely Thebe, suffering through the deadly counsels of the gods; but she went down to the house of Hades, the strong warder. She made a noose fast high up from a lofty beam, overcome by her sorrow; but for him she left behind a great many troubles, all the ones a mother's Avengers bring about.

And I saw beautiful Chloris, whom Neleus once married for her beauty, when he had brought countless wedding gifts. She was the youngest daughter of Amphion, son of Iasus, who once ruled mightily in Orchomenus of the Minyae; and she was queen of Pylos, and bore her husband glorious children, Nestor and Chromius and lordly Periclymenus, and besides these she bore noble Pero, a wonder to men. All who lived round about sought her in marriage, but Neleus would give her to no man except the one who should drive off from Phylace the cattle of mighty Iphicles, sleek and broad of brow; and they were hard to drive. The blameless seer alone undertook to drive them off; but a grievous fate from the gods trapped him, hard bonds and the herdsmen of the field. But when at last the months and the days were coming to fulfilment, as the year rolled round and the seasons came on, then mighty Iphicles released him, when he had told him all the oracles; and the will of Zeus was fulfilled.

And I saw Leda, the wife of Tyndareus, who bore Tyndareus two sons stout of heart, Castor the horse-tamer and Polydeuces the boxer. The earth, the giver of life, covers them both, alive as they are, and even in the world below they have honour from Zeus. One day they live in turn, and one day they are dead; and they have won honour equal to the gods'.

And after her I saw Iphimedeia, the wife of Aloeus, who declared she had lain with Poseidon. She bore two sons, but short-lived they were, godlike Otus and far-famed Ephialtes — men whom the grain-giving earth reared as the tallest, and far the handsomest, after famous Orion. At nine years old they were nine cubits across and nine fathoms in height. And they threatened to raise the din of furious war against the immortals on Olympus. They meant to pile Ossa on Olympus, and Pelion with its waving forests on Ossa, so that heaven could be scaled. And they would have done it, if they had reached the full measure of manhood; but the son of Zeus, whom fair-haired Leto bore, killed them both before the down blossomed under their temples and covered their chins with a full beard.

And I saw Phaedra and Procris, and lovely Ariadne, the daughter of Minos of the deadly mind, whom Theseus once wanted to carry from Crete to the hill of sacred Athens; but he had no joy of her, because Artemis killed her first in sea-girt Dia, on the witness of Dionysus.

And I saw Maera and Clymene, and hateful Eriphyle, who took precious gold as the price of her own husband's life.

But I cannot tell or name all the wives and daughters of heroes that I saw; immortal night would wane before I finished. It is time to sleep now, either when I have gone to the fast ship and the crew, or here. My sending rests with the gods, and with you.

So he spoke, and they were all hushed and silent, and were held spellbound through the shadowy halls. Then white-armed Arete was the first to speak among them.

"Phaeacians, how does this man seem to you, in looks and in build and in the balanced spirit inside him? And besides, he is my guest, though each of you has a share in the honour. So do not be in a hurry to send him away, and do not be sparing with your gifts to a man in such need; you have a great many treasures stored in your halls by the favour of the gods."

Then the old lord Echeneus spoke among them too, who was an elder among the Phaeacians: "Friends, the words of our wise queen are not wide of the mark, or of our own thinking. Do as she says. But it is on Alcinous here that the deed and the word depend."

Then Alcinous answered him and said: "This word of hers shall stand, as surely as I am alive and lord over the Phaeacians, who love the oar. But let our guest bear to stay until tomorrow, for all his great longing to get home, until I have made our gift complete. His sending rests with the men, with all of them, but most of all with me; the control in this country is mine."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Lord Alcinous, renowned above all men, if you told me to stay here even for a year, and would speed my sending and give me splendid gifts, I would choose that too. It would be far better to come to my own dear country with a fuller hand. I would win more respect and more affection from every man who saw me when I had got back to Ithaca."

Then Alcinous answered him again and said: "Odysseus, looking at you we do not take you for a cheat and a dissembler of the kind the dark earth breeds and scatters far and wide, men who make up lies out of things nobody could ever see. There is grace on your words, and a good mind inside you, and you have told your tale with skill, like a singer — the bitter troubles of all the Argives and your own. But tell me this, and tell me the truth. Did you see any of your godlike companions, who went to Ilios along with you and met their fate there? The night is ahead of us, long, extraordinarily long, and it is not yet time to sleep in the hall. Tell me the rest of these marvellous doings. I could hold out until the bright dawn, if you were willing to tell me of these troubles of yours in the hall."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him.

Lord Alcinous, renowned above all men, there is a time for many words and a time for sleep as well. But if you still want to listen, I would not grudge telling you of other things more pitiful still than these — the sorrows of my companions, who died afterwards, who got clear of the dreadful war-cry of the Trojans but died on their homecoming through the will of an evil woman.

When holy Persephone had scattered the spirits of the women this way and that, the spirit of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, came up sorrowing; and round about him others were gathered, the spirits of all those who were killed with him in the house of Aegisthus and met their fate. He knew me at once when he had drunk the dark blood, and wept aloud and shed big tears and stretched his hands out towards me, eager to reach me. But he had no strength or force left in him any more, of the kind there used to be in his supple limbs.

When I saw him I wept, and my heart was full of pity for him, and I spoke to him with winged words: "Most glorious son of Atreus, king of men, Agamemnon, what fate of miserable death overcame you? Did Poseidon strike you aboard your ships, when he had roused a furious blast of cruel winds? Or did enemies do you harm on land, while you were cutting off their cattle and their fine flocks of sheep, or fighting to take their city and their women?"

So I spoke, and he answered at once: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, Poseidon did not strike me aboard my ships, when he had roused a furious blast of cruel winds, and enemies did not do me harm on land. Aegisthus contrived my death and my fate, and killed me with the help of my accursed wife, when he had invited me to his house and made me a feast — the way a man kills an ox at the stall. So I died by the most pitiful death, and round about me the rest of my companions were killed one after another, like white-tusked pigs slaughtered in the house of a rich and powerful man for a wedding feast, or a shared meal, or a rich drinking party.

"You have been present before now at the killing of a great many men, killed in single combat or in the press of a fight; but your heart would have felt the most pity if you had seen that sight, how we lay round the mixing bowl and the loaded tables in the hall, and the whole floor swam with blood. And the most pitiful cry I heard was Cassandra's, the daughter of Priam, whom treacherous Clytemnestra killed beside me. And I tried to lift my hands and strike the murderess down, dying as I was, run through with the sword. But she, the shameless creature, turned her back on me, and though I was going to the house of Hades she did not think fit to draw my eyelids down with her fingers or to close my mouth. So true is it that there is nothing more dreadful or more shameless than a woman who takes such deeds into her heart, as she too devised a monstrous thing, contriving death for her own wedded husband. I thought I would come home welcome to my children and my slaves; but she, with her heart set on utter wickedness, has poured shame on herself and on the women yet to come, even on the one who does right."

So he spoke, and I answered him: "Zeus, whose voice carries far, has visited an extraordinary hatred on the house of Atreus from the beginning, through the counsels of women. A great many of us died for Helen's sake, and Clytemnestra spread a snare for you while you were far away."

So I spoke, and he answered at once: "So in your own case never be gentle even to your wife. Do not tell her everything in your mind, but tell her something and let something be hidden. Yet death will not come to you from your wife, Odysseus; the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, is very prudent and has an understanding heart. We left her a bride newly married when we went to the war, and a boy was at her breast, a baby, who now, I suppose, sits in the ranks of men, a happy man, since his dear father will see him when he comes, and he will greet his father as he should. But my wife did not even let me fill my eyes with the sight of my own son. Before that she killed me too, her husband. And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart: bring your ship in to the shore of your own dear country in secret and not openly, because there is no trusting women any longer.

"But tell me this, and tell me the truth: do you hear of my son as still alive, in Orchomenus, it may be, or in sandy Pylos, or with Menelaus in wide Sparta? Noble Orestes has not died on the earth yet."

So he spoke, and I answered him: "Son of Atreus, why do you ask me this? I do not know at all whether he is alive or dead, and it is a bad thing to speak words as empty as wind."

So the two of us stood and talked sadly to each other, sorrowing and shedding big tears. And the spirit of Achilles, son of Peleus, came up, and those of Patroclus and of faultless Antilochus and of Ajax, who was the finest of all the Danaans in looks and in build after the faultless son of Peleus. And the spirit of the swift-footed grandson of Aeacus recognised me, and spoke to me weeping with winged words:

"Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, rash man, what greater thing than this will you think up in your heart? How did you dare to come down to Hades, where the unheeding dead live, the phantoms of men worn out?"

So he spoke, and I answered him: "Achilles, son of Peleus, far the mightiest of the Achaeans, I came because I needed Teiresias, in case he would tell me some plan by which I might reach rugged Ithaca. I have not come near the land of Achaea yet, nor set foot on my own country, but am always suffering; whereas no man before you was ever more blessed than you, Achilles, and none will be hereafter. In the old days, when you were alive, we Argives honoured you like the gods, and now that you are here you rule mightily among the dead. So do not grieve at all that you are dead, Achilles."

So I spoke, and he answered at once: "Do not try to speak soothingly to me about death, glorious Odysseus. I would rather be above ground still and labouring for some poor man with little land and not much to live on, than be lord over all the dead who have perished.

"But tell me news of my son, that lordly boy — whether he followed to the war to be a leader or not. And tell me of noble Peleus, if you have heard anything, whether he still has honour among the crowd of the Myrmidons, or whether they do him dishonour through Hellas and Phthia because old age binds him hand and foot. I am not there to help him under the rays of the sun, in the strength I once had in wide Troy, when I killed the best of their army in defence of the Argives. If I could come to my father's house in that strength, even for an hour, I would make many a one of those who do him violence and keep him from his honour rue my strength and my invincible hands."

So he spoke, and I answered him: "I have heard nothing of noble Peleus; but as for your dear son Neoptolemus, I will tell you the whole truth, as you ask me. It was I myself who brought him from Scyros in my trim hollow ship to join the army of the well-greaved Achaeans. And whenever we took counsel round the city of Troy he was always the first to speak, and he never missed with a word; godlike Nestor and I alone were better than he was. And whenever we fought with the bronze on the Trojan plain he never stayed back in the crowd or the press of men, but always ran far out to the front, yielding to nobody in his strength; and he killed a great many men in dreadful fighting. I could not tell or name them all, all the army he killed defending the Argives; but what a fighter that son of Telephus was whom he killed with the sword, the prince Eurypylus — and a great many of his companions, the Ceteians, were killed round about him, because of the gifts a woman wanted. He was the handsomest man I ever saw, next to noble Memnon.

"And again, when we, the best of the Argives, were about to go down into the horse that Epeus made, and the whole command was laid on me, both to open and to close the door of our stoutly-built ambush, then the other leaders and counsellors of the Danaans were wiping the tears from their eyes and every man's legs shook under him; but I never once saw his fine face go pale at all, or saw him wiping tears off his cheeks. He kept begging me to let him go out of the horse, and kept handling his sword-hilt and his spear heavy with bronze, and was eager to do the Trojans harm. And after we had sacked the high city of Priam he went aboard his ship with his share of the plunder and a fine prize, unhurt, neither struck by the sharp spear nor wounded in close fighting, as often happens in war; because Ares rages at random."

So I spoke, and the spirit of the grandson of Aeacus went off with long strides over the field of asphodel, glad that I had said his son was outstanding.

And the other spirits of the dead and gone stood there sorrowing, and each asked after those who were dear to him. Only the spirit of Ajax, son of Telamon, stood apart, still full of anger over the victory I had won against him in the contest by the ships for the arms of Achilles, which his honoured mother had set as the prize; and the judges were the sons of the Trojans and Pallas Athena. I wish I had never won in a contest for such a prize; the earth closed over so noble a head because of those arms, over Ajax, who in looks and in deeds of war was above all the other Achaeans, next to the faultless son of Peleus. I spoke to him with soothing words:

"Ajax, son of faultless Telamon, were you not even in death going to forget your anger against me over those accursed arms? The gods must have set them there to be a curse to the Argives: such a tower of strength was lost to them in you, and we Achaeans grieve for you in your death without stopping, as we grieve for the life of Achilles, son of Peleus. And nobody is to blame but Zeus, who bore a terrible hatred against the army of the Danaan spearmen and brought your doom on you. Come here, prince, and hear my word and my speech; and put down your anger and your proud spirit."

So I spoke, but he did not answer me a word, and went off to Erebus to join the other spirits of the dead and gone. Even so he would have spoken to me for all his anger, or I to him; but the heart in my breast wanted to see the spirits of the other dead.

There I saw Minos, the glorious son of Zeus, with the golden sceptre in his hand, giving judgment to the dead from his seat, while they sat and stood about the king through the wide-gated house of Hades and asked him for judgment.

And after him I made out huge Orion, driving together over the field of asphodel the wild animals he had killed himself on the lonely hills, with a club in his hands all of bronze, unbreakable for ever.

And I saw Tityos, the son of glorious Gaea, lying on the ground. He stretched over nine roods, and two vultures sat one on either side of him and tore at his liver, plunging their beaks into his bowels, and he could not beat them off with his hands. He had offered violence to Leto, the glorious wife of Zeus, as she went towards Pytho through Panopeus with its lovely lawns.

And I saw Tantalus in violent torment, standing in a pool, and the water came up close to his chin. He looked like a man dying of thirst, and could not take any and drink; because as often as the old man stooped down, eager to drink, so often the water was swallowed up and vanished away, and the black earth appeared at his feet, because some god dried it up. And tall leafy trees let their fruit stream down over his head, pears and pomegranates and apple trees with their bright fruit, and sweet figs and thriving olives. But as often as the old man reached out towards them to clutch them with his hands, the wind would toss them up to the shadowy clouds.

And I saw Sisyphus in violent torment, trying to lift a monstrous stone with both hands. He would brace himself with hands and feet and thrust the stone up towards the crest of a hill; but as often as he was about to heave it over the top, the weight would turn it back, and then the shameless stone would come rolling down again to the plain. And he would strain and thrust it back again, and the sweat ran down off his limbs and the dust rose up from his head.

And after him I made out mighty Heracles — his phantom; because he himself takes his pleasure at the feast among the immortal gods, and has Hebe for a wife, Hebe of the lovely ankles, the daughter of great Zeus and of Hera of the golden sandals. Round about him rose a clamour from the dead, like birds flying everywhere in terror; and he, like dark night, with his bow bare and an arrow on the string, glared round him terribly, like a man in the act of shooting. There was an awful belt about his breast, a baldric of gold, with marvellous things worked on it, bears and wild boars and lions with flashing eyes, and struggles and battles and murders and killings of men. May the man never design another like it, whoever stored up the design of that belt in his craft.

He knew me in his turn when his eyes took me in, and spoke to me weeping with winged words: "Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks — poor wretch, are you dragging out an evil lot too, of the kind I once bore under the rays of the sun? I was the son of Zeus, son of Cronos, and I had misery beyond measure; because I was made subject to a man far worse than myself, and he laid hard labours on me. He once sent me here to fetch the hound of Hades, because he could think of no other task mightier than that. I carried the hound off and led him out of the house of Hades; and Hermes was my guide, and grey-eyed Athena."

With that he went back into the house of Hades, but I stayed there steadily, hoping that some other of the warrior heroes who died in the old days might still come out.
