And I would have seen still others of the men of former times, whom I wanted to see, Theseus and Peirithous, the glorious children of the gods; but before that the countless tribes of the dead came crowding up with an extraordinary cry, and pale fear seized me, in case august Persephone might send out at me from the house of Hades the head of the Gorgon, that terrible monster.

So I went at once to the ship and told my companions to embark themselves and loose the stern cables. They went aboard quickly and sat down at the benches. And the swelling flood carried the ship down the stream of Oceanus, first with our rowing, and afterwards the wind was fair.

Book Twelve

Now when our ship had left the stream of the river Oceanus and had come to the wave of the broad sea, and to the island of Aeaea, where early Dawn has her home and her dancing-lawns, and the sun his risings, we beached our ship on the sands when we came there, and went out ourselves onto the shore of the sea, and there we fell asleep and waited for the bright Dawn.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, I sent my companions to the house of Circe to fetch the body of the dead Elpenor. We cut billets of wood at once and buried him where the headland runs furthest out to sea, sorrowing and shedding big tears. And when the dead man was burned, and the dead man's armour, we heaped up a mound and dragged a pillar onto it, and planted his well-shaped oar on top of the mound.

So we were busy with these separate tasks; and Circe was not unaware of our coming back from the house of Hades, but got herself ready quickly and came, and her women brought bread and meat in plenty with her, and flaming red wine. And the beautiful goddess stood in the middle of us and spoke:

"Reckless men, who have gone down alive to the house of Hades, to meet death twice when other men die only once. Come, eat food and drink wine here this whole day through; and at the coming of Dawn you shall set sail, and I will point out the way and tell you everything, so that you do not suffer pain and misery through wretched bad planning, either by sea or on land."

So she spoke, and our proud hearts agreed. So all day long until sunset we sat feasting on plenty of meat and sweet wine. But when the sun set and the dark came on, they lay down to rest beside the stern cables of the ship; and Circe took me by the hand and led me apart from my dear companions and made me sit, and lay down close by herself and asked me the whole story. And I told her everything in its order.

Then queenly Circe said to me:

"So all that has found its end. Now listen to what I tell you, and a god himself will bring it back to your mind. You will come first to the Sirens, who beguile every man who comes to them. Whoever draws near them in ignorance and hears the Sirens' voice never comes home again, so that his wife and his little children may stand beside him rejoicing; the Sirens beguile him with their clear-toned song, sitting in a meadow, with a great heap of the bones of rotting men round about them, and the skin shrivelling on the bones. Row past them, and knead sweet wax and stop your companions' ears with it, so that none of the rest can hear. But if you have a mind to listen yourself, let them bind you in the fast ship hand and foot, upright in the step of the mast, with the ropes made fast at the ends to the mast itself, so that you may listen to the voice of the two Sirens with delight. And if you beg your companions and tell them to loose you, then let them tie you with still more bonds.

"But when your companions have rowed past them, then I shall not say for certain which way your course is to lie; think it over in your own mind, and I will tell you of both ways. On one side there are beetling crags, and the great wave of dark-eyed Amphitrite roars against them; the blessed gods call these the Planctae. No winged thing can get past them, not even the timid doves that carry ambrosia to father Zeus: the smooth rock always snatches away even one of these, and the father sends in another to make up the number. And no ship of men that has come there has ever got away, but the planks of ships and the bodies of men are whirled about together by the waves of the sea and the blasts of deadly fire. One seafaring ship alone has passed that way, the Argo that everyone speaks of, on her voyage home from Aeetes; and even her the wave would have dashed against the great crags there, if Hera had not sent her through, because Jason was dear to her.

"On the other path there are two cliffs. One of them reaches the wide sky with its sharp peak, and a dark cloud surrounds it. This never melts away, and clear sky never surrounds that peak, in summer or at harvest time. No mortal man could climb it or set foot on the top, not if he had twenty hands and feet; the rock is smooth, as if it had been polished. And in the middle of the cliff there is a dim cave, turned to the west, towards Erebus, and that is where you are to steer your hollow ship, glorious Odysseus. Not even a strong man could shoot an arrow from the hollow ship so as to reach into that vaulted cave. Scylla lives in there, yelping horribly. Her voice is only the voice of a new-born puppy, but she herself is an evil monster, and nobody would be glad at the sight of her, not even a god who met her. She has twelve feet, all misshapen, and six necks, extraordinarily long, and on each one a terrible head, with three rows of teeth in it, thick and close together and full of black death. She is hidden up to her middle in the hollow cave, but she holds her heads out beyond the dreadful chasm and fishes there, searching eagerly round the rock for dolphins and sea-dogs and any bigger creature she may catch, of the kind deep-moaning Amphitrite rears in numbers past counting. No sailors can yet boast that they have got past her unhurt in their ship, because with each head she carries off a man, snatching him out of the dark-prowed ship.

"But the other cliff, Odysseus, you will notice is lower — they are close to each other; you could shoot an arrow across — and there is a great fig tree on it with rich foliage; and beneath it divine Charybdis sucks down the black water. Three times a day she belches it out, and three times she sucks it down horribly. Do not be there when she sucks it down, because nobody could save you from ruin then, not even the earth-shaker. Keep very close to Scylla's cliff and drive your ship past quickly; it is far better to mourn six companions in your ship than all of them together."

So she spoke, but I answered her: "Come, goddess, tell me this truly: is there any way I might get clear of deadly Charybdis and fight the other off, when she does my companions harm?"

So I spoke, and the beautiful goddess answered: "Reckless man, your heart is set on the works of war again, and on hard labour. Will you not give way even to the immortal gods? She is not mortal; she is an immortal evil, dreadful and hard and fierce, and not to be fought with. There is no defence; the bravest thing is to run from her. If you linger to arm yourself by the cliff, I am afraid she may dart out again and attack you with as many heads and take as many men as before. Row past with all your strength, and call on Crataiis, the mother of Scylla, who bore her as a curse to mortals. Then she will stop her darting out again.

"And you will come to the island of Thrinacia. The cattle of Helios graze there in great numbers, and his fine flocks, seven herds of cattle and as many fine flocks of sheep, fifty in each. They bear no young and they never die, and goddesses are their shepherds, nymphs with lovely hair, Phaethusa and Lampetie, whom beautiful Neaera bore to Helios Hyperion. Their honoured mother, when she had borne them and brought them up, sent them to the island of Thrinacia to live far off and keep their father's flocks and his sleek cattle. If you leave them unharmed and keep your mind on your homecoming, you may still reach Ithaca, though in a bad way. But if you harm them, then I foretell ruin for your ship and for your companions; and even if you get clear yourself, you will come home late and badly, after losing all your companions."

So she spoke, and golden-throned Dawn came at once. Then the beautiful goddess went off up the island, and I went to the ship and roused my companions to embark themselves and loose the stern cables. So they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches, and sitting in their order struck the grey sea with their oars. And Circe of the lovely hair, dread goddess with human speech, sent a fair wind after our dark-prowed ship to help us, filling the sail, a good companion. So when we had quickly made all the tackle fast through the ship we sat down, and the wind and the helmsman guided the ship.

Then I spoke among my companions, grieving in my heart: "Friends, it is not right that one or two alone should know the oracles that Circe, the beautiful goddess, told me. So I will tell you, and then, knowing them, we shall either die or, avoiding death and fate, get clear. First she told us to avoid the voice of the marvellous Sirens and their flowery meadow. Me alone she told to listen to their voice. But you must bind me with hard bonds, so that I stay fast where I am, upright in the step of the mast, with the ropes made fast at the ends to the mast itself; and if I beg you and tell you to loose me, then tie me fast with still more bonds."

So I went through all this and told my companions. Meanwhile the well-built ship came quickly to the island of the two Sirens, because a fair and gentle wind carried her on. Then the wind dropped and there was a windless calm, and a god lulled the waves to sleep. But my companions got up and furled the sail and stowed it in the hollow ship, and then sat down at the oars and made the water white with their polished oars of fir. And I cut a great round cake of wax into small bits with my sharp sword and kneaded it with my strong hands, and the wax soon grew warm, forced by the strong pressure and by the rays of the lord Helios Hyperion. Then I stopped the ears of all my companions with it, one after another; and they bound me in the ship hand and foot, upright in the step of the mast, and made the ropes fast at the ends to the mast itself, and sat down themselves and struck the grey sea with their oars.

But when we were as far off as a man can make himself heard when he shouts, driving swiftly on our way, the Sirens did not fail to notice the fast ship as it drew near, and they raised their clear-toned song:

"Come here, as you go by, famous Odysseus, great glory of the Achaeans; stop your ship, so that you may listen to the voice of us two. No man has ever yet rowed past this island in his black ship until he has heard the sweet voice from our lips. He has his joy of it, and goes on his way a wiser man. We know everything the Argives and the Trojans endured in wide Troy through the will of the gods, and we know everything that happens on the fruitful earth."

So they spoke, sending out their beautiful voices, and my heart wanted to listen, and I told my companions to loose me, nodding at them with my brows; but they fell to their oars and rowed on. And Perimedes and Eurylochus got up at once and bound me with still more bonds and drew them tighter. But when they had rowed past the Sirens, and we could hear their voices and their song no longer, then my trusty companions took away the wax I had stopped their ears with and loosed me from my bonds.

But when we had left the island I saw smoke straight away, and a great billow, and heard a booming. Then the oars flew out of my men's hands in their terror and splashed one and all in the swirl, and the ship stood still where it was, when they no longer worked the tapering oars with their hands. But I went through the ship and cheered my men with gentle words, going up to each man in turn:

"Friends, we have not been strangers to sorrow up to now. This evil that is on us is certainly no worse than when the Cyclops penned us in his hollow cave by brute strength; and even from there we got away by my courage and counsel and wit. I think we shall remember these dangers too, one day. Come now, do as I tell you, all of you. Keep your seats on the benches and strike the deep surf of the sea with your oars, and hope that Zeus grants us to escape and avoid this death. And to you, steersman, I give this order, and take it to heart, since you handle the steering-oar of the hollow ship: keep the ship well away from this smoke and surf, and hug the cliff, or before you know it she will swerve off to the other side and you will throw us into destruction."

So I spoke, and they quickly did as I said. But I said nothing about Scylla, that incurable evil, in case my companions were seized with fear and stopped rowing and huddled together in the hold. And then I forgot Circe's hard command, when she told me not to arm myself at all; but I put my glorious armour on and took two long spears in my hand and went to the fore-deck of the ship, because I thought Scylla of the rock would first be seen from there, who was to bring ruin on my companions. But nowhere could I make her out, and my eyes grew tired with gazing everywhere towards the misty rock.

So we sailed on up the narrow strait, wailing. On one side lay Scylla, and on the other divine Charybdis sucked down the salt water of the sea terribly. Whenever she belched it out, she would seethe and bubble in utter turmoil like a cauldron over a great fire, and high overhead the spray would fall on the tops of both cliffs. But as often as she sucked the salt water down, she could all be seen inside in utter turmoil, and the rock roared terribly round about, while beneath, the earth showed black with sand; and pale fear seized my men.

So we looked towards her, in fear of destruction; and meanwhile Scylla seized six of my companions out of the hollow ship, the best in strength and in force. Turning my eyes to the fast ship and to my men, I saw their feet and hands above me already, lifted high in the air. They cried aloud to me, calling me by name for that last time, in the anguish of their hearts. And as a fisher on a jutting rock, when he throws in his baits as a snare for the little fishes, lets down into the sea the horn of an ox from the farmyard on his long pole, and then flings the fish he catches ashore, writhing — so they were drawn writhing up towards the cliffs. And there at her doors she devoured them, shrieking and stretching their hands out towards me in their terrible death-struggle. That was the most pitiful thing my eyes ever saw of all I bore while I explored the paths of the sea.

Now when we had got clear of the rocks, and dreadful Charybdis and Scylla, we came to the god's fine island, where the beautiful broad-browed cattle were, and the many fine flocks of Helios Hyperion. While I was still out at sea in my black ship I heard the lowing of the cattle being stalled and the bleating of the sheep, and the words of the blind seer, Theban Teiresias, came into my mind, and the words of Aeaean Circe, who charged me very strictly to avoid the island of Helios, who gives joy to mortals. Then I spoke among my companions, grieving in my heart:

"Hear my words, companions, for all the trouble you are in, so that I can tell you the oracles of Teiresias and of Aeaean Circe, who charged me very strictly to avoid the island of Helios, who gives joy to mortals; because there, she said, was our most terrible danger. Row the black ship on past the island."

So I spoke, but their spirits broke inside them, and Eurylochus answered me at once with hateful words:

"You are a hard man, Odysseus. You have strength beyond other men, and your limbs never grow tired. You must be made of iron all through, since you will not let your companions, worn out with labour and drowsiness, set foot on shore, where on this sea-girt island we might get a good supper ready once more; you tell us to wander on just as we are through the swift night, driven away from the island over the misty water. It is out of the night that fierce winds are born, the wreckers of ships. How could a man escape utter destruction if a blast of the south wind or the blustering west wind came on suddenly, the winds that most often wreck ships in spite of the sovereign gods? Let us give way to the black night this time and get our supper ready, staying beside the fast ship, and in the morning we will go aboard and put out into the broad sea."

So spoke Eurylochus, and the rest of my companions agreed with him. Then I knew for certain that some god was contriving trouble, and I spoke to him with winged words:

"Eurylochus, you are forcing me, and I stand alone. But come now, all of you swear me a great oath, that if we find a herd of cattle or a great flock of sheep, no man will kill either cow or sheep in the blind folly of his mind, but will be content to eat the food immortal Circe gave us."

So I spoke; and they swore at once that they would not, as I told them. And when they had sworn and finished the oath, we moored our well-built ship in the hollow harbour near a spring of sweet water, and my companions went out of the ship and skilfully got their supper ready. But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, they began to weep, remembering their dear companions whom Scylla had snatched out of the hollow ship and devoured; and sweet sleep came on them as they wept.

But at the third watch of the night, when the stars had turned their course, Zeus the cloud-gatherer roused a fierce wind against us with an extraordinary storm, and hid land and sea alike in cloud, and night rushed down out of the sky. And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, we dragged our ship up and made her fast in a hollow cave, where the fine dancing-floors and the seats of the nymphs were. Then I called my men together and spoke among them:

"Friends, there is meat and drink in our fast ship. Let us keep our hands off those cattle, then, so that we come to no harm; because these are the cows and the fine sheep of a dread god, of Helios, who oversees everything and overhears everything."

So I spoke, and their proud hearts agreed. Then for a whole month the south wind blew without stopping, and no other wind rose except the east and the south.

Now as long as my men had grain and red wine they kept their hands off the cattle, because they were eager to save their lives. But when all the stores had been used up out of the ship, and they had to roam about hunting for game — fish and birds and whatever might come to their hands, fishing with bent hooks, because hunger was pinching their bellies — then I went off up the island to pray to the gods, hoping one of them would show me a way to go. And when, going through the island, I had got away from my companions, I washed my hands in a place where there was shelter from the wind and prayed to all the gods who hold Olympus; and they poured sweet sleep over my eyelids.

And meanwhile Eurylochus began to give my companions evil counsel:

"Hear my words, companions, for all the trouble you are in. Every kind of death is hateful to wretched mortals, but to die of hunger and meet your doom that way is the most pitiful of all. Come, let us drive off the best of the cattle of Helios and make sacrifice to the immortals who hold the wide sky. And if we ever get to Ithaca, our own country, we will build a rich temple to Helios Hyperion at once and put a great many fine offerings in it. And if he is angry at all about his straight-horned cattle, and means to destroy our ship, and the other gods consent, I would rather lose my life once and for all with a gulp at the wave than waste slowly away on a desert island."

So spoke Eurylochus, and the rest of my companions agreed with him. Straight away they drove off the best of the cattle of Helios from close at hand, because the beautiful sleek broad-browed cattle were grazing not far from the dark-prowed ship. They stood round them and prayed to the gods, plucking the tender leaves off a high-crested oak, because they had no white barley aboard the well-benched ship. And when they had prayed and cut the cattle's throats and flayed them, they cut out the thigh-pieces and covered them with a double layer of fat and laid raw flesh on top. They had no wine to pour over the blazing offering, but they made libations with water and roasted all the entrails over the fire.

Now when the thighs were burned up and they had tasted the inner parts, they cut up the rest and spitted it. And then sweet sleep fled from my eyelids, and I went off to the fast ship and the shore of the sea. But when, as I went, I came near the curved ship, the hot savour of the fat drifted round me, and I groaned and cried aloud to the immortal gods:

"Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live for ever, you lulled me in a pitiless sleep to my ruin, and my companions, left behind, have contrived a monstrous thing."

Lampetie of the long robes went quickly to Helios Hyperion with the news that we had killed his cattle; and he spoke at once among the immortals, angry at heart:

"Father Zeus, and you other blessed gods who live for ever, take vengeance now on the companions of Odysseus, son of Laertes, who have insolently killed my cattle, which I always took delight in when I climbed towards the starry sky and when I turned back to earth from the sky again. If they do not pay me a fitting atonement for the cattle, I will go down to Hades and shine among the dead."

Then Zeus the cloud-gatherer answered him and said: "Helios, go on shining among the immortals and among mortal men on the grain-giving earth. As for these men, I will soon strike their fast ship with my bright thunderbolt and smash it to pieces in the middle of the wine-dark sea."

I heard this from fair-haired Calypso, and she said she had heard it herself from the messenger Hermes.

But when I had come down to the ship and the sea I rebuked my men, going up to each one in turn; but we could find no remedy — the cattle were dead already. And the gods showed my men portents at once. The hides crawled; the flesh, both roast and raw, bellowed on the spits; and there was a lowing as of cattle.

For six days my trusty companions feasted on the best of the cattle of Helios that they had driven off. But when Zeus, the son of Cronos, brought the seventh day on us, then the wind stopped blowing in a storm, and we went aboard at once and put out into the broad sea when we had set up the mast and hoisted the white sail.

But when we had left that island and no other land came in sight, but only sky and sea, then the son of Cronos set a black cloud over the hollow ship, and the sea grew dark beneath it. She ran on for no long time, because the shrieking west wind came at once, blowing with a furious storm, and the blast of the wind snapped both the forestays of the mast, so that the mast fell backwards and all its tackle was strewn in the bilge. The mast struck the head of the pilot in the stern of the ship and crushed all the bones of his skull together, and he fell from the deck like a diver, and his proud spirit left his bones. And Zeus thundered and hurled his bolt at the ship, and she shuddered from stem to stern, struck by the bolt of Zeus, and was filled with sulphurous smoke, and my companions fell out of the ship. They were carried on the waves round the black ship like sea-crows, and the god took their homecoming from them.

But I kept pacing up and down the ship until the surge tore the sides away from the keel, and the wave carried her on dismantled and snapped the mast off at the keel. But the backstay, made of ox-hide, had been flung over the mast; with this I lashed the two together, keel and mast, and sat on them and was carried by the deadly winds.

Then the west wind stopped blowing in a storm, and the south wind came quickly, bringing sorrow to my heart, so that I should travel back the way to deadly Charybdis. All night long I was carried, and at sunrise I came to the cliff of Scylla and to dread Charybdis. She was sucking down the salt water of the sea; but I sprang up to the tall fig tree and took hold of it and clung to it like a bat. Yet I could not plant my feet firmly anywhere or climb the tree, because its roots spread far below and its branches hung out of reach above, long and great, overshadowing Charybdis. There I clung steadily until she should vomit the mast and keel out again; and to my joy they came at last. At the hour when a man gets up from the assembly for his supper, a man who settles the many quarrels of young men seeking judgment, at that hour those spars appeared out of Charybdis. And I let go with hands and feet from above and plunged down into the water out beyond the long spars, and sat on them and rowed on with my hands. And as for Scylla, the father of gods and men did not let her catch sight of me again, or I should never have escaped destruction.

From there I was carried for nine days, and on the tenth night the gods brought me to Ogygia, where Calypso of the lovely hair lives, dread goddess with human speech, who welcomed me and looked after me. But why should I tell you this tale? I told it yesterday in your hall to you and to your noble wife. It is a tiresome thing, I think, to tell a plainly told tale over again.

Book Thirteen

So he spoke, and they were all hushed and silent, and were held spellbound through the shadowy halls. And Alcinous answered him again and said:

"Odysseus, since you have come to my high-roofed house with its floor of bronze, I do not think you will be driven back and go home with your purpose baffled, however much you have suffered. And to each man of you who are always used to drinking the flaming wine of the elders in my halls and listening to the singer, I say this, and give this charge. Clothing for the stranger lies stored already in the polished chest, with finely worked gold and all the other gifts the counsellors of the Phaeacians brought here. But come, let us each give him a great tripod and a cauldron; and we in turn will gather the cost from among the people and repay ourselves. It would be hard for one man to give freely without being repaid."

So spoke Alcinous, and his word pleased them. They went off, each man to his house, to rest; but as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, they hurried to the ship and brought the bronze that gives men strength. And the strong and mighty Alcinous went through the ship himself and stowed the gifts carefully under the benches, so that they should not get in the way of any of the crew at their rowing when they worked the oars hard.

Then they went to the house of Alcinous and prepared a feast. And the strong and mighty Alcinous sacrificed a bull for them to Zeus, son of Cronos, god of the dark clouds, who is lord of all. And when they had burned the thigh-pieces they feasted a splendid feast and enjoyed themselves, and among them the divine singer Demodocus sang to the lyre, honoured by the people.

But Odysseus kept turning his head towards the blazing sun, eager to see it set, because he was eager to be gone home. As a man longs for supper when all day long a yoke of wine-dark oxen has drawn the jointed plough for him through fallow land, and the light of the sun sinks gladly for him so that he can get to his supper, and his knees are weary as he goes — so gladly did the light of the sun sink for Odysseus. And at once he spoke among the Phaeacians, who love the oar, and to Alcinous above all he declared his word:

"Lord Alcinous, renowned above all men, pour the offering now and send me on my way in peace; and to yourselves, farewell. Everything my heart wanted has been brought about: a convoy, and friendship gifts. May the gods of heaven make them a blessing to me, and may I find my faultless wife at home when I get back, and those I love unhurt. And may you, staying here, be a joy to your wedded wives and your children; and may the gods grant you prosperity of every kind, and may no trouble come on your people."

So he spoke, and they all praised his words and said the stranger should be sent on his way, since he had spoken properly. Then mighty Alcinous said to the herald:

"Pontonous, mix the bowl and serve wine out to everyone in the hall, so that when we have prayed to father Zeus we may send the stranger off to his own country."

So he spoke, and Pontonous mixed the honey-hearted wine and served it out to all, coming up to each in turn; and they poured offerings to the blessed gods who hold the wide sky, from where they sat. But noble Odysseus got up and put the two-handled cup into Arete's hand, and spoke to her with winged words:

"Farewell, my queen, through all the years, until old age comes, and death, which are the lot of mortals. I am going on my way; but may you have joy in this house of your children and your people and Alcinous the king."

So noble Odysseus spoke and crossed the threshold. And mighty Alcinous sent a herald with him to lead him to the fast ship and the shore of the sea. And Arete sent slave women with him, one carrying a newly washed cloak and a tunic, and another she told to follow carrying the strong chest, and another carried bread and red wine.

But when they had come down to the ship and the sea, the lordly young men of his escort took these things at once and stowed them in the hollow ship, all the food and drink. Then they spread a rug and a linen sheet for Odysseus on the deck of the hollow ship, at the stern, so that he could sleep soundly; and he went aboard too and lay down in silence. Then they sat down at the benches, each in his place, and loosed the hawser from the pierced stone.

And as soon as they leaned back and tossed the salt water with their oar-blades, sweet sleep fell on his eyelids, an unwaking sleep, the sweetest there is and most like death. And as four yoked stallions on a plain spring forward all together under the strokes of the lash, and leaping high finish their course quickly, so the stern of that ship leapt high, and in her wake the dark wave of the loud-sounding sea foamed mightily, and she ran on safely and surely; not even the wheeling hawk, the swiftest of winged things, could have kept pace with her. So she ran on swiftly and cut through the waves of the sea, carrying a man who was the equal of the gods in counsel, one who in time past had suffered a great deal in his heart, passing through the wars of men and the grievous waves; but now he slept in peace, forgetting all he had suffered.

Now when the brightest of the stars rose, which always comes to herald the light of early Dawn, the seafaring ship drew near to the island.

There is a certain harbour of Phorcys in the land of Ithaca, the old man of the sea, and at its mouth two jutting headlands sheer towards the sea but sloping down on the harbour side. These keep back the great waves raised by heavy winds outside, and inside the benched ships lie unmoored when they have reached the anchorage. At the head of the harbour there is a long-leafed olive tree, and near it a pleasant shadowy cave sacred to the nymphs who are called Naiads. Inside it are mixing bowls and jars of stone, and the bees store honey there too. And there are long looms of stone in the cave, where the nymphs weave purple-dyed webs, a wonder to look at; and there are ever-flowing springs in it as well. There are two doors to the cave, one towards the north wind, by which men go down; but the one towards the south wind is sacred, and men do not go in by it; it is the way of the immortals.

Here they rowed in, knowing the place from of old; and the ship ran a full half of her length up the shore in her swift course, at such a pace was she driven by the arms of the rowers. Then they stepped out of the benched ship onto the land, and first they lifted Odysseus out of the hollow ship, with the linen sheet and the bright rug just as they were, and laid him down on the sand, still overcome with sleep. And they lifted out the goods the lordly Phaeacians had given him as he set out for home, through the favour of great-hearted Athena. They set these all together by the trunk of the olive tree, out of the path, in case some traveller should come on them before Odysseus woke and spoil them. Then they went home again themselves.

But the Shaker of the Earth did not forget the threats he had first made against godlike Odysseus, and he asked what the purpose of Zeus was:

"Father Zeus, I shall not be held in honour among the immortal gods any longer, seeing that mortals do not honour me in the least — the Phaeacians, who as you know are of my own line. I said just now that Odysseus should suffer a great deal before he got home, though I did not rob him of his homecoming altogether, once you had promised it and confirmed it with your nod. And these men have carried him in his sleep over the sea in a fast ship and set him down in Ithaca, and given him gifts past telling, stores of bronze and gold and woven clothing, more than Odysseus would ever have brought back from Troy if he had come home unhurt with his fair share of the plunder."

Then Zeus the cloud-gatherer answered him and said: "What a thing to say, shaker of the earth, wide of sway. The gods do you no dishonour; it would be a hard business to attack our eldest and best with dishonour. But as for men, if anyone gives way to his own strength and force and fails to honour you in anything, you may always take your revenge, even afterwards. Do as you like, and as pleases you."

Then Poseidon the earth-shaker answered him: "I would have done as you say at once, god of the dark clouds, but I always dread your anger and avoid it. Now I mean to strike the fine ship of the Phaeacians as she comes back from her convoy over the misty water, so that they may hold off and stop giving convoy to men, and to throw a great mountain round their city."

Then Zeus the cloud-gatherer answered him and said: "Lazy one, this is what seems best to me. When all the people are looking out from the city at her as she speeds on her way, then turn her to stone close by the land — a stone in the shape of a fast ship, so that everyone may marvel; and throw a great mountain round their city."

Now when Poseidon the earth-shaker heard this he went off to Scheria, where the Phaeacians live, and waited there. And she drew close to shore, the seafaring ship, speeding swiftly on her way. Then the Earth-shaker came up to her and turned her to stone and rooted her fast underneath with a blow of the flat of his hand, and then he was gone.

And they spoke winged words to one another, the long-oared Phaeacians, men famous for their ships; and one would say, with a glance at his neighbour: "Who has fastened our fast ship on the sea like this on her way home? She was in plain sight."

That is how one of them would speak; but they did not know how these things were to go. Then Alcinous spoke to the company and said:

"Now the oracles of my father, spoken long ago, have come on me. He used to say that Poseidon was angry with us because we give safe convoy to everyone. He said that one day, as a beautiful ship of the Phaeacians was coming home from a convoy over the misty water, Poseidon would strike her, and would throw a great mountain round our town. That is what the old man said, and now all of it is coming to pass. But come, do as I say, all of you. Stop giving convoy to mortals, when anyone comes to our city, and let us sacrifice twelve choice bulls to Poseidon, in case he takes pity and does not throw a high mountain round our town."

So he spoke, and they were seized with fear and got the bulls ready.

So they were praying to the lord Poseidon, the leaders and counsellors of the land of the Phaeacians, standing round the altar; and Odysseus woke out of his sleep in his own country. But he did not know it after his long absence, because the goddess had poured a mist round him, Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus, so that she might make him unrecognisable and tell him everything, so that his wife should not know him, nor his townspeople, nor his friends, until the suitors had paid the full price for all their wrongdoing. So everything looked strange to their lord — the long paths, the bays offering safe anchorage, the sheer cliffs, and the thriving trees.

So he sprang up and stood and looked at his own country, and then he groaned and struck both his thighs with the flat of his hands, and said mournfully:

"What country have I come to now? What kind of people? Are they cruel and wild and unjust, or do they welcome strangers, and do their minds fear the gods? Where shall I take all this wealth, and where shall I go wandering myself? I wish I had stayed there among the Phaeacians, and had gone on to some other of the mighty kings, who would have entertained me and sent me on my way home. As it is I do not know where to put this wealth; and I will not leave it here, in case it becomes somebody else's plunder to my cost. It is a disgrace: they were not altogether wise or just, it seems, the leaders and counsellors of the Phaeacians, who have brought me to a strange country. They said they would bring me to clear-seen Ithaca, and they have not made their word good. May Zeus, the suppliant's god, pay them for it, who watches over all men and punishes anyone who sins. But come, I will count the goods and go over them, in case these men have carried something off with them in the hollow ship, to my cost."

So he spoke, and set about counting the beautiful tripods and the cauldrons and the gold and the finely woven clothing; and he missed nothing of them. Then, longing mournfully for his own country, he walked along the shore of the loud-sounding sea, with many a groan.

And Athena came up to him in the form of a young man, a shepherd of sheep, a delicate one, of the kind the sons of princes are. She wore a well-made cloak in a double fold about her shoulders, and had sandals under her shining feet, and a spear in her hands. Odysseus was glad at the sight of her and came to meet her, and spoke to her with winged words:

"Friend, since you are the first person I have come to in this country, greetings; and I hope you do not meet me with any evil in your mind. Save this treasure, and save me. I pray to you as to a god, and I have come to your dear knees. And tell me this too, truly, so that I know for certain: what country is this, and what people? What men live here? Is it some clear-seen island, or a shore of the deep-soiled mainland that lies resting on the sea?"

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "You are a fool, stranger, or you have come from far away, if you really have to ask about this country. It is not nameless at all; a great many people know it, both all those who live towards the dawn and the sun, and all those behind, towards the murky dark. It is a rugged island, not fit for driving horses, and yet it is not altogether poor, though it is narrow. Corn grows in it beyond measure, and the wine-grape too, and it never lacks rain or rich dew. It is a good land for pasturing goats and cattle; there are trees of every kind in it, and pools for watering that do not fail the year round. So the name of Ithaca, stranger, has reached even to the land of Troy, which they say is far from this country of Achaea."

So she spoke, and much-enduring noble Odysseus was glad, and rejoiced in his country, the land of his fathers, hearing the word of Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus who holds the aegis. And he spoke to her with winged words — and did not speak the truth, but checked the word before it was out, always turning over thoughts of great cunning in his breast:

"I heard of Ithaca even in broad Crete, far over the sea; and now I have come here myself with these goods of mine. I left as much again with my children when I fled the country, after I had killed the dear son of Idomeneus, Orsilochus, swift of foot, who in broad Crete outran all the men who live by their labour. He would have robbed me of all that plunder from Troy which I had suffered so much heartache for, passing through the wars of men and the grievous waves — because I would not do his father a favour and serve as his squire in the land of the Trojans, but commanded men of my own. So I struck him with my bronze-tipped spear as he came home from the field, lying in wait for him by the roadside with one of my men. A dark night covered the sky and nobody noticed us, and I took his life unseen.

"Now when I had killed him with the sharp bronze I went straight to a ship and made my plea to some lordly Phoenicians, giving them plunder enough to satisfy them. I told them to take me aboard and land me at Pylos, or at fine Elis, where the Epeans hold sway. But the force of the wind thrust them away from there, badly against their will, and they did not mean to play me false; and driven off our course we came here by night. We rowed on into the harbour in a hurry, and had no thought of supper, badly as we needed it, but went out of the ship just as we were and lay down, every one of us. Then sweet sleep came over me in my weariness, and they took my goods out of the hollow ship and set them where I was lying on the sand. And they went aboard and left for the well-peopled land of Sidon; and I was left here with a badly troubled heart."

So he spoke, and the goddess grey-eyed Athena smiled and stroked him with her hand, and changed herself into the form of a woman, handsome and tall and skilled in glorious handiwork. And she spoke to him with winged words:

"He would have to be cunning and a thief who could get past you in any kind of guile, even if it were a god who met you. Bold man, crafty in counsel, never satisfied with deceiving — not even in your own country, it seems, were you going to give up your tricks and your lying tales, which you love from the bottom of your heart. But come, let us talk no more of this; we are both well versed in craft, since you are far the best of all men in counsel and in speech, and I am famous among all the gods for wisdom and craft. And yet you did not know Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus — me, who always stand at your side and guard you in every trouble. And it was I who made you loved by all the Phaeacians.

"And now I have come here to weave a plan with you, and to hide all the treasure the lordly Phaeacians gave you by my counsel and will when you set out for home; and to tell you the full measure of trouble it is your fate to go through in your own well-built house. Be strong; you must bear it. And tell no man of them all, and no woman, that you have come back from your wanderings, but endure your many griefs in silence, and put up with the violence of men."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "It is hard for a mortal man to know you when he meets you, goddess, however clever he is, because you take whatever shape you like. But this I know well, that you were kind to me in the old days, as long as we sons of the Achaeans were fighting in the land of Troy. But after we had sacked the high city of Priam and gone away in our ships, and a god had scattered the Achaeans, I have never seen you since then, daughter of Zeus, and never noticed you coming aboard my ship to keep sorrow off me. I wandered on and on with a stricken heart in my breast, until the gods delivered me from trouble, until in the rich country of the Phaeacians you cheered me with your words and led me into their city yourself. But now I beg you by your father — because I do not think I have come to clear-seen Ithaca; this is some other country I am roaming in, and you are saying this to mock me and beguile my mind — tell me whether I really have come to my own dear country."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "That is always the way your mind works, and that is why I cannot leave you in your sorrow: because you are soft of speech, sharp-witted and prudent. Any other man coming home from his wanderings would have hurried eagerly to see his children and his wife in his halls; but you are not yet willing to know or learn anything until you have tested your wife, who stays in her halls as she always did, and the nights and the days waste away for her sorrowfully as she weeps.

"As for me, I never doubted this, but knew it well in my heart, that you would come home after losing all your companions. But you must understand, I was not willing to fight against Poseidon, my father's brother, who stored up anger in his heart against you, furious that you blinded his dear son. But come, I will show you the land of Ithaca, so that you can be sure. This is the harbour of Phorcys, the old man of the sea, and here at the head of the harbour is the long-leafed olive tree, and near it the pleasant shadowy cave sacred to the nymphs who are called Naiads. This is the vaulted cave where you used to offer the nymphs many hecatombs that bring fulfilment; and over there is Mount Neriton, clothed with its forests."

So spoke the goddess, and scattered the mist, and the land appeared. Then much-enduring noble Odysseus was glad, rejoicing in his own country, and he kissed the earth, the giver of grain. And at once he prayed to the nymphs with his hands stretched out:

"Naiad Nymphs, daughters of Zeus, I never thought to see you again; but now I greet you with loving prayers. And I will give you gifts too, as I used to, if the daughter of Zeus, who drives the spoil, graciously grants me to live, and brings my dear son to manhood."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him again: "Take heart, and do not let these things trouble your mind. But let us put your goods away at once in the innermost recess of the marvellous cave, where they will stay safe for you; and let us think ourselves how it may all turn out for the best."

With that the goddess went into the shadowy cave and searched out its hiding places. And Odysseus brought all the treasure there, the gold and the stubborn bronze and the finely worked clothing which the Phaeacians had given him. He laid them carefully away, and Pallas Athena, daughter of Zeus who holds the aegis, set a stone at the door.

Then the two of them sat down by the trunk of the sacred olive tree and planned death for the insolent suitors. And the goddess grey-eyed Athena was the first to speak:

"Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, think how you may lay your hands on the shameless suitors, who for three years now have been lording it in your halls, courting your godlike wife and offering wedding gifts. And she, looking mournfully for your coming, holds out hope to all of them and makes promises to each man, sending them messages, when her mind is on something else."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "I was very near dying in my halls by the evil fate of Agamemnon, son of Atreus, if you had not told me all of it properly, goddess. But come, weave some plan by which I can pay them back; and stand beside me yourself and put dauntless courage into me, as when we loosed the bright diadem of Troy. If you would stand at my side, grey-eyed one, as eager as you were then, I would fight three hundred men with you, mighty goddess, if you would give me help with a ready heart."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "I will be with you, and I will not forget you when we are busy with this work; and I think many a one of the suitors who eat up your substance will spatter the wide earth with his blood and brains. But come, I will make you unrecognisable to everyone. I will shrivel the fine skin on your supple limbs, and destroy the flaxen hair on your head, and dress you in a rag that anyone would shudder to see a man wearing. And I will dim your two eyes, that were so beautiful before, so that you will look mean in the sight of all the suitors, and of your wife, and of the son you left in your halls.

"And you must go first of all to the swineherd who keeps your pigs, and who has a kindly heart towards you, and loves your son and steady Penelope. You will find him staying with the pigs, and they are feeding by the rock of Corax and the spring Arethusa, eating acorns to their hearts' content and drinking the black water, the things that make the rich flesh of pigs grow fat. Stay there, and sit beside him and question him about everything, while I go to Sparta, the land of beautiful women, to call your dear son Telemachus back, Odysseus, who went to spacious Lacedaemon, to the house of Menelaus, to look for news of you, and whether you were still alive anywhere."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her: "Why did you not tell him, then, when your mind knows everything? Was it so that he too should suffer, wandering over the restless sea, while others ate up his substance?"

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Do not let your heart be too troubled about him. I guided him myself, so that he should win a good name by going there; and he has no hardship, but sits in peace in the palace of the son of Atreus, with good things past telling in front of him. There are young men lying in wait for him in a black ship, eager to kill him before he comes to his own country; but I do not think that will happen. Before that the earth will cover many a one of the suitors who eat up your substance."

With that Athena touched him with her wand. She withered the fine flesh on his supple limbs and destroyed the flaxen hair on his head, and put the skin of an old man over all his limbs. And she dimmed his two eyes, that were so beautiful before, and dressed him in other clothes, a vile ragged cloak and a tunic, torn and filthy, grimed with dirty smoke. And she threw the great skin of a swift hind over him, with the hair stripped off, and gave him a staff and a miserable bag full of holes, slung on a twisted cord.

So when the two of them had taken counsel together they parted; and the goddess went off to lovely Lacedaemon to fetch the son of Odysseus.

Book Fourteen

But Odysseus went up from the harbour by the rough path, over the woodland and through the heights, to the place where Athena had shown him he would find the good swineherd, who looked after his property better than any of the slaves noble Odysseus had got.

He found him sitting in the front porch of his house, where his yard was built high in a place with a wide view, a great fine yard with open ground round it. The swineherd had built it himself for the pigs of his master, who was gone, without the knowledge of his mistress or of the old man Laertes. He had built it with huge stones and set a coping of thorn on it. Outside he had driven stakes the whole length, this way and that, huge stakes set close together, which he had made by splitting an oak down to the black core. And inside the yard he had made twelve sties close to one another as beds for the pigs, and fifty wallowing sows were penned in each of them, breeding females; but the boars slept outside. They were far fewer in number, because the godlike suitors were feasting on them and thinning them out, since the swineherd always sent in the best of all the fatted hogs, which came to three hundred and sixty. And four dogs, savage as wild beasts, always slept beside them, which the swineherd had reared, a leader of men.

He was fitting boots round his feet, cutting an ox-hide of good colour, while the others had gone off, three of them, one here and one there, with the droves of pigs; and the fourth he had sent to the city to drive a boar for the insolent suitors, because he had to, so that they could kill it and satisfy themselves with meat.

Suddenly the baying hounds caught sight of Odysseus and rushed at him barking loudly; but Odysseus sat down, being cunning, and the staff dropped out of his hand. And then he would have suffered a cruel hurt on his own farm, but the swineherd came running after them with quick steps and hurried out through the gateway, and the hide fell out of his hand. He shouted at the dogs and drove them this way and that with a shower of stones, and said to his master:

"Old man, the dogs nearly tore you to pieces all of a sudden, and you would have poured reproach on me. And the gods have given me other griefs and sorrows already. Here I sit mourning and grieving for a godlike master, and rearing fat pigs for other men to eat, while he, it may be, wants for food and wanders over the land and the city of men of a strange language, if he is still alive at all and sees the light of the sun. But come with me, old man; let us go to the hut, so that when you have satisfied yourself with food and wine you can tell me where you are from and all the troubles you have gone through."

With that the good swineherd led him to the hut and brought him in and sat him down, spreading thick brushwood underneath and laying the skin of a shaggy wild goat over it, big and hairy, the one he slept on himself. Odysseus was glad that he gave him such a welcome, and said to him:

"Stranger, may Zeus and the other immortal gods grant you whatever you want most, since you have given me such a ready welcome."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Stranger, it would not be right for me to slight a stranger, not even if one came who was worse off than you are. All strangers and beggars come from Zeus, and a gift is welcome from people like us even when it is small; because this is the lot of slaves, always in fear when the masters who hold sway over them are young masters, as ours are. For the gods have blocked the homecoming of the man who would have loved me with real kindness and given me possessions of my own, a house and a bit of land and a wife whom many wanted — the kind of thing a kind master gives to a slave who has laboured hard for him, and whose work a god has made prosper, as this work of mine prospers, which I give my mind to. So my master would have rewarded me richly if he had grown old here at home. But he died — and I wish all the family of Helen had died in utter ruin, since she loosened the knees of so many fighting men. For he too went to Ilios, famous for horses, to win recompense for Agamemnon, so that he could fight the Trojans."

With that he quickly bound his tunic up with his belt and went off to the sties where the tribes of pigs were penned. He chose two out of them and brought them in and killed them both, and singed them and cut them up and spitted them. Then, when he had roasted it all, he brought it and set it in front of Odysseus, hot on the spits, and sprinkled white barley meal over it. Then he mixed honey-sweet wine in a bowl of ivy wood, and sat down opposite Odysseus himself, and invited him to eat.

"Eat now, stranger, the kind of food slaves have to offer, the meat of young pigs; the fatted hogs are eaten by the suitors, who take no thought in their hearts of the anger of the gods, and have no pity. The blessed gods do not love reckless doings; they honour justice and the righteous deeds of men. Even cruel enemies who set foot on another people's land, when Zeus gives them plunder and they fill their ships and go home — even on the hearts of those a great fear of the gods' anger falls. But these men here know something, and have heard some voice of a god about my master's pitiful death, seeing that they will not court righteously or go back to their own homes, but waste our substance at their ease in an insolent way, and there is no sparing. Every day and night that comes from Zeus they sacrifice, and not one victim or two either; and they draw the wine off and waste it insolently.

"His substance was great past telling. No lord has so much, either on the dark mainland or in Ithaca itself; not twenty men together have wealth as great. I will tell you the reckoning of it. Twelve herds of cattle he has on the mainland; as many flocks of sheep; as many droves of pigs; as many packed herds of goats, pastured by herdsmen, both foreigners and men of his own. And here too roving herds of goats graze on the edges of the island, eleven of them, and trusty men keep watch over them. And each of these men drives up one of his flock for the suitors every day, whichever of the fatted goats seems the best to him. And I guard and keep these pigs, and choose out the best of the boars carefully and send them in."

So he spoke, and Odysseus ate the meat eagerly and drank the wine, greedily, in silence, and was sowing the seeds of trouble for the suitors. But when he had dined and satisfied himself with food, the swineherd filled the bowl he drank from himself and gave it him brim full of wine, and he took it and was glad at heart; and he spoke to him with winged words:

"Friend, who was it who bought you with his wealth, a man so very rich and powerful, as you tell it? You said he died to win recompense for Agamemnon; tell me, in case I happen to know a man like that. Zeus and the other immortal gods know whether I have seen him and could bring you news; I have wandered a long way."

Then the swineherd, a leader of men, answered him: "Old man, no wanderer who came here bringing news of him could persuade his wife and his dear son. Vagabonds who need entertainment lie at random, and have no wish to speak the truth. Anyone who comes to the land of Ithaca in his wanderings goes to my mistress and tells her a lying tale. And she receives him kindly and entertains him and questions him about everything, and the tears fall from her eyelids as she weeps, the way a woman does when her husband has died far away. And you would soon make up a story yourself, old man, if somebody would give you a cloak and a tunic for clothes. But as for him, by now the dogs and the swift birds have most likely torn the flesh off his bones and his spirit has left him; or the fishes have eaten him in the sea, and his bones lie there on the shore wrapped in deep sand. So he died out there, and grief is appointed for his friends in the days to come, for all of them, and for me most of all. I shall never find so kind a master again, however far I go, not if I came back to the house of my father and mother, where I was born in the first place and they brought me up themselves. And it is not for them that I mourn so much now, eager as I am to see them with my eyes and to be in my own country; it is the longing for Odysseus, who is gone, that takes hold of me. I speak his name with awe, stranger, absent as he is, because he loved me greatly and cared for me at heart; but I call him my lord and my beloved, for all that he is not here."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "Friend, since you deny it altogether and say he will never come again, and your heart is always unbelieving, I will tell you — not at random, but with an oath — that Odysseus is coming back. And let me have a reward for bringing good news as soon as he comes and reaches his home: dress me in a cloak and a tunic, good clothes. Before that I will accept nothing, however badly off I am; because that man is as hateful to me as the gates of Hades who gives way to the pressure of poverty and tells a lying tale. Let Zeus be my witness above all gods, and this hospitable table, and the hearth of noble Odysseus that I have come to, that everything will happen exactly as I tell you. In the course of this very same year Odysseus will come here, as the old moon wanes and the new appears. He will come back, and take his revenge on all those who dishonour his wife and his glorious son here."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Old man, I do not think I shall be paying you this reward for good news, and Odysseus will never come home. Drink in peace, and let us turn our minds to something else, and do not bring this back to my mind; because the heart in my breast is grieved whenever anyone mentions my good master. As for your oath, let it be; but I wish Odysseus would come, as I want him to, and Penelope, and the old man Laertes, and godlike Telemachus.

"But now it is for his son that I grieve without stopping, for Telemachus, whom Odysseus fathered. When the gods had made him grow like a sapling, and I thought he would be no worse a man than his dear father, glorious in shape and looks, then one of the immortals damaged the good sense inside him, or some man did, and he went off to sacred Pylos after news of his father. And now the lordly suitors lie in wait for him on his way home, so that the line of godlike Arceisius may die out of Ithaca and leave no name. But let him be; he may be taken or he may get away, and the son of Cronos may stretch out his hand to guard him.

"But come, old man, tell me your own sorrows, and tell me the truth, so that I know for certain. Who are you among men, and where from? Where is your city, and where are your parents? What sort of ship did you come on, and how did sailors bring you to Ithaca? Who did they say they were? Because you certainly did not get here on foot."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Then I will tell you all of it plainly. I wish the two of us had food and sweet wine for a good while, to feast on quietly here in your hut, with others going about their work; then I could easily talk for a whole year and still not finish the tale of the sorrows of my spirit, all the labours I have gone through by the will of the gods.

"I declare that I come by descent from broad Crete, the son of a wealthy man. And a great many other sons were born and brought up in his halls, true sons of a lawful wife; but the mother who bore me was bought, a concubine. Yet Castor, son of Hylax, whom I say I am sprung from, honoured me the same as his true-born sons. He was honoured like a god among the Cretans in that country in those days, for his good estate and his wealth and his glorious sons. But the fates of death carried him off to the house of Hades, and his proud sons divided his substance among them and cast lots for it. To me they gave a very small portion, and allotted me a dwelling. But I took a wife from a house with wide possessions, winning her by my own courage; because I was no weakling and no coward in a fight. All that strength is gone now; and yet even so, seeing the stubble, I think you can judge what the grain was; because troubles surround me in full measure.

"But in those days Ares and Athena gave me courage, and strength that breaks the ranks of men; and whenever I picked the best fighters for an ambush, sowing the seeds of trouble for the enemy, my proud spirit never foresaw death, but I would always leap out far ahead of the others and kill with my spear whichever of the enemy gave way in flight in front of me. That was the kind of man I was in war; but work in the fields was never to my liking, or the care of a household that rears fine children. Oared ships were always dear to me, and wars, and polished spears, and arrows — grievous things, which other men shudder at. But those were the things dear to me, which a god must have put in my heart; different men take joy in different work. Before the sons of the Achaeans set foot on the land of Troy I had nine times led fighting men and fast-going ships against foreign peoples, and great plunder had always fallen to me. I would choose what pleased my mind out of it, and got a great deal more afterwards by lot. So my house grew rich at once, and after that I became a man feared and honoured among the Cretans.

"But when Zeus, whose voice carries far, contrived that hateful journey which loosened the knees of so many fighting men, then they told me and glorious Idomeneus to lead the ships to Ilios, and there was no way of refusing, because the voice of the people pressed hard on us. There for nine years we sons of the Achaeans made war, and in the tenth we sacked the city of Priam and set out for home in our ships, and a god scattered the Achaeans.

"But for me, wretch that I am, Zeus the counsellor contrived trouble. I stayed only a month, taking joy in my children and my wedded wife and my wealth; and then my spirit told me to sail to Egypt with my godlike companions, when I had fitted my ships out carefully. I fitted out nine ships, and the crews gathered quickly. For six days my trusty companions feasted, and I gave them a great many victims so that they could sacrifice to the gods and prepare a feast for themselves; and on the seventh we embarked and set sail from broad Crete, with the north wind blowing fresh and fair, and ran on as easily as if we were going downstream. No harm came to any of my ships, but we sat there free of injury and of sickness, and the wind and the helmsmen guided the ships.

"On the fifth day we came to the fair-flowing Aegyptus, and I moored my curved ships in the river Aegyptus. Then I told my trusty companions to stay there by the ships and guard the ships, and I sent scouts out to go to places of outlook. But my companions gave way to their own wantonness, and, led on by their own strength, set about wasting the fine fields of the men of Egypt straight away; and they carried off the women and the little children and killed the men; and the cry went up to the city quickly.

"Then, hearing the shouting, the people came out at daybreak, and the whole plain was filled with foot-soldiers and chariots and the flashing of bronze. And Zeus, who hurls the thunderbolt, threw an evil panic on my companions, and not one of them had the courage to hold his ground and face the enemy; trouble surrounded us on every side. So they killed a great many of us with the sharp bronze, and led others up to their city alive, to work for them because they had to. But Zeus himself put this thought in my heart — I wish I had died and met my fate there in Egypt instead, because sorrow was still waiting to welcome me. Straight away I took my well-made helmet off my head, and the shield off my shoulders, and let the spear fall out of my hand, and went towards the king's chariot horses. I took hold of his knees and kissed them, and he saved me and took pity on me, and set me in his chariot and took me home weeping. A great many of them rushed at me with their ash spears, eager to kill me, because they were furious; but he kept them off, and had regard for the anger of Zeus, the stranger's god, who more than anyone is indignant at evil deeds.

"There I stayed seven years, and gathered a great deal of wealth among the Egyptians, because everyone gave me gifts. But when the eighth year came round in its circle, then a man of Phoenicia came, well versed in guile, a greedy rogue who had already done a great deal of harm among men. He talked me round with his cunning and took me with him, until we reached Phoenicia, where his house and his possessions were. There I stayed with him for a whole year. But when at last the months and the days were coming to fulfilment, as the year rolled round and the seasons came on, he put me on a seafaring ship bound for Libya, giving me lying advice that I should carry a cargo with him, but in truth so that when we got there he could sell me and get a huge price.

"So I went aboard the ship with him, suspecting his guile but having no choice. And she ran before the north wind, blowing fresh and fair, on a mid-sea course to windward of Crete; and Zeus contrived destruction for the men. But when we had left Crete and no other land appeared, but only sky and sea, then the son of Cronos set a black cloud over the hollow ship, and the sea grew dark beneath it. And Zeus thundered and hurled his bolt at the ship, and she shuddered from stem to stern, struck by the bolt of Zeus, and was filled with sulphurous smoke, and the whole crew fell out of the ship. They were carried on the waves round the black ship like sea-crows, and the god took their homecoming from them.

"But as for me, Zeus himself, when my heart was hemmed in with trouble, put the tossing mast of the dark-prowed ship into my hands, so that I might escape destruction again. I clung round it and was carried by the deadly winds. For nine days I was carried, and on the tenth black night the great rolling wave brought me to the land of the Thesprotians. There Pheidon, the king of the Thesprotians, took me in and asked no ransom, because his dear son came on me when I was overcome with cold and weariness and raised me up by the hand and led me until he came to his father's palace; and he dressed me in a cloak and a tunic for clothes.

"There I learned of Odysseus, because the king said he had entertained him and given him welcome on his way to his own country. And he showed me all the treasure Odysseus had gathered, bronze and gold and iron worked with labour; it would feed his children after him to the tenth generation, so great was the wealth lying stored for him in the king's halls. But Odysseus, he said, had gone to Dodona, to hear the will of Zeus from the high-crested oak of the god, and how he might come back to the rich country of Ithaca after so long an absence, openly or in secret. And besides that he swore in my own presence, as he poured offerings in his house, that the ship was launched and the men ready who were to convey him to his own dear country.

"But he sent me off first, because a ship of the Thesprotians happened to be setting out for Dulichium, rich in wheat. He told them to convey me there with kindly care, to king Acastus. But an evil plan about me found favour in their hearts, so that I should be brought into utter misery even yet. When the seafaring ship had sailed far from the land, they set about bringing on the day of slavery for me. They stripped me of my clothes, my cloak and tunic, and dressed me in other clothes, a vile ragged cloak and tunic, the tattered things you see in front of your eyes. And in the evening they reached the tilled fields of clear-seen Ithaca. Then they bound me fast in the benched ship with a twisted rope, and went ashore themselves and hurried to take their supper on the shore of the sea.

"But the gods themselves undid my bonds easily enough, and I wrapped the tattered cloak round my head and slid down the smooth loading-plank and brought my breast to the sea, and then struck out with both hands and swam, and was very soon out of the water and away from them. Then I went up to a place where there was a thicket of leafy wood and lay there crouching. And they went this way and that with loud cries; but as there seemed no profit in going any further with the search, they went back aboard their hollow ship. And the gods themselves hid me easily and led me and brought me to the farm of a wise man; because it seems it is still my lot to live."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Poor stranger, you have stirred my heart deeply, telling the whole tale of your suffering and your wanderings. But in this you have not spoken rightly, and you will not persuade me with your tale about Odysseus. Why should a man in your state lie for no purpose? I know well enough myself about my master's homecoming: he was hated utterly by all the gods, in that they did not kill him among the Trojans, or in the arms of his friends, when he had wound up the whole skein of the war. Then the whole army of the Achaeans would have made him a tomb, and he would have won great glory for his son as well in the days to come. As it is, the spirits of the storm have swept him away and left no news.

"For my part I live off by myself with the pigs, and do not go to the city, unless wise Penelope calls me there when news comes to her from somewhere. Then men sit round the one who has come and question him closely, both those who grieve for their lord, who has long been gone, and those who are glad, as they eat up his substance without paying for it. But I have no wish to ask or enquire, since the time when an Aetolian took me in with his story — a man who had killed somebody, and came to my house after wandering over the wide earth, and I gave him a kindly welcome. He said he had seen Odysseus among the Cretans, at the house of Idomeneus, mending his ships which storms had shattered. And he said he would come either by summer or by harvest time, bringing a great deal of treasure along with his godlike companions. So you too, old man of many sorrows, since a god has brought you to me — do not try to win my favour with lies, or to cajole me in any way. It is not for that that I shall show you respect or kindness, but out of fear of Zeus, the stranger's god, and out of pity for you."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "You have a heart in your breast that is slow to believe, seeing that even with an oath I have not won you or persuaded you. But come, let us make an agreement, and the gods who hold Olympus shall be witnesses for us both in time to come. If your master comes back to this house, dress me in a cloak and a tunic for clothes and send me on my way to Dulichium, where I want to be. And if your master does not come as I say, set the slaves on me and throw me down from a great cliff, so that the next beggar takes care about deceiving people."

And the good swineherd answered him and said: "Yes, stranger — that would win me fine fame and prosperity among men, now and hereafter, if I first brought you to my hut and gave you hospitality and then killed you and took away your dear life. I should have a ready heart for praying to Zeus, son of Cronos, after that. But it is supper time now, and I hope my companions come soon, so that we can get a good supper ready in the hut."

So they talked to each other; and the pigs and the swineherds came up. They shut the sows in to sleep in their usual sties, and an extraordinary noise rose from them as they were penned in. Then the good swineherd called to his companions:

"Bring out the best of the boars, so that I can kill him for the stranger who comes from far away; and we shall have some profit from it ourselves, who have long borne labour and suffering for the sake of the white-tusked pigs, while others eat up our work without paying for it."

With that he split wood with the pitiless bronze, and the others brought in a fatted boar of five years old and set him by the hearth. And the swineherd did not forget the immortals, because he had an understanding heart, but as a first offering he threw bristles from the head of the white-tusked boar into the fire and prayed to all the gods that wise Odysseus might come back to his own house. Then he raised himself up and struck the boar with a billet of oak he had left over from splitting the wood, and the life left him. And the others cut his throat and singed him and cut him up quickly, and the swineherd took bits of raw flesh from all the limbs as first offerings and laid them in the rich fat. These he threw into the fire when he had sprinkled them with barley meal; and the rest they cut up and spitted and roasted carefully, and drew it all off the spits and threw it in a heap on the platters.

Then the swineherd stood up to carve, because his heart knew well what was fair, and he cut up the meat and divided it into seven portions. One he set aside with a prayer for the nymphs and for Hermes, son of Maia, and the rest he handed round to each man. And he honoured Odysseus with the long chine of the white-tusked boar, and gladdened his master's heart; and Odysseus of the many tricks said to him:

"Eumaeus, may you be as dear to father Zeus as you are to me, since you honour me with a good portion, in the state I am in."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Eat, unhappy stranger, and enjoy such fare as there is here. It is the god who will give one thing and hold back another, whatever seems good to his heart; because he can do anything."

He spoke, and sacrificed the first pieces to the gods who live for ever; and when he had poured offerings of the flaming wine, he put the cup into the hands of Odysseus, the sacker of cities, and sat down by his own portion. And bread was served to them by Mesaulius, whom the swineherd had got by himself while his master was away, without the knowledge of his mistress or of old Laertes, buying him from the Taphians with his own goods. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them. And when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, Mesaulius took the food away, and they were ready to go to bed, full of bread and meat.

Now the night came on, foul and moonless, and Zeus rained the whole night through, and the west wind, always the rainy wind, blew hard. Then Odysseus spoke among them, testing the swineherd, to see whether he would take off his own cloak and give it to him, or tell one of his companions to do it, since he cared for him so much:

"Listen to me now, Eumaeus, and all the rest of you, his men. There is a wish in my heart, and I will tell a tale; the wine tells me to, that fooling wine, which sets a man singing and laughing softly however wise he is, and makes him get up and dance, and brings out a word that would be better unsaid. Still, since I have spoken out once, I will hide nothing. I wish I were young and my strength firm, as when we made our ambush ready and led it up under the walls of Troy. The leaders were Odysseus and Menelaus, son of Atreus, and I was third in command with them; that was how they arranged it themselves.

"Now when we had come to the city and the steep wall, we lay round the town in the thick brushwood, among the reeds and the swamp land, crouching under our arms; and night came on, foul, when the north wind had dropped, and frosty, and snow came down on us from above, covering us like rime, bitterly cold, and ice formed on our shields. All the rest had cloaks and tunics and slept in peace, with their shields covering their shoulders; but I had left my cloak behind with my companions when I set out, in my folly, because I did not think I would be cold even so, and had come with only my shield and my bright kilt.

"But at the third watch of the night, when the stars had turned their course, I spoke to Odysseus, who was near me, nudging him with my elbow; and he listened at once. 'Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, I shall not be among the living much longer. The cold is killing me; I have no cloak. Some god beguiled me into wearing only my tunic, and now there is no way out.'

"So I spoke, and he thought up this plan in his heart — such a man he was both at planning and at fighting — and he said to me in a low voice: 'Be quiet now, in case one of the other Achaeans hears you.'

"With that he raised his head on his elbow and said: 'Listen, friends. A dream from the gods came to me in my sleep. We have come very far from the ships, and I wish there were somebody to take word to Agamemnon, son of Atreus, shepherd of the army, in the hope that he might order more men to come from the ships.'

"So he spoke, and Thoas, son of Andraemon, sprang up quickly and flung his purple cloak off and set out to run to the ships. And I lay gladly in his garment, and golden-throned Dawn appeared. I wish I were as young as I was then, and my strength as firm; then one of the swineherds on the farm would give me a cloak, both out of kindness and out of respect for a brave fighting man. As it is they look down on me, since I have foul clothes on me."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Old man, the tale you have told is a good one, and you have said nothing amiss or unprofitable so far. So you shall not lack clothing or anything else a hard-pressed suppliant should get when he meets somebody — for this night at least; but in the morning you will shake those rags of yours about you again.
