There are not many cloaks here or changes of tunics to put on; each man has one only. But when the dear son of Odysseus comes, he will give you a cloak and a tunic for clothes himself, and will send you wherever your heart and spirit tell you to go."

With that he sprang up and put a bed for Odysseus near the fire and threw skins of sheep and goats on it. Odysseus lay down there, and the swineherd threw a great thick cloak over him, which he kept at hand for a change of clothes whenever a terrible storm should come on.

So Odysseus slept there, and the young men slept beside him. But the swineherd did not like a bed in that place, where he would be lying away from the boars, so he got ready to go outside. And Odysseus was glad that he took such care of his master's property while he was away. First Eumaeus flung his sharp sword over his strong shoulders, and then put on a cloak, very thick, to keep the wind off; and he picked up the fleece of a large well-fatted goat and took a sharp javelin to keep off dogs and men, and went out to lie down and sleep where the white-tusked boars slept, under a hollow rock in a place sheltered from the north wind.

Book Fifteen

But Pallas Athena went to spacious Lacedaemon to remind the glorious son of great-hearted Odysseus about his homecoming and to hurry him on his way. She found Telemachus and the noble son of Nestor lying in the front porch of the palace of glorious Menelaus. Nestor's son was overcome with soft sleep, but sweet sleep did not hold Telemachus: all through the immortal night anxious thoughts about his father kept him awake. And grey-eyed Athena stood near him and said:

"Telemachus, you do wrong to wander any longer far from home, leaving your wealth behind you and men in your house as insolent as those — in case they divide it all up and eat your possessions, and you have made your journey for nothing. Rouse Menelaus, good at the war-cry, as fast as you can, to send you on your way, so that you may find your noble mother still at home. Her father and her brothers are telling her now to marry Eurymachus, because he is ahead of all the suitors in his presents and has increased his wedding gifts. Take care she does not carry some treasure out of your halls against your will. You know what sort of spirit there is in a woman's breast: she wants to build up the house of the man who marries her, and takes no thought for her earlier children or the husband of her youth, once he is dead, and asks about them no longer. So go, and put all your possessions in the charge of whichever of the women seems to you the best, until the gods show you a noble bride.

"And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. The best men among the suitors are lying in wait for you deliberately in the strait between Ithaca and rugged Samos, eager to kill you before you come to your own country. But I do not think that will happen; before that the earth will cover many a one of the suitors who eat up your substance. Keep your well-built ship well away from the islands, and sail by night as well as by day; and that one of the immortals who keeps and guards you will send a fair breeze after you. And when you have reached the nearest shore of Ithaca, send your ship and all your companions on to the city, but go yourself first of all to the swineherd who keeps your pigs, and who has a kindly heart towards you. Spend the night there, and tell him to go to the city and take word to wise Penelope that she has you safe, and that you have come back from Pylos."

With that she went off to high Olympus. And Telemachus woke the son of Nestor out of sweet sleep, rousing him with a touch of his heel, and said to him:

"Wake up, Peisistratus, son of Nestor; bring up your fiery-hoofed horses and yoke them under the car, so that we can get on our way."

Then Peisistratus, son of Nestor, answered him and said: "Telemachus, there is no way we can drive through the dark night, however eager we are for our journey; and it will soon be dawn. Wait until the warrior son of Atreus, Menelaus, famous for his spear, brings his gifts and sets them in the car and sends us off with kindly words of farewell. A guest remembers all his days the host who shows him kindness."

So he spoke, and golden-throned Dawn came soon. Menelaus came up to them, good at the war-cry, getting up from his bed beside fair-haired Helen. And when the prince, the dear son of Odysseus, saw him, he hurried to put his bright tunic on and to fling a great cloak over his powerful shoulders, and went out. Then Telemachus, the dear son of godlike Odysseus, came up to Menelaus and said to him:

"Menelaus, son of Atreus, brought up by Zeus, leader of armies, send me back now at last to my own dear country; my heart is eager to be home."

Then Menelaus answered him, good at the war-cry: "Telemachus, I shall not hold you here a long time when you are eager to go back. I would blame another man who as a host loves too much or hates too much; due measure is better in everything. It is an equal wrong to speed a guest on who does not want to go and to hold back one who is eager to be gone. One should make the guest who is present welcome, and send off the one who wants to go. But wait until I bring fine gifts and put them in your car, and your own eyes see them, and until I tell the women to get a meal ready in the halls out of the plentiful store that is inside. It is a double good — it brings honour and glory, and profit as well — that a traveller should dine before he goes out over the wide and boundless earth. And if you would like to travel through Hellas and the middle of Argos, then let it be so, and I will go with you myself, and yoke horses for you and take you to the cities of men. And nobody will send us away empty-handed; every one of them will give us some one thing at least to take away, a fine bronze tripod or cauldron, or a pair of mules, or a golden cup."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Menelaus, son of Atreus, brought up by Zeus, leader of armies, I would rather go home at once, because when I left I left nobody behind to watch over my possessions. I do not want to die myself while looking for my godlike father, or to lose some fine treasure out of my halls."

Now when Menelaus heard this, good in battle, he told his wife and her women at once to get a meal ready in the halls out of the plentiful store that was inside. Eteoneus, son of Boethous, came up to him, just out of bed, because he lived not far off. Menelaus, good at the war-cry, told him to kindle a fire and roast some of the meat; and he heard and obeyed.

And Menelaus went down himself to his vaulted treasure chamber, not alone, because Helen went with him, and Megapenthes. And when they came to the place where his treasures were stored, the son of Atreus took a two-handled cup and told his son Megapenthes to carry a mixing bowl of silver. And Helen went to the chests where her richly embroidered robes were, which she had made herself. One of these Helen, that beautiful woman, lifted out and carried away, the one that was finest in its embroidery and the largest. It shone like a star, and it lay beneath all the rest.

Then they went through the house until they came to Telemachus; and fair-haired Menelaus said to him: "Telemachus, may Zeus, the loud-thundering lord of Hera, bring about your homecoming, just as your heart wants it. And of all the gifts stored as treasure in my house, I will give you the finest and the most valuable. I will give you a well-made mixing bowl. It is all of silver, with the rims finished in gold, the work of Hephaestus; and the warrior Phaedimus, king of the Sidonians, gave it to me when his house sheltered me on my way there; and now I have a mind to give it to you."

With that the warrior son of Atreus put the two-handled cup into his hands. And strong Megapenthes brought the bright mixing bowl of silver and set it in front of him. And Helen of the lovely cheeks came up with the robe in her hands and said to him:

"Here, I give you this gift too, dear child, a remembrance of the hands of Helen, for the day of the marriage you long for, for your bride to wear. Until then let it lie in your halls in your dear mother's keeping. And for yourself, I hope you reach your well-built house and your own country with joy."

With that she put it in his hands, and he took it gladly. And prince Peisistratus took the gifts and laid them in the box of the chariot, and looked at them all, marvelling in his heart. Then fair-haired Menelaus led them into the house, and the two of them sat down on chairs and high seats. And a maid brought water for their hands in a lovely golden jug and poured it over a silver basin for them to wash, and drew a polished table up beside them. And the housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it in front of them, and plenty of meat with it, giving freely from her stores. And the son of Boethous carved the meat close by and divided the portions, and the son of glorious Menelaus poured the wine. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them.

But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, then Telemachus and the glorious son of Nestor yoked the horses and got up into the inlaid car, and drove out through the gateway and the echoing portico. And the son of Atreus, fair-haired Menelaus, went after them, carrying honey-hearted wine in a golden cup in his right hand, so that they might pour an offering before they set out. He took his stand in front of the horses and pledged the young men and said:

"Farewell, young men, and take my greetings to Nestor, shepherd of the army; he was as kind as a father to me while we sons of the Achaeans were making war in the land of Troy."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Yes, king brought up by Zeus, we will tell him all this when we come, as you say. And I wish that when I get back to Ithaca I might find Odysseus at home just as surely, to tell him how I met with every kindness at your hands before I left, and am bringing away treasures many and fine."

Even as he spoke a bird flew past on the right, an eagle, carrying a great white goose in its talons, a tame bird from the yard, and men and women followed shouting. And the eagle came near them and darted off to the right in front of the horses; and they were glad when they saw it, and the hearts in all their breasts were cheered. And Peisistratus, son of Nestor, was the first to speak among them:

"Consider, Menelaus, brought up by Zeus, leader of armies, whether the god showed this sign for the two of us or for yourself."

So he spoke, and Menelaus, dear to Ares, turned over how he might interpret the sign rightly with understanding. But long-robed Helen took the word from him and said:

"Listen to me, and I will prophesy as the immortals put it in my heart, and as I think it will come about. As this eagle came from the mountain, where his kin are and where he was born, and snatched up the goose that was reared in the house, so Odysseus will come home after many labours and many wanderings, and will take his revenge; or he is at home even now, and sowing the seeds of trouble for all the suitors."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "May Zeus grant it so, the loud-thundering lord of Hera; then I will pray to you there as to a god."

He spoke, and touched the two horses with the lash, and they sped quickly towards the plain, running eagerly through the city. So all day long they shook the yoke they carried on their necks. And the sun set, and all the ways grew dark. And they came to Pherae, to the house of Diocles, son of Ortilochus, whose father was Alpheus. There they spent the night, and he set out for them the entertainment due to strangers.

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, they yoked the horses and got up into the inlaid car and drove out through the gateway and the echoing portico. Then Peisistratus touched the horses with the whip to start them, and the pair went on willingly, and soon after that they reached the steep citadel of Pylos. Then Telemachus said to the son of Nestor:

"Son of Nestor, will you make me a promise now, and keep it, as I ask? We call ourselves friends from of old, through our fathers' friendship, and we are the same age besides, and this journey will settle us still more firmly in one mind. Do not take me past my ship, you whom Zeus brought up, but leave me there, in case that old man keeps me in his house against my will, wanting to show me kindness; and I must hurry home."

So he spoke, and the son of Nestor took counsel with his heart as to how he might properly give the promise and keep it. And as he turned it over, this seemed the better course. He turned his horses towards the fast ship and the shore of the sea, and took out the beautiful gifts Menelaus had given him, the clothes and the gold, and set them in the stern of the ship. And he urged Telemachus on and spoke to him with winged words:

"Go aboard quickly now, and tell all your companions to do the same, before I get home and bring the old man word. I know this well in my mind and heart: his spirit is so masterful that he will not let you go, but will come here himself to invite you to his house; and I tell you he will not go back without you, because he will be very angry, whatever anyone says."

With that he drove his horses with the beautiful manes back to the city of the Pylians, and quickly reached the palace. And Telemachus called to his men and gave them their orders:

"Set all the gear in order in the black ship, men, and let us go aboard ourselves and get on our way."

So he spoke, and they listened readily and obeyed; and they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches.

So he was busy with this, and was praying and making an offering to Athena by the stern of the ship, when a man from a far country came up to him, one who was fleeing out of Argos because he had killed a man; and he was a seer. By descent he came from Melampus, who lived long ago in Pylos, mother of flocks, a rich man with a very wealthy house among the Pylians, but who afterwards came to a land of strangers, fleeing from his country and from great-hearted Neleus, the most lordly of living men, who for a whole year had kept a great deal of wealth from him by force. Melampus meanwhile lay bound with bitter bonds in the halls of Phylacus, suffering hard pains because of the daughter of Neleus and the terrible blindness of heart the goddess had laid on him, the Erinys who brings houses to ruin.

But he escaped his fate, and drove the deep-lowing cattle from Phylace to Pylos, and took his revenge on godlike Neleus for that cruel deed, and brought the girl home to be his own brother's wife. And for himself he went to the country of other men, to horse-pasturing Argos, because it was appointed for him to live there, holding sway over many Argives. There he married a wife and built himself a high-roofed house and fathered Antiphates and Mantius, two stalwart sons. And Antiphates fathered great-hearted Oicles, and Oicles fathered Amphiaraus, the rouser of armies, whom Zeus who holds the aegis and Apollo loved heartily with every kind of love. Yet he did not reach the threshold of old age, but died at Thebe, because of a woman's gifts. Sons were born to him, Alcmaeon and Amphilochus. And Mantius fathered Polypheides and Cleitus. Golden-throned Dawn snatched Cleitus away for his beauty, so that he might live with the immortals; and Apollo made Polypheides, high of heart, a seer, far the best of mortals, after Amphiaraus was dead. He moved to Hyperesia, having grown angry with his father, and lived there and prophesied to all men.

It was his son, Theoclymenus by name, who now came and stood beside Telemachus. He found him pouring offerings and praying by his fast black ship, and spoke to him with winged words:

"Friend, since I find you making a burnt offering in this place, I beg you by your offerings and by the god, and by your own life and the lives of the companions who follow you, tell me truly what I ask and do not hide it. Who are you among men, and where from? Where is your city, and where are your parents?"

And clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Then I will tell you all of it plainly, stranger. I am from Ithaca by birth, and my father is Odysseus, as surely as there ever was such a man; but now he has died by a pitiful fate. So I have taken my companions and a black ship and come to look for news of my father, who has been gone so long."

Then godlike Theoclymenus answered him: "I too have fled from my country, because I killed a man, one of my own kin. And he has many brothers and kinsmen in horse-pasturing Argos, and they hold great sway among the Achaeans. It is to avoid death and black fate at their hands that I am running, because it seems it is my lot to be a wanderer among men. Take me aboard your ship, since I have made my plea to you in my flight, in case they kill me outright; I think they are after me."

And clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Then I will certainly not thrust you away from my trim ship, since you are eager to come. Follow with us, and you shall have such hospitality in our home as we have."

With that he took the man's bronze spear from him and laid it at full length on the deck of the curved ship, and went aboard the seafaring ship himself. Then he sat down in the stern and made Theoclymenus sit beside him; and his men loosed the stern cables. And Telemachus called to his men and told them to lay hold of the tackle, and they obeyed quickly. They raised the fir mast and set it in the hollow socket and made it fast with the forestays, and hauled the white sail up with twisted ox-hide ropes.

And grey-eyed Athena sent them a favourable wind, blowing strongly through the sky, so that the ship should run swiftly and finish her way over the salt water of the sea. So they went past Crouni and Chalcis with its beautiful streams. Now the sun set and all the ways grew dark. And the ship drew near to Pheae, sped by the wind of Zeus, and past fine Elis, where the Epeans hold sway. And from there he steered for the sharp isles, turning over whether he would escape death or be taken.

But the two of them, Odysseus and the good swineherd, were eating supper in the hut, and the other men ate with them. And when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, Odysseus spoke among them, testing the swineherd to see whether he would go on entertaining him with kindly care and tell him to stay there at the farm, or send him off to the city:

"Listen to me now, Eumaeus, and all you other men. In the morning I mean to go off to the city to beg, so that I am not the ruin of you and your men. Give me good advice, then, and send a trusty guide with me to lead me there. And through the city I shall have to wander by myself, hoping somebody will give me a cup of water and a loaf. And I would go to the house of godlike Odysseus and take news to wise Penelope, and join the company of the insolent suitors, in case they give me a meal, since they have plenty of good things. I could do them good service in anything they wanted at once. I will tell you, and you take heed and listen: by the favour of Hermes the messenger, who gives grace and glory to all men's work, no man can compete with me in the business of serving — at piling a fire well, at splitting dry faggots, at carving and roasting meat, and at pouring wine — in all the things in which meaner men serve the noble."

And you were deeply moved, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said to him: "Oh, stranger, how has such a thought come into your mind? You must be wanting to die on the spot, if you really mean to go into the crowd of the suitors, whose wantonness and violence reach the iron sky. Their serving men are nothing like you; the ones who serve them are young, well dressed in cloaks and tunics, and their heads and their bright faces are always sleek, and the polished tables are loaded with bread and meat and wine. No, stay here. Nobody is troubled by your being here, not I, and none of the other men with me. And when the dear son of Odysseus comes, he will dress you in a cloak and a tunic for clothes himself, and will send you wherever your heart and spirit tell you to go."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "I wish, Eumaeus, that you might be as dear to father Zeus as you are to me, since you have made me stop wandering and stop my miserable hardship. Nothing is worse for mortals than roaming; and yet for their cursed belly's sake men put up with terrible troubles, when wandering and sorrow and pain come on them. But now, since you keep me here and tell me to wait for your master, come, tell me about the mother of godlike Odysseus, and about his father, whom he left behind on the threshold of old age when he went away. Are they still alive under the rays of the sun, or are they dead now and in the house of Hades?"

Then the swineherd, a leader of men, answered him: "Then I will tell you plainly, stranger. Laertes is still alive, but he prays to Zeus all the time that the life may waste out of his limbs in his halls. He grieves extraordinarily for his son, who is gone, and for the wise lady his wedded wife, whose death troubled him more than anything and brought him to an untimely old age. And she died of grief for her glorious son, a miserable death, and I hope no man dies that way who lives here as my friend and does me kindness.

"As long as she lived, sorrowful as it was, it was always a pleasure to me to ask and enquire after her, because she had brought me up herself along with long-robed Ctimene, her noble daughter, the youngest child she bore. I was brought up with her, and her mother honoured me only a little less than her own children. But when we both reached the longed-for prime of youth, they sent Ctimene to Same to be married, and got themselves countless bridal gifts; and as for me, my lady dressed me in a cloak and a tunic, very good clothes, and gave me sandals for my feet and sent me out to the fields; but in her heart she loved me all the more.

"Now I go without all that; and yet for my own part the blessed gods make the work prosper that I give my mind to. From it I have eaten and drunk and given to strangers who deserve respect. But from my mistress I can hear nothing pleasant, in word or deed, because a plague has fallen on the house, these overbearing men. And servants long very much to speak before their mistress and learn everything, and to eat and drink, and then to carry something off to the fields as well — the kind of thing that always warms a servant's heart."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "So you must have wandered far from your country and your parents when you were only a child, Eumaeus the swineherd. But tell me this, and tell me the truth. Was some broad-wayed city of men sacked, where your father and your honoured mother lived? Or did enemies take you in their ships when you were alone with your sheep or your cattle, and carry you off for sale to this master's house, and he paid a good price for you?"

Then the swineherd, a leader of men, answered him: "Stranger, since you ask and question me about this, listen now in silence, and enjoy yourself, and drink your wine as you sit here. These nights are extraordinarily long. There is time for sleep, and time to take pleasure in hearing tales; you do not need to lie down before it is time. There is weariness even in too much sleep. As for the rest, if any man's heart and spirit tell him to, let him go out and sleep, and at daybreak let him eat and follow our master's pigs. But we two will drink and eat in the hut, and take delight each in the other's hard troubles as we call them to mind. In later time a man finds joy even in his troubles, if he has suffered a great deal and wandered a great deal. But I will tell you what you ask and enquire about.

"There is an island called Syria, if you have heard of it, above Ortygia, where the turning-places of the sun are. It is not so very thickly settled, but it is a good country, rich in herds, rich in flocks, full of wine, plentiful in wheat. Famine never comes into that country, and no hateful sickness besides falls on wretched mortals; but when the tribes of men grow old through the city, Apollo of the silver bow comes with Artemis and attacks them with his gentle arrows and kills them. There are two cities in that island, and all the land is divided between them; and my father ruled as king over both, Ctesius, son of Ormenus, a man like the immortals.

"Phoenicians came there, men famous for their ships, greedy rogues, bringing countless trinkets in their black ship. Now there was a Phoenician woman in my father's house, handsome and tall and skilled in glorious handiwork. The wily Phoenicians beguiled her. First, as she was washing clothes, one of them lay with her in love beside the hollow ship; and that beguiles the minds of women, even an upright one. Then he asked her who she was and where she came from, and she pointed out at once the high-roofed house of my father, and said:

"'I declare I come from Sidon, rich in bronze, and I am the daughter of Arybas, to whom wealth flowed in streams. But Taphian pirates seized me as I was coming from the fields, and brought me here and sold me to that man's house, and he paid a good price for me.'

"Then the man who had lain with her in secret answered her: 'Would you go back home with us, then, and see the high-roofed house of your father and mother, and see them too? They are still alive, and they are accounted rich.'

"Then the woman answered him and said: 'That might well be, if you sailors will pledge yourselves by an oath to bring me safely home.'

"So she spoke, and they all swore to it, as she told them. But when they had sworn and finished the oath, the woman spoke among them again and said:

"'Be quiet now, and let none of your company speak to me if he meets me in the street, or at the well, in case somebody goes to the palace and tells the old king, and he grows suspicious and binds me with hard bonds and contrives death for you. Keep my words in mind, and hurry on the trading of your wares. And when your ship is loaded with goods, let a message come to me quickly at the palace; because I will bring whatever gold comes to my hand as well. And I would gladly give another thing for my passage. There is a child of my noble master, whose nurse I am in the palace, such a knowing child, who is always running out with me. I would bring him aboard, and he would fetch you an enormous price, wherever you took him for sale among men of a strange language.'

"With that she went off to the fine palace. And they stayed there in our country a whole year, and got a great deal of substance by trading in their hollow ship. But when their hollow ship was loaded for the voyage home, then they sent a messenger to bring the woman word. A man came to my father's house, well versed in guile, with a golden necklace strung with amber beads between. The girls in the hall and my honoured mother were handling it and gazing at it and offering him their price; and he nodded at the woman in silence. And when he had nodded to her he went off to the hollow ship; and she took me by the hand and led me out of the house.

"In the front hall of the palace she found the cups and the tables of the banqueters who waited on my father. They had gone out to the council and the people's place of debate; and she quickly hid three goblets in her dress and carried them off, and I followed her without a thought. Then the sun set and all the ways grew dark. And we hurried and came to the fine harbour where the fast Phoenician ship was. Then they went aboard, putting the two of us on board as well, and sailed over the watery roads, and Zeus sent them a favourable wind.

"For six days we sailed, night and day alike; but when Zeus, son of Cronos, brought the seventh day on us, then Artemis the archer struck the woman, and she fell with a thud into the hold the way a sea bird plunges. They threw her out to be a prey for seals and fishes, and I was left with my heart badly stricken. And the wind and the wave carried them and brought them to Ithaca, where Laertes bought me with his wealth. That is how my eyes came to see this country."

Then Odysseus, born of Zeus, answered him and said: "Eumaeus, you have deeply stirred the heart in my breast, telling all this tale of the sorrow you have borne. And yet in your case Zeus has given good side by side with the bad, since after all your labour you have come to the house of a kindly man, who gives you food and drink, and does it kindly, and you live well; whereas I have come here wandering through the many cities of men."

So they talked to one another, and then lay down to sleep, not for long, but for a little; because fair-throned Dawn came soon.

But the companions of Telemachus, drawing in to shore, furled the sail and took the mast down quickly, and rowed the ship to her anchorage with their oars. Then they threw out the mooring-stones and made the stern cables fast, and went out themselves onto the shore of the sea and got their meal ready and mixed the flaming wine. But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, clear-headed Telemachus was the first to speak among them:

"Row the black ship on to the city now; but I will visit the fields and the herdsmen, and come down to the city in the evening when I have looked over my lands. And in the morning I will set a good feast of meat and sweet wine before you as wages for your journey."

Then godlike Theoclymenus answered him: "Where shall I go, dear child? Whose house shall I come to, of the men who rule in rocky Ithaca? Or shall I go straight to your mother's house and yours?"

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "If things were otherwise I would tell you to go to our house, because there is no lack of hospitality for strangers there; but it would be worse for you, since I shall be away and my mother will not see you. She does not often appear before the suitors in the house, but weaves at her loom in an upper room, away from them. But I will tell you of another man you may go to, Eurymachus, the glorious son of wise Polybus, whom the men of Ithaca now look on as on a god. He is far the best man of them, and the most eager to marry my mother and to have the honour of Odysseus. But Olympian Zeus, who lives in the sky, knows whether he will bring the evil day on them before that marriage."

Even as he spoke a bird flew out on the right, a hawk, the swift messenger of Apollo. He held a dove in his talons and was plucking her and shedding the feathers on the ground, midway between the ship and Telemachus himself. Then Theoclymenus called him apart from his companions and took his hand and said to him:

"Telemachus, this bird has not flown out on our right without a god's warrant; I knew as I looked at him that he was a bird of omen. There is no house in the land of Ithaca more kingly than yours; you are supreme for ever."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him again: "Stranger, I wish this word of yours might be fulfilled. Then you would soon know kindness and many a gift from me, so that anyone who met you would call you a lucky man."

Then he spoke to Peiraeus, his trusty companion: "Peiraeus, son of Clytius, you are the one who in other matters listens to me above all my companions who went with me to Pylos; so now take this stranger, please, and give him a kindly welcome in your house and treat him with honour until I come."

Then Peiraeus, the famous spearman, answered him: "Telemachus, however long you stay here, I will entertain him, and he shall have no lack of what is due to a stranger."

With that he went aboard the ship and told his companions to embark themselves and loose the stern cables. So they went aboard at once and sat down at the benches. But Telemachus bound the fine sandals under his feet and took his mighty spear, tipped with sharp bronze, from the deck of the ship. Then the men loosed the stern cables and thrust off and sailed to the city, as Telemachus told them, the dear son of godlike Odysseus.

But his feet carried him quickly on as he strode forward, until he reached the farm where his countless pigs were, and where the worthy swineherd slept among them with a heart loyal to his masters.

Book Sixteen

Meanwhile the two in the hut, Odysseus and the good swineherd, had kindled a fire and were getting their breakfast ready at dawn, and had sent the herdsmen out with the droves of pigs. And the baying hounds fawned round Telemachus and did not bark as he came near. And noble Odysseus noticed the fawning of the hounds, and the sound of footsteps came to his ears, and at once he spoke winged words to Eumaeus:

"Eumaeus, some companion of yours must be coming, or somebody you know at least, because the hounds are not barking but fawning round him, and I hear the sound of footsteps."

The word was not fully out of his mouth before his own dear son stood in the doorway. The swineherd sprang up in astonishment, and the vessels dropped out of his hands that he was busy with, mixing the flaming wine. He went to meet his lord and kissed his head and both his beautiful eyes and his two hands, and a big tear fell from him. As a loving father greets his own dear son, who comes in the tenth year from a distant country — his only son and best loved, for whose sake he has borne much sorrow — so the good swineherd took godlike Telemachus in his arms and kissed him all over, like a man escaped from death; and he spoke to him with winged words, wailing:

"You have come, Telemachus, sweet light of my eyes. I thought I would never see you again after you had gone to Pylos in your ship. But come in, dear child, so that I can delight my heart with looking at you here in my house, newly come from other countries. You do not often visit the farm and the herdsmen, but stay in the town; it seems good to your heart, I suppose, to keep an eye on the destructive crowd of the suitors."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "So it shall be, father. It is for your sake that I have come here, to see you with my eyes and to hear you tell me whether my mother is still in the halls, or whether some other man has married her by now, and the bed of Odysseus lies without its bedding, covered with foul spiders' webs."

Then the swineherd, a leader of men, answered him: "Yes indeed, she stays in your halls with a steady heart, and the nights and the days waste away for her sorrowfully as she weeps."

With that he took the bronze spear from him, and Telemachus went in and crossed the stone threshold. As he came in, his father Odysseus got up from his seat to give him place; but Telemachus checked him and said:

"Sit down, stranger. We shall find a seat somewhere else in our farm; here is a man who will set one for us."

So he spoke, and Odysseus went back and sat down again; and the swineherd strewed green brushwood underneath for Telemachus and a fleece over it, and the dear son of Odysseus sat down there. Then the swineherd set platters of roast meat in front of them, left over from their meal the day before, and quickly heaped bread up in baskets and mixed honey-sweet wine in a bowl of ivy wood, and sat down himself opposite godlike Odysseus. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them.

But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, Telemachus said to the good swineherd: "Father, where did this stranger come to you from? How did sailors bring him to Ithaca? Who did they say they were? Because he certainly did not get here on foot."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Then I will tell you the whole truth, my child. He says he is from broad Crete by birth, and says he has wandered roaming through many cities of mortals; that is the lot a god has spun for him. And now he has run away from a ship of the Thesprotians and come to my farm, and I put him in your hands. Do what you like. He declares himself your suppliant."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Eumaeus, this word of yours stings me to the heart. How am I to welcome this stranger in my house? I am young myself, and I have no trust yet in my strength to defend me against a man when one of them grows angry over nothing. And as for my mother, the heart in her breast wavers this way and that, whether to stay here with me and keep the house, respecting her husband's bed and the voice of the people, or to go now with whichever of the Achaeans who court her in the halls is the best and offers the most wedding gifts. But as for this stranger, now that he has come to your house, I will dress him in a cloak and a tunic, good clothes, and give him a two-edged sword and sandals for his feet, and send him wherever his heart and spirit tell him to go. Or, if you like, keep him here at the farm and look after him, and I will send clothes here and all his food to eat, so that he is not the ruin of you and your men. But I will not let him go there, to join the company of the suitors; they are too full of wanton insolence. They might mock him, and I would be badly grieved. And it is hard for one man to achieve anything against many, however strong he is; they are far stronger."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "Friend, since it must be right for me to answer — you two tear my heart when I hear your words, the kind of wantonness you say the suitors contrive in the halls in spite of you, a man as fine as you are. Tell me, are you letting yourself be put down like this? Or do the people through the country hate you, following the voice of a god? Or have you cause to blame your brothers, whose fighting a man trusts in even when a great quarrel arises?

"I wish I were as young as you are with the temper I have now, either the son of blameless Odysseus, or Odysseus himself — some stranger might cut my head off my neck straight away if I did not prove myself the ruin of the whole lot of them when I had got to the halls of Odysseus, son of Laertes. And if they overwhelmed me by their numbers, alone as I was, I would far rather die, killed in my own halls, than go on seeing these shameful things — strangers mishandled, and men dragging the women through the fine halls in a shameful way, and wine drawn off and wasted, and men eating up my bread without a thought, without limit, with no end to the business."

And clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Then I will tell you all of it plainly, stranger. The people at large bear me no grudge and no hatred, and I have no cause to blame brothers, whose fighting a man trusts in even when a great quarrel arises. The son of Cronos has made our house run in a single line: Arceisius fathered Laertes as his only son, and his father again fathered Odysseus as his only son, and Odysseus fathered me as his only son and left me in his halls, and had no joy of me. That is why enemies past counting are in the house now: all the princes who hold sway over the islands — Dulichium and Same and wooded Zacynthus — and those who lord it over rocky Ithaca, every one of them is courting my mother and wasting my house. And she will not refuse the marriage, which she hates, and cannot bring the matter to an end; and they eat up my substance with their feasting, and will bring me to ruin too before long. But all this lies on the knees of the gods.

"But go quickly, father, and tell steady Penelope that she has me safe and that I have come back from Pylos. I will stay here; and you come back here when you have told your tale to her alone. And let none of the rest of the Achaeans learn it; there are a great many contriving harm against me."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "I see, I take your point; you are telling this to a man who understands. But tell me this, and tell me the truth: shall I go the same way with news to Laertes too, wretched man? For a time, though he grieved badly for Odysseus, he used to oversee the fields and eat and drink with the slaves in the house, as the heart in his breast told him. But now, from the day you went to Pylos in your ship, they say he has not eaten and drunk the same way, or overseen the fields, but sits groaning and wailing and weeping, and the flesh wastes off his bones."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "That is sadder still; but even so we will let him be, for all our sorrow. If mortals could have everything they wished for, we should choose first of all the day of my father's homecoming. No, come back when you have given your message, and do not wander over the fields looking for Laertes. But tell my mother to send her housekeeper out to him as fast as she can, and secretly; she could take word to the old man."

With that he roused the swineherd, and he took his sandals in his hands and bound them under his feet and went off to the city.

And Athena was not unaware that Eumaeus the swineherd had gone from the farm, but she came near in the likeness of a woman, handsome and tall and skilled in glorious handiwork. And she stood opposite the door of the hut, showing herself to Odysseus; but Telemachus did not see her in front of him or notice her, because the gods do not appear openly to everyone. But Odysseus saw her, and the hounds did; and they did not bark, but slunk whining in fear to the far side of the farm. She made a sign with her brows, and noble Odysseus understood it and went out of the hall, past the great wall of the court, and stood in front of her; and Athena said to him:

"Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, reveal your word to your son now and do not hide it, so that when the two of you have planned death and doom for the suitors you may go to the famous city. And I myself will not be far from you for long, because I am eager for the fight."

With that Athena touched him with her golden wand. First of all she threw a well-washed cloak and a tunic round his breast, and increased his height and his youthful bloom. He grew dark of colour again, and his cheeks filled out, and the beard about his chin grew dark. And when she had done this she went away; and Odysseus went into the hut.

And his dear son marvelled, and turned his eyes aside in fear, in case it should be a god; and he spoke to him with winged words: "You seem to me another sort of man now, stranger, than you were a little while ago; the clothes you have on are different and your colour is not the same. You must be a god, one of those who hold the wide sky. Be gracious, then, so that we may offer you acceptable sacrifices and golden gifts, finely made; and spare us."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "I am no god; why do you compare me to the immortals? I am your father, for whose sake you groan and endure so many griefs and put up with the violence of men."

With that he kissed his son and let a tear fall from his cheeks to the ground, though before this he had always held them back steadily.

But Telemachus — because he still did not believe it was his father — answered him again and said: "You are not my father Odysseus; some god is beguiling me, so that I may weep and groan still more. No mortal man could contrive this by his own wit, unless a god came to him himself and made him young or old easily, by his will. Just now you were an old man and meanly dressed, and now you are like the gods who hold the wide sky."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Telemachus, it does not become you to marvel too much that your father is in the house, or to be amazed. No other Odysseus will ever come here; I am the man, just as you see me, come to my own country in the twentieth year after suffering and many wanderings. This is the work of Athena, driver of the spoil, who makes me whatever she likes — because she has the power — now like a beggar, and now again like a young man wearing fine clothes on his body. It is easy for the gods who hold the wide sky both to glorify a mortal man and to bring him low."

With that he sat down, and Telemachus flung his arms round his noble father and wept and shed tears, and a longing for lamentation rose in the hearts of both of them. They wailed aloud more insistently than birds, sea-eagles or vultures with crooked talons, whose young the country people have taken out of the nest before they were fledged; just so pitifully did they let the tears fall from under their brows. And the light of the sun would have gone down on their weeping, if Telemachus had not spoken suddenly to his father:

"In what kind of ship, dear father, have sailors brought you here to Ithaca now? Who did they say they were? Because you certainly did not get here on foot."

And much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "Then I will tell you the whole truth, my child. The Phaeacians brought me, men famous for their ships, who send other men on their way too, whoever comes to them. They brought me over the sea in a fast ship while I slept, and set me down in Ithaca, and gave me glorious gifts, stores of bronze and gold and woven clothing. These treasures are lying in caves, by the favour of the gods. And now I have come here at Athena's bidding, so that we can plan the killing of our enemies. Come, count the suitors for me and give me their number, so that I know how many they are and what kind of men, and can turn it over in my noble heart and decide whether the two of us are able to take them on alone, without others, or whether we should look for others too."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Father, I have always heard of your great fame, that you were a fighter in strength of hand and in wise counsel; but this is too much of a thing to say. Amazement takes hold of me. It could not be that two men should fight against so many, and mighty men at that. The suitors are not ten, or twice ten, but a great many more. You shall learn their number here and now. From Dulichium there are fifty-two picked young men, and six serving men attend them; from Same came twenty-four men; from Zacynthus there are twenty young Achaeans; and from Ithaca itself twelve men, all of them the noblest, and with them Medon the herald and the divine singer and two squires skilled at carving meat. If we meet all of them inside the halls, I am afraid your coming to avenge their violence will be bitter and deadly. Think whether you can think of some helper, one who would help us two with a ready heart."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "Then I will tell you, and you take heed and listen to my words, and consider whether Athena, with father Zeus, will be enough for the two of us, or whether I should think of some other helper."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Those two helpers you name are good ones, certainly, though they live high up in the clouds; and they rule over all men alike and over the immortal gods."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "Those two will not hold off long from the mighty struggle, when the strength of Ares is put to the test between the suitors and us in my halls. But for now, go home at daybreak and join the company of the haughty suitors. The swineherd will bring me to the city later on, in the likeness of a miserable old beggar. And if they treat me with contempt in the house, let the heart in your breast endure while I am badly used — even if they drag me by the feet through the house to the door, or throw things at me and strike me; you must endure the sight of it. You may tell them to stop their folly, trying to talk them round with gentle words; but they will not listen to you at all, because their day of doom is at hand.

"And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. When Athena, rich in counsel, puts it into my mind, I will nod to you with my head; and when you notice it, take all the weapons of war that are lying in your halls and put every one of them away in the secret place of the high store-room. And when the suitors miss the arms and question you, beguile them with gentle words, and say:

"'I have put them away out of the smoke, since they are no longer like the ones Odysseus left behind him when he went off to Troy, but are all fouled as far as the breath of the fire has reached them. And the son of Cronos has put this greater fear in my heart besides: that when you are heated with wine you may start a quarrel among yourselves and wound one another, and so bring shame on your feasting and your courting. Iron draws a man to it of itself.'

"But leave two swords behind for the two of us alone, and two spears, and two ox-hide shields for us to take hold of, so that we can rush at them and seize them; and as for the suitors, Pallas Athena and Zeus the counsellor will beguile them.

"And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. If you are truly my son and of our blood, then let nobody hear that Odysseus is at home; let Laertes not know it, or the swineherd, or anybody in the household, or Penelope herself. You and I by ourselves will learn the temper of the women; and we will make trial of a good many of the serving men too, and see which of them honours us and fears us at heart, and which takes no notice of us and scorns you, a man as fine as you are."

Then his glorious son answered him and said: "Father, I think you will come to know my spirit hereafter; there is no slackness of will in me. But I do not think this plan will be a gain to us both, and I tell you to think it over. You will go about for a long time to no purpose, testing each man as you visit the farms, while in your halls those others are wasting your substance insolently at their ease, and there is no sparing. As for the women, I do tell you to find out which of them dishonour you and which are innocent. But I would not have us make trial of the men at the farms; let us deal with that afterwards, if you really do know some sign from Zeus who holds the aegis."

So they talked to one another; and meanwhile the well-built ship that had brought Telemachus and all his companions from Pylos put in to Ithaca. And when they had come into the deep harbour they drew the black ship up on the shore, while proud squires carried their armour out and took the beautiful gifts straight to the house of Clytius. And they sent a herald to the house of Odysseus to take word to wise Penelope that Telemachus was at the farm and had told the ship to sail on to the city, so that the noble queen should not grow anxious and let the round tears fall.

So the two of them met, the herald and the good swineherd, on the same errand, to bring the lady news. And when they reached the palace of the godlike king, the herald spoke out in the middle of the women and said: "Your son has come back from Pylos just now, my queen."

But the swineherd came close to Penelope and told her everything her dear son had told him to say. And when he had told her the whole of what he had been ordered, he went off to the pigs and left the courtyard and the hall.

But the suitors were dismayed and downcast in spirit, and went out of the hall past the great wall of the court, and sat down there in front of the gates. Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, was the first to speak among them:

"My friends, a great piece of work has been insolently brought off by Telemachus, this journey, and we thought he would never get it done. Come, let us launch a black ship, the best we have, and get seamen together as rowers, to take word to the others as quickly as possible to come home."

The word was not fully out of his mouth when Amphinomus, turning where he sat, saw a ship in the deep harbour and men furling the sail with oars in their hands. And he broke into a merry laugh and spoke among his companions:

"Let us not send any message; here they are at home. Either some god told them of this, or they saw the ship of Telemachus sailing past themselves and could not catch her."

So he spoke, and they got up and went to the shore of the sea. The men quickly drew the black ship up on the shore, and proud squires carried their armour out. And they went all together to the meeting place, and would not let anyone else sit with them, young or old. Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them:

"See how the gods have delivered this man from destruction. Day after day watchmen sat on the windy heights, watch following watch; and at sunset we never spent a night on shore, but sailed over the deep in our fast ship and waited for the bright Dawn, lying in wait for Telemachus so that we could take him and kill the man himself; and meanwhile some god has brought him home. But let us contrive a miserable death for him here, for Telemachus, and let him not slip out of our hands; because while he lives, I think, this work of ours will not prosper. He is shrewd himself in counsel and in wisdom, and the people show us no favour any longer.

"Come, before he gathers the Achaeans to the meeting place — because I do not think he will be slow to act, but will be full of anger, and will stand up and declare among them all how we contrived his utter destruction and did not catch him; and they will not praise us when they hear of our wickedness. Take care, then, that they do not do us some harm and drive us out of our country, and we come to a land of strangers. Let us act first, and seize him in the fields far from the city, or on the road; and let us keep his substance and his wealth ourselves, dividing it fairly among us — though we would give the house to his mother to keep, and to the man who marries her.

"But if this plan does not please you, and you would rather he lived and kept all his father's wealth, then let us stop eating up his store of pleasant things gathering here, and let each man court her from his own hall with his gifts and try to win her; and she would marry the man who offers most and comes as her fated husband."

So he spoke, and they were all hushed and silent. Then Amphinomus spoke to the gathering and said his word among them. He was the glorious son of prince Nisus, son of Aretias, and he led the suitors who came from Dulichium, rich in wheat and in grass; and he pleased Penelope with his words more than all the others, because he had an understanding heart. He meant it kindly, and he spoke to the gathering:

"Friends, I would not choose to kill Telemachus. It is a dreadful thing to kill one of royal stock. Let us first try to learn the will of the gods. If the oracles of great Zeus approve, I will kill him myself and tell all the others to do it; but if the gods turn us from the act, I tell you to hold off."

So spoke Amphinomus, and his word pleased them. So they got up at once and went to the house of Odysseus, and went in and sat down on the polished seats.

Then wise Penelope thought of something else, to show herself to the suitors, overbearing in their insolence. For she had learned of the death threatened against her son in her halls, since the herald Medon told her, who had heard their plan. So she went towards the hall with her women. But when that lovely woman reached the suitors she stood by the doorpost of the well-built hall, holding her shining veil in front of her face; and she rebuked Antinous and said to him:

"Antinous, full of insolence, deviser of evil — and yet they say you are the best of all your years in the land of Ithaca in counsel and in speech. You are nothing of the kind. Madman, why do you contrive death and doom for Telemachus, and take no thought for suppliants, whom Zeus is witness for? It is an impious thing to plot evil against one another. Do you not know of the time your father came to this house as a fugitive, in terror of the people? They were furious with him, because he had joined Taphian pirates and harried the Thesprotians, who were in league with us. They meant to kill him and take his life by violence and eat up his great and pleasant living altogether; but Odysseus held them back and stopped them, for all their eagerness. And it is his house you consume now without paying for it, and his wife you court, and his son you try to kill, and on me you bring great distress. Stop it, I tell you, and tell the rest to stop."

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered her: "Daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, take heart, and do not let these things distress you. The man is not alive, and shall not live, and shall never be born, who will lay hands on your son Telemachus while I live and look on the light of the earth. I tell you this outright, and it will happen: that man's black blood will soon be running round my spear. Odysseus, the sacker of cities, often set me on his knees too, and put roast meat in my hands and held red wine to my lips. So Telemachus is by far the dearest of all men to me, and I tell him to have no fear of death, from the suitors at least; but from the gods no man can avoid it."

So he spoke to cheer her, and was plotting death against that son himself. So she went up to her bright upper room and wept for Odysseus, her dear husband, until grey-eyed Athena put sweet sleep on her eyelids.

But in the evening the good swineherd came back to Odysseus and his son, and they were busy getting supper ready and had killed a boar of a year old. And Athena came close to Odysseus, son of Laertes, and struck him with her wand and made him an old man again, and put mean clothes on his body, in case the swineherd should look at him and know him and go to bring news to steady Penelope, and not hold the secret fast in his heart.

Then Telemachus spoke first to the swineherd and said: "You have come, good Eumaeus. What news is there in the city? Have the proud suitors come home from their ambush by now, or are they still watching for me where they were, to take me on my way home?"

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "I had no mind to go about the city asking and enquiring about that; my heart told me to come back here as fast as I could when I had given my message. But a swift messenger from your companions joined me, a herald, who was the first to tell your mother the news. And I know this much besides, because I saw it with my own eyes. I was above the city as I went on my way, at the hill of Hermes, when I saw a fast ship putting into our harbour, and there were a great many men in her, and she was loaded with shields and double-pointed spears. And I thought it was them, but I have no knowledge of it."

So he spoke, and the strong young Telemachus smiled, and glanced at his father with his eyes, and avoided the swineherd's eye.

And when they had finished their work and got the meal ready they fell to feasting, and their hearts lacked nothing of the equal feast. But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, they thought about rest, and took the gift of sleep.

Book Seventeen

As soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, Telemachus, the dear son of godlike Odysseus, bound the fine sandals under his feet and took his mighty spear, which fitted his grip, hurrying to the city; and he said to his swineherd:

"Father, I am going to the city, so that my mother may see me; because I do not think she will stop her miserable wailing and tearful lamenting until she sees me in person. And this is my charge to you: bring this wretched stranger to the city, so that he can beg his food there, and whoever wants to will give him a loaf and a cup of water. I cannot possibly burden myself with everybody, when I have grief at heart. And if the stranger is badly angered at that, it will be the worse for him. I like to speak the truth."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Friend, I have no wish to be left here myself. It is better for a beggar to beg his food in the town than in the fields, and whoever wants to will give it me. I am no longer of an age to stay at a farm and do everything an overseer tells me. Go on, then; this man you have told will bring me as soon as I have warmed myself at the fire and the sun has grown hot. These clothes I have on are miserably poor, and I am afraid the morning frost will finish me; and you say the city is a long way off."

So he spoke, and Telemachus went out through the farm with rapid strides, and was sowing the seeds of trouble for the suitors. And when he came to the stately house he set his spear in place, leaning it against a tall pillar, and went in himself and crossed the stone threshold.

The nurse Eurycleia was far the first to see him, as she was spreading fleeces over the richly worked chairs. She came straight towards him, bursting into tears; and the other women of Odysseus, who endures everything, gathered round them and kissed his head and his shoulders in loving welcome. Then wise Penelope came out of her room, like Artemis or golden Aphrodite, and burst into tears and flung her arms round her dear son and kissed his head and both his beautiful eyes; and she spoke to him with winged words, wailing:

"You have come, Telemachus, sweet light of my eyes. I thought I would never see you again after you had gone to Pylos in your ship — secretly, and in spite of me, to look for news of your dear father. Come, tell me what sight you had of him."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Mother, do not stir up lamenting, and do not rouse the heart in my breast, when I have escaped utter destruction. Wash, and take clean clothes for your body, and go up to your room with your women and make a vow to all the gods that you will offer hecatombs that bring fulfilment, in the hope that Zeus may one day bring about deeds of requital. And I will go to the meeting place to invite to our house a stranger who followed me from Pylos on my way here. I sent him on ahead with my godlike companions, and told Peiraeus to take him home and give him a kindly welcome and show him honour until I came."

So he spoke, and her word stayed unwinged. Then she washed and took clean clothes for her body and made a vow to all the gods that she would offer hecatombs that bring fulfilment, in the hope that Zeus would one day bring about deeds of requital.

But Telemachus went out through the hall with his spear in his hand, and two swift hounds went with him. And Athena poured a marvellous grace over him, and all the people stared at him as he came. The proud suitors thronged round him, speaking him fair and turning over evil in the depths of their hearts. But he avoided the great crowd of them, and went and sat down where Mentor was sitting, and Antiphus, and Halitherses, who were friends of his father's house from of old; and they questioned him about everything.

Then Peiraeus, the famous spearman, came up, leading the stranger through the city to the meeting place; and Telemachus did not turn away from his guest for long, but went up to him. Peiraeus was the first to speak:

"Telemachus, send women to my house quickly, so that I can send you the gifts Menelaus gave you."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Peiraeus, we do not know how these things will go. If the proud suitors kill me secretly in my hall and divide all my father's goods among them, I would rather you kept these things and enjoyed them yourself than one of them. But if I sow the seeds of death and doom for them, then bring it all to my house gladly, as I shall be glad."

With that he led the sorely tried stranger to the house. Now when they had come to the stately house they laid their cloaks on the chairs and high seats, and went into the polished baths and washed. And when the maids had bathed them and rubbed them with oil and put fleecy cloaks and tunics on them, they came out of the baths and sat down on the chairs. And a maid brought water for their hands in a lovely golden jug and poured it over a silver basin for them to wash, and drew a polished table up beside them. And the housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it in front of them, and plenty of meat with it, giving freely from her stores. And his mother sat opposite Telemachus by the doorpost of the hall, leaning against a chair and spinning fine threads of yarn. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them.

But when they had eaten and drunk as much as they wanted, wise Penelope spoke first among them: "Telemachus, I shall go up to my room and lie down on my bed, which has become a bed of wailing for me, always wet with my tears, since the day Odysseus set out for Ilios with the sons of Atreus. And you took no care, before the proud suitors come into this house, to tell me plainly about your father's homecoming, if you heard anything."

And clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Then I will tell you the whole truth, mother. We went to Pylos and to Nestor, the shepherd of the people, and he received me in his high house and gave me a kindly welcome, as a father might his own son who had newly come home after a long time from far away; that is how kindly he tended me, with his glorious sons. But of Odysseus, who endures everything, he said he had heard nothing from any man on earth, whether he was living or dead. But he sent me on with horses and a jointed car to Menelaus, son of Atreus, the famous spearman. There I saw Argive Helen, for whose sake Argives and Trojans laboured so much by the will of the gods.

"And Menelaus, good at the war-cry, asked me at once what I had come to lovely Lacedaemon in search of; and I told him the whole truth. Then he answered me and said:

"'The disgrace of it — that men with no courage in them should want to lie in the bed of a man of such a heart. It is as when a hind puts her new-born suckling fawns to sleep in the thicket lair of a strong lion, and goes off over the mountain slopes and the grassy valleys looking for pasture, and then the lion comes back to his lair and lets loose a cruel doom on the two of them. That is how Odysseus will let loose a cruel doom on these men. Father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo — I wish that Odysseus might come among the suitors with the strength he had that time in well-built Lesbos, when he stood up and wrestled a match with Philomeleides and threw him hard, and all the Achaeans were delighted. If he came in that strength, every one of them would find a quick death and a bitter courtship. But as for what you are asking me and begging me about, I will not turn aside and talk of other things, or deceive you; of everything the unerring old man of the sea told me, I will not hide or keep back one word. He said he had seen Odysseus on an island in bitter distress, in the halls of the nymph Calypso, who keeps him there by force. And he cannot come to his own country, because he has no ships with oars at hand and no companions to send him on his way over the broad back of the sea.'

"That is what Menelaus said, son of Atreus, the famous spearman. And when I had finished all this I set out for home, and the immortals gave me a fair wind and brought me quickly to my own dear country."

So he spoke, and stirred the heart in her breast. Then godlike Theoclymenus spoke among them too, and said:

"Honoured wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes — he truly has no clear understanding of it; but listen to my words, because I will prophesy to you with certain knowledge and hide nothing. Let Zeus be my witness above all gods, and this hospitable table, and the hearth of noble Odysseus that I have come to, that Odysseus is in his own country even now, resting or moving about, learning of these evil doings, and sowing the seeds of trouble for all the suitors. So plain a bird of omen did I mark as I sat on the benched ship, and I declared it to Telemachus."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Stranger, I wish this word of yours might be fulfilled. Then you would soon know kindness and many a gift from me, so that anyone who met you would call you a lucky man."

So they talked to one another. And meanwhile the suitors in front of the palace of Odysseus were amusing themselves, throwing the discus and the javelin on the levelled ground, in their usual insolence of heart. But when it was the hour for dinner, and the flocks came in from the fields on all sides, and the men who usually led them led them in, then Medon spoke to them, who of all the heralds was the most to their liking and was always present at their feasts:

"Young men, now that you have all gladdened your hearts with sport, come to the house so that we can get a feast ready; it is no bad thing to take one's dinner at the right hour."

So he spoke, and they got up and went, and did as he said. And when they had come to the stately house they laid their cloaks on the chairs and high seats, and men set about killing great sheep and fat goats, and fatted hogs, and a heifer from the herd, and so got the meal ready.

But Odysseus and the good swineherd were hurrying to go from the farm to the city; and the swineherd, a leader of men, spoke first and said:

"Stranger, since you are eager to go to the city today, as my master told you — though for myself I would rather you were left here to keep the farm, but I respect him and I am afraid of him, in case he scolds me afterwards, and masters' rebukes are hard — come now, let us go. The day is far gone, and you will soon find it colder towards evening."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "I see, I take your point; you are telling this to a man who understands. Come, let us go, and be my guide the whole way. But give me a staff to lean on, if you have one cut anywhere, because you said the road was treacherous."

He spoke, and flung his miserable bag round his shoulders, full of holes, slung on a twisted cord, and Eumaeus gave him a staff that suited him. So the two of them set out, and the dogs and the herdsmen stayed behind to guard the farm; and the swineherd led his master to the city in the likeness of a miserable old beggar, leaning on a staff, and the clothes he wore on his body were wretched.

But when, going along the rugged path, they were near the city and had come to a well-made, fair-flowing fountain where the townspeople drew their water — Ithacus had made it, and Neritus, and Polyctor — and there was a grove of poplars round about it, the kind that grow by water, circling it on every side, and the cold water flowed down from the rock above, and on the top an altar to the nymphs was built where everyone who passed made offerings — there Melanthius, son of Dolius, met them, driving his she-goats, the best in all the herds, to make a feast for the suitors; and two herdsmen followed with him.

When he saw them he spoke and abused them in terrible and unseemly words, and stirred the heart of Odysseus:

"Now here is the vile leading the vile, truly. As always, the god brings like and like together. Where are you taking this filthy wretch, you miserable swineherd, this nuisance of a beggar to spoil our feasts? He is the kind of man to stand and rub his shoulders on a great many doorposts, begging for scraps and not for swords or cauldrons. If you would give me this fellow to keep my farm, to sweep out the pens and carry young shoots to the kids, he might get himself a sturdy thigh by drinking whey. But since he has learned nothing but evil ways, he will not want to busy himself with work, but would rather go skulking through the country and feed his insatiable belly by begging. But I tell you outright, and this word will come to pass: if he comes to the palace of godlike Odysseus, a good many footstools flung at his head by the hands of men will be broken on his ribs as he is pelted through the house."

So he spoke, and as he passed he kicked Odysseus on the hip, in his folly; but he did not drive him off the path, and Odysseus stood firm. And Odysseus turned over whether to leap on him and take his life with the staff, or to seize him round the middle and lift him up and dash his head on the ground. But he endured it and held himself back from his purpose.

And the swineherd looked the man in the face and rebuked him, and lifted up his hands and prayed aloud: "Nymphs of the fountain, daughters of Zeus, if ever Odysseus burned pieces of the thighs of lambs or kids on your altars, wrapped in rich fat, fulfil this prayer for me: grant that he, my master, may come back, and that some god may guide him. Then he would scatter all these proud airs you put on in your insolence, always roaming about the city, while evil herdsmen destroy the flock."

Then Melanthius the goatherd answered him: "Listen to the cur talk, with his mind full of mischief. One day I will take him on a black benched ship far from Ithaca, so that he brings me in a great deal of wealth. I wish Apollo of the silver bow would strike Telemachus down in the halls today, or that he might be killed by the suitors, as surely as the day of homecoming has been lost for Odysseus in a far country."

With that he left them there, walking slowly on, and strode forward himself and came very quickly to the palace of the king. He went straight in and sat down among the suitors opposite Eurymachus, because he loved him best of all. And the servants set a portion of meat beside him, and the housekeeper, a grave woman, brought bread and set it in front of him to eat.

And Odysseus and the good swineherd halted as they came near, and the sound of the hollow lyre rang round them, because Phemius was striking the chords to sing before the suitors. Then Odysseus took the swineherd by the hand and said:

"Eumaeus, this must be the beautiful house of Odysseus. It would be easy to recognise, even seen among a great many others. There is building on building, and the court is built with a wall and a coping, and the double gates are well fenced; no man could look down on it. And I can tell that a great many men are feasting inside, because the savour of meat rises from it, and the sound of the lyre goes with it, which the gods have made the companion of the feast."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "You have recognised it easily; you are quick-witted in everything. But come, let us think how this shall be done. Either you go first into the stately palace and go in among the suitors, and I will stay behind here; or, if you like, stay here and I will go in front of you. But do not wait long, in case somebody sees you outside and pelts you or strikes you. I tell you to think about that."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "I see, I take your point; you are telling this to a man who understands. Go on ahead, and I will stay here; I am not unused to blows and peltings. My heart is steady; I have suffered a great deal on the waves and in war. Let this be added to what has gone before. But a ravening belly is a thing no man can hide, an accursed plague that brings a great many troubles on men. It is because of the belly that the benched ships are fitted out too, carrying harm to enemies over the restless sea."

So they talked to one another. And a dog that was lying there raised his head and pricked up his ears: Argos, the dog of Odysseus, who endures everything, whom he had bred himself long ago and had no joy of, because he went to sacred Ilios before that. In the old days the young men used to take the dog out to hunt wild goats and deer and hares; but now he lay neglected, with his master gone, in the deep dung of mules and cattle which lay in heaps in front of the doors, until the slaves of Odysseus should take it away to manure his wide lands. There the dog Argos lay, full of vermin.

And even now, when he saw Odysseus standing near, he wagged his tail and dropped both his ears; but he had no strength left to come nearer to his master. And Odysseus looked aside and wiped away a tear, hiding it easily from Eumaeus; and at once he questioned him and said:

"Eumaeus, it is strange that this dog lies here in the dung. He is fine in form, but I cannot tell clearly whether he had speed to match his beauty, or whether he was only the kind of dog men keep at their tables for show."

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "That is the dog of a man who has died far away. If he were still in body and in action what he was when Odysseus left him and went to Troy, you would be amazed straight away, seeing his speed and his strength. Nothing he started in the depths of the thick wood could get away from him, and he was keen of scent in tracking too. But now he is in a bad way, and his master has died far from his own country, and the heedless women give him no care. Slaves, when their masters lose their power, no longer want to do honest service afterwards; because Zeus, whose voice carries far, takes half a man's worth away from him when the day of slavery comes on him."

With that he went into the stately house and straight to the hall, to join the company of the lordly suitors. And as for Argos, the fate of black death took him at once, when he had seen Odysseus in the twentieth year.

Now as the swineherd came through the hall, godlike Telemachus was far the first to see him, and called him quickly to his side with a nod. And Eumaeus looked about and took a stool that lay near, where the carver used to sit when he was carving the many joints of meat for the suitors as they feasted in the hall. He took this and set it at the table of Telemachus, opposite him, and sat down there himself. And a herald took a portion of meat and set it in front of him, and bread out of the basket.

And Odysseus came into the palace after him in the likeness of a miserable old beggar, leaning on a staff, and the clothes he wore on his body were wretched. He sat down on the ashen threshold inside the doorway, leaning against a post of cypress wood which a carpenter had planed skilfully long ago and made straight to the line.

Then Telemachus called the swineherd to him, and took a whole loaf out of the beautiful basket, and all the meat his hands could hold in their grasp, and said to him: "Take this and give it to the stranger over there, and tell him to go about and beg from the suitors, every one of them. Shame is no good companion for a man in need."

So he spoke, and the swineherd went when he had heard this, and came up to Odysseus and spoke winged words to him: "Stranger, Telemachus gives you these, and tells you to go about and beg from the suitors, every one of them. Shame, he says, is no good thing in a beggar man."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "King Zeus, grant that Telemachus may be blessed among men, and may have everything his heart desires."

He spoke, and took the food in both hands and set it down there in front of his feet on his miserable bag. And he ate as long as the singer sang in the halls. But when he had dined and the divine singer was leaving off, the suitors broke into uproar through the halls; and Athena came close to Odysseus, son of Laertes, and roused him to go among the suitors and gather bits of bread, and learn which of them were righteous and which lawless. But even so she did not mean to save one of them from ruin.

So he set out to beg from every man, beginning on the right, holding his hand out on every side as though he had long been a beggar. And they pitied him and gave, and marvelled at him, asking one another who he was and where he came from. Then Melanthius the goatherd spoke among them:

"Listen to me, suitors of the glorious queen, about this stranger; I have seen him before. It was certainly the swineherd who brought him here, but I do not know for certain about the man himself, where he says he was born."

So he spoke, and Antinous rebuked the swineherd, saying: "Notorious swineherd, why did you bring this man to the city?
