Have we not enough vagabonds without him, nuisances of beggars to spoil our feast? Is it not enough for you that they gather here and eat up your master's substance, that you invite this one too?"

And you answered him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Antinous, those are not fair words, noble as you are. Who ever goes out and invites a stranger from abroad himself, unless it is one of those who are masters of some public craft — a prophet, or a healer of ills, or a builder, or a divine singer, who gives delight with his song? Those men are invited all over the boundless earth. But nobody would invite a beggar to be a burden to himself. You are always harsh above all the suitors to the slaves of Odysseus, and to me most of all; but I do not care, as long as my lady, steady Penelope, lives in the hall, and godlike Telemachus."

Then clear-headed Telemachus said to him: "Be quiet; do not answer that man with many words. Antinous is always provoking people to anger with harsh words, and urging the others on too."

And with that he spoke winged words to Antinous: "Antinous, you certainly care for me well, like a father for his son, when you tell me to drive that stranger from the hall with a word of compulsion. May the god never bring such a thing to pass. Take something and give it to him; I do not grudge it, I tell you to give it. And in this matter take no notice of my mother, or of any of the slaves who are in the house of godlike Odysseus. But the thought in your breast is quite different; you are far more eager to eat yourself than to give to another."

Then Antinous answered him and said: "Telemachus, you big talker with no check on your nerve, what a thing to say. If all the suitors handed him as much as I shall, this house would keep him at a distance for three whole months."

So he spoke, and seized the footstool he used to rest his shining feet on as he feasted, and showed it from under the table where it lay. But all the rest gave gifts, and filled his bag with bread and bits of meat. And Odysseus was about to go back to the threshold and to have tried the Achaeans at no cost to himself; but he stopped by Antinous and said to him:

"Friend, give me something. You do not seem to me the meanest of the Achaeans, but the noblest, because you are like a king. So it is fitting that you should give a better portion of bread than the rest; and then I would make your fame known all over the boundless earth. I too once lived in a house of my own among men, a rich man in a wealthy house, and often gave gifts to a wanderer, whoever he was and with whatever need he came. And I had slaves past counting, and everything else in plenty, the things by which men live well and are reputed wealthy.

"But Zeus, son of Cronos, brought it all to nothing — that must have been his pleasure — who sent me off with roaming pirates to go to Egypt, a long voyage, so that I should meet my ruin; and I moored my curved ships in the river Aegyptus. Then I told my trusty companions to stay there by the ships and guard the ships, and I sent scouts out to go to places of outlook. But my companions gave way to their own wantonness, and, led on by their own strength, set about wasting the fine fields of the men of Egypt straight away; and they carried off the women and the little children and killed the men; and the cry went up to the city quickly. Then, hearing the shouting, the people came out at daybreak, and the whole plain was filled with foot-soldiers and chariots and the flashing of bronze. And Zeus, who hurls the thunderbolt, threw an evil panic on my companions, and none of them had the courage to stand his ground and face the enemy; trouble surrounded us on every side. So they killed a great many of us with the sharp bronze, and led others up to their city alive, to work for them because they had to. But they gave me to a friend of theirs who met them, to take to Cyprus, to Dmetor, son of Iasus, who ruled mightily over Cyprus; and from there I have come here now, in bitter distress."

Then Antinous answered him and said: "What god has brought this pest here to spoil our feast? Stand off over there in the middle, away from my table, or you may come to a bitter Egypt and a bitter Cyprus, seeing that you are a bold and shameless beggar. You come up to every man in turn, and they give recklessly; there is no restraint or scruple in giving freely from another man's goods, since each of them has plenty beside him."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks drew back and said to him: "So it seems you have no wits to match your beauty. You would not give a suppliant a grain of salt out of your own substance, you who now, sitting at another man's table, could not bring yourself to take a piece of the bread and give it me — and here it lies in plenty at your hand."

So he spoke, and Antinous grew angrier still at heart, and gave him a black look from under his brows and spoke winged words to him: "Now I do not think you will get out of the hall in good order any more, since you go so far as to speak abuse."

With that he seized the footstool and flung it, and struck Odysseus at the base of the right shoulder, where it joins the back. But he stood firm as a rock, and the missile of Antinous did not make him reel; he only shook his head in silence, turning evil over in the depths of his heart. Then he went back to the threshold and sat down and laid down his well-filled bag; and he spoke among the suitors:

"Listen to me, suitors of the glorious queen, so that I can say what the heart in my breast tells me. There is no pain of heart and no grief when a man is struck fighting for his own possessions, for his cattle or for his white sheep. But Antinous has struck me for my wretched belly's sake, an accursed plague that brings a great many troubles on men. And if there are gods and avengers for beggars, may the doom of death come on Antinous before his marriage."

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered him: "Sit still and eat, stranger, or go somewhere else; or the young men will drag you by hand or foot through the house for words like these, and strip all the skin off you."

So he spoke, but they were all filled with enormous indignation; and one of the proud young men would say: "Antinous, you did wrong to strike that wretched wanderer. Doomed man, what if he happens to be some god come down from heaven? The gods do take the likeness of strangers from far away and put on every kind of shape, and visit the cities, looking at the violence and the righteousness of men."

That is what the suitors said, but Antinous paid no attention to their words. And Telemachus nursed great grief in his heart at the blow, though he let no tear fall from his eyelids to the ground; he only shook his head in silence, turning evil over in the depths of his heart.

But when wise Penelope heard of the man being struck in the hall, she spoke among her women and said: "May the famous archer Apollo strike you the same way."

And Eurynome the housekeeper said to her: "I wish our prayers might be fulfilled. Then not one of these men would come to fair-throned Dawn."

And wise Penelope answered her: "Nurse, they are all enemies, because they contrive evil. But Antinous more than any of them is like black fate. Some wretched stranger is roaming through the house, begging alms of the men, because want drives him to it, and all the others filled his bag and gave him things, and Antinous flung a footstool and struck him at the base of the right shoulder."

So she spoke among her women, sitting in her room, while noble Odysseus was at his meal. Then she called the good swineherd to her and said:

"Go, good Eumaeus, and tell the stranger to come here, so that I can greet him and ask him whether he has heard anything of Odysseus, who endures everything, or seen him with his eyes. He looks like a man who has wandered far."

And you answered her, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "I wish the Achaeans would keep quiet, my queen; he says such things as would charm your very soul. I had him with me three nights, and kept him three days in my hut — because it was to me he came first when he ran away from a ship — and he had still not finished the tale of his sufferings. As a man gazes at a singer who sings songs of longing to mortals which the gods have taught him, and their desire to hear him has no end, however long he sings, so he charmed me as he sat in my house. He says he is a friend of Odysseus by descent, and that he lives in Crete, where the race of Minos is. From there he has come on this journey here, suffering as he wanders on and on. And he insists he has heard news of Odysseus, alive and near at hand, in the rich land of the Thesprotians; and he is bringing a great deal of treasure home."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Go and call him here, so that he can tell me to my face. And as for these men, let them make sport, sitting in the doorway or here in the house, since their hearts are merry. Their own possessions lie untouched in their homes, bread and sweet wine, and their servants feed on them. But they crowd our house day after day, killing our oxen and sheep and fat goats, and feasting and drinking the flaming wine recklessly, and all this wealth is being wasted; because there is no man here as Odysseus was, to keep ruin off the house. But if Odysseus should come back to his own country, he and his son would take vengeance on these men for their violence at once."

So she spoke, and Telemachus sneezed loudly, and the whole room echoed round about extraordinarily. And Penelope laughed, and at once spoke winged words to Eumaeus:

"Go, call the stranger here in front of me. Do you not notice that my son has sneezed at everything I said? So utter death will fall on the suitors, every one of them, and not one of them will escape death and the fates. And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. If I find that he speaks the truth in everything, I will dress him in a cloak and a tunic, good clothes."

So she spoke, and the swineherd went when he had heard this; and he came up to Odysseus and spoke winged words to him: "Sir stranger, wise Penelope is calling for you, the mother of Telemachus, and her heart tells her to make enquiries about her husband, though she has suffered a great deal. And if she finds you speak the truth in everything, she will dress you in a cloak and a tunic, which you need most of all. And as for your food, you shall beg it through the country and feed your belly, and whoever wants to will give it you."

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus answered him: "Eumaeus, I will soon tell the whole truth to the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope. I know all about Odysseus, and we have borne affliction in common. But I am afraid of this crowd of harsh suitors, whose wantonness and violence reach the iron sky. Just now, as I was going through the hall doing no harm, that man struck me and hurt me, and neither Telemachus nor anyone else did anything to keep the blow off. So tell Penelope to wait in the halls, eager as she is, until sunset; and then let her ask me about her husband and the day of his homecoming, giving me a seat nearer the fire, since the clothes I have on are poor — and you know this yourself, because it was to you that I made my plea first."

So he spoke, and the swineherd went when he had heard this. And as he crossed the threshold Penelope said to him: "You are not bringing him, Eumaeus. What does the wanderer mean by this? Is he afraid of somebody beyond all measure, or does he feel ashamed for no reason in the house? Shame is a bad thing in a beggar."

And you answered her, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "He speaks rightly, and any other man would think the same, in trying to avoid the insolence of overbearing men. He tells you to wait until sunset. And for yourself too it is far more seemly, my queen, to speak to the stranger alone and hear what he says."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "The stranger is not without wisdom; he sees how it may be. There are no mortal men, I think, who contrive such wicked folly in their wantonness as these do."

So she spoke, and the good swineherd went off into the crowd of the suitors when he had told her everything. And at once he spoke winged words to Telemachus, holding his head close to him so that the others should not hear:

"Friend, I am going out to guard the pigs and everything there, your living and mine; and you take charge of everything here. Keep yourself safe first of all, and let your mind be on guard in case some harm comes to you; a great many of the Achaeans are contriving evil, and I hope Zeus destroys them utterly before harm falls on us."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "So it shall be, father; go on your way when you have eaten. And come in the morning and bring good victims. But everything here shall be a care to me and to the immortals."

So he spoke, and the swineherd sat down again on the polished chair. And when he had satisfied his heart with meat and drink he went off to the pigs, and left the courts and the hall full of banqueters. And they were making merry with dance and song, because evening had come on by now.

Book Eighteen

Now there came up a public beggar, who used to beg through the town of Ithaca and was known for his greedy belly, eating and drinking without end. He had no strength or force in him, but in bulk he was big enough to look at. Arnaeus was his name, which his honoured mother had given him at his birth; but all the young men called him Irus, because he used to run errands whenever anybody told him to.

He came now, and set about driving Odysseus out of his own house, and began abusing him with winged words: "Get out of the doorway, old man, or you will be dragged out by the foot before long. Do you not see that they are all winking at me and telling me to drag you out? I am ashamed to do it myself. Get up, or our quarrel will come to blows."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave him a black look from under his brows and answered him: "My good fellow, I am doing you no harm in word or deed, and I do not grudge it that anybody should give to you, however big the portion he takes up. This threshold will hold us both, and you have no need to be jealous over other people's goods. You seem to me to be a vagrant, as I am; and as for good fortune, it is the gods who are likely to give us that. But do not provoke me too far with your fists, in case you make me angry and I foul your chest and your lips with blood, old man as I am. Then I should have more peace tomorrow, because I do not think you would come back a second time to the hall of Odysseus, son of Laertes."

Then the vagrant Irus grew angry and said to him: "Listen how glibly the filthy wretch talks, like an old kitchen woman. But I will contrive trouble for him, hitting him left and right, and I will scatter all the teeth out of his jaws on the ground, like a pig caught wasting the corn. Gird yourself up now, so that all these men may know about our fighting. But how are you going to fight a younger man?"

So on the polished threshold in front of the high doors they stirred each other's rage in good earnest. And the strong and mighty Antinous heard the two of them, and broke into a merry laugh and spoke among the suitors:

"Friends, nothing like this has ever happened before: a god has brought sport of this kind into the house. That stranger and Irus are provoking each other to blows. Come, let us set them on quickly."

So he spoke, and they all sprang up laughing and gathered round the tattered beggars. And Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them and said:

"Listen to me, proud suitors, and let me say something. There are goats' paunches lying here at the fire, which we set there for supper when we had filled them with fat and blood. Whichever of the two wins and proves himself the better man, let him get up and choose which of these he likes for himself. And besides that, he shall always feast with us, and we will not let any other beggar join our company and beg from us."

So spoke Antinous, and his word pleased them. Then Odysseus of the many tricks spoke among them, with cunning in his mind: "Friends, an old man worn down with trouble has no business fighting a younger one. But my belly, that worker of evil, urges me on, to be beaten down by his blows. But come now, all of you swear me a great oath, that no man will do Irus a favour and deal me a foul blow with a heavy hand, and beat me down for this fellow by force."

So he spoke, and they all swore the oath not to strike him, as he told them. But when they had sworn and finished the oath, the strong young Telemachus spoke among them again:

"Stranger, if your heart and your proud spirit tell you to beat this fellow off, then do not be afraid of any man of the Achaeans, because whoever strikes you will have more than you to fight. I am your host, and the princes agree with me, Antinous and Eurymachus, both men of prudence."

So he spoke, and they all praised his words. But Odysseus girded his rags round his loins and showed his thighs, handsome and big, and his broad shoulders came into view, and his chest and mighty arms. And Athena came near and made the limbs of the shepherd of the people greater. Then all the suitors marvelled exceedingly, and one would say with a glance at his neighbour:

"Irus will very soon be un-Irused, and it will be a ruin of his own bringing, with such a thigh as that old man shows from under his rags."

So they spoke, and the mind of Irus was miserably shaken; but even so the serving men girded him and led him out because they had to, in a state of dread, and the flesh trembled on his limbs. And Antinous scolded him and said to him:

"You would be better off now not living, you braggart, or never having been born, if you quail like this and are so terribly afraid of a man who is old and worn down by the trouble that has come on him. But I tell you outright, and this word will come to pass: if this fellow beats you and proves the better man, I will fling you into a black ship and send you to the mainland, to king Echetus, the maimer of all men, who will cut off your nose and your ears with the pitiless bronze and pull out your vitals and give them raw to the dogs to tear."

So he spoke, and a still greater trembling seized the other man's limbs; and they led him into the ring, and both men put up their hands.

Then much-enduring noble Odysseus was divided in his mind, whether to hit him so that the life should leave him where he fell, or to deal him a light blow and stretch him on the ground. And as he turned it over, this seemed the better course, to deal him a light blow, so that the Achaeans should not take notice of him. So when they had put up their hands, Irus let drive at his right shoulder; but Odysseus hit him on the neck under the ear and crushed in the bones, and the red blood ran out of his mouth at once, and down he fell in the dust with a moan, and gnashed his teeth, kicking the ground with his feet. And the lordly suitors threw up their hands and were nearly dying of laughter.

Then Odysseus took him by the foot and dragged him out through the doorway until he came to the court and the gates of the portico. And he set him down and leaned him against the wall of the court and thrust his staff into his hand and spoke to him with winged words:

"Sit there now and scare off the pigs and dogs, and do not set yourself up as lord over strangers and beggars, miserable as you are, or you may meet with something worse still."

He spoke, and flung his miserable bag round his shoulders, full of holes and slung on a twisted cord; and he went back to the threshold and sat down. And the suitors went in, laughing merrily, and greeted him, saying:

"May Zeus grant you what you want most, stranger, and the other immortal gods, and the dearest wish of your heart, since you have stopped this insatiable fellow from begging in the country. We shall soon take him to the mainland, to king Echetus, the maimer of all men."

So they spoke, and noble Odysseus was glad at the word of omen. And Antinous set the great paunch in front of him, filled with fat and blood; and Amphinomus took two loaves out of the basket and set them in front of him and pledged him in a golden cup and said:

"Greetings, sir stranger; may good fortune be yours in time to come, though at present you are the thrall of many sorrows."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Amphinomus, you seem to me a man of prudence; and your father was such a man too, because I have heard of his good name, that Nisus of Dulichium was brave and wealthy. You are his son, they say, and you seem a man who speaks softly. So I will tell you, and you take heed and listen.

"Earth nurtures nothing feebler than a man, of all the things that breathe and move on the earth. He thinks he will never suffer evil in time to come, as long as the gods give him prosperity and his knees are quick; and when the blessed gods decree him sorrow, he bears that too, badly against his will, with a steady heart. The spirit of men on the earth is just like the day the father of gods and men brings on them. I too was once likely to be prosperous among men, and I did a great many wanton things, giving way to my might and my strength and trusting in my father and my brothers. So let no man ever be lawless at any time, but let him keep in silence whatever gifts the gods give.

"I see the suitors contriving wantonness, wasting the wealth and dishonouring the wife of a man who, I tell you, will not be away from his friends and his own country long; he is very near. And may some god take you home out of here, and may you not meet him when he comes home to his own dear country. Because I do not think the suitors and he will part from one another without bloodshed, once he comes under his own roof."

So he spoke, and poured an offering and drank of the honey-sweet wine, and then gave the cup back into the hands of the marshaller of the people. And Amphinomus went through the hall with a heavy heart, bowing his head; because his spirit foreboded ruin. Yet even so he did not escape his fate: Athena bound him too, to be killed outright at the hands of Telemachus and by his spear. So he sat down again on the chair he had got up from.

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena put it into the heart of the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, to show herself to the suitors, so that she might set their hearts fluttering and win greater honour from her husband and her son than before. Then she laughed a meaningless laugh and said to the nurse:

"Eurynome, my heart longs, though it has never longed before, to show myself to the suitors, hateful as they are. And I would say a word to my son as well that will do him good: that he should not always keep company with the overbearing suitors, who speak him fair and have evil plans afterwards."

Then Eurynome the housekeeper said to her: "Yes, child, all this you have said rightly. Go, then, and tell your son your word and do not hide it. But first wash your body and anoint your face, and do not go as you are, with both cheeks stained with tears. Go; it is a bad thing to grieve for ever without stopping. Your son is of an age now, and it has been your dearest prayer to the immortals to see him a bearded man."

Then wise Penelope answered her again: "Eurynome, do not beguile me like this, in your love for me, telling me to wash my body and anoint myself with oil. The gods who hold Olympus destroyed all the beauty I had on the day my lord went away in the hollow ships. But tell Autonoe and Hippodameia to come to me, so that they can stand by my side in the hall. I will not go among men alone; I am ashamed."

So she spoke, and the old woman went out through the room to bring the women word and tell them to come.

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena thought of something else. She poured sweet sleep over the daughter of Icarius, and she leaned back and slept there on her couch, and all her joints went slack. And meanwhile the beautiful goddess was giving her immortal gifts, so that the Achaeans should marvel at her. First she made her lovely face fair with an ambrosial balm, the kind Cytherea of the fair crown anoints herself with when she goes into the lovely dance of the Graces; and she made her taller too, and statelier to look at, and made her whiter than newly sawn ivory.

Now when she had done this the beautiful goddess went away, and the white-armed women came out of the room, drawing near with the sound of talking. And sweet sleep let Penelope go, and she rubbed her cheeks with her hands and said:

"In my utter wretchedness a soft slumber wrapped me round. I wish pure Artemis would give me a death as soft as that even now, so that I might not go on wasting my life away with sorrow at heart, longing for the many excellences of my dear husband, because he stood out above the Achaeans."

With that she went down from the bright upper room, not alone, because two women went with her. Now when that lovely woman reached the suitors she stood by the doorpost of the well-built hall, holding her shining veil in front of her face, with a faithful maid on either side of her. And the knees of the suitors were loosened at once and their hearts enchanted with love, and every one of them prayed to be the man to lie beside her.

But she spoke to Telemachus, her dear son: "Telemachus, your mind and your thoughts are no longer steady as they used to be. When you were only a child you used to turn more cunning thoughts over in your mind; but now that you are grown and have reached the bounds of manhood, and would be called a rich man's son by anyone from far away who looked only at your height and your looks, your mind and your thoughts are no longer right as they were. What a thing this is that has been done in these halls, that you have let this stranger be maltreated so. What if the stranger, sitting in our house like this, should come to some harm through bad handling? Then shame and disgrace would fall on you among men."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Mother, I do not take it badly that you are angry over this. But I know each thing in my heart and understand it, the good and the bad, whereas before this I was only a child. But I cannot plan everything wisely, because these men here thwart my will, keeping close by me, one on this side and one on that, with evil purpose, and I have nobody to help me. And I can tell you, this fight between the stranger and Irus did not turn out the way the suitors wanted; the stranger proved the better man. Father Zeus, and Athena, and Apollo — I wish the suitors were beaten down like that in our halls even now, and were hanging their heads, some in the court and some inside the hall, and every man's limbs were loosened, the way Irus sits over there now by the gate of the court, hanging his head like a drunken man, and cannot stand upright on his feet or go home wherever it is he goes, because his limbs are loosened."

So they talked to one another. But Eurymachus spoke to Penelope and said: "Daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, if all the Achaeans through Iasian Argos could see you, there would be even more suitors feasting in your halls from tomorrow on, because you are ahead of all women in beauty and in stature and in the wise heart inside you."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Eurymachus, the immortals destroyed every excellence of mine, of beauty and of form, on the day the Argives embarked for Ilios, and my husband Odysseus went with them. If he might only come and watch over this life of mine, my fame would be greater and finer. But now I am in sorrow, so many troubles has some god brought on me.

"When he went out and left his own country, he took my right hand by the wrist and said: 'Wife, I do not think the well-greaved Achaeans will all come back from Troy safe and unhurt. They say the Trojans are men of war, spear-throwers and drawers of the bow and drivers of swift horses, the kind who most quickly decide the great struggle of an equal war. So I do not know whether the god will bring me back, or whether I shall be cut off there in the land of Troy. Have charge of everything here. Remember my father and my mother in the halls as you do now, or more so, while I am far away. And when you see my son a bearded man, marry whom you like and leave your house.'

"That is what he said, and now all of it is coming about. The night will come when a hateful marriage will fall to me, accursed as I am, whose happiness Zeus has taken away. And this has come as a bitter grief to my heart and soul: yours was never the way of suitors before this. Men who want to court a woman of worth, a rich man's daughter, and compete with one another, bring cattle and fine flocks of their own, a banquet for the bride's friends, and give her glorious gifts; they do not eat up somebody else's living without paying for it."

So she spoke, and much-enduring noble Odysseus was glad, because she was drawing gifts out of them and beguiling their souls with gentle words, while her mind was set on something else.

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke to her again and said: "Daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, as for gifts, take them, if any man of the Achaeans wants to bring them here; it is not right to refuse a gift. But we shall not go to our lands or anywhere else until you marry whichever of the Achaeans is best."

So spoke Antinous, and his word pleased them, and each man sent a herald to bring his gifts. For Antinous the herald brought a large and beautiful robe, richly embroidered, with golden brooches in it, twelve of them, fitted with curved clasps. And another brought Eurymachus a chain at once, cunningly made of gold and strung with amber beads, bright as the sun. His squires brought Eurydamas a pair of earrings with three clustering drops, and great grace shone from them. And out of the house of lord Peisander, son of Polyctor, his squire brought a necklace, a jewel of extraordinary beauty. So one of the Achaeans brought one fine gift and one another.

And she, that lovely woman, went up to her upper room afterwards, and her women carried the beautiful gifts for her. But the suitors turned to dance and cheerful song, and made merry, and waited for the evening to come on. And as they made merry, the dark evening came on them.

They set up three braziers in the hall at once to give them light, and put dry faggots round them, long seasoned and hard and newly split with the axe; and in the spaces between they set torches. And the women of Odysseus, who endures everything, kindled the flame by turns. Then Odysseus himself, born of Zeus, of the many tricks, spoke among the maids and said:

"Women of Odysseus, who has been gone so long, go to the rooms where your honoured queen is, and twist the yarn beside her and gladden her heart, sitting in the room, or card the wool with your hands. I will give light to all these men. Even if they want to wait for fair-throned Dawn, they will not outlast me; I am a man who can endure a great deal."

So he spoke, and the maids broke into laughter and glanced at one another. And Melantho of the lovely cheeks scolded him shamefully — Melantho, whom Dolius had fathered, but whom Penelope had reared and cherished like her own child and given playthings to whenever she wanted them. Even so she had no sorrow in her heart for Penelope, but loved Eurymachus and used to lie with him. She scolded Odysseus with abusive words:

"Wretched stranger, you are a crack-brained creature, unwilling to go and sleep at a smithy or at a public lodge, but chattering on and on here, unabashed among a crowd of lords, and with no fear in your heart. Wine has certainly mastered your wits, or else your mind is always like this and you babble idly. Are you beside yourself because you beat that vagrant Irus? Take care that a better man than Irus does not stand up against you before long, to beat you about the head with heavy hands and foul you with streams of blood and send you out of the house."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave her a black look from under his brows and answered her: "I shall go over there presently, you shameless thing, and tell Telemachus what you say, so that he can cut you limb from limb on the spot."

So he spoke, and with his words scattered the women, who fled through the hall, and the limbs of each of them went slack beneath her in terror, because they thought he spoke the truth.

But Odysseus took his stand by the burning braziers to give light, and looked at all the men; and his heart inside him was turning over other things, which were not to go unfulfilled.

But Athena would not let the proud suitors hold off from bitter outrage at all, so that the pain might sink deeper still into the heart of Odysseus, son of Laertes. So Eurymachus, son of Polybus, began to speak among them, jeering at Odysseus and making sport for his companions:

"Listen to me, suitors of the glorious queen, and let me say what the heart in my breast tells me. This man has not come to the palace of Odysseus without the will of the gods; at any rate there is a glare of torches coming from him — from his head, because there is no hair on it, not a trace."

And with that he called to Odysseus, sacker of cities: "Stranger, would you have a mind to work for hire, if I took you on at an outlying farm — your pay would be assured — gathering stones for walls and planting tall trees? There I would provide you with food the year round and clothe you and give you sandals for your feet. But since you have learned nothing but evil ways, you will not want to busy yourself with work, but would rather go skulking through the country, so that you have something to feed your insatiable belly with."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Eurymachus, I wish the two of us might have a match at working in the spring season, when the long days come, at mowing grass — I with a curved scythe in my hands and you with another like it, and the grass in plenty, so that we might test our work, fasting till late evening. Or I wish there were oxen to drive, the best there are, tawny and big, both of them well fed with grass, of the same age and the same power to bear the yoke, tireless in strength, and a field of four acres, and the soil giving way before the plough: then you would see whether I could cut a straight furrow to the end or not. Or I wish the son of Cronos might bring war on us today from wherever he liked, and I had a shield and two spears and a bronze helmet that fitted my temples well: then you would see me mixing among the front-rank fighters, and you would not prate and taunt me about this belly of mine.

"But you are thoroughly insolent, and your heart is cruel, and you think yourself some great and mighty man because you keep company with a few weak ones. If Odysseus could come back and reach his own country, those doors over there, wide as they are, would prove far too narrow for you as you fled out through the doorway."

So he spoke, and Eurymachus grew angrier still at heart, and gave him a black look from under his brows and spoke winged words to him: "Wretch, I will do you harm presently, for prating like this, unabashed among a crowd of lords, and with no fear in your heart. Wine has certainly mastered your wits, or else your mind is always like this and you babble idly. Are you beside yourself because you beat that vagrant Irus?"

With that he seized a footstool; but Odysseus sat down at the knees of Amphinomus of Dulichium, in fear of Eurymachus. And so Eurymachus struck a cup-bearer on the right hand, and the wine-jug fell to the ground with a clang, and the boy groaned and fell backwards in the dust.

Then the suitors broke into uproar through the shadowy halls, and one man would say with a glance at his neighbour: "I wish that stranger had died somewhere else in his wanderings before he came here; then he would never have brought all this tumult among us. As it is we are brawling about beggars, and there will be no pleasure in our rich feast, since worse things are winning."

Then the strong young Telemachus spoke among them: "Strange sirs, you are mad, and you no longer hide that you have eaten and drunk; some god must be moving you. Now that you have feasted well, go home and rest, when your spirits tell you to. I drive nobody out."

So he spoke, and they all bit their lips and marvelled at Telemachus, that he spoke so boldly. Then Amphinomus spoke to them — he was the son of the noble prince Nisus, son of Aretias:

"Friends, no man should grow angry at what has been fairly said and answer with quarrelling words. Do not abuse this stranger any more, or any of the slaves in the house of godlike Odysseus. Come, let the bearer pour drops into the cups for an offering, so that we can pour our offerings and go home to rest. And as for this stranger, let us leave him in the halls of Odysseus to be looked after by Telemachus; it is to his house that he has come."

So he spoke, and the words he said pleased them all. Then a bowl was mixed for them by lord Mulius, a herald from Dulichium, who was squire to Amphinomus; and he served it out to all of them, coming up to each in turn. And they poured offerings to the blessed gods and drank the honey-sweet wine. And when they had poured their offerings and drunk as much as they wanted, they went off, each man to his own house, to rest.

Book Nineteen

So noble Odysseus was left behind in the hall, planning the killing of the suitors with Athena's help; and at once he spoke winged words to Telemachus:

"Telemachus, you must lay the weapons of war away inside, every one of them; and when the suitors miss them and question you, beguile them with gentle words, and say: 'I have put them away out of the smoke, since they are no longer like the ones Odysseus left behind him when he went off to Troy, but are all fouled as far as the breath of the fire has reached them. And a god has put this greater fear in my heart besides: that when you are heated with wine you may start a quarrel among yourselves and wound one another, and so bring shame on your feasting and your courting. Iron draws a man to it of itself.'"

So he spoke, and Telemachus did as his dear father said, and called the nurse Eurycleia and said to her: "Nurse, shut the women up in their rooms, I tell you, while I lay away my father's weapons in the store-room — the fine weapons which lie uncared-for and dulled by the smoke in the hall, since my father went away and I was still a child. Now I mean to lay them away where the breath of the fire will not reach them."

Then his dear nurse Eurycleia answered him: "Yes, child, I wish you would always take thought to look after the house and guard all its wealth. But come, who is to fetch a light and carry it for you, since you would not let the maids go in front of you, who could have given light?"

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "This stranger here. I will let no man be idle who touches my portion of meal, even if he has come from far away."

So he spoke, and her word stayed unwinged, and she locked the doors of the stately hall.

Then the two of them sprang up, Odysseus and his glorious son, and set about carrying in the helmets and the bossy shields and the sharp-pointed spears; and Pallas Athena went in front of them carrying a golden lamp and made a most beautiful light. Then Telemachus suddenly said to his father:

"Father, this is a great marvel that my eyes are looking at. The walls of the house and the fine beams and cross-beams of fir and the pillars that reach up high glow before my eyes as if with the light of a blazing fire. Some god must be inside, one of those who hold the wide sky."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Hush, hold back your thought and ask no question. This is the way of the gods who hold Olympus. Go and rest, and I will stay behind here, so that I can stir up the minds of the maids and of your mother still more; and she will ask me about each thing separately, weeping."

So he spoke, and Telemachus went out through the hall by the light of blazing torches, to go to his room and lie down where he always used to rest when sweet sleep came on him. He lay down there now too, and waited for the bright Dawn.

But noble Odysseus was left behind in the hall, planning the killing of the suitors with Athena's help.

Then wise Penelope came out of her room, like Artemis or golden Aphrodite; and they set a chair for her by the fire where she always sat, inlaid with spirals of ivory and silver, which the craftsman Icmalius had made long ago, and had set a footstool below it for the feet, which was part of the chair, and a great fleece used to be laid on it. Wise Penelope sat down on this, and the white-armed maids came out of the women's hall. They set about taking away the plentiful food and the tables and the cups the lordly men had been drinking from, and they threw the embers out of the braziers onto the floor and piled fresh logs on the braziers in plenty, to give light and warmth.

But Melantho started scolding Odysseus a second time: "Stranger, are you still going to be a plague to us through the night, roaming through the house and spying on the women? Get out, you wretch, and be content with your supper, or you will be struck with a torch and go out that way."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave her a black look from under his brows and answered her: "My good woman, why do you attack me like this with an angry heart? Is it because I am filthy and wear mean clothes on my body and beg through the country? Necessity drives me to it. That is what beggars and vagabonds are like. I too once lived in a house of my own among men, a rich man in a wealthy house, and often gave gifts to a wanderer, whoever he was and with whatever need he came. And I had slaves past counting and everything else in plenty, the things by which men live well and are reputed wealthy. But Zeus, son of Cronos, brought it all to nothing; that must have been his pleasure.

"So take care, woman, that you do not lose one day all the glory that puts you above the other women now; in case your mistress grows angry and is furious with you, or Odysseus comes home — because there is still room for hope. And if he is dead, as it seems, and is never to come back, then his son is by now such a man, by the favour of Apollo, as he was — Telemachus. It does not escape him if any of the women in the halls behaves wantonly; he is no longer the child he was."

So he spoke, and wise Penelope heard him; and she rebuked the maid and said to her: "You bold and shameless thing, be sure your outrageous behaviour is not hidden from me at all, and you shall wipe out the stain of it with your own head. You knew perfectly well, because you heard it from my own lips, that I meant to question the stranger in my halls about my husband; because I am in bitter distress."

And with that she spoke to Eurynome the housekeeper, and said: "Eurynome, bring a chair here and a fleece on it, so that the stranger can sit down and tell his tale and listen to me; I want to ask him about everything."

So she spoke, and Eurynome quickly brought a polished chair and set it in place, and threw a fleece on it. Then much-enduring noble Odysseus sat down on it, and wise Penelope spoke first:

"Stranger, this is the question I will ask you first myself. Who are you among men, and where from? Where is your city, and where are your parents?"

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Lady, nobody of all the mortals on the boundless earth could find fault with you; your fame goes up to the wide sky, like the fame of some blameless king who, with the fear of the gods in his heart, is lord over many strong men and upholds justice; and the black earth bears wheat and barley, and the trees are loaded with fruit, and the flocks bring forth young without stopping, and the sea yields fish, all from his good leading, and the people prosper under him. So question me now in your house about anything else, but do not ask about my family and my own country, in case you fill my heart with still more pain as I think of it; I am a man of many sorrows. And besides, it is not fitting that I should sit weeping and wailing in another man's house; it is a bad thing to grieve for ever without stopping. I would not have one of your maids, or you yourself, vexed with me and saying that I swim in tears because my mind is heavy with wine."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Stranger, the immortals destroyed every excellence of mine, of beauty and of form, on the day the Argives embarked for Ilios and my husband Odysseus went with them. If he might only come and watch over this life of mine, my fame would be greater and finer. But now I am in sorrow, so many troubles has some god brought on me. All the princes who hold sway over the islands — Dulichium and Same and wooded Zacynthus — and those who live round about in clear-seen Ithaca itself, all of them court me against my will and lay my house waste. So I take no notice of strangers, or of suppliants, or of heralds at all, whose trade is a public one; but I waste my heart away longing for Odysseus.

"So these men press on my marriage, and I wind a skein of tricks. First some god breathed the thought into my heart to set up a great web in my halls and start weaving a robe — fine in the thread and very wide; and I said to them at once: 'Young men, my suitors, since noble Odysseus is dead, be patient about marrying me, however eager you are, until I finish this robe. I do not want my spinning to be wasted. It is a shroud for lord Laertes, for the time when the cruel fate of death lays him low, so that none of the Achaean women in the country can be angry with me for letting a man who won so much lie without a shroud.'

"That is what I said, and their proud hearts agreed. And then day by day I would weave at the great web, and by night I would unravel it, when I had had torches set beside me. So for three years I kept the Achaeans from finding out, and fooled them; but when the fourth year came, as the seasons rolled on, as the months waned and the many days came round in their course, then with the help of my maids, shameless and reckless creatures, they came on me and caught me, and abused me loudly. So I finished the web because I had to, against my will.

"And now I cannot escape the marriage, and I cannot think of any other plan, and my parents are pressing me to marry, and my son frets while these men eat up his living, and he takes note of it all; because he is a man by now, and quite able to look after a household to which Zeus grants honour. But even so, tell me about your stock and where you come from; you were not born from an oak of the old story, or from a stone."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Honoured wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, will you never stop asking me about my family? Well, I will tell you, though you will give me over to still more pain than I am held in now; because that is always how it is, when a man has been away from his country as long as I have now, wandering through the many cities of men in bitter distress. But even so I will tell you what you ask and enquire about.

"There is a land called Crete, in the middle of the wine-dark sea, a fine rich land, girt about with water, and there are a great many men in it, past counting, and ninety cities. They do not all have the same speech, but their tongues are mixed. There are Achaeans there, and great-hearted native Cretans, and Cydonians, and Dorians with waving plumes, and fine Pelasgians. Among their cities is the great city of Cnosus, where Minos was king from nine years old, who held converse with great Zeus, and was the father of my father, great-hearted Deucalion. Deucalion fathered me and prince Idomeneus. Idomeneus had gone off in his beaked ships to Ilios with the sons of Atreus; and my own famous name is Aethon. I was the younger by birth, and he was the elder and the better man.

"It was there that I saw Odysseus and gave him guest-gifts. The force of the wind had brought him to Crete too, as he was making for the land of Troy, and drove him off his course past Malea. So he anchored his ships at Amnisus, where the cave of Eilithyia is, in a difficult harbour, and he only just escaped the storm. Then he went up to the city at once and asked for Idomeneus, because he said he was his friend, loved and honoured. But it was the tenth or the eleventh dawn since Idomeneus had gone in his beaked ships to Ilios. So I took him to the house and gave him hospitality with a kindly welcome out of the rich store that was in the house; and to the rest of his companions who came with him I gathered barley meal and flaming wine and bulls for sacrifice out of the public store and gave it out, so that their hearts should be satisfied. There the good Achaeans stayed twelve days, because the strong north wind penned them there and would not let them stand on their feet on the land; some angry god had roused it. But on the thirteenth day the wind dropped and they put to sea."

He spoke, and made the many falsehoods of his tale seem like the truth; and as she listened her tears flowed and her face melted, as the snow melts on the high mountains, the snow which the east wind thaws when the west wind has strewn it, and as it melts the streams of the rivers run full: so her lovely cheeks melted as she wept and mourned for her husband, who was sitting beside her even then. And Odysseus pitied his weeping wife in his heart, but his eyes stood fixed between his lids as though they were horn or iron, and he hid his tears with guile.

But she, when she had had her fill of tearful wailing, answered him again and said: "Now, stranger, I mean to test you, and see whether you really did entertain my husband there in your halls with his godlike companions, as you say. Tell me what kind of clothes he wore on his body, and what kind of man he was himself; and tell me about the companions who went with him."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Lady, it is hard for a man who has been away so long to tell you this; it is the twentieth year now since he went away and left my country. But I will tell you as my mind pictures him. Noble Odysseus wore a fleecy purple cloak, a cloak of double fold; and the brooch on it was made of gold with double clasps, and on the front it was curiously worked: a hound held a dappled fawn in his forepaws and pinned it in his jaws as it writhed. And everyone marvelled at it, how, gold as they were, the hound was pinning the fawn and strangling it and the fawn was writhing with its feet and struggling to get away. And I noticed the tunic on his body, all shining like the sheen on the skin of a dried onion, it was so soft; and it glistened like the sun. A great many women gazed at him in wonder.

"And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. I do not know whether Odysseus was dressed like that at home, or whether one of his companions gave him the clothes when he went aboard the fast ship, or perhaps even some stranger; because Odysseus was dear to a great many men, and few of the Achaeans were his equal. I gave him a bronze sword myself, and a fine purple cloak of double fold and a fringed tunic, and sent him off on his benched ship with every honour. And a herald attended him, a little older than he was, and I will tell you about him too, what kind of man he was. He was round-shouldered, dark of skin, and curly-haired, and his name was Eurybates; and Odysseus honoured him above his other companions, because he was of one mind with him."

So he spoke, and roused in her still more the desire to weep, as she recognised the sure tokens that Odysseus told her of. But when she had had her fill of tearful wailing she answered him and said:

"Now, stranger, though you were pitied before, you shall be dear and honoured in my halls; because it was I who gave him those clothes, since you describe them so, and folded them and brought them out of the store-room, and added the shining brooch to be a joy to him. But my husband I shall never welcome back, coming home to his own dear country. So it was with an evil fate that Odysseus went off in the hollow ship to see evil Ilios, that should never be named."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Honoured wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, do not spoil your lovely face any more now, and do not waste your heart at all in weeping for your husband. I count it no blame in you; any woman weeps when she has lost the husband she married, to whom she has borne children in her love, even if he were a very different man from Odysseus, who they say is like the gods. But stop weeping and listen to me; I will tell you the sure truth and hide nothing.

"I heard only lately of the homecoming of Odysseus, that he is near at hand in the rich land of the Thesprotians, and alive, and is bringing many rich treasures with him, begging through the country. But he lost his trusty companions and his hollow ship on the wine-dark sea, as he was travelling from the island of Thrinacia; because Zeus and Helios grew angry with him, since his companions had killed the cattle of Helios. So they all died in the surging sea; but he was flung out by the wave on the keel of his ship onto the shore, onto the land of the Phaeacians, who are close kin to the gods. They honoured him heartily as if he were a god, and gave him many gifts, and wanted to send him home unhurt themselves. And Odysseus would have been here long ago, only it seemed to his mind more profitable to gather wealth by roaming over the wide earth; so truly does Odysseus know more gainful ways than any mortal man, and no other mortal could compete with him.

"That is the tale Pheidon, king of the Thesprotians, told me. And he swore in my own presence, as he poured offerings in his halls, that the ship was launched and the men ready who were to convey him to his own dear country. But he sent me off first, because a ship of the Thesprotians happened to be setting out for Dulichium, rich in wheat. And he showed me all the treasure Odysseus had gathered; it would feed his children after him to the tenth generation, so great was the wealth lying stored for him in the king's halls. But Odysseus, he said, had gone to Dodona to hear the will of Zeus from the high-crested oak of the god, and how he might come back to his own dear country after so long an absence, openly or in secret.

"So, as I tell you, he is safe and will come soon; he is very near, and he will not be far from his friends and his own country much longer. And I will give you an oath on it. Let Zeus be my witness first, the highest and best of gods, and the hearth of noble Odysseus that I have come to, that all this will happen exactly as I tell you. In the course of this very month Odysseus will come here, as the old moon wanes and the new appears."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Stranger, I wish this word of yours might be fulfilled. Then you would soon know kindness and many a gift from me, so that anyone who met you would call you a lucky man. But my heart forebodes how it will be: Odysseus will never come home any more, and you will not get a convoy from here, since there are no masters in the house now such as Odysseus was among men — as surely as there ever was such a man — to send strangers who deserve respect on their way, and to welcome them.

"But still, my maids, wash the stranger's feet and make his bed ready — a bedstead and cloaks and bright coverlets — so that he may come to golden-throned Dawn in warmth and comfort. And early in the morning bathe him and rub him with oil, so that he can think about his food in our house, sitting in the hall beside Telemachus. And it will be the worse for any man among them who vexes this man's soul with pain; he shall accomplish nothing here afterwards, however fierce his anger. Because how are you to learn about me, stranger, whether I am at all ahead of other women in wit and prudent counsel, if you sit at meat in my halls all unkempt and in poor clothes? Men are short-lived. If a man is hard himself and has a hard heart, all mortal men call down troubles on him for the time to come, while he is still alive, and when he is dead they all mock him. But if a man is blameless and has a blameless heart, strangers carry his fame far and wide among all men, and a great many call him a true man."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Honoured wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, cloaks and bright coverlets became hateful to me on the day I first left the snowy mountains of Crete behind me, travelling on my long-oared ship. I will lie down as I used to rest through sleepless nights in the past; because I have lain many a night on a foul bed and waited for bright-throned Dawn. And baths for the feet give my heart no pleasure either, and no woman shall touch my foot of all those who are serving-women in your hall — unless there is some old true-hearted woman who has suffered as many troubles in her heart as I have; her I would not grudge to touch my feet."

Then wise Penelope answered him again: "Dear stranger, no man as discreet as you, of all the strangers from far away, has ever come to my house as a more welcome guest, so wise and prudent is everything you say. I have an old woman with an understanding heart in her breast, who lovingly nursed and cherished my unlucky husband and took him in her arms on the day his mother bore him. She shall wash your feet, weak with age as she is. Come, wise Eurycleia, get up and wash the feet of a man of the same age as your master. The feet of Odysseus are probably like his now, and his hands too; men grow old quickly in bad fortune."

So she spoke, and the old woman hid her face in her hands and let the hot tears fall, saying words of lamentation: "Oh, my grief for you, child, and I can do nothing. Zeus must have hated you above all men, though you had a god-fearing heart. No mortal ever burned so many fat thigh-pieces to Zeus who hurls the thunderbolt, or so many choice hecatombs, as you gave him, praying that you might reach a sleek old age and rear your glorious son. And now from you alone he has wholly cut off the day of homecoming.

"So women mocked at him too, I suppose, in a strange and distant land, when he came to some man's glorious house, as all these shameless creatures here mock at you. It is to avoid their insults and their many taunts that you will not let them wash your feet; and the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, has told me to do it, and I am not unwilling. So I will wash your feet, both for Penelope's own sake and for yours, because the heart in me is stirred with sorrow. But listen to what I say. Many hard-tried strangers have come here, but I declare I have never yet seen any man so like another as you are like Odysseus, in build and in voice and in your feet."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Old woman, that is what everyone says who has seen the two of us, that we are very like each other, just as you notice and say yourself."

So he spoke, and the old woman took the shining cauldron with the water she was about to wash his feet with, and poured in cold water in plenty and then added the warm. But Odysseus sat down away from the hearth and turned himself at once towards the dark, because he suddenly had a foreboding in his heart that as she handled him she might notice a scar, and the truth would come out.

So she came near and began to wash her lord, and knew the scar of the wound at once, which a boar had dealt him long ago with his white tusk, when Odysseus had gone to Parnassus to visit Autolycus and the sons of Autolycus, his mother's noble father, who was better than any man at thieving and at oaths. It was a god himself who had given him that skill, Hermes; because he used to burn acceptable sacrifices to him of the thighs of lambs and kids, and Hermes befriended him with a ready heart.

Now Autolycus, coming once to the rich land of Ithaca, had found his daughter's son a new-born baby; and when he was finishing his supper Eurycleia laid the child on his knees and said to him: "Autolycus, find a name yourself to give to your child's own child; he has been prayed for a long time."

Then Autolycus answered her and said: "My daughter's husband, and my daughter — give him whatever name I say. Since I have come here as a man who has been angry with a great many, both men and women, over the fruitful earth, let the name the child is named by be Odysseus. And when he is grown and comes to the great house of his mother's kin at Parnassus, where my possessions are, I will give him some of them and send him back rejoicing."

That was why Odysseus had come, so that Autolycus might give him the glorious gifts. And Autolycus and the sons of Autolycus took his hands in welcome and greeted him with gentle words, and Amphithea, his mother's mother, took Odysseus in her arms and kissed his head and both his beautiful eyes. And Autolycus called to his glorious sons to get the meal ready, and they did as he called. At once they led in a bull, five years old, and flayed it and dressed it and cut up all the limbs; and then they sliced them cunningly and pierced them with spits and roasted them skilfully and shared out the portions. So all day long until sunset they feasted, and their hearts lacked nothing of the equal feast. But when the sun set and the dark came on they lay down to rest and took the gift of sleep.

And as soon as the early dawn appeared, rosy-fingered, they went out to the hunt, the hounds and the sons of Autolycus, and noble Odysseus went with them. They climbed the steep mountain Parnassus, clothed with forests, and soon reached its windy hollows. The sun was just striking on the fields, rising from the softly-gliding, deep-flowing Oceanus, when the beaters came to a glade. The hounds went in front of them, tracking the scent, and behind them the sons of Autolycus, and noble Odysseus among them, following close on the hounds, brandishing his long spear.

Now a great wild boar was lying there in a thick lair, through which the strength of the wet winds could never blow, and the rays of the bright sun could not beat, and the rain could not get through, it was so thick; and fallen leaves were in it in plenty. And the noise of the feet of men and dogs came round the boar as they pressed on in the chase, and he came out of his lair against them with his back bristling and his eyes flashing fire, and stood there at bay close in front of them.

Then Odysseus rushed on first of all, holding his long spear high in his strong hand, eager to strike him; but the boar was too quick for him and struck him above the knee, charging at him sideways, and tore a long gash in the flesh with his tusk, but did not reach the man's bone. But Odysseus struck him with sure aim on the right shoulder, and the point of the bright spear went clean through, and the boar fell in the dust with a cry, and his life flew from him.

Then the dear sons of Autolycus busied themselves with the carcase, and bound up the wound of noble godlike Odysseus skilfully, and checked the black blood with a charm, and went back at once to their dear father's house. And when Autolycus and the sons of Autolycus had fully healed him and given him glorious gifts, they sent him back quickly and with joy to his own country, to Ithaca. And his father and his honoured mother rejoiced at his coming, and asked him the whole story, how he got his wound; and he told them the whole truth, how a boar had struck him with his white tusk while he was hunting, when he had gone to Parnassus with the sons of Autolycus.

This scar the old woman knew by the touch when she had taken the limb in the flat of her hands, and she let the foot go. The leg fell into the basin and the bronze vessel rang; it tilted over, and the water was spilled on the ground. Then joy and grief came on her soul in one moment, and both her eyes filled with tears and the flow of her voice was checked. And she touched the chin of Odysseus and said:

"You are Odysseus, dear child, and I did not know you, until I had handled all the body of my lord."

She spoke, and looked towards Penelope with her eyes, wanting to show her that her dear husband was at home. But Penelope could not meet her glance or understand, because Athena had turned her thoughts aside. But Odysseus felt for the woman's throat and seized it with his right hand, and with the other drew her closer to him and said:

"Mother, why do you want to destroy me? You nursed me yourself at this breast of yours, and now after many hard labours I have come in the twentieth year to my own country. But since you have found me out, and a god has put this in your heart, be silent, in case anyone else in the halls learns of it. Because I will tell you outright, and it will happen: if a god beats the lordly suitors down for me, I will not spare you, my nurse though you are, when I kill the other serving-women in my halls."

Then wise Eurycleia answered him: "My child, what a thing to let past your teeth. You know how firm my spirit is, and unyielding; I shall be as close as hard stone or iron. And there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. If a god beats the lordly suitors down for you, then I will name over the women in your halls for you, which of them dishonour you and which are innocent."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Mother, why should you speak of them? There is no need at all. I shall mark them well myself and come to know each one. Keep the matter to yourself, and leave the outcome to the gods."

So he spoke, and the old woman went out through the hall to bring water for his feet, because all the first lot was spilled. And when she had washed him and rubbed him richly with oil, Odysseus drew his chair nearer the fire again to warm himself, and hid the scar with his rags.

Then wise Penelope was the first to speak: "Stranger, there is this one small thing more I will ask you myself, because it will soon be the hour for pleasant rest, for anyone at least who can have sweet sleep come over him for all his care. But a god has given me sorrow beyond all measure. Day by day I find my joy in mourning and lamenting while I look to my household tasks and my women's in the house; but when night comes and sleep takes hold of everyone, I lie on my bed, and sharp cares crowd close about my throbbing heart and disquiet me as I mourn.

"As when the daughter of Pandareus, the nightingale of the greenwood, sings sweetly when spring is newly come, perched among the thick leaves of the trees, and pours out her rich voice in many trilling notes, wailing for her child, dear Itylus, whom she once killed with the sword without knowing it, Itylus, the son of king Zethus — so my heart sways to and fro in doubt, whether to stay with my son and keep everything safe, my possessions, my slaves, and my great high-roofed house, respecting my husband's bed and the voice of the people, or to go now with whichever of the Achaeans is best, who courts me in the halls and offers bride-gifts past counting. And my son, as long as he was a child and slack of wit, would not let me marry and leave my husband's house; but now that he is grown and has reached the bounds of manhood, he even begs me to go away out of these halls, because he is vexed for his substance, which the Achaeans eat up at his cost.

"But listen to this dream of mine and interpret it for me. I have twenty geese in the house that come out of the water and eat wheat, and my heart warms with joy as I watch them. But a great eagle with a crooked beak came down from the mountain and broke all their necks and killed them; and they lay strewn in a heap in the halls, while he was carried up to the bright sky. And I wept and wailed, dream as it was, and the fair-haired Achaean women crowded round me while I grieved pitifully, because the eagle had killed my geese.

"Then back he came and perched on a projecting roof-beam, and checked my weeping with the voice of a mortal man, and said: 'Take heart, daughter of far-famed Icarius. This is no dream, but a true vision of good which will really be fulfilled. The geese are the suitors, and I, who was the eagle before, have now come back as your husband, and I will let loose a cruel doom on all the suitors.'

"So he spoke, and sweet sleep let me go; and looking about I saw the geese in the halls, feeding on wheat beside the trough, where they always used to feed."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Lady, there is no way of twisting this dream aside and giving it another meaning, since Odysseus himself has shown you how he will bring it about. The destruction of the suitors is plain to see, for every one of them; not one of them will escape death and the fates."

Then wise Penelope answered him again: "Stranger, dreams are baffling and unclear of meaning, and they are not fulfilled in everything for men. There are two gates of shadowy dreams: one is made of horn and one of ivory. Those dreams that come through the gate of sawn ivory deceive men, bringing words that are never fulfilled; but those that come out through the gate of polished horn bring true things to pass, when any mortal sees them. But I do not think my strange dream came from there. It would have been welcome to me if it had, and to my son.

"But there is another thing I will tell you, and you must take it to heart. This morning of evil name is coming now which is to cut me off from the house of Odysseus; because now I shall set as a contest those axes which he used to set up in a line in his halls, like the props of a ship that is building, twelve in all; and he would stand a long way off and shoot an arrow through them. Now I shall set this contest before the suitors: whoever most easily strings the bow in his hands and shoots an arrow through all twelve axes, with him I will go, and leave this house of my marriage, a house most beautiful and full of livelihood, which I think I shall remember even in my dreams."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Honoured wife of Odysseus, son of Laertes, do not put off this contest in your halls any longer; because I tell you Odysseus of the many tricks will be here before these men, handling this polished bow, have strung it and shot an arrow through the iron."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "If you would only be willing to sit here in my halls and give me pleasure, stranger, sleep would never be shed over my eyelids. But it is not possible for men to be sleepless for ever; the immortals have appointed a proper time for everything on the earth, the giver of grain. But I will go up to my upper room and lie on my bed, which has become a bed of wailing for me, always wet with my tears, since the day Odysseus went to see evil Ilios, that should never be named. I will lie down there; and you lie down here in the hall, when you have strewn bedding on the floor, or let the maids set a bedstead for you."

With that she went up to her bright upper room, not alone, because her women went with her too. And when she had gone up to her upper room with her women, she wept for Odysseus, her dear husband, until grey-eyed Athena put sweet sleep on her eyelids.

Book Twenty

But noble Odysseus lay down to sleep in the front hall of the house. He spread an undressed ox-hide on the ground and many fleeces of sheep over it, from the sheep the Achaeans kept killing; and Eurynome threw a cloak over him when he had lain down. There Odysseus lay sleepless, turning over evil for the suitors in his heart.

And the women came out of the hall, the ones who had been in the habit of sleeping with the suitors, laughing and joking among themselves. And the heart was stirred in his breast, and he debated a great deal in his mind and heart, whether to rush after them and deal death to each of them, or to let them lie with the insolent suitors for the last and latest time; and his heart growled inside him. As a bitch stands over her tender puppies growling when she sees a man she does not know, and is eager to fight, so his heart growled inside him in his anger at their evil doings. But he struck his breast and rebuked his heart:

"Endure, my heart. You endured a worse thing than this once, on the day the Cyclops, with no check on his daring, devoured my mighty companions; and you endured until cunning got you out of the cave where you thought you were going to die."

So he spoke, chiding the heart in his breast; and his heart stayed bound inside him and endured steadily. But he himself lay tossing this way and that. As a man before a great blazing fire turns a paunch full of fat and blood swiftly this way and that, very eager to have it roasted quickly, so Odysseus tossed from side to side, turning over how he might lay his hands on the shameless suitors, one man as he was against so many.

Then Athena came down from heaven and drew near to him in the likeness of a woman, and stood over his head and said to him: "Why are you awake again, unluckiest of all men? This is your house, and your wife is inside it, and your child, the kind of man anybody might pray to have for a son."

And Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Goddess, all this you have said rightly. But the heart in my breast is turning over how I may lay my hands on the shameless suitors, all alone as I am, while they stay in the house always in a body. And besides that I turn this other and harder thing over in my mind: even if I killed them by the will of Zeus and of yourself, where should I then find any escape from ruin? I ask you to think about that."

Then the goddess grey-eyed Athena answered him: "Obstinate man, a great many people trust even in a weaker friend than I am, one who is mortal and does not know such wisdom as mine. But I am a god, who guard you to the end in all your labours. And I will tell you openly: if fifty companies of mortal men stood round us, eager to kill us in battle, you would drive off their cattle and their fine sheep even then. Let sleep come over you now. There is weariness in keeping awake on watch the whole night through as well; and you are going to come out of your dangers now."

So she spoke, and poured sleep over his eyelids; and the beautiful goddess went back to Olympus herself.

Now while sleep took hold of him, loosening the cares of his heart, sleep that loosens the limbs of men, his true-hearted wife woke and wept, sitting up on her soft bed. And when her heart had had its fill of weeping, she first of all made her prayer to Artemis, that lovely woman:

"Artemis, mighty goddess, daughter of Zeus, I wish you would fix your arrow in my breast and take my life away this very hour; or that a storm wind would catch me up and carry me off over the murky ways and throw me out at the mouth of backward-flowing Oceanus, as the storm winds once carried off the daughters of Pandareus. The gods had killed their parents, and they were left orphans in the halls; and beautiful Aphrodite tended them with cheese and sweet honey and pleasant wine, and Hera gave them beauty and wisdom above all women, and chaste Artemis gave them stature, and Athena taught them skill in famous handiwork. But while beautiful Aphrodite was going to high Olympus to ask for the girls the accomplishment of a joyful marriage — going to Zeus who hurls the thunderbolt, because he knows everything well, both the happiness and the unhappiness of mortal men — meanwhile the spirits of the storm snatched the girls away and gave them to the hateful Erinyes to deal with.

"I wish those who live on Olympus would blot me out of sight like that, or that fair-haired Artemis would strike me, so that I might go down under the hateful earth with Odysseus before my mind, and never gladden the heart of a lesser man in any way. Yet when a man weeps by day with a heart in bitter distress, and sleep holds him at night, it brings an evil with it that can be borne — because sleep makes one forget everything, the good and the bad, once it wraps the eyelids. But on me a god sends evil dreams as well. This night again one lay beside me who was like him, just as he was when he went out with the army, and my heart was glad, because I thought it was no dream but the truth at last."

So she spoke, and golden-throned Dawn came at once. But noble Odysseus heard her voice as she wept, and thought about it, and it seemed to his heart that she knew him and was standing by his head. Then he gathered up the cloak and the fleeces he had been lying on and laid them on a chair in the hall, and carried the ox-hide out of doors and set it down; and he lifted up his hands and prayed to Zeus:

"Father Zeus, if you gods have brought me over land and sea to my own country of your goodwill, when you had afflicted me badly, let one of those who are waking speak a word of omen for me inside, and let a sign from Zeus be shown outside besides."

So he spoke in prayer, and Zeus the counsellor heard him. At once he thundered from gleaming Olympus, high up out of the clouds; and noble Odysseus was glad.

And a woman grinding at the mill spoke a word of omen from inside the house close by, where the mills of the shepherd of the people were set. Twelve women in all used to work at these mills, making meal of barley and of wheat, the marrow of men. The others were sleeping, because they had ground their wheat; but she alone had not finished yet, because she was the weakest of them all. She stopped her mill now and spoke a word, a sign for her master:

"Father Zeus, who are lord over gods and men, you have thundered loud from the starry sky, and there is not a cloud anywhere: this must be a sign you are showing to somebody. Fulfil for me too, wretched as I am, the word I shall speak. May the suitors hold their glad feast in the halls of Odysseus today for the last and latest time. They have loosened my limbs with bitter labour, making them barley meal; may this be the last supper they eat."

So she spoke, and noble Odysseus was glad at the word of omen and at the thunder of Zeus, because he thought he had got his revenge on the guilty.

Now the other maids in the fine palace of Odysseus had gathered and were kindling an unwearied fire on the hearth. And Telemachus got up from his bed, a godlike man, and put on his clothes. He slung his sharp sword over his shoulder and bound the fine sandals under his shining feet and took his mighty spear, tipped with sharp bronze, and went and stood on the threshold, and said to Eurycleia:

"Dear nurse, have you honoured the stranger in our house with a bed and food, or does he lie all uncared for? That is my mother's way, wise as she is: she honours one mortal man extraordinarily, though he is the worse man, and sends the better away without honour."

Then wise Eurycleia answered him: "You should not blame her in this, child; she is blameless. He sat here and drank wine as long as he wanted; and as for food, he said he had no more hunger, because she asked him. And when he thought about rest and sleep she told the maids to strew his bed. But he, like a man wholly wretched and unlucky, would not sleep on a bed and under blankets, but slept in the front hall on an undressed ox-hide and fleeces of sheep, and we flung a cloak over him."
