So she spoke, and Telemachus went out through the hall with his spear in his hand, and two swift hounds went with him. And he went off to the meeting place to join the company of the well-greaved Achaeans.

But Eurycleia, that good lady, daughter of Ops, son of Peisenor, called to her maids: "Come, some of you sweep the hall busily and sprinkle it, and throw purple coverlets over the shapely chairs; and let others wipe all the tables with sponges and clean the mixing-bowls and the well-made double cups; and let others go to the spring for water and bring it here quickly. The suitors will not be away from the hall long, but will come back very early; it is a feast-day for everyone."

So she spoke, and they listened readily and obeyed. Twenty of them went to the spring of dark water, and the others busied themselves in the house there, skilfully. Then the serving-men of the Achaeans came in, and split logs of wood well and skilfully; and the women came back from the spring.

After them came the swineherd, driving three boars, the best in all his herd. He left them to feed in the fine courtyards, and spoke to Odysseus himself with gentle words: "Stranger, do the Achaeans look on you with any more regard, or do they dishonour you in the halls as before?"

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Eumaeus, I wish the gods might take vengeance for the outrage these men contrive in their wantonness, in another man's house, wicked folly, with no room in them for shame."

So they talked to one another. And Melanthius the goatherd came near them, leading she-goats that were the best in all the herds, to make a feast for the suitors, and two herdsmen followed with him. He tethered the goats under the echoing portico and spoke to Odysseus himself with taunting words:

"Stranger, are you still going to be a plague to us here in the hall, asking alms of men, and will you not be gone? I think the two of us will not part company until we taste each other's fists; your begging is not decent in any way. And there are feasts of the Achaeans in other places besides this one."

So he spoke, but Odysseus of the many tricks made no answer; he only shook his head in silence, turning evil over in the depths of his heart.

Besides these a third man came, Philoetius, a leader of men, driving a barren heifer and fat she-goats for the suitors. They had been brought over from the mainland by ferrymen, who send other men on their way too, whoever comes to them. He tethered the beasts carefully under the echoing portico, and came close to the swineherd and questioned him:

"Who is this stranger, swineherd, who has just come to our house? What men does he say he is sprung from? Where are his kinsmen and his native fields? Unlucky man — and yet in build he is like a royal prince. But the gods bring far-wandering men to misery whenever they spin the threads of trouble for them, even if they are kings."

And with that he came up to Odysseus and held out his right hand in greeting and spoke to him with winged words: "Greetings, sir stranger; may good fortune be yours in time to come, though now you are the thrall of many sorrows. Father Zeus, no other god is more deadly than you: you have no pity on men when you have given them birth yourself, but bring them into misery and wretched pain.

"The sweat broke out on me when I noticed this man, and my eyes are full of tears as I think of Odysseus; because he too, I suppose, is dressed in rags like these and wandering among men, if he is still alive and sees the light of the sun. And if he is dead already and in the house of Hades, then my grief is for blameless Odysseus, who set me over his cattle when I was still a boy, in the land of the Cephallenians. And now they have grown past counting; the breed of broad-browed cattle could not increase better for any mortal man in any other way. But strangers tell me to drive these in for themselves to eat, and they care nothing for the son in the house, and do not tremble at the anger of the gods; they are eager now to divide up the possessions of our lord, who has been gone so long.

"Now the heart in my breast keeps turning this matter over: it is a very bad thing, while the son is alive, to go off with my cattle to a land of strangers, to a foreign people; but this is worse still, to stay here and suffer in charge of cattle that are handed over to others. I would have run away long ago and gone to some other of the proud kings, because things are past bearing now; but I still think of that unlucky man, in case he comes back from somewhere and scatters the suitors through his house."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Herdsman, since you seem to be neither an evil man nor a fool, and I can see for myself that you have an understanding heart, I will speak out and swear a great oath to confirm what I say. Let Zeus be my witness above all gods, and this hospitable table, and the hearth of noble Odysseus that I have come to, that while you are here Odysseus will come home, and you will see the killing of the suitors with your own eyes, if you want to, who lord it here."

Then the herdsman of the cattle answered him: "Stranger, I wish the son of Cronos would fulfil that word of yours. Then you would know what kind of might is mine, and how my hands obey me."

And in the same way Eumaeus prayed to all the gods that wise Odysseus might come back to his own home.

So they talked to one another. And the suitors meanwhile were plotting death and doom for Telemachus; but a bird came to them on their left, an eagle flying high, clutching a timid dove. Then Amphinomus spoke in their assembly and said: "Friends, this plan of ours will not run the way we want it to, the killing of Telemachus. Let us think about the feast."

So spoke Amphinomus, and his word pleased them. Then they went into the house of godlike Odysseus and laid their cloaks on the chairs and high seats, and men set about killing great sheep and fat goats, and fatted hogs, and the heifer from the herd. Then they roasted the entrails and served them out, and mixed wine in the bowls, and the swineherd handed out the cups. And Philoetius, a leader of men, handed them bread in a beautiful basket, and Melanthius poured their wine. So they put out their hands to the good food in front of them.

But Telemachus, with crafty thought, made Odysseus sit inside the well-built hall by the stone threshold, and set a mean stool and a little table for him. He set portions of the entrails beside him and poured wine into a golden cup, and said to him:

"Sit down here among the lords and drink your wine; and I will keep off the revilings and the blows of all the suitors from you myself. This is no public resort, but the house of Odysseus, and he won it for me. And you, suitors, keep your minds off rebukes and blows, so that no strife or quarrel arises."

So he spoke, and they all bit their lips and marvelled at Telemachus, that he spoke so boldly. And Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them: "Hard as it is, Achaeans, let us accept what Telemachus says, though he threatens us boldly in his speech. Zeus, son of Cronos, did not allow it, or we would have silenced him in the halls before now, clear-voiced talker as he is."

So spoke Antinous, but Telemachus paid no attention to his words.

Meanwhile the heralds were leading the holy hecatomb of the gods through the city, and the long-haired Achaeans gathered under a shady grove of Apollo, the archer god. And when they had roasted the outer flesh and drawn it off the spits they divided the portions and feasted a splendid feast. And those who served set a portion beside Odysseus equal to the one they took themselves, because Telemachus had ordered it, the dear son of godlike Odysseus.

But Athena would not let the proud suitors hold off from bitter outrage at all, so that the pain might sink deeper still into the heart of Odysseus, son of Laertes. There was a man among the suitors with his heart set on lawlessness — Ctesippus was his name, and his home was in Same — who, trusting in his boundless wealth, was courting the wife of Odysseus, who had been gone so long. He spoke now among the haughty suitors:

"Listen to me, proud suitors, and let me say something. The stranger has had a portion for a while now, an equal one, as is right; because it is neither good nor just to rob the guests of Telemachus of their due, whoever comes to this house. Come, I will give him a stranger's gift too, so that he can give a present in his turn either to the bath-woman or to some other of the slaves in the house of godlike Odysseus."

With that he took the hoof of an ox out of the basket where it lay and hurled it with a strong hand. But Odysseus avoided it with a quick turn of his head, and in his heart he smiled a grim and bitter smile; and the ox's hoof struck the well-built wall.

Then Telemachus rebuked Ctesippus and said: "Ctesippus, that turned out more to the profit of your soul. You did not hit the stranger, because he avoided your missile himself; or I would have run you through the middle with my sharp spear, and your father would have been busy with a funeral in this country instead of a wedding. So let nobody make a display of forwardness in my house, I warn you. I notice and understand everything now, the good and the bad, whereas before this I was only a child. And even so we still endure to watch these doings, while sheep are slaughtered and wine drunk and bread eaten up, because it is hard for one man to restrain many. But do me no more harm out of your own wickedness. And if you mean to kill me myself with the sword even now, I would choose that too; it would be far better to die than to go on watching these shameful things, strangers mishandled and men dragging the women through the fine hall in a shameful way."

So he spoke, and they were all hushed and silent; but at last Agelaus, son of Damastor, spoke among them: "Friends, no man should grow angry at what has been fairly said and answer with quarrelling words. Do not abuse the stranger any more, or any of the slaves in the house of godlike Odysseus. But to Telemachus and his mother I would say a gentle word, in case it finds favour in the minds of both. As long as the hearts in your breasts had hope that wise Odysseus would come back to his own house, there was no blame in your waiting and holding the suitors off in your halls; that was the better course, if Odysseus had come back to his house. But now it is plain that he will never come back. So come, sit by your mother and tell her this: that she must marry whichever man is the best and offers the most gifts, so that you may enjoy all your father's heritage in peace, eating and drinking, and she may keep another man's house."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "By Zeus, Agelaus, and by the sufferings of my father, who has died somewhere far from Ithaca or is wandering, I do not delay my mother's marriage in any way. I tell her to marry whatever man she likes, and I offer gifts past counting besides. But I am ashamed to drive her out of the hall against her will with a word of compulsion. May God never bring such a thing to pass."

So spoke Telemachus; but Pallas Athena roused unquenchable laughter among the suitors and turned their wits awry. And now they laughed with alien lips, and the flesh they ate was all dabbled with blood, and their eyes filled with tears and their spirits were set on wailing.

Then godlike Theoclymenus spoke among them: "Wretched men, what evil is this you are suffering? Your heads and your faces and your knees beneath you are shrouded in night; a sound of wailing has been kindled, your cheeks are bathed in tears, and the walls and the fine rafters are sprinkled with blood. And the porch is full of ghosts, and the court is full of them, ghosts hurrying down to Erebus under the dark; and the sun has perished out of heaven, and an evil mist hovers over everything."

So he spoke, but they all laughed merrily at him. And Eurymachus, son of Polybus, was the first to speak among them: "The stranger who has just come from abroad is mad. Quick, young men, take him out of doors to go to the meeting place, since he finds it like night here."

Then godlike Theoclymenus answered him: "Eurymachus, I am not asking you to give me guides for my way. I have eyes and ears and my two feet, and a mind in my breast that is not meanly made. With these I will go out of doors, because I can see evil coming on you which not one of the suitors will escape or avoid — all you who insult men in the house of godlike Odysseus and contrive wicked folly."

With that he went out of the stately halls and came to Peiraeus, who received him with a ready heart.

But all the suitors, looking at one another, set about provoking Telemachus by laughing at his guests. And one of the proud young men would say: "Telemachus, nobody is more unlucky in his guests than you, seeing that you keep a filthy vagabond like this man here, always wanting bread and wine, and not skilled in the works of peace or of war either, but a mere burden on the earth. And this other one stood up to prophesy. But it would be far better if you would listen to me: let us fling these strangers aboard a benched ship and send them to the Sicilians, where they would bring you in a fitting price."

So spoke the suitors; but he paid no attention to their words. In silence he watched his father, waiting all the time for the moment when he would lay his hands on the shameless suitors.

But the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, had set her beautiful chair opposite them, and heard what every man said in the hall. For they had got their meal ready in the middle of their laughing, a sweet meal and one to satisfy the heart, since they had killed a great many beasts. But no meal could ever have been more graceless than the supper a goddess and a mighty man were soon to set before them. For they had been contriving deeds of shame, and were not provoked to it.

Book Twenty-One

But the goddess grey-eyed Athena put it into the heart of the daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, to set the bow and the grey iron before the suitors in the halls of Odysseus, to be a contest and the beginning of death.

She climbed the high stairway to her room and took the bent key in her strong hand, a fine key of bronze with an ivory handle on it. And she went with her women to a store-room far off, where the treasures of her lord lay, bronze and gold and iron worked with labour. And the back-bent bow lay there, and the quiver that held the arrows, and there were many arrows in it, loaded with groans — gifts which a friend of Odysseus had given him when he met him once in Lacedaemon, Iphitus, son of Eurytus, a man like the immortals.

The two of them had met one another in Messene, in the house of wise Ortilochus. Odysseus had come to collect a debt the whole people owed him, because the men of Messene had lifted three hundred sheep from Ithaca in their benched ships, and the shepherds with them. It was on that embassy that Odysseus had come a long journey, while he was still a boy; his father and the other elders had sent him. And Iphitus for his part had come looking for twelve brood mares he had lost, with sturdy mules at the teat; but they brought him death and doom afterwards, when he came to the stout-hearted son of Zeus, the man Heracles, who knew all about deeds of daring. Heracles killed him, guest though he was, in his own house, ruthlessly, and had no regard for the anger of the gods or for the table he had set in front of him, but killed the man and kept the strong-hoofed mares in his halls himself.

It was while asking for these that Iphitus met Odysseus, and gave him the bow, which great Eurytus had carried in the old days and had left to his son at his death in his high house. And Odysseus gave Iphitus a sharp sword and a mighty spear, as the beginning of a loving friendship; but they never knew each other at the table, because before that could happen the son of Zeus killed Iphitus, son of Eurytus, a man like the immortals, who had given Odysseus the bow. Odysseus would never take this bow with him on the black ships when he went out to war, but it lay in his halls at home as a memorial of a dear friend, and he carried it in his own country.

Now when that lovely woman had come to the store-room and stepped on the threshold of oak, which the carpenter had planed skilfully long ago and made straight to the line — and had fitted doorposts to it and set bright doors on them — she quickly loosed the thong from the handle and thrust in the key and shot the bolts back with sure aim. And as a bull bellows grazing in a meadow, so the fine doors bellowed, struck by the key; and they flew open quickly in front of her.

Then she stepped up onto the high floor, where the chests stood with fragrant clothing stored in them, and stretched out her hand from there and took the bow off its peg, with the bright case that surrounded it. And she sat down there and laid the case on her knees and wept aloud, and took out her lord's bow.

But when she had had her fill of tearful wailing she went to the hall, to the company of the lordly suitors, carrying the back-bent bow in her hands and the quiver that held the arrows, and there were many arrows in it, loaded with groans. And her women carried a chest beside her, with plenty of iron and bronze in it, her lord's battle-gear.

Now when that lovely woman reached the suitors she stood by the doorpost of the well-built hall, holding her shining veil in front of her face, with a faithful maid on either side of her. And at once she spoke among the suitors:

"Listen to me, proud suitors, who have beset this house to eat and drink without end, since its master has been gone so long, and could find no other plea to urge except that you wanted to marry me and take me for a wife. Come then, suitors, since this is shown to be your prize. I will set the great bow of godlike Odysseus in front of you; and whoever most easily strings the bow in his hands and shoots an arrow through all twelve axes, with him I will go, and leave this house of my marriage, a house most beautiful and full of livelihood, which I think I shall remember even in my dreams."

So she spoke, and told Eumaeus, the good swineherd, to set the bow and the grey iron before the suitors. And Eumaeus took them and laid them down, bursting into tears; and in another part of the hall the herdsman wept when he saw his lord's bow.

Then Antinous rebuked them and said: "You foolish country creatures, who mind only the things of the day. Wretched pair, why do you shed tears now and trouble the soul in the lady's breast, when her heart lies low in pain as it is, since she has lost her dear husband? Sit and feast in silence, or go outside and weep, and leave the bow here behind for the suitors as a decisive contest; because I do not think this polished bow will be easy to string. There is no man among all these here such as Odysseus was; I saw him myself, and I remember him, though I was still only a child."

So he spoke; but the heart in his breast hoped that he would string the bow and shoot an arrow through the iron. Yet he was to be the first to taste an arrow from the hands of noble Odysseus, whom he was dishonouring then as he sat in the halls, and urging on all his companions.

Then the strong young Telemachus spoke among them: "Zeus, son of Cronos, must have made me witless. My dear mother, wise as she is, says she will follow another lord and leave this house; and I laugh, and am glad, with a witless mind. Come then, suitors, since this is shown to be your prize — a lady whose like is not in the Achaean land now, not in sacred Pylos or in Argos or in Mycene, nor in Ithaca itself, nor on the dark mainland. But you know that yourselves; what need have I to praise my mother? Come, do not put the matter aside with excuses, and do not turn away any longer from the drawing of the bow, so that we may see the outcome. And I would make trial of the bow myself. If I string it and shoot an arrow through the iron, it will not vex me that my honoured mother should leave this house and go off with another man, since I should be left here able to wield my father's fine battle-gear at last."

With that he flung the scarlet cloak off his back and sprang up, and laid his sharp sword off his shoulders. First he set the axes up, digging a trench, one long trench for all of them, and made it straight to the line, and stamped the earth in about them. And amazement seized everyone who saw him, that he set them out so orderly, when he had never seen it done before.

Then he went and stood on the threshold and began to try the bow. Three times he made it quiver in his eagerness to draw it, and three times he relaxed his effort, though in his heart he hoped to string the bow and shoot an arrow through the iron. And now at last he would have strung it by main strength, as he tried a fourth time to draw the string up; but Odysseus nodded in refusal and checked him in his eagerness.

Then the strong young Telemachus spoke among them again: "I shall be a coward and a weakling in the days to come, it seems, or else I am too young, and have no trust yet in my strength to defend me against a man when one grows angry over nothing. But come, you who are mightier than I am, try the bow and let us end the contest."

With that he set the bow away from him on the ground, leaning it against the jointed polished door, and leaned the swift arrow close by against the fine bow-tip; and then sat down again on the seat he had got up from.

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, spoke among them: "Get up in order, all of you, from left to right, beginning from the place where the cup-bearer pours the wine."

So spoke Antinous, and his word pleased them. Then Leiodes got up first, the son of Oenops, who was their soothsayer and always sat by the fine mixing-bowl in the innermost part of the hall. He alone hated deeds of wanton folly, and was full of indignation at all the suitors. He was the first to take the bow and the swift arrow now, and he went and stood on the threshold and began to try the bow; but he could not string it. His hands grew tired before that, as he tried to draw the string up, his soft unworn hands. And he spoke among the suitors:

"Friends, I am not the one to string it; let another take it. This bow will rob a great many princes of spirit and of life, since it is far better to die than to live on and fail of the thing we always gather here for, waiting expectantly day after day. There is many a man who hopes in his heart even now and wants to marry Penelope, the wife of Odysseus. But when he has tried the bow and seen the outcome, then let him court some other of the fair-robed Achaean women with his gifts and try to win her; and let Penelope marry the man who offers most and comes as her fated husband."

So he spoke, and set the bow away from him, leaning it against the jointed polished door, and leaned the swift arrow close by against the fine bow-tip; and then sat down on the seat he had got up from.

But Antinous rebuked him and said to him: "Leiodes, what a word has escaped the barrier of your teeth, a dreadful and grievous word. It makes me angry to hear it, if this bow is really going to rob princes of spirit and of life, because you are not able to string it. Your honoured mother did not bear you with the kind of strength to draw a bow and shoot arrows; but others of the lordly suitors will string it soon enough."

So he spoke, and called to Melanthius the goatherd: "Come, light a fire in the hall, Melanthius, and set a great seat beside it with a fleece on it, and bring out a great cake of the fat that is inside, so that we young men can warm the bow and rub it with fat, and try it, and end the contest."

So he spoke, and Melanthius at once rekindled the unwearied fire and brought and set a great seat by it with a fleece on it, and brought out a great cake of the fat that was inside. So the young men warmed the bow and tried it; but they could not string it, because they fell far short in strength.

Now Antinous was still holding off, and godlike Eurymachus, leaders of the suitors, who were far the best of them in ability. But those other two had gone out of the hall together, the herdsman and the swineherd of godlike Odysseus; and Odysseus himself went out of the house after them. And when they were outside the gates and the courtyard he spoke to them with gentle words:

"Herdsman, and you too, swineherd, shall I tell you something, or keep it to myself? My spirit tells me to say it. What kind of men would you be to defend Odysseus, if he came from somewhere suddenly like this, and some god brought him? Would you bring help to the suitors or to Odysseus? Speak out as your heart and spirit tell you."

Then the herdsman of the cattle answered him: "Father Zeus, I wish you would fulfil this wish. Grant that that man may come back, and that some god may guide him. Then you would know what kind of might is mine, and how my hands obey me."

And in the same way Eumaeus prayed to all the gods that wise Odysseus might come back to his own home.

But when he knew the minds of these two for certain, he answered and spoke to them again: "I am here at home before you, my very self. After many hard labours I have come in the twentieth year to my own country. And I know that of all my slaves it is you two alone who wanted me to come; of the rest I have not heard a single one praying that I might come back to my home. So I will tell you the truth, exactly as it will be. If a god beats the lordly suitors down for me, I will bring you each a wife, and will give you possessions and a house built near my own, and after that you two will be friends and brothers to Telemachus in my eyes. And more than that, I will show you a plain sign as well, so that you may know me well and be sure in your hearts: the scar of the wound a boar dealt me long ago with his white tusk, when I went to Parnassus with the sons of Autolycus."

With that he drew the rags aside from the great scar. And when the two of them had seen it and taken it all in, they flung their arms round wise Odysseus and wept; and they kissed his head and shoulders in loving welcome, and in the same way Odysseus kissed their heads and hands. And the light of the sun would have gone down on their weeping, if Odysseus himself had not checked them and said:

"Stop weeping and wailing now, in case somebody comes out of the hall and sees us and makes it known inside as well. Go in one after another, not all together — I first and you afterwards. And let this be the sign: all the rest, all the lordly suitors, will not allow the bow and the quiver to be given to me; but you, good Eumaeus, as you carry the bow through the halls, put it into my hands, and tell the women to bar the close-fitting doors of their hall. And if any of them hears groaning or the din of men inside our walls, let them not rush out, but stay where they are in silence at their work. And to you, good Philoetius, I give the charge of fastening the gate of the court with a bar, and quickly throwing a cord over it."

With that he went into the stately house and sat down on the seat he had got up from; and the two slaves of godlike Odysseus went in as well.

Eurymachus was handling the bow by now, warming it on this side and on that in the light of the fire; but even so he was not able to string it. And he groaned in his noble heart, and spoke to them with a burst of anger:

"I am grieved for myself and for all of you. It is not for the marriage that I mourn so much, grieved as I am; there are a great many other Achaean women, some in sea-girt Ithaca itself and some in other cities. But I mourn if we really fall so far short of godlike Odysseus in strength that we cannot string his bow. This is a reproach for men yet to come to hear of."

Then Antinous, son of Eupeithes, answered him: "Eurymachus, that will not be so, and you know it yourself. Today is the feast of the god through the land, a holy feast. Who would bend a bow on it? Set it quietly by; and as for the axes — what if we let them all stand as they are? Nobody, I think, will come to the hall of Odysseus, son of Laertes, and carry them off. Come, let the bearer pour drops into the cups for an offering, so that we can pour our offerings and lay the curved bow aside. And in the morning tell Melanthius the goatherd to bring she-goats, far the best in all the herds, so that we can lay thigh-pieces on the altar of Apollo, the famous archer; and then try the bow and end the contest."

So spoke Antinous, and his word pleased them. Then the heralds poured water over their hands, and boys filled the bowls to the brim and served them out to all, pouring the first drops into the cups for an offering. But when they had poured their offerings and drunk as much as they wanted, then Odysseus of the many tricks spoke among them, with cunning in his mind:

"Listen to me, suitors of the glorious queen, so that I can say what the heart in my breast tells me. It is to Eurymachus most of all that I make my plea, and to godlike Antinous, because this word of his was rightly spoken too, that you should stop trying the bow for the present and leave the outcome to the gods; and in the morning the god will give the victory to whoever he likes. But give me the polished bow, so that I can prove my hands and my strength among you, and see whether I still have the might I once had in my supple limbs, or whether my wanderings and my lack of food have destroyed it by now."

So he spoke, and they were all exceedingly angry, afraid he might string the polished bow. And Antinous rebuked him and said to him:

"Wretched stranger, you have no wits, not a trace of them. Are you not content to feast undisturbed in our proud company, and go short of nothing at the banquet, and hear our words and our talk, when no other stranger and beggar hears our words? It is wine that wounds you, honey-sweet wine, which does harm to others too, if a man takes it in great gulps and drinks beyond measure. It was wine that made even the centaur foolish, glorious Eurytion, in the hall of great-hearted Peirithous, when he went to the Lapiths. And when his heart had been made foolish with wine, in his madness he did evil in the house of Peirithous. Then grief seized the heroes, and they leapt up and dragged him out through the gateway when they had sheared his ears and his nostrils off with the pitiless bronze; and he went off with his heart made foolish, carrying the curse of his sin with him in the folly of his heart. That is where the feud between the centaurs and mankind began; but it was for himself first that he found the evil, being heavy with wine.

"So I declare great harm for you, if you string the bow. You will meet with no kindness at anyone's hands in our country, and we will send you straight away in a black ship to king Echetus, the maimer of all men, out of whose hands you will not get away alive. So be still and drink your wine, and do not compete with men younger than you are."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Antinous, it is neither good nor just to rob the guests of Telemachus of their due, whoever comes to this house. Do you think that if that stranger strings the great bow of Odysseus, trusting in his strength and his might, he will take me home and make me his wife? He has no such hope in his own breast, I am sure of it. So let none of you sit at meat here with sorrow in your heart on that account; that would be quite unseemly."

Then Eurymachus, son of Polybus, answered her: "Daughter of Icarius, wise Penelope, it is not that we think the man will take you home — that would be quite unseemly. It is that we dread the talk of men and women, in case some base fellow among the Achaeans should say afterwards: 'Truly, men far weaker are courting the wife of a noble man, and cannot string his polished bow. But another man, a beggar, who came in his wanderings, strung the bow easily and shot through the iron.' That is what men will say, and it would become a reproach to us."

Then wise Penelope answered him again: "Eurymachus, there can be no good report in the land for men who dishonour and consume the house of a prince. Why do you make this matter a reproach? This stranger is very tall and well built, and says he was born the son of a good father. Come, give him the polished bow and let us see. I tell you outright, and this word will come to pass: if he strings the bow, and Apollo grants him the glory, I will dress him in a cloak and tunic, good clothes, and give him a sharp javelin to keep off dogs and men, and a two-edged sword; and I will give him sandals to bind under his feet, and send him wherever his heart and spirit tell him to go."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered her: "Mother, as for the bow, no man of the Achaeans has a better right than I have to give it or refuse it to whoever I like — not one of all those who lord it in rocky Ithaca or in the islands towards horse-pasturing Elis. Not one of them shall thwart me against my will, even if I wanted to give this bow outright to the stranger to carry away with him. But go to your room and see to your own work, the loom and the distaff, and tell the women to get on with theirs. The bow will be the men's business, all of them, and mine most of all: the authority in this house is mine."

She went back to her room astonished, taking her son's good sense to heart. She went up to her upper room with her women, and wept for Odysseus, her dear husband, until grey-eyed Athena put sweet sleep on her eyelids.

Now the good swineherd had taken the curved bow and was carrying it, and the suitors all cried out in the halls. And one of the proud young men would say: "Where are you carrying the curved bow, you miserable swineherd, you distracted creature? Soon the swift hounds will devour you among your pigs, alone and apart from men, hounds you reared yourself — if only Apollo is gracious to us, and the other immortal gods."

So they spoke, and he set the bow down where he was carrying it, seized with fear because so many men were shouting in the halls. But Telemachus called out threateningly from the other side: "Father, carry the bow on — you will soon regret it if you listen to everybody — or I will drive you off to the fields, young as I am, and pelt you with stones; I am the better man in strength. I wish I were as much better in strength and might than all the suitors in the house; then I would soon send many a one of them out of our house to go on his way in a bad state, because they contrive wickedness."

So he spoke, and all the suitors laughed merrily at him, and relaxed the bitterness of their anger against Telemachus. And the swineherd carried the bow through the hall and came up to wise Odysseus and put it in his hands. Then he called the nurse Eurycleia out and said to her:

"Telemachus tells you, wise Eurycleia, to bar the close-fitting doors of the hall; and if any of the women hears groaning or the din of men inside our walls, they are not to rush out, but to stay where they are in silence at their work."

So he spoke, and her word stayed unwinged, and she barred the doors of the stately halls. And Philoetius hurried out of the house in silence and barred the gates of the well-fenced court. Now the cable of a curved ship was lying under the portico, made of byblus plant, and he made the gates fast with it, and then went inside himself. Then he came and sat down on the seat he had got up from, and gazed at Odysseus.

He was already handling the bow, turning it round and round and trying it this way and that, in case worms had eaten the horns while its lord was away. And one man would say with a glance at his neighbour: "He has a shrewd eye, and he is a cunning rogue with a bow. Perhaps he has bows like this stored away at home himself, or else he means to make one, the way he turns it this way and that in his hands, the rascally vagabond."

And another of the proud young men would say: "I hope the fellow finds profit in just the measure that he proves able to string this bow."

So the suitors spoke. But Odysseus of the many tricks, as soon as he had lifted the great bow and looked it over on every side — as a man well skilled in the lyre and in song stretches a string easily round a new peg, making the twisted sheep-gut fast at either end — so without effort Odysseus strung the great bow. And he held it in his right hand and tried the string, which sang sweetly under his touch, like a swallow in tone.

But great grief came on the suitors, and the colour of their faces changed; and Zeus thundered loud, showing his signs. Then much-enduring noble Odysseus was glad at heart, that the son of crooked-counselling Cronos had sent him an omen; and he took up a swift arrow, which lay by him on the table, bare, while the others were stored inside the hollow quiver — the ones the Achaeans were soon to taste. He took this and laid it on the bridge of the bow and drew the bowstring and the notched arrow, from the very chair where he sat, and let the shaft fly with sure aim; and he did not miss the end of the handle of a single one of the axes, but the bronze-weighted arrow passed clean through and out at the end.

And he said to Telemachus: "Telemachus, the stranger sitting in your halls brings no shame on you. I did not miss the mark at all, and I did not labour long in stringing the bow; my strength is unbroken still — not as the suitors taunt me and hold me in scorn. But now it is time that supper should be made ready for the Achaeans too, while there is still light; and after that there must be other sport as well, with song and with the lyre, since those are the accompaniments of a feast."

He spoke, and made a sign with his brows; and Telemachus, the dear son of godlike Odysseus, girded his sharp sword about him and took his spear in his grasp and stood by the chair at his father's side, armed in gleaming bronze.

Book Twenty-Two

Then Odysseus of the many tricks stripped off his rags and sprang to the great threshold with the bow and the quiver full of arrows, and poured the swift arrows out right there in front of his feet, and spoke among the suitors:

"So this decisive contest is over at last. Now I will see whether I can hit another mark, which no man has ever hit yet, and whether Apollo grants me the glory."

He spoke, and aimed a bitter arrow at Antinous. He was on the point of lifting a fine goblet to his lips, a two-eared cup of gold, and was handling it at that moment to drink the wine; and death was not in his thoughts. For who among men sitting at meat could think that one man among so many, however strong, would bring evil death and black fate on him?

But Odysseus took aim and struck him with an arrow in the throat, and the point went clean out through the tender neck. He sank to one side, and the cup fell from his hand as he was struck, and a thick jet of human blood came up at once through his nostrils; and he quickly thrust the table away from him with a kick of his foot and spilled all the food on the floor, and the bread and the roast meat were fouled.

Then the suitors broke into uproar through the halls when they saw the man fall, and they sprang up from their high seats, driven in fear through the hall, looking everywhere along the well-built walls; but nowhere was there a shield or a mighty spear to seize. And they railed at Odysseus with angry words:

"Stranger, you shoot at men to your own cost. You will never take part in any other contest; your utter destruction is certain now. You have killed a man who was far the best of the young men in Ithaca; so vultures will devour you here."

That is what each man said, because they thought he had not killed the man on purpose; and in their folly they did not know this, that the cords of destruction had been made fast over every one of them.

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave them a black look from under his brows and answered them: "You dogs, you thought I would never come home again from the land of the Trojans, and so you wasted my house and lay with the serving-women by force, and courted my wife by stealth while I was still alive, with no fear of the gods who hold the wide sky, or of the indignation of men that was to come afterwards. Now the cords of destruction have been made fast over every one of you."

So he spoke, and pale fear seized them all, and every man looked about to see how he might escape utter destruction. Only Eurymachus answered him:

"If you really are Odysseus of Ithaca, come home again, what you say is just about everything the Achaeans have done — many deeds of wanton folly in your halls and many in the fields. But the man who was to blame for all of it lies dead now, Antinous. He set these things going, not so much out of desire or need of the marriage but with another purpose, which the son of Cronos did not bring about for him: that he might be king himself in the land of settled Ithaca, and might lie in wait for your son and kill him. Now he has been killed, as he deserved. But spare your own people; and we will go about the land afterwards and get you recompense for everything that has been drunk and eaten in your halls, and each man will bring the worth of twenty oxen for himself in requital, and pay you back in bronze and gold until your heart is warmed. Until then nobody could blame you for being angry."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave him a black look from under his brows and answered him: "Eurymachus, not even if you gave me all your fathers' inheritance in requital, everything you have now, and added other wealth to it from wherever you could — not even then would I hold my hands from killing from now on, until the suitors had paid the full price for all their wrongdoing. It lies before you now to fight in open fight or to run, if any man can avoid death and the fates; but I think a good many of you will not escape utter destruction."

So he spoke, and their knees were loosened where they stood and their hearts melted. And Eurymachus spoke among them a second time:

"Friends, this man will not hold his invincible hands. Now that he has got the polished bow and the quiver, he will shoot from the smooth threshold until he has killed us all. So let us think about fighting. Draw your swords and hold the tables in front of you against the arrows that bring swift death, and let us all go at him in a body, hoping we may thrust him from the threshold and the doorway and get out through the city, and raise the alarm quickly; then this fellow would soon have shot his last."

With that he drew his sharp bronze sword, two-edged, and sprang at Odysseus with a terrible cry. But at the same instant noble Odysseus let an arrow fly and struck him on the breast beside the nipple, and fixed the swift shaft in his liver. And Eurymachus let the sword fall out of his hand to the ground, and writhed over the table and bowed and fell, and spilled the food and the two-handled cup on the floor. He beat the earth with his brow in the agony of his soul, and spurned and shook the chair with both his feet; and a mist was shed over his eyes.

Then Amphinomus went at glorious Odysseus, rushing straight at him, and had drawn his sharp sword, hoping Odysseus might give way in front of him from the door. But Telemachus was too quick for him, and cast and struck him from behind with his bronze-tipped spear between the shoulders, and drove it through his breast; and he fell with a thud and struck the ground full with his forehead. And Telemachus sprang back, leaving the long spear where it was, fixed in Amphinomus, because he was badly afraid that as he tried to draw the long spear out one of the Achaeans might rush at him and stab him with his sword, or deal him a blow as he stooped over the corpse.

So he started to run and came quickly to his dear father, and stood beside him and spoke winged words: "Father, now I will bring you a shield and two spears and a helmet all of bronze, well fitted to the temples; and when I come back I will arm myself, and give armour to the swineherd and to the herdsman too; it is better to be clothed in armour."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Run and bring them, while I still have arrows to defend me, in case they thrust me from the door, alone as I am."

So he spoke, and Telemachus did as his dear father said, and went to the store-chamber where the glorious arms were kept. He took four shields from there and eight spears and four bronze helmets with thick horse-hair plumes, and carried them out and came quickly to his dear father. Then first of all he girded the bronze about his own body, and the two slaves put the beautiful armour on in the same way, and took their stand on either side of Odysseus, the wise and cunning-minded.

And he, as long as he had arrows to defend him, kept aiming and striking the suitors one by one in his house, and they fell thick and fast. But when the arrows failed the prince as he shot, he leaned the bow against the doorpost of the well-built hall and let it stand against the bright entrance wall. And he put a four-fold shield about his shoulders and set a well-made helmet with a horse-hair plume on his mighty head, and the plume waved terribly above him; and he took two mighty spears tipped with bronze.

Now there was a certain postern door in the well-built wall, and along the topmost level of the threshold of the well-built hall there was a way into a passage, and well-fitting folding doors closed it. Odysseus told the good swineherd to watch this postern, taking his stand close by, because there was only one way to reach it.

Then Agelaus spoke among the suitors and declared his word to them all: "Friends, will not one of us climb up by the postern door and tell the people, so that the alarm may be raised at once? Then this fellow would soon have shot his last."

Then Melanthius the goatherd answered him: "It cannot be done, Agelaus, brought up by Zeus; the fine door of the court is terribly close, and the mouth of the passage is hard. One man could bar the way for everybody, if he were brave. But come, let me bring you arms to put on out of the store-room, because I think it is inside there, and nowhere else, that Odysseus and his glorious son have laid the arms."

With that Melanthius the goatherd climbed up by the steps of the hall to the store-rooms of Odysseus. He took twelve shields from there, and as many spears, and as many bronze helmets with thick horse-hair plumes, and went off and quickly brought them and gave them to the suitors.

Then the knees of Odysseus were loosened and his heart melted, when he saw them putting armour on and brandishing long spears in their hands, and his task looked great to him. And he quickly spoke winged words to Telemachus: "Telemachus, one of the women in the halls is rousing an evil battle against us — or perhaps it is Melanthius."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Father, it is I myself who am at fault in this, and nobody else is to blame, because I left the close-fitting door of the store-room open; and their watcher was better than I was. Go now, good Eumaeus, and shut the door of the store-room, and see whether it is one of the women who does this, or Melanthius, son of Dolius, as I suspect."

So they talked to one another. But Melanthius the goatherd went to the store-room again to bring back beautiful armour; and the good swineherd noticed him, and said at once to Odysseus, who was near:

"Son of Laertes, sprung from Zeus, Odysseus of the many tricks, there is that pestilent fellow again, the one we suspected ourselves, going to the store-room. Tell me truly, shall I kill him if I prove the better man, or bring him here to you, so that the fellow may pay for the many crimes he has planned in your house?"

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Telemachus and I will keep the lordly suitors inside the hall, however fierce they are; but you two bend his feet and his arms up behind him and throw him into the store-room and tie boards behind his back; and then make a twisted rope fast to his body and hoist him up the tall pillar until you bring him near the roof-beams, so that he stays alive a long while and suffers grievous torment."

So he spoke, and they listened readily and obeyed. They went out to the store-room, unseen by the man inside. He was searching for armour in the innermost part of the store-room, and the two of them lay in wait, standing on either side of the doorposts. And when Melanthius the goatherd was about to cross the threshold, carrying a fine helmet in one hand and a broad old shield in the other, flecked with rust — the shield of lord Laertes, which he used to carry in his youth, but which was laid by now with the seams of its straps loosened — then the two of them sprang on him and seized him. They dragged him in by the hair and flung him down on the ground in bitter terror, and bound his feet and hands with galling bonds, tying them firmly behind his back, as the son of Laertes told them, much-enduring noble Odysseus; and they made a twisted rope fast to his body and hoisted him up the tall pillar until they brought him near the roof-beams.

Then you mocked him, Eumaeus the swineherd, and said: "Now you will keep watch the whole night through, Melanthius, lying on a soft bed, as suits you; and you will not fail to notice the early Dawn, golden-throned, as she comes out from the streams of Oceanus, at the hour when you usually drive your she-goats for the suitors, to prepare a feast in the halls."

So he was left there, stretched in that deadly bond; and the two of them put their armour on and closed the bright door and went to Odysseus, the wise and cunning-minded. And there they stood, breathing fury — those on the threshold only four, while those inside the hall were many and brave.

Then Athena, daughter of Zeus, came near them, like Mentor in shape and voice; and Odysseus saw her and was glad, and said: "Mentor, keep off ruin, and remember me, your dear companion, who often did you good. You are of the same age as I am."

So he spoke, though he believed it was Athena, the rouser of armies. But the suitors on the other side shouted aloud in the hall, and Agelaus, son of Damastor, was the first to rebuke Athena:

"Mentor, do not let Odysseus beguile you with his words into fighting against the suitors and bringing help to himself. This is how our will is going to be brought about, I think: when we have killed these men, father and son, you shall be killed with them afterwards, for the kind of thing you mean to do in these halls; you shall pay the price with your own head. And when we have stripped you of your strength with the sword, all the possessions you have indoors and in the fields we will mingle with those of Odysseus, and we will not let your sons or your daughters live in your halls, or your faithful wife go about the city of Ithaca."

So he spoke, and Athena grew angrier still at heart, and rebuked Odysseus with angry words: "Odysseus, you no longer have the steady might or the courage that was yours when you fought the Trojans for nine years without stopping for high-born Helen of the white arms, and killed a great many men in dreadful fighting, and the broad-wayed city of Priam was taken by your counsel. How is it that now, when you have come to your house and your own possessions, you shrink and wail at playing the man, and against suitors? Come here, friend, and take your stand beside me and watch what I do, so that you may know what kind of man Mentor, son of Alcimus, is at repaying kindness in the middle of the enemy."

She spoke, but did not give him the strength to turn the course of the battle altogether; she was still testing the might and the courage of Odysseus and his glorious son. And for herself, she flew up to the roof-beam of the smoky hall and sat there in the likeness of a swallow, to look on.

Now the suitors were urged on by Agelaus, son of Damastor, and by Eurynomus, and Amphimedon and Demoptolemus and Peisander, son of Polyctor, and wise Polybus; because these were far the best in ability of all the suitors who were still alive and fighting for their lives; the rest the bow and the swiftly falling arrows had laid low by now.

And Agelaus spoke among them and declared his word to them all: "Friends, this man will hold his invincible hands at last. Look, Mentor has gone off from him and has only uttered empty boasts, and they are left alone there at the outer doors. So do not hurl your long spears at them all at once. Come, let six of you throw first, and hope that Zeus grants Odysseus to be hit and that we win the glory. Of the rest there is no need to think, once he has fallen."

So he spoke, and they all hurled their spears as he told them, eagerly. But Athena made them all come to nothing. One man struck the doorpost of the well-built hall, another the close-fitting door, another's ash spear, heavy with bronze, struck the wall. But when they had avoided the suitors' spears, much-enduring noble Odysseus spoke first among his men:

"Friends, now I give the word that we too should cast our spears into the crowd of the suitors, who mean to kill us on top of all their earlier wrongs."

So he spoke, and they all hurled their sharp spears with sure aim. Odysseus struck Demoptolemus, Telemachus struck Euryades, the swineherd struck Elatus, and the herdsman of the cattle killed Peisander. So all of these at the same moment bit the vast floor with their teeth, and the suitors drew back to the innermost part of the hall. And the others sprang forward and drew their spears out of the dead bodies.

Then the suitors hurled their sharp spears again, eagerly; but Athena made them come to nothing, many as they were. One man struck the doorpost of the well-built hall, another the close-fitting door, another's ash spear, heavy with bronze, struck the wall. But Amphimedon struck Telemachus on the hand by the wrist, a grazing blow, and the bronze tore the surface of the skin. And Ctesippus grazed the shoulder of Eumaeus with his long spear, over his shield, but the spear flew on and fell on the ground.

Then once more Odysseus, the wise and cunning-minded, and his company hurled their sharp spears into the crowd of the suitors; and again Odysseus, the sacker of cities, struck Eurydamas, and Telemachus struck Amphimedon, and the swineherd struck Polybus, and then the herdsman of the cattle struck Ctesippus in the breast, and boasted over him:

"Son of Polytherses, you lover of abuse, never speak big again, giving way to folly, but leave the matter to the gods, since they are far mightier. This is my gift of welcome to match the hoof you gave godlike Odysseus lately, when he went begging through the house."

So spoke the herdsman of the sleek cattle. And Odysseus wounded the son of Damastor in close fighting with a thrust of his long spear, and Telemachus wounded Leiocritus, son of Evenor, with a spear-thrust full in the groin, and drove the bronze clean through; and he fell headlong and struck the ground full with his forehead.

Then Athena held her aegis up on high from the roof, the bane of mortals, and the minds of the suitors were panic-stricken, and they fled through the halls like a herd of cattle that the darting gadfly falls on and drives along in the spring season, when the long days come. And as vultures with crooked talons and curved beaks come out of the mountains and dart on smaller birds, which scour the plain flying low beneath the clouds, and the vultures pounce on them and kill them, and they have no defence and no way of escape, and men are glad at the chase — so those others set on the suitors and struck them left and right through the hall. And a hideous groaning rose from it as heads were struck, and the whole floor swam with blood.

But Leiodes rushed forward and took hold of the knees of Odysseus and made his plea to him and spoke winged words: "I beg you by your knees, Odysseus; respect me and have pity. I declare to you that I never wronged one of the women in your halls by a wanton word or deed. I tried to check the other suitors when any of them would do such things. But they would not listen to me and hold their hands from evil, and so through their wanton folly they have met a cruel doom. And I, who was the soothsayer among them and have done no wrong, shall be laid low the same as they are; so true is it that there is no gratitude afterwards for good deeds done."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks gave him a black look from under his brows and answered him: "If you really declare yourself the soothsayer among these men, you must often have prayed in the halls that the outcome of a joyful homecoming should be kept far from me, and that my dear wife should go with you and bear you children. So you shall not escape a grievous death."

With that he seized in his strong hand a sword that lay near, which Agelaus had let fall to the ground when he was killed, and struck him full on the neck with it. And his head was mingled with the dust while he was still speaking.

Now the son of Terpes, the singer, was still trying to escape black fate — Phemius, who sang for the suitors because they made him. He stood with the clear-toned lyre in his hands near the postern door, and was divided in his mind whether to slip out of the hall and sit down by the well-built altar of great Zeus, the god of the court, where Laertes and Odysseus had burned so many thigh-pieces of oxen, or whether to rush forward and take hold of the knees of Odysseus in supplication. And as he turned it over, this seemed the better course, to take hold of the knees of Odysseus, son of Laertes. So he laid the hollow lyre on the ground between the mixing-bowl and the silver-studded chair, and rushed forward himself and took Odysseus by the knees and made his plea to him and spoke winged words:

"I beg you by your knees, Odysseus; respect me and have pity. Sorrow will come to you yourself afterwards if you kill a singer, one who sings to gods and men. I am self-taught, and the god has planted every kind of song in my heart; and I am fit to sing to you as to a god. So do not be eager to cut my throat. And Telemachus will bear witness to this too, your dear son, that it was through no will or wish of mine that I used to come to your house to sing to the suitors at their feasts; they were far more and stronger, and they brought me here by force."

So he spoke, and the strong young Telemachus heard him, and quickly said to his father, who was near: "Hold your hand, and do not wound this guiltless man with the sword. And let us save the herald Medon too, who always cared for me in our house when I was a child — unless Philoetius has killed him already, or the swineherd, or he ran into you as you raged through the house."

So he spoke, and Medon, who had good sense, heard him, because he was lying crouched under a chair and had wrapped himself in the skin of an ox, newly flayed, trying to avoid black fate. He got up at once from under the chair and stripped the ox-hide off, and rushed forward and took Telemachus by the knees and made his plea and spoke winged words:

"Friend, here I am. Hold your hand and tell your father to hold his, in case in the greatness of his strength he harms me with the sharp bronze in his anger against the suitors, who wasted his possessions in the halls and in their folly gave you no honour at all."

But Odysseus of the many tricks smiled and said to him: "Take heart; he has delivered you and saved you, so that you may know in your heart and tell another man too how much better the doing of good deeds is than of evil. But go out of the halls and sit down outside in the court, away from the slaughter, you and the singer of many songs, until I have finished everything I have to do in the house."

So he spoke, and the two of them went out of the hall and sat down by the altar of great Zeus, looking about on every side, expecting death all the time.

And Odysseus too looked about all through his house, to see if any man were still alive and hiding there, trying to avoid black fate. But he found every one of them fallen in the blood and the dust — the whole host of them, like fish that fishermen have drawn out of the grey sea in the meshes of their net onto the curving beach, and they all lie heaped on the sand longing for the waves of the sea, and the bright sun takes their life away: so the suitors lay now, heaped on one another.

Then Odysseus of the many tricks said to Telemachus: "Telemachus, go and call me the nurse Eurycleia, so that I can tell her what is in my mind."

So he spoke, and Telemachus did as his dear father said, and shook the door and said to Eurycleia: "Up and come here, old woman, who have charge of all our serving-women in the halls. Come; my father is calling you, so that he can tell you something."

So he spoke, and her word stayed unwinged. She opened the doors of the stately hall and came out, and Telemachus led the way in front of her.

There she found Odysseus among the bodies of the dead, all fouled with blood and filth, like a lion that comes from feeding on an ox of the farmyard, with all his breast and both his cheeks stained with blood, and he is terrible to look at: that was how Odysseus was fouled, his feet and his hands above.

But she, when she saw the corpses and the great welter of blood, was ready to raise loud cries of joy, seeing what a thing had been done. But Odysseus stopped her and checked her in her eagerness, and spoke to her with winged words:

"Rejoice in your own heart, old woman, but restrain yourself and do not cry out aloud. It is an unholy thing to boast over slain men. The fate of the gods destroyed these men, and their own reckless deeds; they honoured nobody on earth, evil or good, whoever came among them, and so by their wanton folly they brought a shameful death on themselves. But come, name over to me the women in the halls, which of them dishonour me and which are innocent."

Then his dear nurse Eurycleia answered him: "Then I will tell you the whole truth, my child. You have fifty serving-women in the halls, women we have taught to do their work, to card the wool and bear the lot of slaves. Of these, twelve in all have set their feet in the way of shamelessness, and take no notice of me or of Penelope herself. And Telemachus is only newly grown to manhood, and his mother would not let him rule over the serving-women. But let me go up to the bright upper room and take word to your wife, on whom some god has sent sleep."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her and said: "Do not wake her yet; tell those women to come here, the ones who have contrived shameful deeds in the past."

So he spoke, and the old woman went out through the hall to bring the women word and tell them to come. And Odysseus called Telemachus and the herdsman and the swineherd to him, and spoke winged words to them:

"Begin carrying the dead bodies out now, and tell the women to help you; and after that clean the beautiful chairs and the tables with water and porous sponges. And when you have set the whole house in order, lead the women out of the well-built hall to a place between the dome and the good fence of the court, and strike them down there with your long swords, until you have taken the life out of all of them, and they forget the love they had at the suitors' bidding, when they lay with them in secret."

So he spoke, and the women came all in a crowd, wailing terribly and shedding big tears. First they carried out the bodies of the dead and set them down under the portico of the well-fenced court, propping them one against another; and Odysseus gave the orders himself and hurried the work on, and they carried the bodies out because they had to. Then they cleaned the beautiful high seats and the tables with water and porous sponges. And Telemachus and the herdsman and the swineherd scraped the floor of the well-built house with hoes, and the women carried the scrapings out and threw them out of doors.

But when they had set the whole hall in order, they led the women out of the well-built hall to a place between the dome and the good fence of the court, and shut them into a narrow space, where there was no possible way of escape. Then clear-headed Telemachus was the first to speak to the others:

"Let it not be by any clean death that I take the lives of these women, who have poured reproaches on my own head and on my mother, and used to lie with the suitors."

So he spoke, and tied the cable of a dark-prowed ship to a great pillar and flung it round the dome, stretching it high up so that none of them could reach the ground with her feet. And as long-winged thrushes or doves fall into a snare set in a thicket as they try to reach their resting-place, and the bed that welcomes them is hateful — so the women held their heads in a row, and nooses were laid round the necks of all of them, so that they should die most pitifully. And they writhed a little while with their feet, but not long.

Then they led Melanthius out through the doorway and the court, and cut off his nostrils and his ears with the pitiless bronze, and drew out his vitals for the dogs to eat raw, and cut off his hands and his feet in their furious anger.

After that they washed their own hands and feet and went into the house to Odysseus, and the work was done. But Odysseus said to his dear nurse Eurycleia: "Bring sulphur, old woman, to cleanse from pollution, and bring me fire, so that I can purge the hall. And tell Penelope to come here with her women, and order all the women in the house to come."

Then his dear nurse Eurycleia answered him: "Yes, my child, all this you have said rightly. But come, let me bring you a cloak and a tunic for clothes, and do not stand like this in the halls with your broad shoulders wrapped in rags; that would give cause for blame."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered her: "First of all let a fire be made for me in the hall."

So he spoke, and his dear nurse Eurycleia did not disobey, but brought fire and sulphur; and Odysseus purged the hall thoroughly, and the house, and the court.

Then the old woman went back through the fine house of Odysseus to bring the women word and tell them to come; and they came out of their hall with torches in their hands. They crowded round Odysseus and embraced him, and clasped and kissed his head and his shoulders and his hands in loving welcome; and a sweet longing seized him to weep and wail, because in his heart he knew every one of them.

Book Twenty-Three

Then the old woman went up to the upper room, laughing aloud, to tell her mistress that her dear husband was in the house. Her knees moved nimbly, and her feet trotted along under her. And she stood over her lady's head and said to her:

"Wake up, Penelope, dear child, and see with your own eyes what you have wanted all your days. Odysseus is here; he has come home, late as his coming has been, and he has killed the proud suitors who troubled his house and ate up his substance and oppressed his son."

Then wise Penelope answered her: "Dear nurse, the gods have made you mad — they who can make even a very wise person foolish, and set the simple-minded on the paths of understanding. It is they who have spoiled your wits, when you were sound in mind before. Why do you mock me, when my heart is full of sorrow, telling me this wild tale, and rouse me out of sleep, the sweet sleep that bound me and wrapped my eyelids? I have never slept so soundly since the day Odysseus went off to see evil Ilios, that should never be named. Go down now and back to the women's hall. If any other of my women had come and told me this and roused me out of sleep, I would have sent her back into the hall in a sorry state at once. But your old age will bring you this much profit."

Then her dear nurse Eurycleia answered her: "I am not mocking you, dear child. Odysseus really is here, and has come home, just as I tell you. He is that stranger they all dishonoured in the halls. But Telemachus knew he was here long ago, and in his prudence hid his father's purpose, until he should take vengeance on the violence of overbearing men."

So she spoke, and Penelope was glad, and leapt out of bed and flung her arms round the old woman and let the tears fall from her eyelids; and she spoke to her with winged words:

"Come now, dear nurse, tell me truly: if he really has come home, as you say, how did he lay his hands on the shameless suitors, all alone as he was, when they stayed in the house always in a body?"

Then her dear nurse Eurycleia answered her: "I did not see, I did not ask; I only heard the groaning of men being killed. We women sat terror-stricken in the innermost part of our well-built rooms, and the close-fitting doors shut us in, until the hour when your son Telemachus called me from the hall — his father had sent him to call me. Then I found Odysseus standing among the bodies of the killed, and they were stretched all round him on the hard floor, lying one on another; the sight would have warmed your heart with cheer.

"And now the bodies are all gathered together at the gates of the court, and he is purging the fine house with sulphur and has kindled a great fire, and he has sent me to call you. Come with me, so that the hearts of you two may come into joy; you have suffered so much. And now at last this long desire of yours has been fulfilled: he has come himself, alive, to his own hearth, and has found both you and his son in the halls; and as for those who did him harm, the suitors, he has taken vengeance on every one of them in his house."

Then wise Penelope answered her: "Dear nurse, do not boast loudly over them with laughter yet. You know how welcome the sight of him in the halls would be to everyone, but most of all to me and to the son we two had. But this is no true tale as you tell it. One of the immortals has killed the lordly suitors in anger at their grievous insolence and their evil doings. They honoured nobody on earth, evil or good, whoever came among them; and so it is through their own wanton folly that they have suffered. But Odysseus has lost his homecoming to the land of Achaea far away, and is lost himself."

Then her dear nurse Eurycleia answered her: "My child, what a word has escaped the barrier of your teeth, saying that your husband, who is here at his own hearth this very moment, would never come home again. Your heart is always unbelieving. Come, I will tell you a plain sign besides: the scar of the wound the boar dealt him long ago with his white tusk. I noticed it while I was washing his feet, and I wanted to tell you as well, but he laid his hand on my mouth and in the great wisdom of his heart would not let me speak. Come with me. And I will stake my very life on it, that if I am deceiving you, you should kill me by the most pitiful death."

Then wise Penelope answered her: "Dear nurse, it is hard for you to understand the counsels of the gods who live for ever, however wise you are. But even so let us go to my son, so that I may see the suitors dead, and the man who killed them."

With that she went down from the upper room, and her heart turned over a great deal, whether to stand apart and question her dear husband, or to go up to him and clasp and kiss his head and hands. But when she had come in and crossed the stone threshold, she sat down opposite Odysseus in the light of the fire, by the further wall. And he was sitting by a tall pillar, looking down, waiting to see whether his noble wife would say anything to him when her eyes took him in. But she sat a long while in silence, and amazement came on her soul. Now with her eyes she would look full at his face, and now again she would fail to know him, because he had mean clothes on.

But Telemachus rebuked her and said to her: "Mother, cruel mother, with your unyielding heart, why do you hold off from my father like this, and not sit beside him and ask him questions? No other woman would harden her heart as you do and stand apart from a husband who has come back to her in the twentieth year to his own country after so much hard suffering. But your heart is always harder than stone."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "My child, the heart in my breast is lost in wonder, and I have no power to speak at all, or to ask a question, or to look him in the face. But if he really is Odysseus and has come home, then the two of us will know each other more surely; because we have signs which the two of us alone know, hidden from everyone else."

So she spoke, and much-enduring noble Odysseus smiled, and at once spoke winged words to Telemachus: "Telemachus, let your mother test me in the halls now; she will come to a surer knowledge presently. As it is, because I am filthy and dressed in mean clothing about my body, she looks down on me and will not yet admit that I am he. But let us think how it may all turn out for the best. When a man has killed only one man in a country, even a man who leaves few behind him to avenge him, he goes into exile and leaves his kinsmen and his own country; and we have killed men who were the very stay of the city, far the noblest of the young men of Ithaca. I ask you to think about that."

Then clear-headed Telemachus answered him: "Look to it yourself, dear father; they say your counsel is the best among men, and no other mortal could compete with you. We will follow with you eagerly, and I do not think we shall be wanting in courage, as far as our strength goes."

Then Odysseus of the many tricks answered him and said: "Then I will tell you what seems to me the best way. First bathe yourselves and put your tunics on, and tell the women in the halls to take their clothes. And let the divine singer, with his clear-toned lyre in his hand, lead us in a cheerful dance, so that any man who hears the sound from outside — a passer-by or one of the neighbours — may say it is a wedding feast; and then the rumour of the killing of the suitors will not spread through the city before we go out to our well-wooded farm. There we will afterwards think out whatever advantage the Olympian may grant us."

So he spoke, and they all listened readily and obeyed. First they bathed and put on their tunics, and the women dressed themselves, and the divine singer took the hollow lyre and roused in them the desire for sweet song and good dancing. So the great hall rang all round with the tread of dancing men and fair-girdled women; and anyone who heard the noise from outside the house would say:

"So somebody has married the queen who was courted by so many. Cruel woman, she had not the heart to keep the great house of the husband she married to the end, until he should come."

That is what they would say; but they did not know how these things were.

Meanwhile Eurynome the housekeeper bathed great-hearted Odysseus in his house and rubbed him with oil and threw a fine cloak and a tunic round him; and Athena shed abundant beauty over his head, making him taller to look at and stronger, and made the hair flow down from his head in curls like the hyacinth flower. As when a man lays gold over silver, a skilled workman whom Hephaestus and Pallas Athena have taught every kind of craft, and the work he turns out is full of grace — so the goddess shed grace on his head and shoulders. And he came out of the bath like the immortals in form.

Then he sat down again on the chair he had got up from, opposite his wife, and said to her: "Strange lady, to you beyond all women the dwellers on Olympus have given a heart that cannot be softened. No other woman would harden her heart as you do and stand apart from a husband who has come to her in the twentieth year to his own country after so much hard suffering. Come then, nurse, strew me a bed, so that I may lie down alone; the heart in her breast is made of iron."

Then wise Penelope answered him: "Strange man, I am not proud in any way, and I do not scorn you, and I am not too greatly astonished either; but I know very well what kind of man you were when you went out of Ithaca on your long-oared ship. Come then, Eurycleia, strew the strong bedstead for him outside the well-built bridal chamber which he made himself. Bring the strong bedstead out there for him and throw bedding on it, fleeces and cloaks and bright coverlets."

So she spoke, and made trial of her husband. But Odysseus spoke to his true-hearted wife in a burst of anger:

"Woman, that is a bitter word you have spoken. Who has moved my bed somewhere else? It would be hard for anybody, however skilled, unless a god came himself and easily set it in another place by his will. Of men there is no mortal alive, not the youngest and strongest, who could easily prise it from its place; because a great token is worked into that fashioned bed, and it was I who built it and nobody else. A bush of long-leafed olive was growing inside the court, strong and vigorous, and thick as a pillar. I built my chamber round it, until I had finished it, with close-set stones, and roofed it over well, and added jointed doors, close-fitting. And then I cut away the leafy branches of the long-leafed olive, and trimmed the trunk from the root, and smoothed it round with the adze, well and cunningly, and made it straight to the line, so making the bed-post; and I bored it all with the auger. Beginning from that, I hewed out my bed until I had finished it, inlaying it with gold and silver and ivory; and I stretched a thong of ox-hide on it, bright with purple. That is the token I declare to you. But I do not know, woman, whether my bedstead is still fast in its place, or whether some man has cut it away from under the olive stump by now and set the bedstead somewhere else."

So he spoke, and her knees were loosened where she sat, and her heart melted, as she knew the sure tokens Odysseus told her. Then she ran straight towards him with a burst of tears, and flung her arms round the neck of Odysseus and kissed his head, and said:

"Do not be vexed with me, Odysseus; in everything else you were always the wisest of men. It was the gods who gave us sorrow, the gods who grudged that we two should stay with each other and enjoy our youth and come to the threshold of old age together. Do not be angry with me now over this, or full of indignation, because when I first saw you I did not welcome you like this. The heart in my breast was always full of dread, in case some man should come and beguile me with his words; there are a great many who plan wicked devices. Argive Helen, daughter of Zeus, would not have lain in love with a man of another people if she had known that the warlike sons of the Achaeans were going to bring her home again to her own dear country. It was a god who drove her to do that shameful thing; she never laid up the thought of that folly in her mind until then, the grievous folly that first brought sorrow on us too. But now, since you have told the clear tokens of our bed, which no mortal has ever seen except you and me alone and one single woman, the daughter of Actor, whom my father gave me before I came here, who kept the doors of our strong bridal chamber — now you convince my heart, unbending as it is."

So she spoke, and roused in him a still greater desire for lamentation; and he wept, holding his dear and true-hearted wife in his arms.
